Frans Hals heaved a deep sigh of satisfaction. Cornelius Beresteyn, overcome by emotion, could not at first utter a word. He put out his hand, groping for that of the man who had fanned the flames of hope into living activity.
Diogenes, solemnly trying to look grave and earnest, took the hand thus loyally offered to him. He could have laughed aloud at the absurdity of the present situation.

===Chinese===

“朋友的赞词没什么可羞的，先生，”科尼利厄斯·贝雷斯坦安详庄重地说，“对像我这样的老人，看一眼您这样仪表堂堂的人，就是一件乐事。唉，先生！往后的日子，我认得的只有悲伤。……我的女儿……您已经听说了……？”
“压在您灵魂上的这份苦楚，我知道，先生。”第欧根尼平平地答。
“那么您也听见了您朋友对您的夸奖？”老人接着说，被泪浸花的眼里闪着新生的希望的微光，“咱们这位好哈尔斯热心，爱浪漫……兴许他说过了头……实际上兴许看错了人。……”
看着这不幸的父亲这样明明白白悬在指望和害怕之间，实在凄惨：他的手抖着，破碎的嗓音里带着哀告——告求不要受骗，告求不要从晕眩的希望之巅，重新掼回先前那绝望的深渊。
“我的女儿，先生……”他有气无力地喃喃，“她就是我的整个世界……她母亲死时她还在襁褓……她就是我的整个世界……他们把她从我身边抢走了……她还这么年轻，先生……这么美……她就是我的整个世界……只要她能平平安安回到我怀里，我愿拿一半家财去换。……”
此后，这间古雅的老式屋子里一片寂静——只有那不幸人压抑的呜咽在打破它。其余人围桌而坐，一言不发：贝雷斯坦的悲痛勾起的哀恸，不是言语装得下的。哈尔斯的眼睛盯着朋友，想读懂那张易变的脸上每一道线里悬着的谜样的笑，却怎么也读不懂。静默只撑了几秒：下一刻，第欧根尼那响亮的嗓音重新叫每一处藏着的回声从这根椽子跳到那根椽子。
“先生！”他高声道，“您丢了女儿。我在这儿听候您的差遣，我以上帝起誓，定把您的女儿平平安安给您带回来。”
弗兰斯·哈尔斯长长舒了一口满足的气。科尼利厄斯·贝雷斯坦激动得一时说不出话。他伸出手，去摸那扇起了希望的火、把它扇成活焰的人的手。
第欧根尼板起脸，装出一副郑重诚恳，握住了这样忠义地伸来的手。眼前的处境荒唐透顶，他真想放声大笑。

===Original===

He﻿—pledged by solemn word of honour to convey Jongejuffrouw Beresteyn to Rotterdam and there to place her into the custody of Ben Isaje, merchant of that city, he﻿—carrying inside his doublet an order to Ben Isaje to pay him 3,000 guilders, he﻿—known to the jongejuffrouw as the author of the outrage against her person, he was here solemnly pledging himself to restore her safely into her father’s arms. How this was to be fulfilled, how he would contrive to earn that comfortable half of a rich Haarlem merchant’s fortune, he had﻿—we may take it﻿—at the present moment, not the remotest idea: for indeed, the conveying of the jongejuffrouw back to Haarlem would be no difficult matter, once his promise to Nicolaes Beresteyn had been redeemed. The question merely was how to do this without being denounced by the lady herself as an impudent and double-dealing knave, which forsooth she already held him to be.
Cornelius and his friends, however, gave him no time now for further reflection. All the thinking out would have to be done presently﻿—no doubt on the way between Haarlem and Houdekerk, and probably in a mist of driving snow﻿—for the nonce he had to stand under the fire of unstinted eulogy hurled at him from every side.
“Well spoken, young man!”
“ ’Tis gallant bearing forsooth!”
“Chivalry, indeed, is not yet dead in Holland.”
“Are you a Dutchman, sir?”
To this direct query he gave reply:
“My father was one of those who came in English Leicester’s train, whose home was among the fogs of England and under the shadow of her white, mysterious cliffs. My mother was Dutch and he broke her heart﻿ ﻿…”
“Not an unusual story, alas, these times!” quoth a sober mynheer with a sigh. “I know of more than one case like your own, sir. Those English adventurers were well favoured and smooth tongued, and when they gaily returned to their seagirt island they left a long trail behind them of broken hearts﻿—of sorrowing women and forsaken children.”
“My mother, sir, was a saint,” rejoined Diogenes earnestly, “my father married her in Amsterdam when she was only eighteen. She was his wife, yet he left her homeless and his son fatherless.”
“But if he saw you, sir, as you are,” said Cornelius Beresteyn kindly, “he would surely make amends.”
“But he shall not see me, sir,” retorted Diogenes lightly, “for I hate him so, because of the wrong he did to my mother and to me.

===Chinese===

他——以名誉的庄严誓言许下，要把贝雷斯坦小姐送往鹿特丹，交到该城商人本·伊萨耶的看管之下；他——贴身的上衣里揣着给本·伊萨耶的指令，叫那人付他三千盾；他——在那位小姐眼里，正是对她本人施暴的元凶——如今却在这里庄严起誓，要把她平平安安送回她父亲的怀抱。这誓怎么兑现，这半个哈勒姆富商家财的舒服日子他怎么挣到手——我们不妨认定——此刻他连最模糊的主意也没有。因为说实在的，对尼古莱斯·贝雷斯坦的诺言一兑现，把小姐送回哈勒姆原非难事。难只难在：怎样办才不叫那位小姐本人把他当个无耻的两面派无赖揭出来——她已经拿他当无赖看了。
可科尼利厄斯和他的朋友们没容他多想。想，只能留待回头——无疑是在哈勒姆到豪德凯尔克的路上，多半还得顶着漫天飞雪——眼下他得顶住四面八方掼来的不吝赞美的炮火。
“说得好，年轻人！”
“好一副豪侠气概！”
“侠义在荷兰总算没死。”
“先生是荷兰人吗？”
对这直统统的一问，他答：
“我父亲是随英国人莱斯特来的那伙人里的一个，家在英格兰的雾里，在她那白色的神秘峭壁的影子底下。我母亲是荷兰人，他伤了她的心……”“
“唉，这些年这样的故事不新鲜了！”一位持重的先生叹道，“跟您一样的情形，我见得不止一桩，先生。那些英国冒险家仪表好、舌头甜，等他们欢欢喜喜回他们四面环海的岛子，身后留下长长一串碎掉的心——伤心的女人和没人认的孩子。”
“我的母亲，先生，是一位圣女，”第欧根尼恳切地应，“我父亲在阿姆斯特丹娶她时，她才十八岁。她是他的妻子，他却撇下她无家可归，撇下他的儿子没有父亲。”
“可他若见到如今的您，先生，”科尼利厄斯·贝雷斯坦好心地说，“他必定要补偿的。”
“可他不许见我，先生，”第欧根尼轻轻回敬，“因为我恨他，为他加给我母亲和我的冤屈。

===Original===

He shall never even hear of me unless I succeed in carving mine own independent fortune, or contrive to die like a gentleman.”
“Both of which, sir, you will surely do,” now interposed Beresteyn with solemn conviction. “Your acts and words do proclaim you a gentleman, and therefore you will die one day, just as you have lived. In the meanwhile, I am as good as my word. My daughter’s safety, her life and her honour are worth a fortune to me. I am reputed a wealthy man. My business is vast, and I have one million guilders lying at interest in the hands of Mynheer Bergansius the world-famed jeweller of Amsterdam. One-half that money, sir, shall be yours together with my boundless gratitude, if you deliver my daughter out of the hands of the malefactors who have seized her person and bring her back safe and sound to me.”
“If life is granted me, sir,” rejoined Diogenes imperturbably, without a blush or a tremor, “I will find your daughter and bring her safely to you as speedily as God will allow me.”
“But you cannot do this alone, sir﻿ ﻿…” urged Cornelius, on whom doubt and fear had not yet lost their hold. “How will you set to work?”
“That, mynheer, is my secret,” rejoined Diogenes placidly, “and the discussion of my plans might jeopardise their success.”
“True, sir; but remember that the anxiety which I suffer now will be increased day by day, until it brings me on the threshold of the grave.”
“I will remember that, mynheer, and will act as promptly as may be; but the malefactors have twenty-four hours start of me. I may have to journey far ere I come upon their track.”
“But you will have companions with you, sir? Friends who will help and stand by you. Those sea-wolves are notorious for their daring and their cruelty﻿ ﻿… they may be more numerous too than you think.﻿ ﻿…”
“The harder the task, mynheer,” said Diogenes with his enigmatical smile, “the greater will be my satisfaction if I succeed in fulfilling it.”
“But though you will own to no kindred, surely you have friends?” insisted Beresteyn.
“Two faithful allies, and my sword, the most faithful of them all,” replied the other.
“You will let me furnish you with money in advance, I hope.”
“Not till I have earned it, mynheer.”
“You are proud, sir, as well as chivalrous,” retorted Cornelius.
“I pray you praise me not, mynheer. Greed after money is my sole motive in undertaking this affair.”

===Chinese===

（接上）他永远不许听到我的下落——除非我自己闯出一份独立的家业，或者想法子死得像个绅士。”
“而这两样，先生，您都必定办得到，”贝雷斯坦这时插进来，信念郑重，“您的言行为您作证：您是位绅士，因此有一天您也会死得像您活得一样。至于眼下，我一诺千金。我女儿的安全、性命和名节，在我抵得一份家财。人人都说我是有钱人。我的买卖大，阿姆斯特那位名满天下的珠宝商贝尔甘修斯先生手里，还放着一百万盾生利息。那一半的钱，先生，就是您的了，连同我无边的感激——只要您把我女儿从掳她的恶徒手里解出来，完完整整带回我的身边。”
“只要上天容我活着，先生，”第欧根尼不动声色地应道，脸不红，心不跳，“我就会找到您的女儿，在上帝容许的范围里，尽快平安带到您面前。”
“可您一个人办不成，先生……”科尼利厄斯催道，疑惧还没放过他，“您打算怎么着手？”
“那个，先生，是我的秘密，”第欧根尼安然地答，“我的计划一议论，恐怕就要坏了事。”
“不错，先生；可记住，我眼下受的煎熬会一天重似一天，直到把我拖到坟墓门槛上。”
“这个我记下了，先生，也会尽力从速；可恶徒们抢在我前头整整二十四个钟头。追上他们的踪迹之前，我可能要走很远。”
“可您总得有伴儿吧，先生？帮您、护您的朋友。那伙海狼是以胆大心狠出名的……人数兴许也比您想的多。……”
“差事越难，先生，”第欧根尼带着他那谜一样的笑说，“办成了，我的得意就越大。”
“可您就算没有亲眷，总有朋友吧？”贝雷斯坦不依。
“两个忠实的盟友，还有我的剑——它是最忠实的一个。”对方答。
“让我先给您些盘缠，总可以吧。”
“挣到之前不要，先生。”
“您骄傲，先生，同您的侠义一样。”科尼利厄斯回敬。
“请您别夸我，先生。贪图钱财，才是我揽下这桩事的唯一动机。”

===Original===

“This I’ll not believe,” concluded Beresteyn as he now rose to go. “Let me tell you, sir, that by your words, your very presence, you have put new life, new hope into me. Something tells me that I can trust you﻿ ﻿… something tells me that you will succeed.﻿ ﻿… Without kith or kindred, sir, a man may rise to fortune by his valour: ’tis writ in your face that you are such an one. With half a million guilders so earned a man can aspire to the fairest in the land,” he added not without significance, “and there is no father who would not be proud to own such a son.”
He then shook Diogenes warmly by the hand. He was a different man to the poor grief-stricken rag of humanity who had entered this tavern a few hours ago. His friends also shook the young man by the hand and said a great many more gracious and complimentary words to him which he accepted in grave silence, his merry eyes twinkling with the humour of it all.
The worthy burghers filed out of the taproom one by one, in the wake of Cornelius. It was bitterly cold and the snow was again falling: they wrapped their fur-lined mantles closely round them ere going out of the warm room, but their hats they kept in their hands until the last, and were loth to turn their backs on Diogenes as they went. They felt as if they were leaving the presence of some great personage.
It was only when the heavy oaken door had fallen to for the last time behind the pompous soberly-clad figures of the mynheers and Diogenes found himself alone in the *tapperij* with his friend Frans Hals that he at last gave vent to that overpowering sense of merriment which had all along threatened to break its bonds. He sank into the nearest chair:
“Dondersteen! Dondersteen!” he exclaimed between the several outbursts of irrepressible laughter which shook his powerful frame and brought the tears to his eyes, “Gods in Olympia! have you ever seen the like? *Verrek jezelf*, my good Hals, you should go straight to Paradise when you die for having brought about this heaven-born situation. Dondersteen! Dondersteen! I had promised myself two or three hours’ sleep, but we must have a bottle of Beek’s famous wine on this first!”
And Frans Hals could not for the life of him understand what there was in this fine situation that should so arouse Diogenes’ mirth.

===Chinese===

“这个我不信，”贝雷斯坦起身要走时收尾道，“告诉您，先生，您这一番话，您这个人往这儿一站，就给了我新生命、新指望。有什么在告诉我，我可以信您……有什么在告诉我，您会成事。……无亲无故，先生，一个人凭勇武也能立起家业：您脸上写明，您就是这样的人。挣来五十万盾，这样一个人就有资格求全荷兰最好的人家，”他意有所指地补上一句，“没有哪个父亲，不以有这样的儿子为荣。”
说着他热情地同第欧根尼握手。他已不是几个钟头前走进这家客店时那可怜的、被悲痛压垮的一把骨头了。朋友们也一一同年轻人握手，又说了许多客气夸奖的话，他一概庄重地默默领受，一双笑眼为这满场的滑稽闪个不停。
体面的市民们一个接一个，随科尼利厄斯之后鱼贯出了酒室。外头冷得刺骨，雪又下了起来：出这暖屋之前，他们把毛皮衬里的斗篷紧了又紧，帽子却直到最后还掂在手里，走时恋恋不舍，不肯把背亮给第欧根尼。他们觉得自己像是在告退某位大人物。
直到沉重的橡木门最后一次在那几位衣着庄重的先生身后合拢、第欧根尼发现自己同朋友弗兰斯·哈尔斯独处在酒室里，他才终于放开了那股一直挣着要出笼的欢快。他倒进最近的一把椅子：
“该死的雷公！该死的雷公！”他喊，一阵阵忍不住的大笑摇着他魁伟的身架，笑出了眼泪，“奥林匹亚的诸神啊！你见过这样的场面没有？见你的鬼去（Verrek jezelf），我的好哈尔斯，你死的时候该直接升天堂，就为你搅出了这天上掉下来的局面。该死的雷公！该死的雷公！我本打算睡两三个钟头，可先得来一瓶贝克家出名的好酒！”
而这局面妙在哪儿、竟能这样勾起第欧根尼的兴致，弗兰斯·哈尔斯死活也想不明白。

===Original===

But then Diogenes had always been an irresponsible creature, who was wont to laugh even at the most serious crisis of his life.

===Chinese===

可第欧根尼向来就是这么个没心没肺的东西，哪怕在他人生最凶险的关头，也照笑不误。

## XXIII A Spy from the Camp / 二十三 营中来的奸细
===Original===

“Come to my lodgings, Nicolaes. I have good news for you, and you do no good by cooling your temper here in the open.”
Stoutenburg, coming out of his lodgings half an hour later to look for his friend, had found Beresteyn in the Hout Straat walking up and down like a caged beast in a fury.
“The *vervloekte Keerl*! the *plepshurk*! the *smeerlap*!” he ejaculated between his clenched teeth. “I’ll not rest till I have struck him in the face first and killed him after!”
But he allowed Stoutenburg to lead him down the street to the narrow gabled house where he lodged. Neither of them spoke, however; fury apparently beset them both equally, the kind of fury which is dumb, and all the more fierce because it finds no outlet in words.
Stoutenburg led the way up the wooden stairs to a small room at the back of the house. There was no light visible anywhere inside the building, and Nicolaes, not knowing his way about, stumbled upwards in the dark keeping close to the heels of his friend. The latter had pushed open the door of his room. Here a tallow candle placed in a pewter sconce upon a table shed a feeble, flickering light around. The room by this scanty glimmer looked to be poorly but cleanly furnished; there was a curtained bed in the panelling of the wall, and a table in the middle of the room with a few chairs placed in a circle round it.
On one of these sat a man who appeared to be in the last stages of weariness. His elbows rested on the table and his head was buried in his folded arms. His clothes looked damp and travel-stained; an empty mug of ale and a couple of empty plates stood in front of him, beside a cap made of fur and a pair of skates.
At the sound made by the opening of the door and the entrance of the two men, he raised his head and seeing the Lord of Stoutenburg he quickly jumped to his feet.
“Sit down, Jan,” said Stoutenburg curtly, “you must be dog-tired. Have you had enough to eat and drink?”
“I thank you, my lord, I have eaten my fill,” replied Jan, “and I am not so tired now that I have had some rest.”
“Sit down,” reiterated Stoutenburg peremptorily, “and you too, my good Nicolaes,” he added as he offered a chair to his friend. “Let me just tell you the news which Jan has brought, and which should make you forget even your present just wrath, so glorious, so important is it.”

===Chinese===

“到我住处来，尼古莱斯。我有好消息给你，你在这露天里白白淘神也淘不出好来。”
半个钟头前，斯陶滕堡从住处出来寻朋友，在豪特街找到了他——他正像一头狂怒的笼中猛兽，来回踱着。
“那个该死的家伙（Keerl）！那个窝囊废（plepshurk）！那个贱胚（smeerlap）！”他从咬紧的牙缝里迸出这几句，“不先捆他一个耳光、再一刀杀了他，我誓不罢休！”
可他还是由着斯陶滕堡把他拉下街去，走向他寄住的那座窄窄的人字顶小屋。两人谁也不说话；怒火显然同样攢住他们俩，那种说不出话的怒火，正因为没有言语的出口，烧得更凶。
斯陶滕堡引路上木楼梯，到屋子后头一间小屋。整座宅子里看不见一点灯光，尼古莱斯路不熟，黑里磕磕碰碰，紧跟着朋友的脚跟。后者推开自己房间的门。屋里一支牛油烛，插在桌上的白镖烛台里，撒着一圈微弱摇曳的光。就这点惨光看去，屋子陈设简陋却干净：墙板里嵌着一张挂幔子的床，屋子中央一张桌子，几把椅子围成一圈。
其中一把椅子上坐着一个人，一副累到了极点的样子。胳膊肘支在桌上，头埋在交叠的臂弯里。衣裳潮乎平的，满是旅途的风尘；面前一只喝空了的麦酒杯、两只空盘，旁边一顶毛皮帽、一双冰鞋。
门响、两个男人进来时，他抬起头，一见斯陶滕堡勋爵，霍地站起来。
“坐，扬，”斯陶滕堡简短地说，“你准是累坏了。吃喝够了吗？”
“谢您，大人，吃饱了，”扬答，“歇了一阵，这会儿不那么累了。”
“坐下，”斯陶滕堡不容分说地重申一遍，“你也坐，我的好尼古莱斯，”他推给朋友一把椅子，“先让我告诉你扬带来的消息——它够辉煌、够重大，保你连眼下这股怒火都能忘了。”

===Original===

He went up to a cabinet which stood in one corner of the room, and from it took a bottle and three pewter mugs. These he placed on the table and filled the mugs with wine. Then he drew another chair close to the table and sat down.
“Jan,” he resumed, turning to Beresteyn, “left the Stadtholder’s camp at Sprang four days ago. He has travelled the whole way along the frozen rivers and waterways only halting for the nights. The news which he brings carries for the bearer of such splendid tidings its own glorious reward; Jan, I must tell you, is with us heart and soul and hates the Stadtholder as much as I do. Is that not so, Jan?”
“My father was hanged two years ago,” replied Jan simply, “because he spoke disparaging words of the Stadtholder. Those words were called treason, and my father was condemned to the gallows merely for speaking them.”
Stoutenburg laughed, his usual harsh, mirthless laugh.
“Yes! that is the way justice is now administered in the free and independent United Provinces,” he said roughly; “down on your knees, ye lumbering Dutchmen! lick the dust off the boots of His Magnificence Maurice of Nassau Prince of Orange! kiss his hand, do his bidding! give forth fulsome praise of his deeds!﻿ ﻿… How long, O God? how long?” he concluded with a bitter sigh.
“Only for a few more days, my lord,” said Jan firmly. “The Stadtholder left his camp the same day as I did. But he travels slowly, in his sledge, surrounded by a bodyguard of an hundred picked men. He is sick and must travel slowly. Yesterday he had only reached Dordrecht, today﻿—if my information is correct﻿—he should sleep at Ijsselmunde. But tomorrow he will be at Delft where he will spend two days at the Prinsenhof.”
“At Delft!” exclaimed Stoutenburg as he brought his clenched fist down upon the table. “Thank God! I have got him at last.”
He leaned across nearer still to Nicolaes and in his excitement clutched his friend’s wrists with nervy trembling fingers, digging his nails into the other man’s flesh till Beresteyn could have screamed with pain.
“From Delft,” he murmured hoarsely, “the only way northwards is along the left bank of the Schie, the river itself is choked with ice-floes which renders it impassable. Just before Ryswyk the road crosses to the right bank of the river over a wooden bridge which we all know well.

===Chinese===

他走到屋角一只柜子前，取出一瓶酒、三只白镖杯，摆上桌，斟满，又拉过一把椅子坐下。
“扬，”他转向贝雷斯坦继续说，“四天前离开斯普朗的执政大营。一路全沿冻住的河流水道走，只在夜里歇。他带来的消息，对送这等辉煌信报的人，自带着辉煌的犒赏；扬，我得告诉你，他是死心塌地跟我们走的，恨执政恨得同我一样。是不是，扬？”
“我父亲两年前被绞死了，”扬平平地答，“就因为他说了执政几句不好听的话。那几句话被叫作叛国，我父亲仅为说了它们，就被判了绞刑。”
斯陶滕堡笑了，照例是那种又干又冷的笑。
“是啊！自由独立的联合省，如今就是这样行司法的，”他粗声说，“跪下吧，你们这些笨拙的荷兰人！去舐拿骚的莫里斯、奥兰治亲王大人阁下靴子上的尘土！亲他的手，听他的差遣！把对他的功业的隩词唱足！……还要多久，哦上帝？还要多久？”他苦涩地叹了收尾。
“只再等几天了，大人，”扬坚定地说，“执政同我同一天离营。可他走得慢，坐着雪橇，有百名精选卫士护卫。他有病，只能慢慢走。昨天他才到多德雷赫特，今天——若我的消息不错——该睡在艾瑟尔蒙德。明天他到代尔夫特，要在王子府住两天。”
“代尔夫特！”斯陶滕堡喊，攢紧的拳头砰地砸在桌上，“谢谢上帝！我终于把他逮着了。”
他朝尼古莱斯又偪近了些，激动之下，用紧张的颤指攢住朋友的手腕，指甲扣进对方的肉里，痛得贝雷斯坦几乎要喊出声。
“从代尔夫特，”他哑着嗓子低语，“往北只有一条路：沿斯希河左岸走，河道本身被浮冰壅塞，走不通。到赖斯韦克之前不远，路面经过一座木桥转到右岸——那桥咱们都熟。

===Original===

Half a league to the south of the bridge is the molens which has been my headquarters ever since I landed at Scheveningen three weeks ago; there I have my stores and my ammunition. Do you see it all, friend?” he queried whilst a feverish light glowed in his eyes. “Is it not God who hath delivered the tyrant into my hands at last? I start for Ryswyk tonight with you to help me, Nicolaes, with van Does and all my friends who will rally round me, with the thirty or forty men whom they have recruited for placing at my disposal. The molens to the south of the wooden bridge which spans the Schie is our rallying point. In the night before the Stadtholder starts on his way from Delft we make our final preparations. I have enough gunpowder stowed away at the mill to blow up the bridge. We’ll dispose it in its place during that night. Then you Nicolaes shall fire the powder at the moment when the Stadtholder’s escort is halfway across the bridge.﻿ ﻿… In the confusion and panic caused by the explosion and the collapse of the bridge our men can easily overpower the Prince’s bodyguard﻿—whilst I, dagger in hand, do fulfil the oath which I swore before the altar of God, to kill the Stadtholder with mine own hand.”
Gradually as he spoke his voice became more hoarse and more choked with passion; his excitement gained upon his hearers until both Nicolaes Beresteyn his friend and Jan the paid spy and messenger felt their blood tingling within their veins, their throats parched, their eyes burning as if they had been seared with living fire. The tallow-candle flickered in its socket, a thin draught from the flimsily constructed window blew its flame hither and thither, so that it lit up fitfully the faces of those three men drawn closely together now in a bond of ambition and of hate.
“ ’Tis splendidly thought out,” said Beresteyn at last with a sigh of satisfaction. “I do not see how the plan can fail.”
“Fail?” exclaimed Stoutenburg with a triumphant laugh, “of course it cannot fail! There are practically no risks even. The place is lonely, the molens a splendid rallying point. We can all reach it by different routes and assemble there tomorrow eve or early the next day. That would give us another day and night at least to complete our preparations. I have forty barrels of gunpowder stowed away at the mill, I have new pattern muskets, cullivers, swords and pistols﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

桥往南半里格，就是那几座磨坊——自我三个星期前在斯海弗宁恩上岸，大营一直扎在那里；我的辐重弹药都在那儿。你看明白了吗，朋友？”他问，眼里燃着热病似的光，“把暴君交到我手里的，难道不正是上帝？我今夜就动身去赖斯韦克，你去帮我，尼古莱斯，还有范杜斯和所有会聚拢来的朋友们，带上他们募集的、听我调遣的三四十人。斯希河木桥以南的那座磨坊，就是咱们的集合点。执政从代尔夫特动身前的那个夜里，咱们完成最后的准备。我在磨坊里存的火药，足够炸塌那座桥。当夜就把它安置到位。然后你，尼古莱斯，在执政的护卫走到桥心的一刻，引燃火药。……爆炸和塌桥引起的混乱惊惶里，咱们的人轻而易举就能制服亲王的卫队——而我，匕首在手，去兑现在上帝祭坛前发下的誓言：亲手杀死执政。”
他越说嗓音越哑、越被激情哽住；他的亢奋传给了听的人，尼古莱斯·贝雷斯坦和他的朋友、拿钱的奸细兼信使扬，都觉着血管里的血在刺痛，喉咙发干，眼睛发烧，像被活火燎着。牛油烛在烛窝里摇，钉得不严的窗缝透进一线风，把烛火吹得东倒西歪，忽明忽暗地照着这三张脸——他们此刻已在一根野心和仇恨的绳上拴作一处。
“想得真漂亮，”贝雷斯坦终于满意地叹了一声，“我看不出这计划怎么失败。”
“失败？”斯陶滕堡得意地笑起来，“当然失败不了！简直没什么风险。那地方僻静，那座磨坊是顶好的集合点。咱们可以分头走不同的路，明天傍晚或后天一早在那儿聚齐。这样咱们至少还有一天一夜把准备做足。我在磨坊里藏了四十桶火药，还有新式的火枪、短炮、剑和手枪……

===Original===

gifts to me from the Archduchess Isabella﻿ ﻿… enough for our coup﻿ ﻿… Fail? How can we fail when everything has been planned, everything thought out? and when God has so clearly shown that He is on our side?”
Jan said nothing for the moment; he lowered his eyes not caring just then to encounter those of his leader, for the remembrance had suddenly flashed through his mind of that other day﻿—not so far distant yet﻿—when everything too had been planned, everything thought out and failure had brought about untold misery and a rich harvest for the scaffold.
Beresteyn too was silent now. Something of his friend’s enthusiasm was also coursing through his veins, but with him it was only the enthusiasm of ambition, of discontent, of a passion for intrigue, for plots and conspiracies, for tearing down one form of government in order to make room for another﻿—but his enthusiasm was not kept at fever-heat by that all-powerful fire of hate which made Stoutenburg forget everything save his desire for revenge.
The latter had pushed his chair impatiently aside and now was pacing up and down the narrow room like some caged feline creature waiting for its meal. Beresteyn’s silence seemed to irritate him for he threw from time to time quick, furtive glances on his friend.
“Nicolaes, why don’t you speak?” he said with sudden impatience.
“I was thinking of Gilda,” replied the other dully.
“Gilda? Why of her?”
“That knave has betrayed me I am sure. He has hidden her away somewhere, not meaning to stick to his bargain with me, and then has come back to Haarlem in order to see if he can extort a large ransom for her from my father.”
“Bah! He wouldn’t dare﻿ ﻿… !”
“Then why is he here?” exclaimed Beresteyn hotly. “Gilda should be in his charge! If he is here, where is Gilda?”
“Good God, man!” ejaculated Stoutenburg, pausing in his restless walk and looking somewhat dazed on his friend, as if he were just waking from some feverish sleep. “Good God! you do not think that﻿ ﻿…”
“That her life is in danger from that knave?” rejoined Beresteyn quietly. “Well, no! I do not think that.﻿ ﻿… I do not know what to think﻿ ﻿… but there is a hint of danger in that rascal’s presence here in Haarlem today.”
He rose and mechanically readjusted his cloak and looked round for his hat.
“What are you going to do?” asked Stoutenburg.

===Chinese===

（接上）都是伊莎贝尔大公夫人送给我的礼……够咱们干这一票了……失败？一切都计划好了、一切都想透了，又明明白白是上帝站在咱们一边，怎么会失败？”
扬一时没说话；他垂下眼，不愿这时候去接头领的目光——因为另一个日子的记忆忽然闪过心头：那一天并不太远，那时也是一切都计划好了、一切都想透了，而失败带来的，是说不尽的苦难，是断头台的一场丰收。
贝雷斯坦此刻也沉默了。朋友的一部分热确也在他血管里奔流，可他那一份，只是野心的热、不平的热、对密谋对计策对明谋暗策的热，对拆掉一种政体好腾位置给另一种政体的热——可他的热，烧不到斯陶滕堡那般高热：那股烧尽一切的恨火，叫斯陶滕堡什么都忘了，只剩复仇的欲望。
后者已不耐烦地把椅子推到一边，在窄屋里来回踱起步来，像一头等食的笼中猫。贝雷斯坦的沉默像是在磨他，他不时朝朋友飞快地觰一眼。
“尼古莱斯，你怎么不说话？”他忽然不耐烦地说。
“我在想吉尔达。”对方滞滞地答。
“吉尔达？想她做什么？”
“那无赖一定背叛了我。他把她藏到了什么鬼地方，并不打算遵守同我的交易，然后回了哈勒姆，看能不能从我父亲那里敲一笔大赎金。”
“呸！他不敢……！”
“那他在这儿做什么？”贝雷斯坦火了，“吉尔达该在他的看管之下！他在这儿，吉尔达在哪儿？”
“老天，伙计！”斯陶滕堡喊，停下那停不住的踱步，有点发懵地望着朋友，像刚从一场热梦里醒来，“老天！你总不会以为……”
“以为她的命有危险，危险在那无赖手里？”贝雷斯坦平静地接道，“喏，不！我不这么以为。……我不知道该怎么想……可那流氓今天出现在哈勒姆，透着一股危险的味儿。”
他站起身，机械地拢好斗篷，四下找帽子。
“你要做什么？”斯陶滕堡问。

===Original===

“Find the knave,” retorted the other, “and wring his neck if he does not give some satisfactory account of Gilda.”
“No! no! you must not do that﻿ ﻿… not in a public place at any rate﻿ ﻿… the rascal would betray you if you quarrelled with him﻿ ﻿… or worse still you would betray yourself. Think what it would mean to us now﻿—at this moment﻿—if it were known that you had a hand in the abduction of your sister﻿ ﻿… if she were traced and found! think what that would mean﻿—denunciation﻿—failure﻿—the scaffold for us all!”
“Must I leave her then at the mercy of a man who is proved to be both a liar and a cheat?”
“No! you shall not do that. Let me try and get speech with him. He does not know me; and I think that I could find out what double game he is playing and where our own danger lies. Let me try and find him.”
“How can you do that?”
“You remember the incident on New Year’s Eve, when you and I traced that cursed adventurer to his own doorstep?”
“Yes!”
“Then you remember the Spanish wench and the old cripple to whom our man relinquished his lodgings on that night.”
“Certainly I do.”
“Well! yesterday when the hour came for the rascal to seize Gilda, I could not rest in this room. I wanted to see, to know what was going on. Gilda means so much to me, that remorse I think played havoc with my prudence then and I went out into the Groote Markt to watch her come out of church. I followed her at a little distance and saw her walking rapidly along the bank of the Oude Gracht. She was accosted by a woman who spoke to her from out the depths of the narrow passage which leads to the disused chapel of St. Pieter. Gilda was quickly captured by the brute whom you had paid to do this monstrous deed, and I stood by like an abject coward, not raising a hand to save her from this cruel outrage.”
He paused a moment and passed his hand across his brow as if to chase away the bitter and insistent recollection of that crime of which he had been the chief instigator.
“Why do you tell me all that?” queried Beresteyn sombrely. “What I did, I did for you and for the triumph of your cause.”
“I know, I know,” replied Stoutenburg with a sigh, “may Heaven reward you for the sacrifice. But I merely acted for mine own selfish ends, for my ambition and my revenge. I love Gilda beyond all else on earth, yet I saw her sacrificed for me and did not raise a finger to save her.”

===Chinese===

“找到那无赖，”对方回敬，“他不对吉尔达的下落给个像样的交代，就拧断他的脖子。”
“不！不！你可不能……至少不能在大庭广众……你同他一吵，那流氓就会出卖你……更糟的是你自己出卖自己。想想看，此刻——就在这个节骨眼上——若是让人知道你对劫掳亲妹妹的事插过一手……若是她被追到、被找到！想想那意味着什么——告发——失败——咱们全体上断头台！”
“那我就该把她撂在一个既证明是骗子、又证明是无赖的人手里？”
“不！不会叫你那样。让我去同他搭话。他不认得我；我想我能探出他玩的是什么两面把戏、咱们自己的危险在哪里。让我去找他。”
“你怎么找？”
“你还记得除夕那晚的事吗，你我把那可咒的冒险家一直追到他自家门口？”
“记得！”
“那你记得那个西班牙姑娘，还有那瘫老头——咱们那位爷们儿当晚把铺位让给的那两位。”
“当然记得。”
“喏！昨天到了那流氓对吉尔达下手的时辰，我在这屋里坐不住。我想看，想知道出了什么事。吉尔达在我心里的分量太重，我想是悔恨搅了我的谨慎：我出去，到了大市场，看她走出教堂。我隔着不远不近跟着，看见她沿老运河的岸快步走。一个女人从那条通废弃的圣彼得小堂的窄过道深处唤住她。吉尔达很快就被你花钱雇来干这桩罪孽的畜生掳走了，而我站在一旁，像个下作的懦夫，没抬一根手指救她脱这场暴行。”
他顿了顿，一只手抹过额头，像要赶走那桩罪残忍而不肯散去的记忆——他自己正是那桩罪的头号教唆者。
“你跟我说这些做什么？”贝雷斯坦阴郁地问，“我做的事，是为你、为你大业的胜利。”
“我知道，我知道，”斯陶滕堡叹道，“愿上天酬报你的牺牲。而我不过是为自己自私的目的行事，为我的野心、我的复仇。我爱吉尔达胜过世上一切，却眼睁睁看她为我作了牺牲，没抬一根手指救她。”

===Original===

“It is too late for remorse,” retorted Beresteyn roughly, “if Gilda had been free to speak of what she heard in the cathedral on New Year’s Eve, you and I today would have had to flee the country as you fled from it once before, branded as traitors, recaptured mayhap, dragged before the tribunal of a man who has already shown that he knows no mercy. Gilda’s freedom would have meant for you, for me, for Heemskerk, van Does and all the others, torture first and a traitor’s death at the last.”
“You need not remind me of that,” rejoined Stoutenburg more calmly. “Gilda has been sacrificed for me and by God I will requite her for all that she has endured! My life, my love are hers and as soon as the law sets me free to marry she will have a proud position higher than that of any other woman in the land.”
“For the moment she is at the mercy of that blackguard﻿ ﻿…”
“And I tell you that I can find out where she is.”
“How?”
“The woman who accosted Gilda last night, who acted for the knave as a decoy, was the Spanish wench whom he had befriended the night before.”
“You saw her?”
“Quite distinctly. She passed close to me when she ran off after having done her work. No doubt she is that rascal’s sweetheart and will know of his movements and of his plans. Money or threats should help me to extract something from her.”
“But where can you find her?”
“At the same lodgings where she has been these two nights, I feel sure.”
“It is worth trying,” mused Beresteyn.
“And in the meanwhile we must not lose sight of our knave. Jan, my good man, that shall be your work. Mynheer Beresteyn will be good enough to go with you as far as the *tapperij* of the Lame Cow, and there point out to you a man whom it will be your duty to follow step by step this evening until you find out where he intends to pitch his tent for the night. You understand?”
“Yes, my lord,” said Jan, smothering as best he could an involuntary sigh of weariness.
“It is all for the ultimate triumph of our revenge, good Jan,” quoth Stoutenburg significantly, “the work of watching which you will do this night is at least as important as that which you have so bravely accomplished these past four days. The question is, have you strength left to do it?”
Indeed the question seemed unnecessary now.

===Chinese===

“现在后悔晚了，”贝雷斯坦粗声回敬，“若是吉尔达自由着，把除夕夜在大教堂听见的说出去，你和我今天就得再次逃出国门，像你上回那样顶着叛贼的名，兴许还被抓回来，拖到一个早已表明不懂得怜悯的人的法庭前。吉尔达的自由，对你、对我、对海姆斯凯尔克、范杜斯和所有其他人，意味着先上酷刑、最后上一个叛徒的死。”
“不必提醒我这个，”斯陶滕堡平静些了，接道，“吉尔达为我作了牺牲，我以上帝起誓，她受的这一切，我都要偿她！我的命、我的爱都是她的，法律一许我再娶，她就得到一个比这国中任何女人都高的体面位置。”
“眼下她可在一个恶棍的掌心里……”
“我告诉你，我能查出她在哪儿。”
“怎么查？”
“昨晚唤住吉尔达、替那无赖做诱子的那个女人，就是他前一夜发善心帮过的那个西班牙姑娘。”
“你看见她了？”
“看得清清楚楚。她办完事跑开时，打我近旁过。毫无疑问她是那流氓的相好，他的行踪、他的打算，她一定知道。钱或者恐吓，总能帮我从她嘴里撞出点什么。”
“可你上哪儿找她？”
“还在原先那个铺位上，这两个夜她都在那儿，我有数。”
“值得一试。”贝雷斯坦沉吟。
“与此同时，咱们的这位爷们儿一步也不能丢。扬，我的好人，这趟归你。贝雷斯坦先生劳驾送你去跛脚母牛的酒室（tapperij），指给你一个人：今晚你的差事就是一步一步盯住他，直到查出他打算在哪里过夜。明白吗？”
“明白，大人。”扬说，尽力压下那声不由自主的疲乏的叹息。
“全是为了咱们复仇大业的最后胜利，好扬，”斯陶滕堡意味深长地说，“你今夜的守望，至少同你过去四天里英勇办成的差事一样要紧。问题是，你还有力气办吗？”
其实这问题已属多余。

===Original===

At the word “revenge” Jan had already straightened out his long, lean figure and though traces of fatigue might still linger in his drawn face, it was obvious that the spirit within was prepared to fight all bodily weaknesses.
“There is enough strength in me, my lord,” he said simply, “to do your bidding now as always for the welfare of Holland and the triumph of our faith.”
After which Stoutenburg put out the light, and with a final curt word to Jan and an appeal to Beresteyn he led the way out of the room, down the stairs and finally into the street.

===Chinese===

“复仇”二字一出口，扬早已挺直了他那瘦长的身量；憔悴的脸上纵然还挂着倦色，那股子精气神显然已经准备好压倒肉体的一切软弱。
“我身上的力气够用，大人，”他平平地说，“同往常一样听候您的差遣，为了荷兰的福祉、为了咱们信仰的胜利。”
说罢，斯陶滕堡熄了灯，对扬撂下最后一句话，又对贝雷斯坦叮嘱一句，便领头出了房，下了楼，最终走到街上。

## XXIV The Birth of Hate / 二十四 仇恨的诞生
===Original===

Here the three men parted; Beresteyn and Jan to go to the Lame Cow where the latter was to begin his work of keeping track of Diogenes, and Stoutenburg to find his way to that squalid lodging house which was situate at the bottom of the Kleine Hout Straat where it abuts on the Oude Gracht.
It had been somewhat impulsively that he had suggested to Beresteyn that he would endeavour to obtain some information from the Spanish wench as to Diogenes’ plans and movements and the whereabouts of Gilda, and now that he was alone with more sober thoughts he realised that the suggestion had not been over-backed by reason. Still as Beresteyn had said: there could be no harm in seeking out the girl. Stoutenburg was quite satisfied in his mind that she must be the rascal’s sweetheart, else she had not lent him an helping hand in the abduction of Gilda, and since he himself was well supplied with money through the generosity of his rich friends in Haarlem, he had no doubt that if the wench knew anything at all about the rogue, she could easily be threatened first, then bribed and cajoled into telling all that she knew.
Luck in this chose to favour the Lord of Stoutenburg, for the girl was on the doorstep when he finally reached the house where two nights ago a young soldier of fortune had so generously given up his lodgings to a miserable pair of beggars. He had just been vaguely wondering how best he could﻿—without endangering his own safety﻿—obtain information as to which particular warren in the house she and her father inhabited, when he saw her standing under the lintel of the door, her meagre figure faintly lit up by the glimmer of a streetlamp fixed in the wall just above her head.
“I would have speech with thee,” he said in his usual peremptory manner as soon as he had approached her, “show me the way to thy room.”
Then as, like a frightened rabbit, she made ready to run away to her burrow as quickly as she could, he seized hold of her arm and reiterated roughly:
“I would have speech of thee, dost hear? Show me the way to thy room at once. Thy safety and that of thy father depend on thy obedience. There is close search in the city just now for Spanish spies.”
The girl’s pale cheeks took on a more ashen hue, her lips parted with a quickly smothered cry of terror.

===Chinese===

三人在此分手：贝雷斯坦带扬去跛脚母牛——扬要在那里开始盯梢第欧根尼；斯陶滕堡则去寻那座破败的下等客店——它坐落在小豪特街底、接上老运河的地方。
他向贝雷斯坦提出要从西班牙姑娘嘴里探一探第欧根尼的打算、行踪和吉尔达的下落，未免有点冲动；如今独自一人，头脑清醒些，他明白这个提议并没有多少道理撑着。不过正如贝雷斯坦说的：找一找那姑娘，总没有坏处。斯陶滕堡心里十拿九稳：她必是那流氓的相好，不然不会在劫掳吉尔达的事上帮他一手；何况他本人靠哈勒姆有钱朋友的慷慨，手头宽绰，他毫不怀疑：只要那丫头当真知道那流氓点什么，先吓、再买、再哄，不难叫她和盘托出。
这一回，运偏心了斯陶滕堡勋爵：他终于走到那座房子跟前时，姑娘正在门槛上——两个夜晚之前，一个年轻的冒险家就在这里，把自己铺位慷慨地让给了一对可怜的乞丐。他正模糊地盘算着，怎样在不累及自身安全的前提下，探明她和她父亲到底住在哪个洞，一抬眼就看见她立在门楣下，瘦削的身影被门楣上方壁灯的微光照出个淡淡的轮廓。
“我有话同你说，”他一走近，便用惯常那副不容分说的腔调说，“带我去你的房间。”
可她像一只受惊的兔子，撒腿就要往自己的洞里窜，他一把攢住她的胳膊，粗声重申：
“我有话同你讲，听见没有？立刻带我上你的房间。你的安危、你父亲的安危，全看你的听话。眼下城里正大搜西班牙奸细。”
姑娘苍白的两颊顿时灰了下去，嘴唇一张，一声吓出来的喊叫又被生生压了回去。

===Original===

She knew﻿—as did every stranger in these Dutch cities just now﻿—that the words “Spanish spy” had a magical effect on the placid tempers of their inhabitants, and that many a harmless foreign wayfarer had suffered imprisonment, aye and torture too, on the mere suspicion of being a “Spanish spy.”
“I have nothing to fear,” she murmured under her breath.
“Perhaps not,” he rejoined, “but the man who shelters and protects thee is under suspicion of abetting Spanish spies. For his sake ’twere wiser if thou didst obey me.”
Stoutenburg had every reason to congratulate himself on his shrewd guess, for at his words all resistance on the girl’s part vanished, and though she began to tremble in every limb and even for a moment seemed ready to swoon, she murmured words which if incoherent certainly sounded submissive, and then silently led the way upstairs. He followed her closely, stumbling behind her in the dark, and as he mounted the rickety steps he was rapidly rehearsing in his mind what he would say to the wench.
That the girl was that abominable villain’s sweetheart he was not for a moment in doubt, her submission just now, at the mere hint of the fellow’s danger, showed the depth of her love for him. Stoutenburg felt therefore that his success in obtaining what information he wanted would depend only on how much she knew. In any case she must be amenable to a bribe for she seemed wretchedly poor; even in that brief glimpse which he had had of her by the dim light of the street-door lamp, he could not help but see how ragged was her kirtle and how pinched and wan her face.
On the landing she paused and taking a key from between the folds of her shift she opened the door of her lodging and humbly begged the gracious mynheer to enter. A tallow candle placed upon a chair threw its feeble light upon the squalid abode, the whitewashed walls, the primitive bedstead in the corner made up of deal planks and covered with a paillasse and a thin blanket. From beneath that same blanket came the gentle and fretful moanings of the old cripple.

===Chinese===

她知道——这些荷兰城里的外乡人如今谁不知道——“西班牙奸细”四个字，在那班居民平静的脾气上有奇效；不知多少无害的外乡行路人，只因为一句“形迹可疑”，蹲了大狱，是啊，还上过酷刑。
“我没什么可怕的。”她压着嗓子喃喃。
“兴许没有，”他应道，“可收留你、护着你的那个男人，正涉嫌窝藏西班牙奸细。为他着想，你最好听话。”
斯陶滕堡大有理由为自己的精明猜测得意：话一出口，姑娘的抗拒全散了；她四肢发着抖，有一瞬简直要晕，咕哝出的字句虽不成句，听得出是顺服，随即默默引路上楼。他紧跟着，黑里磕磕绊绊，一面登着摇摇欲坠的楼梯，一面在心里飞快排演着要对那丫头说的话。
这姑娘是那个万恶恶棍的相好，他一刻也没怀疑过；方才只消一点那人有难，她就服了帖，足见她爱他多深。斯陶滕堡因此觉得，能不能问到他要的情报，只看她知道多少了。无论如何她必是见钱眼开的：她穷得叮当响；就着门口那盏昏灯的匆匆一瞥，他也看得出她的裙子多么褴褛、她的脸多么憔悴蜡黄。
在楼梯口她停下，从内衣褶里摸出钥匙，开了自己那间房的门，卑卑怯怯地请贵大人进屋。椅上一支牛油烛，把微光照上这肮脏的住处：白灰的墙，墙角一张木板搭的简陋床，铺着草垫和一条薄毯。毯子底下，传出那瘫老人轻声的、闹脾气的呻吟。

===Original===

But Stoutenburg was far too deeply engrossed in his own affairs to take much note of his surroundings; as soon as the girl had closed the door behind her, he called her roughly to him and she﻿—frightened and obedient﻿—came forward without a word, standing now before him, with hanging arms and bowed head, whilst a slight shiver shook her girlish form from time to time.
He dragged a chair out to the middle of the room and sat himself astride upon it, his arms resting across the back, his booted and spurred feet thrust out in front of him, whilst his hollow, purple-rimmed eyes with their feverish glow of ever-present inward excitement were fixed upon the girl.
“I must tell thee, wench,” he began abruptly, “that I mean to be thy friend. No harm shall come to thee if thou wilt answer truthfully certain questions which I would ask of thee.”
Then as she appeared too frightened to reply and only cast a furtive, timorous glance on him, he continued after a slight pause:
“The man who protected thee against the rabble the other night, and who gave thee shelter afterwards, the man in whose bed thy crippled father lies at this moment﻿—he is thy sweetheart, is he not?”
“What is that to you?” she retorted sullenly.
“Nothing in itself,” he said quietly. “I merely spoke of it to show thee how much I know. Let me tell thee at once that I was in the tavern with him on New Year’s Eve when his boon-companions told the tale of how he had protected thee against a crowd; and that I was in this very street not twenty paces away when in response to thy appeal he gave up his room and his bed to thee, and for thy sake paced the streets for several hours in the middle of the night and in weather that must have frozen the marrow in his bones.”
“Well? What of that?” said the girl simply. “He is kind and good, and hath that pity for the poor and homeless which would grace many a noble gentleman.”
“No doubt,” he retorted dryly, “but a man will not do all that for a wench, save in expectation of adequate payment for his trouble and discomfort.”
“What is that to you?” she reiterated, with the same sullen earnestness.
“Thou art in love with that fine gallant, eh, my girl?” he continued with a harsh, flippant laugh, “and art not prepared to own to it. Well! I’ll not press thee for a confession. I am quite satisfied with thine evasive answers.

===Chinese===

可斯陶滕堡一心沉在自己的事里，顾不上多看四周；姑娘随手掩上门，他便粗声唤她过来。她惶惶而顺从，一言不发地上前，立在他面前，双臂垂着，头低着，瘦瘦的少女身量不时轻轻一颤。
他拖过一把椅子到屋子中央，跨坐上去，双臂搭着椅背，带马刺的靴子直伸在前；那双深陷的、围青紫圈的眼睛，燃着那股常驻不散的内心亢奋，直盯着姑娘。
“我得告诉你，丫头，”他直统统地开口，“我是要做你朋友的。你若老老实实回答我几个问题，必不伤你分毫。”
她像是吓得太狠，答不上来，只偷偷怯怯地看他一眼；他略顿一瞬，继续说：
“那晚护你脱了暴民、后来又给你地方住的男人，此刻他床上躺着你瘫腿父亲的那男人——他是你的情郎，对不对？”
“与你何干？”她明沉着脸回。
“本来不相干，”他平静地说，“我提这个，只为让你知道我了解多少。这就告诉你：除夕夜，我在酒馆里，听他那帮酒肉伙伴讲他怎么从人堆里护下了你；也是在这条街上，不到二十步远，我亲眼看见他应你的哀告，把房、把床让给了你，为你顶着半夜的街，顶着冻透骨髓的天气，来回避酘了几个钟头。”
“嗯？那又怎么样？”姑娘平平地说，“他心善，心好，对穷人、对没家的人有那份怜惘，多少高贵的先生还比不上他。”
“不消说，”他干巴巴地回，“可一个男人为一个丫头做到这个地步，总要指望他的辛苦和吃苦，得到相当的报偿。”
“与你何干？”她重申，还是那样执拗地认真。
“你是爱上那位漂亮爷们儿了，嗯，丫头？”他带着刺耳轻浮的笑接着说，“又不好意思认。好！我不逼你招。你这几句搪塞，我听着已经满意。

===Original===

Let me but tell thee this; that the man whom thou lovest is in deadly danger of his life.”
“Great God, have pity on him!” she exclaimed involuntarily.
“In a spirit of wanton mischief﻿—for he is not so faithful to thee as thou wouldst wish﻿—he has abducted a lady from this city, as thou well knowest, since thou didst lend him thy help in the committal of this crime. Thou seest,” he added roughly, “that denials on thy part were worse than useless, since I know everything. The lady’s father is an important magistrate in this city, he has moved every process of the law so that he may mete out an exemplary punishment to the blackguard who has dared to filch his daughter. Hanging will be the most merciful ending to thy lover’s life, but Mynheer Beresteyn talks of the rack, of quartering and of the stake, and he is a man of boundless influence in the administration of the law.”
“Lord, have mercy upon us,” once again murmured the wretched girl whose cheeks now looked grey and shrunken; her lips were white and quivering and her eyes with dilated pupils were fixed in horror on the harbinger of this terrible news.
“He will have none on thy sweetheart, I’ll warrant thee unless﻿ ﻿…”
He paused significantly, measuring the effect of his words and of that dramatic pause upon the tense sensibilities of the girl.
“Unless﻿ ﻿… what?” came almost as a dying murmur from her parched throat.
“Unless thou wilt lend a hand to save him.”
“I?” she exclaimed pathetically, “I would give my hand﻿ ﻿… my tongue﻿ ﻿… my sight﻿ ﻿… my life to save him.”
“Come!” he said, “that’s brave! but it will not be necessary to make quite so violent a sacrifice. I have great power too in this city and great influence over the bereaved father,” he continued, lying unblushingly, “I know that if I can restore his daughter to him within the next four and twenty hours, I could prevail upon him to give up pursuit of the villain who abducted her, and to let him go free.”
But these words were not yet fully out of his mouth, before she had fallen on her knees before him, clasping her thin hands together and raising up to his hard face large, dark eyes that were brimful of tears.
“Will you do that then, O my gracious lord,” she pleaded. “Oh! God will reward you if you will do this.”
“How can I, thou crazy wench,” he retorted, “how can I restore the damsel to her sorrowing father when I do not know where she is?”

===Chinese===

（接上）只告诉你这一件：你爱的那个男人，性命正悬在生死线上。”
“伟大的上帝，可怜可怜他吧！”她不由自主地喊。
“他兴之所至干下的胡作非为——他对你可没你盼的那么忠实——他从这城里劫走了一位小姐，这你清楚，因为在这桩罪里你还帮了他一手。你看，”他粗声补上，“你抵赖比不抵赖还糟，我什么都清楚。那位小姐的父亲是本城有势力的法官，他把法律的全套程序都发动起来了，非要狠狠惩办那个敢偷他女儿的恶棍不可。绞死，算是你情郎最轻的死法；可贝雷斯坦先生还提着拉肢架，提着车裂，提着火刑柱——而他在司法上，是个势力没有边的人。”
“主啊，怜惘我们吧，”那可怜的姑娘又喃喃了一遍，两颊这会儿灰败而塌陷；嘴唇发白发颤，瞳孔放大的眼睛惊恐地盯着这个可怕消息的报丧人。
“他不会怜惘你的情郎，我向你担保，除非……”
他意味深长地顿住，量着这些话、这个戏剧性的停顿，对姑娘绷紧的神经起的作用。
“除非……什么？”从她干透的喉咙里挤出的，几乎是一声垂死的低语。
“除非你肯搭手救他。”
“我？”她凄惨地喊，“为救他，我这只手……这条舌头……这双眼……这条命，都舍得。”
“来！”他说，“痛快！不过用不着这么惨烈的牺牲。在这座城里我也有大权，对那位痛失爱女的父亲也有大影响，”他面不改色地继续撒谎，“我知道，只要我能在二十四小时内把女儿还给他，我就能劝他放过那个劫女的恶徒，放他自由。”
这话还没说完，她已扑倒在他膝前，瘦瘦的双手合着，一双盈满泪的大眼睛仰向他那张冷酷的脸。
“那么您肯这样做了，哦，仁慈的大人？”她哀求，“哦！您若肯这样做，上帝必报答您。”
“我怎么做得到呢，你这傻丫头，”他回敬，“我连她在哪儿都不知道，怎么把姑娘还给她悲伤的父亲？”

===Original===

“But﻿—”
“It is from thee I want to hear where the lady is.”
“From me?”
“Why yes! of course! Thou art in the confidence of thy lover, and knowest where he keeps the lady hidden. Tell me where she is, and I will pledge thee my word that thou and he will have nothing more to fear.”
“He is not my lover,” she murmured dully, “nor am I in his confidence.”
She was still on her knees, but had fallen back on her heels, with arms hanging limp and helpless by her side. Hope so suddenly arisen had equally quickly died out of her heart, and her pinched face expressed in every line the despair and misery which had come in its wake.
“Come!” he cried harshly, “play no tricks with me, wench. Thou didst own to being the rascal’s sweetheart.”
“I owned to my love for him,” she said simply, “not to his love for me.”
“I told thee that he will hang or burn unless thou art willing to help him.”
“And I told thee, gracious sir, that I would give my life for him.”
“Which is quite unnecessary. All I want is the knowledge of where he keeps the lady whom he has outraged.”
“I cannot help you, mynheer, in that.”
“Thou wilt not!” he cried.
“I cannot,” she reiterated gently. “I do not know where she is.”
“Will fifty guilders help thy memory?” he sneered.
“Fifty guilders would mean ease and comfort to my father and to me for many months to come. I would do much for fifty guilders but I cannot tell that which I do not know.”
“An hundred guilders, girl, and the safety of thy lover. Will that not tempt thee?”
“Indeed, indeed, gracious sir,” she moaned piteously, “I swear to you that I do not know.”
“Then dost perjure thyself and wilt rue it, wench,” he exclaimed as he jumped to his feet, and with a loud curse kicked the chair away from him.
The Lord of Stoutenburg was not a man who had been taught to curb his temper; he had always given way to his passions, allowing them as the years went on to master every tender feeling within him; for years now he had sacrificed everything to them, to his ambition, to his revenge, to his loves and hates. Now that this fool of a girl tried to thwart him as he thought, he allowed his fury against her full rein, to the exclusion of reason, of prudence, or ordinary instincts of chivalry. He stooped over her like a great, gaunt bird of prey and his thin claw-like hand fastened itself on her thin shoulder.

===Chinese===

“可是——”
“我要从你嘴里听到那位小姐在哪儿。”
“从我嘴里？”
“可不是！当然！你是在你情郎的心腹里，他把你藏在哪儿，你知道。告诉我她在哪儿，我拿话担保：你和他从此再没什么可怕的。”
“他不是我的情郎，”她滞滞地喃喃，“他的心腹里也没有我。”
她仍跪着，却已颓坐回脚跟上，双臂无力地垂在身侧。骤然升起的指望，又同样快地在她心里熄了；瘦削的脸上，每一道线条都写着随之而来的绝望和凄苦。
“来！”他狠狠地喊，“少跟我耍花招，丫头。你自己认过是那流氓的相好。”
“我认的是我对他的爱，”她平平地说，“没认过他对我的爱。”
“我告诉过你，你不肯帮他，他就得上绞架、上火刑柱。”
“我也告诉过您了，仁慈的先生，我肯为他去死。”
“那完全用不着。我只要一样：他把他糟蹋的那位小姐藏在哪儿。”
“这个我帮不了您，先生。”
“你是不肯！”他喊。
“是不能，”她轻轻地重申，“我不知道她在哪儿。”
“五十盾能不能帮你的记性？”他讥笑。
“五十盾，够我父亲和我往后好几个月的安逸。为了五十盾我什么都肯干，可我不知道的事，说不出来。”
“一百盾，丫头，外加你情郎的平安。还不动心？”
“真的，真的，仁慈的先生，”她凄凄地哀告，“我向您发舎，我不知道。”
“那你就是在发假舎，丫头，有你后悔的！”他喊着一跃而起，大声咒骂一句，把椅子踢开。
斯陶滕堡勋爵不是个学过收脾气的人；他向来放任情欲，任它们一年年吞掉心里每一分柔情；多年来他把一切牺牲给它们：野心、复仇、爱恨。如今这个傻丫头——他以为——敢拦他，他便把对她的狂怒完全放开，任它踩过理智、踩过审慎、踩过起码的侠义本能。他像一头又高又瘦的猛禽朝她俯下来，枯爪似的手扣住她瘦削的肩。

===Original===

“Thou liest, girl,” he said hoarsely, “or art playing with me? Money thou shalt have. Name thy price. I’ll pay thee all that thou wouldst ask. I’ll not believe that thou dost not know! Think of thy lover under torture, on the rack, burnt at the stake. Hast ever seen a man after he has been broken on the wheel? his limbs torn from their sockets, his chest sunken under the weights﻿—and the stake? hast seen a heretic burnt alive﻿ ﻿… ?”
She gave a loud scream of agony: her hands went up to her ears, her eyes stared out of her head like those of one in a frenzy of terror.
“Pity! pity! my lord, have pity! I swear that I do not know.”
“*Verdomme!*” he cried out in the madness of his rage as with a cruel twist of his hand he threw the wretched girl off her balance and sent her half-fainting, cowering on the floor.
“*Verdommt* be thou, *plepshurk*,” came in a ringing voice from behind him.
The next moment he felt as if two grapnels made of steel had fastened themselves on his shoulders and as if a weight of irresistible power was pressing him down, down on to his knees. His legs shook under him, his bones seemed literally to be cracking beneath that iron grip, and he had not the power to turn round in order to see who his assailant was. The attack had taken him wholly by surprise and it was only when his knees finally gave way under him, and he too was down on the ground, licking the dust of the floor﻿—as he had forced the wretched girl to do﻿—that he had a moment’s respite from that cruel pressure and was able to turn in the direction whence it had come.
Diogenes with those wide shoulders of his squared out to their full breadth, legs apart and arms crossed over his mighty chest was standing over him, his eyes aflame and his moustache bristling till it stood out like the tusks of a boar.
“Dondersteen!” he exclaimed as he watched the other man’s long, lean figure thus sprawling on the ground, “this is a pretty pass to which to bring this highly civilized and cultured country. Men are beginning to browbeat and strike the women now! Dondersteen!”
Stoutenburg, whose vocabulary of oaths was at least as comprehensive as that of any foreign adventurer, had﻿—to its accompaniment﻿—struggled at last to his feet.
“You﻿ ﻿…” he began as soon as he had partially recovered his breath.

===Chinese===

“你撒谎，丫头，”他哑着嗓子说，“还是在耍我？钱你会有的。开个价。你要多少我付多少。我不信你不知道！想想你那情郎：上刑、拉肢架、火刑柱。见过受轮刑之后的人吗？四肢从臼里扯脱，胸口被秤锤压塌——还有火刑柱？见过异端活活烧吗……？”
她痛苦地尖叫一声：双手捂上耳朵，眼睛瞪得脱出眼眶，像吓疯了的人。
“怜惘！怜惘！大人，怜惘！我发舎，我不知道。”
“该死！（Verdomme）”他狂怒地喊，手上一记狠拧，把那可怜的姑娘掼得失去平衡，半晕着缩倒在地板上。
“你才该死，窝囊废（plepshurk）！”背后一个响亮的嗓音说。
下一刻，他觉得像两只钢铁的锚钩扣上了他的双肩，一股不可抗拒的力道把他往下压、往下压，压上他的膝盖。两腿在他身下发抖，骨头在那铁钳下真像要断；他连回头看清袭击者是谁的力气都没有。这一击全然出他不备，直到双膝终于一软、他也倒在地上、舐着地板的尘土——方才他逼那可怜姑娘舐的——那股残酷的压力才稍松一瞬，容他转向来处。
第欧根尼，宽阔的肩膀全然张开，两腿叉开，双臂抱在雄伟的胸前，正居高临下地站着，眼睛冒火，髭须戟张，張得像野猪的獠牙。
“该死的雷公！”他望着那人瘦长的身形四仰八又躺在地上，喊道，“这个高度文明、高度教化的国家，竟沦落到了这步田地。如今男人们开始威逼、殴打女人了！该死的雷公！”
斯陶滕堡——他那套咒骂的词汇，至少不输任何一个外国冒险家——就着咒骂的伴奏，好容易挣起了身。
“你……”他喘过一口气，刚开口。

===Original===

But Diogenes putting up his hand hastily interrupted him:
“Do not speak just now, mynheer,” he said with his wonted good-humour. “Were you to speak now, I feel that your words would not be characterized by that dignity and courtesy which one would expect from so noble a gentleman.”
“*Smeerlap!*﻿—” began Stoutenburg once more.
“There now,” rejoined the other with imperturbable bonhomie, “what did I tell you? Believe me, sir, ’tis much the best to be silent if pleasant words fail to reach one’s lips.”
“A truce on this nonsense,” quoth Stoutenburg hotly, “you took me unawares﻿—like a coward.﻿ ﻿…”
“Well said, mynheer! Like a coward﻿—that is just how I took you﻿—in the act of striking a miserable atom of humanity﻿—who is as defenceless as a sparrow.”
“ ’Tis ludicrous indeed to see a man of your calling posing as the protector of women,” retorted Stoutenburg with a sneer. “But enough of this. You find me unarmed at this moment, else you had already paid for this impudent interference.”
“I thank you, sir,” said Diogenes as he swept the Lord of Stoutenburg a deep, ironical bow, “I thank you for thus momentarily withholding chastisement from my unworthiness. When may I have the honour of calling on your Magnificence in order that you might mete unto me the punishment which I have so amply deserved?”
“That chastisement will lose nothing by waiting, since indeed your insolence passes belief,” quoth Stoutenburg hotly. “Now go!” he added, choosing not to notice the wilfully impertinent attitude of the other man, “leave me alone with this wench. My business is with her.”
“So is mine, gracious lord,” rejoined Diogenes with a bland smile, “else I were not here. This room is mine﻿—perhaps your Magnificence did not know that﻿—you would not like surely to remain my guest a moment longer than you need.”
“Of a truth I knew that the baggage was your sweetheart﻿—else I had not come at all.”
“Leave off insulting the girl, man,” said Diogenes whose moustache bristled again, a sure sign that his temper was on the boil, “she has told you the truth, she knows nothing of the whereabouts of the noble lady who has disappeared from Haarlem. An you desire information on that point you had best get it elsewhere.”
But Stoutenburg had in the meanwhile succeeded in recovering﻿—at any rate partially﻿—his presence of mind.

===Chinese===

第欧根尼慌忙抬手，把他打断：
“这会儿先别说，先生，”他带着惯常的好脾气说，“您现在开口，我怕字句里够不上那份庄重和客气——这么高贵的绅士，总该有那么一份。”
“贱胚！——”斯陶滕堡又要开口。
“瞧见没有，”对方带着纹丝不动的好性儿应道，“我说什么来着？信我，先生，嘴上出不来好话的时候，沉默最妙。”
“别扯这些胡话了，”斯陶滕堡火道，“你是趁我不备——像个懦夫。……”
“说得好，先生！像个懦夫——我正是这样对付你的：趁你正挥拳打一个可怜的小小生命——一只麻雀那样毫无还手之力的小生命。”
“瞧瞧这有多滑稽：你这种营生的人，居然扮起女人的保护者来了，”斯陶滕堡冷笑，“不过够了。算你赶上我没带家伙，不然你这番放肆的多管闲事，早付过代价了。”
“多谢您，先生，”第欧根尼说着，朝斯陶滕堡勋爵深深一躬，躬得满面讥诮，“多谢您暂缓了对在下的责罚，在下实在当不起。几时容我登门拜见大人阁下，领受这份我受之有愧的惩处？”
“这份惩处放多久也少不了分毫，你的无礼实在出人相信之外，”斯陶滕堡怒道，“滚！”他补上，存心不去理会对方那副存心无礼的架势，“留我一个人同这丫头在一起。我的事同她有关。”
“同我也有关，仁慈的大人，”第欧根尼带着和霭的笑应道，“不然我到这儿来做什么。这间房是我的——大人阁下兴许不知道——您总不至于愿意在我这儿多留一分钟客人吧。”
“我早知道这贱货是你的相好——不然我根本不来。”
“住口，别侮辱这姑娘，伙计，”第欧根尼说，髭须又张起来——这是他脾气烧开的可靠信号，“她同你讲的是实话：那位从哈勒姆失踪的高贵小姐在哪儿，她一无所知。您想打听那个，最好另找别人。”
可斯陶滕堡这当口已把神志——至少部分地——收了回来。

===Original===

All his life he had been accustomed to treat these foreign adventurers with the contempt which they deserved. In the days of John of Barneveld’s high position in the State, his sons would never have dreamed of parleying with the knaves, and if﻿—which God forbid!﻿—one of them had dared then to lay hands on any member of the High Advocate’s family, hanging would certainly have been the inevitable punishment of such insolence.
Something of that old haughtiness and pride of caste crept into the attitude of the Lord of Stoutenburg now, and prudence also suggested that he should feign to ignore the rough usage which he had received at the hands of this contemptible rascal. Though he was by no means unarmed﻿—for he never went abroad these days without a poniard in his belt﻿—he had, of a truth, no mind to engage in a brawl with this young Hercules whose profession was that of arms and who might consequently get easily the better of him.
He made every effort therefore to remain calm and to look as dignified as his disordered toilet would allow.
“You heard what I said to this girl?” he queried, speaking carelessly.
“You screamed loudly enough,” replied Diogenes lightly. “I heard you through the closed door. I confess that I listened for quite a long while: your conversation greatly interested me. I only interfered when I thought it necessary.”
“So then I need not repeat what I said,” quoth the other lightly. “Hanging for you, my man, unless you tell me where you have hidden Jongejuffrouw Beresteyn.”
“I? What have I to do with that noble lady, pray?”
“It is futile to bandy words with me. I know every circumstance of the disappearance of the lady, and could denounce you to the authorities within half an hour, and see you hanged for the outrage before sunrise.”
“Then I do wonder,” said Diogenes suavely, “that your Magnificence doth not do this, for of a truth you must hate me fairly thoroughly by now.”
“Hate you, man? I’d gladly see you hang, or better still broken on the wheel. But I must know from you first where you have hidden the jongejuffrouw.”
“If I am to hang anyway, sir, why should I trouble to tell you?”

===Chinese===

他这一辈子，习惯了对这班外国冒险家恰如其分地加以鄙夷。想当年约翰·巴内费尔德位极人臣，他的儿子们做梦也不会同这等无赖平起平坐地说话；而那时——上帝不容！——若有一个敢动大辩护官家里任何一根毫毛，绞架必是那份胆大包天少不了的下场。
斯陶滕堡勋爵此刻的姿态里，又渗进了几分旧日那种门第的骄倨；审慎也在提醒他：对这个可鄙流氓加在他身上的粗辱，不如装作不知。他倒并非没有家伙——这些日子他出门，腰带上从不缺一柄匕首——可他实在没心思同这个年轻的赫剌克勒斯打一场：人家吃的就是武饭，占便宜多半是他。
于是他竭力保持平静，在凌乱的衣冠许可的范围里，摆出尽量体面的样子。
“你听见我方才对这丫头说的话了？”他漫不经心地问。
“您嗓门够大，”第欧根尼轻轻答，“隔着关着的门我都听见了。我承认听了好一阵：你们的谈话让我大感兴趣。直到觉得非出手不可，我才插的手。”
“那我就不必重复了，”对方也轻轻地说，“绞死你，小子——除非你说出你把贝雷斯坦小姐藏在了哪里。”
“我？请问，我同那位高贵小姐有什么相干？”
“同我绕舌头没用。小姐失踪的每一个情节我都清楚，半小时内就能把你们告到官府，天亮前看着你为这桩暴行吊上绞架。”
“那我倒奇怪，”第欧根尼温温地说，“大人阁下怎么还不去告——您这会儿对我，想必恨得相当透彻了。”
“恨你，小子？看你上绞架我求之不得，或者更妙：上轮刑。可我得先从你嘴里知道，你把那小姐藏在了哪里。”
“横竖是死，先生，我又何苦费神告诉您？”

===Original===

“The lady is my affianced wife,” said Stoutenburg haughtily, “I have every right to demand an explanation from you, why you are here when by the terms of your contract with my friend Nicolaes Beresteyn you should at this moment be on your way to Rotterdam, escorting the jongejuffrouw to the house of Ben Isaje, the banker.﻿ ﻿… You see that I am well informed,” he added impatiently, seeing that Diogenes had become suddenly silent, and that a curious shadow had spread over his persistently smiling face.
“So well informed, sir,” rejoined the latter after a slight pause, and speaking more seriously than he had done hitherto, “so well informed that I marvel you do not know that by the terms of that same contract I pledged my word to convey the jongejuffrouw safely to a certain spot and with all possible speed, but that further actions on my part were to remain for mine own guidance. I also pledged my word of honour that I would remain silent about all these matters.”
“Bah!” broke in Stoutenburg roughly, “knaves like you have no honour to pledge.”
“No doubt, sir, you are the best judge of what a knave would do.”
“Insolent﻿ ﻿… do you dare﻿ ﻿… ?”
“If you like it better, sir, I’ll say that I have parleyed long enough with you to suit my temper. This room is mine,” he added, speaking every whit as haughtily as did the other man. “I have business with this wench, and came here, desirous to speak with her alone, so I pray you go! this roof is too lowly to shelter the Lord of Stoutenburg.”
At mention of his name Stoutenburg’s sunken cheeks took on the colour of lead, and with a swift, instinctive gesture, his hand flew to the hilt of the dagger under his doublet. During this hot and brief quarrel with this man, the thought had never entered his mind that his identity might be known to his antagonist, that he﻿—a fugitive from justice and with a heavy price still upon his head﻿—was even now at the mercy of this contemptible adventurer whom he had learnt to hate as he had never hated a single human soul before now.
Prudence, however, was quick enough to warn him not to betray himself completely. The knave obviously suspected his identity﻿—how he did that, Stoutenburg could not conjecture, but after all he might only have drawn a bow at a venture: it was important above all not to let him see that that bow had struck home.

===Chinese===

“那位小姐是我的未婚妻，”斯陶滕堡倨傲地说，“我完全有权要你解释：你为什么在这儿——照你同我朋友尼古莱斯·贝雷斯坦立的约，你此刻本该在去鹿特丹的路上，护送小姐去银行家本·伊萨耶的宅子。……您看，我消息灵通，”见第欧根尼忽然哑了，那张一贯含笑的脸上掠过一道古怪的暗影，他不耐烦地补上。
“灵通得很，先生，”对方略顿一瞬后应道，语气比先前郑重，“灵通得叫我奇怪：您竟不知道按同一份契约，我只许下把小姐平安、尽快送到一处指定的地方，此外的行止，全凭我自家做主。我还以名誉许下，对这一切守口如瓶。”
“呸！”斯陶滕堡粗鲁地插进来，“你们这号无赖，没有名誉可押。”
“无疑，先生，无赖会做什么，您是最高的裁判。”
“放肆……你敢……？”
“您听着更顺耳的话也可以，先生：同您周旋了这么久，已经超出我的耐心了。这间房是我的，”他补上，说得同对方一般倨傲，“我同这丫头有事，是特来找她单独说话的。所以请您走吧！这屋顶太低微，遮不住斯陶滕堡勋爵。”
一听见自己的名字，斯陶滕堡深陷的两颊顿时变成铅色，一个迅疾的本能动作，他的手飞向紧身上衣底下的匕首柄。同这个人这场短促的口角里，他从没想过：自己的身份可能已被对手知晓——他，一个在逃的钦犯、头上还悬着重赏——此刻竟捏在这个可鄙的冒险家手里；而这个人，他已恨之入骨，恨得生平从未恨过任何一个活人。
不过审慎来得也快，警告他不可全盘自露。这无赖显然疑到了他的身份——怎么疑到的，斯陶滕堡猜不出；可他到底也可能只是瞎猫碰上死耗子：要紧的是千万不能让他看出，这一箭正中靶心。

===Original===

Therefore after the first instant of terror and surprise he resumed as best he could his former haughty attitude, and said with well-feigned carelessness:
“The Lord of Stoutenburg? Do you expect his visit then? What have you to do with him? ’Tis dangerous, you know, to court his friendship just now.”
“I do not court his friendship, sir,” replied Diogenes with his gently ironical smile; “the Lord of Stoutenburg hath many enemies these days; and, methinks, that if it came to a question of hanging he would stand at least as good a chance of the gallows as I.”
“No doubt, an you knew how to lay hands on him; you would be over ready to denounce him to the Stadtholder for the sake of the blood-money which you would receive for this act.”
“Well played, my lord,” retorted Diogenes with a ringing laugh. “Dondersteen! but you apparently think me a fool as well as a knave. Lay my hands on the Lord of Stoutenburg did you say? By St. Bavon, have I not done so already? aye! and made him lick the dust, too, at my feet? I could sell him to the Stadtholder without further trouble﻿—denounce him even now to the authorities only that I do not happen to be a vendor of swine-flesh﻿—or else.﻿ ﻿…”
A double cry interrupted the flow of Diogenes’ wrathful eloquence: a cry of rage from Stoutenburg and one of terror from the girl, who all this while﻿—not understanding the cause and purport of the quarrel between the two men﻿—had been cowering in a remote corner of the room anxious only to avoid observation, fearful lest she should be seen.
But now she suddenly ran forward, swift as a deer, unerring as a cat, and the next moment she had thrown herself on the upraised arm of Stoutenburg in whose hand gleamed the sharp steel of his dagger.
“Murder!” she cried in a frenzy of sorrow. “Save thyself! he will murder thee!”
Diogenes, as was his wont, threw back his head and sent his merry laugh echoing through the tumbledown house from floor to floor, until, in response to that lightheartedness which had burst forth in such a ringing laugh, pallid faces were lifted wearily from toil, and around thin, pinched lips the reflex of a smile came creeping over the furrows caused by starvation and misery.
“Let go his arm, wench,” he cried gaily; “he’ll not hurt me, never fear. Hatred has drawn a film over his eyes and caused his hand to tremble.

===Chinese===

于是，恐惧和惊愕的头一瞬过后，他尽力摆回先前那副倨傲，装出满不在乎的样子说：
“斯陶滕堡勋爵？那么你是等他来喽？你同他有什么相干？告诉你，眼下巴结他，可危险。”
“我不巴结他，先生，”第欧根尼带着他温和的讥诮答，“斯陶滕堡勋爵这些天仇人不少；而且我想，真到了要吊谁的时候，他上绞架的把握，至少同我不相上下。”
“那是，只要你知道上哪儿抓他；为那笔血赏，你怕是巴不得把他告到执政跟前。”
“接得好，大人，”第欧根尼朗声大笑，“该死的雷公！您显然把我当个傻子，外加无赖。您说，把斯陶滕堡勋爵拿到手？以圣巴沃起誓，我不是已经拿到了吗？是啊！还叫他在我脚底下舐了土呢？我毫不费事就能把他卖给执政——这会儿就能告到官府——只可惜我这人不做猪啰买卖——再不然。……”
一声双人的喊叫打断了第欧根尼这番怒气冲冲的滔滔：斯陶滕堡一声怒吼，姑娘一声惊叫——她一直缩在屋子最远的角落，听不懂这两个男人为何争吵，只求躲开目光，怕被人看见。
可这时她突然冲出来，快得像鹿，准得像猫，下一刻已扑上斯陶滕堡高高扬起的手臂——那手里，匕首的寒光一闪。
“杀人啦！”她悲疯般地喊，“快逃！他要杀你！”
第欧根尼照他的老样子，头往后一仰，欢快的笑声在这座破楼里从一层震到一层；应着这阵朗声迸发的轻快，一张张苍白的脸从劳作里疲惫地抬起来，一条条瘦瘪的唇边，饥饿和苦难刻下的沟壑上，爬过一丝笑的反光。
“撒开他的胳膊，丫头，”他欢快地喊，“他伤不了我，别怕。仇恨糊了他的眼，也叫他的手发抖。

===Original===

Put back your poniard, my lord,” he added lightly, “the penniless adventurer and paid hireling is unworthy of your steel. Keep it whetted for your own defence and for the protection of the gracious lady who has plighted her troth to you.”
“Name her not, man!” cried Stoutenburg, whose arm had dropped by his side, but whose voice was still hoarse with the passion of hate which now consumed him.
“Is her name polluted through passing my lips? Yet is she under my protection, placed there by those who should have guarded her honour with their life.”
“Touch my future wife but with the tips of thy fingers, *plepshurk*, and I’ll hang thee on the nearest tree with mine own hands.”
“Wait to threaten, my lord, until you have the power: until then go your way. I﻿—the miserable rascal whom you abhor, the knave whom you despise﻿—do give you your life and your freedom which, as you well know, I hold at this moment in the hollow of my hand. But remember that I give it you only because to my mind one innocent woman has already suffered quite enough because of you, without having to mourn the man whom she loves and being widowed ere she is a wife. Because of that you may go out of this room a free man﻿—free to pursue your tortuous aims and your ambitious scheme. They are naught to me and I know nothing about them. But this I do know﻿—that a woman has been placed in my charge by one who should deem her honour more sacred than his own; in this infamy I now see that you too, my lord, have had a hand. The lady, you say, is your future wife, yet you placed her under my care﻿—a knave, a rascal﻿—miserable *plepshurk* was the last epithet which you applied to me﻿—you! who also should have guarded her good name with your very life. To suit your own ends, you entrusted her to me! Well! to suit mine own I’ll not let you approach her, until﻿—having accomplished the errand for which I am being paid﻿—I will myself escort the lady back to her father. To this am I also pledged! and both these pledges do I mean to fulfil and you, my lord, do but waste your time in arguing with me.”
The Lord of Stoutenburg had not attempted to interrupt Diogenes in his long peroration. All the thoughts of hatred and revenge that sprang in his mind with every word which this man uttered, he apparently thought wisest to conceal for the moment.

===Chinese===

（接上）收起你的匕首吧，大人，”他轻轻补上，“身无分文的冒险家、拿钱的雇佣工，配不上您的钢。留着磨快了自卫，护那位同您订了婚的尊贵小姐吧。”
“不许提她，小子！”斯陶滕堡喊，手臂已垂回身侧，嗓音却仍被那股正烧着他的仇恨哽得沙哑，“她名讳出你的口，便是玷汙！可她如今在我的保护之下——是那些本该拿命护她名节的人，把她放到这保护之下的。”
“你敢拿指尖碰一碰我未来的妻子，窝囊废，我就亲手把你吊上最近的一棵树。”
“等您有了那个力道，大人，再来威胁不迟：眼下请上路吧。我——您深恶的那个下贱流氓，您鄙视的那个无赖——饶您一命，还您自由——您清楚，这会儿两样都攥在我手心里。我所以放您，只因在我看来，一个无辜的女人已为您吃了够多的苦，犯不上再为她爱着的男人戴孝、没过门就守寡。为这个，您可以走出这间房，还是个自由人——自由地继续您曲里弯弯的图谋和您的野心。那些与我无干，我一概不知。可这一条我知道——有位小姐被人托到了我手里，托的人本该把她的名节看得比自己的命还重；如今我看出，这桩丑事里，大人，您也插过一手。您说那小姐是您未来的妻子，可您把她交给了我照看——一个无赖、一个恶棍——您骂我的最后一个词是“可怜的窝囊废”——您！您也本该拿命护她的清名。为遂您自己的目的，您把她托给了我！好！为遂我的目的，我不许您接近她，直到——我领了钱办的这趟差办完——由我亲自护送小姐回她父亲身边。这一条我也许下了！两个诺言我都打算兑现，您，大人，同我多争一句，也只是白费工夫。”
这长长一篇话，斯陶滕堡勋爵没有试图打断。这个人吐出的每一个字，都在他心里激起一串仇恨与复仇的念头；他把它们统统按住，显然觉得眼下以不露为上。

===Original===

Now that Diogenes, after he had finished speaking, turned unceremoniously on his heel and left Stoutenburg standing in the middle of the room, the latter hesitated for a few minutes longer. Angry and contemptuous words were all ready to his lips, but Diogenes was paying no heed to him; he had drawn the girl with him to the bedside of the cripple, and there began talking quietly in whispers to her. Stoutenburg saw that he gave the wench some money.
Smothering a final, comprehensive oath the noble lord went quietly out of the room.
“How that man doth hate thee,” whispered the girl in awestruck tones, as soon as she saw that the door had closed behind him. “And I hate him, too,” she added, as she clenched her thin hands, “he is cruel, coarse and evil.”
“Cruel, coarse and evil?” said Diogenes with a shrug of his wide shoulders, “and yet there is a delicate, innocent girl who loves him well enough to forget all his crimes and to plight her troth to him. Women are strange creatures, wench﻿—’tis a wise philosopher who steers widely clear of their path.”

===Chinese===

第欧根尼说完，不容分说地一转身，把斯陶滕堡撂在屋子中央；后者又踌躇了几分钟。愤怒和轻蔑的词句早已到了唇边，可第欧根尼再不看他：他拉着姑娘走到瘫子床边，低声同她细细说起话来。斯陶滕堡看见他给了那丫头一些钱。
这位尊贵的勋爵压下最后一句包罗万象的咒骂，悄悄出了屋。
“那人多么恨你，”见他走远，门在身后合上，姑娘敬畏地低语，“我也恨他，”她攥紧瘦手又补一句，“他残忍、粗野、恶毒。”
“残忍、粗野、恶毒？”第欧根尼耸耸宽阔的肩膀，“可偏偏有个娇弱、天真的姑娘爱他，爱得足以忘掉他全部的罪，还同他订了婚。女人真是奇怪的造物，丫头——绕开她们的道走远些的，才算得上聪明的哲人。

## XXV An Arrant Knave / 二十五 一个彻头彻尾的无赖
===Original===

In the street below, not far from the house which he had just quitted, Stoutenburg came on Nicolaes and Jan ensconced in the dark against a wall. Beresteyn quickly explained to his friend the reason of his presence here.
“I came with Jan,” he said, “because I wished to speak with you without delay.”
“Come as far as the cathedral then,” said Stoutenburg curtly. “I feel that in this *vervloekte* street the walls and windows are full of ears and prying eyes. Jan,” he added, turning to the other man, “you must remain here and on no account lose sight of that rascal when he leaves this house. Follow him in and out of Haarlem, and if you do not see me again tonight, join me at Ryswyk as soon as you can, and come there prepared with full knowledge of his plans.”
Leaving Jan in observation the two men made their way now in the direction of the Groote Markt. It was still very cold, even though there was a slight suspicion in the air of a coming change in the weather: a scent as of the south wind blowing from over the estuaries, while the snow beneath the feet had lost something of its crispness and purity. The thaw had not yet set in, but it was coquetting with the frost, challenging it to a passage of arms, wherein either combatant might completely succumb.
As Stoutenburg had surmised the porch of the cathedral was lonely and deserted, even the beggars had all gone home for the night. A tiny lamp fixed into the panelling of the wall flickered dimly in the draught. Stoutenburg sat down on the wooden bench﻿—dark and polished with age, which ran alongside one of the walls, and with a brusque and febrile gesture drew his friend down beside him.
“Well?” he asked in that nervous, jerky way of his, “What is it?”
“Something that wilt horrify you, just as it did me,” replied Beresteyn, who spoke breathlessly as if under stress of grave excitement. “When I parted from you awhile ago, I did what you asked me to do. I posted Jan outside the door of the *tapperij* after I had pointed out our rogue to him through the glass door. Imagine my astonishment when I saw that at that moment our rascal was in close conversation with my father.”
“With your father?”
“With my father,” reiterated Beresteyn. “That fool, Hals, was with him, and there were another half dozen busybodies sitting round the table.

===Chinese===

楼下街上，离他刚离开的那所房子不远，斯陶滕堡撞见了尼古拉斯和扬，两人正贴墙藏身在暗处。贝雷斯坦赶紧向朋友说明自己为何在此。
“我是同扬一起来的，”他说，“因为有话要立刻跟你说。”
“那就到教堂门廊那边去说。”斯陶滕堡没好气地说，“我觉得这条该死的（vervloekte）街上，墙上窗里全是耳朵和窥探的眼睛。扬，”他转向另一人补了一句，“你必须守在这儿，那无赖一出这所房子，你无论如何不能让他走脱。跟着他进出哈勒姆；今晚若是见不着我，就尽快到赖斯韦克来找我，来之前把他的一应打算摸个一清二楚。”
留下扬守望，两人朝大广场方向走去。天依然很冷，只是空气里隐隐透出一丝天气将变的迹象：仿佛有南风从河口那边吹来的气息，脚下的雪也失了几分脆硬和洁净。解冻天气还没有真正到来，却在同严寒卖弄风情，向它挑战交锋，而交锋之下，无论哪一方都可能一败涂地。
不出斯陶滕堡所料，教堂的门廊冷冷清清、空无一人，连叫花子也都回家过夜去了。嵌在墙板里的一盏小灯，在穿堂风里幽幽地闪。斯陶滕堡在一条木长凳上坐下——凳子年头久了，乌黑发亮，靠墙摆着——又用一个急躁而亢奋的手势把朋友拉到身边坐下。
“怎么样？”他用他那副紧张急促的腔调问，“什么事？”
“一件会让你惊骇的事，就跟吓着了我一样。”贝雷斯坦答道，他说话气喘吁吁，像是被巨大的激动压着。“方才同你分手后，我照你的吩咐做了。我先隔着玻璃门把咱们那只流氓指给扬看，随后让他守住酒馆堂座（tapperij）的门口。你想想我那时的惊愕吧——咱们那无赖，正当口，正同我父亲促膝密谈呢。”
“同你父亲？”
“同我父亲。”贝雷斯坦重说一遍，“那个傻瓜哈尔斯也在他旁边，桌边还围坐着另外六个爱管闲事的人。

===Original===

Our man was evidently the centre of interest; I could not then hear what was said, but at one moment I saw that my father shook him cordially by the hand.”
“*Vervloekte Keerl!*” exclaimed Stoutenburg.
“I didn’t know at first what to do. I didn’t want to go into the *tapperij* and to show myself just then, but at all costs I wished to know what my father and that arrant rascal had to say to one another. So, bidding Jan on no account to lose sight of the man, I made my way round to the service door behind the bar, and there bribed one of the wenches to let me stand under the lintel and to remain on the watch. It was quite dark where I stood and I had a good view of the *tapperij* without fear of being seen, and as my father and that cursed adventurer were speaking loudly enough I could hear all that they said.”
“Well?” queried Stoutenburg impatiently.
“Well, my friend,” quoth Beresteyn with slow emphasis, “that *vervloekte* scoundrel was making a promise to my father to bring Gilda safely back to Haarlem, and my father was promising him a fortune as his reward.”
“I am not surprised,” remarked Stoutenburg calmly.
“But﻿ ﻿…”
“That man, my friend, is the most astute blackguard I have ever come across in the whole course of my life. His English blood I imagine hath made him into a thoroughgoing rogue. He has played you false﻿—always did mean to play you false if it suited his purpose! By God, Nicolaes! what fools we were to trust one of these foreign adventurers. They’ll do anything for money, and this man instead of being﻿—as we thought﻿—an exception to the rule, is a worse scoundrel than any of his compeers. He has simply taken Gilda a little way out of Haarlem, and then came back here to see what bargain he could strike with your father for her return.”
“Gilda is some way out of Haarlem,” rejoined Beresteyn thoughtfully. “Jan and I heard that knave talking to his friend Hals later on. Hals was asking him to sup and sleep at his house. But he declined the proffered bed, though he accepted the supper: ‘I have a journey before me this night,’ he said, ‘and must leave the city at moonrise.’ It seemed to me that he meant to travel far.”
“She may be still at Bennebrock, or mayhap at Leyden﻿—he could not have taken her further than that in the time. Anyhow it would be quite easy for him to go back to her during the night, and bring her into Haarlem tomorrow. Friend!”

===Chinese===

咱们那个人显然是众人瞩目的中心；当时我听不见他们说什么，可有一会儿，我看见我父亲热络络地同他握手。”
“该死的（vervloekte）家伙（Keerl）！”斯陶滕堡骂出声来。
“起初我不知道如何是好。我不想那会儿进堂座去露面，可又不惜一切代价想知道我父亲同那个彻头彻尾的无赖要说些什么。于是我吩咐扬无论如何盯住那人，自己绕到柜台后面的后门，塞了钱给一个女招待，让她容我站在门框底下望风。我站的地方一片漆黑，堂座里看得清清楚楚，又不怕被人瞧见，何况我父亲和那个该诅咒的冒险家说话嗓门够大，他们说的话我一字不落地全听见了。”
“后来呢？”斯陶滕堡不耐烦地追问。
“是这样的，我的朋友，”贝雷斯坦字斟句酌地说，“那个恶棍正在向我父亲许诺，要把吉尔达平平安安带回哈勒姆，我父亲呢，许给他一笔家财作酬报。”
“我不奇怪。”斯陶滕堡淡淡地说。
“可是……”
“那个人，我的朋友，是我这辈子见过的最精明的恶棍。我想，是他那英格兰血把他造就成了一个十足的流氓。他一直在骗你——只要合他的目的，他从来就存心骗你！天哪，尼古拉斯！我们竟蠢到相信这么一个外邦冒险家。这种人为了钱什么都干得出来，而我们原以为此人是个例外，谁知他比他那伙人里任何一个都更无赖。他不过是把吉尔达带出哈勒姆不远的地方，然后回到这儿来，看看能同你父亲就送她回来做笔什么交易。”
“吉尔达确实在离哈勒姆有一程的地方。”贝雷斯坦若有所思地应道，“扬和我后来听见那无赖同他朋友哈尔斯说话。哈尔斯请他去家里吃晚饭、过夜，饭他吃了，床却推了：‘今晚我还要赶路，’他说，‘月出时分必须出城。’照我看，他打算走远路。”
“她也许还在本内布罗克，或者到了莱顿——这点工夫他带不了她更远。无论如何，他夜里赶回她那里轻而易举，明天就能把她带进哈勒姆。朋友！”

===Original===

he added earnestly, “the situation is intolerable﻿—unthinkable! After all that we have done, the risks which we have taken, Gilda’s return now﻿—a certain denunciation from her﻿—and failure and death once more stare us in the face, and this time more insistently.”
“It is unthinkable, as you say,” cried Beresteyn vehemently, “but the situation is not so hopeless as you seem to think. I can go at once to my father and denounce the rogue to him. I can tell him that I have reason to believe that the man to whom he has just promised a fortune for the return of Gilda is the very man who hath abducted her.”
“Impossible,” said Stoutenburg calmly.
“Why?”
“Your father would have the man arrested, he would be searched, and papers and letters writ by you to Ben Isaje of Rotterdam will be found in his possession. These papers would proclaim you the prime mover in the outrage against your sister.”
“True! I had not thought of that. But, instead of going to my father, I could denounce the rascal to the city magistrate on suspicion of having abducted my sister. Van der Meer would give me the command of the town guard sent out to arrest him, I could search him myself and take possession of all his papers ere I bring him before the magistrate.”
“Bah! the magistracy of Haarlem moves with ponderous slowness. While that oaf, Van der Meer, makes preparations for sending out the town guard, our rogue will slip through our fingers, and mayhap be back in Haarlem with Gilda ere we find him again.”
“Let me have Jan and one or two of Heemskerk’s mercenaries,” urged Beresteyn, “we could seize him and his papers tonight as soon as he leaves the city gates.”
“Then, out of revenge,” said Stoutenburg, “he will refuse to tell us what he hath done with Gilda.”
“Bah!” retorted Beresteyn cynically, “here in Haarlem we can always apply torture.”
“Then, if he speaks, Gilda can be back here in time to denounce us all. No, no, my friend,” continued Stoutenburg firmly, “let us own at once that by trusting that scoundrel we have run our heads into a noose out of which only our wits can extricate us. We must meet cunning with cunning, treachery if need be with treachery. Gilda﻿—of course﻿—must not remain at the mercy of brigands, but she must not be given her freedom to do us the harm which she hath already threatened.

===Chinese===

他恳切地接着说，“这局面叫人无法容忍——想都不敢想！我们做了这一切，冒了这些险，如今吉尔达一回来——她必然告发我们——失败和死亡就又一次瞪眼望着我们，这一回逼得更紧了。”
“确如你所说，想都不敢想，”贝雷斯坦激动地喊道，“可局面并不像你想的那样无望。我可以立刻去找我父亲，当面揭发那流氓。我可以告诉他，我有理由相信，他刚许以重金求其送回吉尔达的那个人，正是劫走她的那一个。”
“不行。”斯陶滕堡平静地说。
“为什么？”
“你父亲会把那人抓起来，会搜他的身，你写给鹿特丹的本·伊萨耶的文书和信件就会落到他手里。那些文书会昭示天下：坑害你妹妹的主谋正是你。”
“是啊！这我倒没想到。不过，我可以不去找我父亲，改向市政法官告发那恶棍，说他涉嫌劫走我妹妹。范德米尔会把派出去抓人的城防卫队的指挥权交给我，我可以亲自搜他的身，把他所有文书都拿到手，再带他去见法官。”
“呸！哈勒姆的官府行事笨重得要命。等那个蠢货范德米尔磨磨蹭蹭调齐城防卫队，咱们的流氓早从指缝里溜了，说不定已经带着吉尔达回转哈勒姆，我们还没寻着他的影呢。”
“把扬和海姆斯凯克麾下的一两个雇佣兵给我，”贝雷斯坦急切地说，“今晚他一出城门，我们就能把他连人带文书一齐拿下。”
“那他出于报复，”斯陶滕堡说，“就死也不肯告诉我们他把吉尔达怎么了。”
“呸！”贝雷斯坦冷冷地顶了一句，“在哈勒姆，我们还愁没有刑具伺候。”
“那他一开口，吉尔达就能及时赶回来告发我们大家。不，不，我的朋友，”斯陶滕堡斩钉截铁地接着说，“咱们得马上承认，信了那个恶棍，我们等于自己把脑袋伸进了绞索，如今只有靠咱们的脑子才能挣脱出来。得以诈还诈，必要的时候，以背叛还背叛。吉尔达——那自然——不能任她落在匪徒手里听凭摆布，可也不能放她自由，让她照她已经威胁过的那样害我们。”

===Original===

Remember this, Nicolaes,” he added, placing his hand upon his friend’s shoulder and forcing him to look straight into his own feverishly glowing eyes, “remember that, when all these troubles are over, Gilda will become my wife. The devotion of my entire life shall then compensate her for the slight wrong which fate compels us to do her at this moment. Will you remember that, my friend?”
“I do remember it,” replied the other, “but﻿ ﻿…”
“And will you try and trust me as you would yourself?”
“I do trust you, Willem, as I would trust myself; only tell me what you want to do.”
“I want to bring that knave to the gallows without compromising you and the success of our cause,” said Stoutenburg firmly.
“But how can you do it?”
“That I do not know yet; I have only vague thoughts in my mind. But hate, remember, is a hard and very efficient taskmaster, and I hate that man, Nicolaes, almost as much as I hate the Prince of Orange. But ’tis the Prince’s death which I want first; because of this my hatred of the rascal must lie dormant just a few days. But it shall lose nothing by waiting, and already I see before me visions of an exemplary revenge which shall satisfy you and gratify my hate.”
“Can I help you in any way?”
“Not at present; I have no definite plans just now. All I know is that we must possess ourselves of the rascal’s person as well as of Gilda without the risk of compromising ourselves. In this, of course, we have now Jan’s valuable help; he is a splendid leader and entirely trustworthy where the cause of his own hatred against the Prince is served.”
“And, of course, you have the thirty or forty men﻿—mercenaries and louts﻿—whom Heemskerk, van Does and the others have been recruiting for you.”
“Exactly. I can easily detail half a dozen of them to follow Jan. That is our first move, my good Beresteyn,” he added emphatically, “to gain possession of Gilda, and to capture the rascal. Only tell me this, what are the papers now in that knave’s possession which might compromise you if they were found?”
“I had to write a letter to Ben Isaje, telling him to convince himself that Gilda was safe and in good health, ere he paid the rascal a sum of 3,000 guilders. This letter is writ in mine own hand and signed with my name.

===Chinese===

“记住这个，尼古拉斯，”他补了一句，一只手按在朋友肩上，逼他直视自己那双烧得发亮的眼睛，“记住，等这一切风波过去，吉尔达就要做我的妻子。到那时，我毕生的深情挚爱，会补偿命运此刻逼着我们对她犯下的这点小小委屈。你能记住吗，我的朋友？”
“我记住了，”对方答道，“可是……”
“你能不能试着信我，像信你自己一样？”
“我信你，威廉，像信我自己一样信你；只告诉我，你想怎么做。”
“我要把那无赖送上绞架，又不连累你，不耽误我们大业成功。”斯陶滕堡斩钉截铁地说。
“可你怎么做得到？”
“这我还不知道；心里只有些模糊的念头。可是记住，仇恨是个严酷又极有效率的监工，而我恨那个人，尼古拉斯，几乎同恨奥兰治亲王一样狠。只是我要的先是亲王的命；为此，我对这流氓的恨只得先蛰伏几天。可蛰伏不会折损它分毫，我眼前已经浮现出一番以儆效尤的复仇景象，保管叫你满意，也叫我的仇恨得偿。”
“我能帮上什么忙吗？”
“眼下还不用；我此刻还没有定盘的计。我只知道，我们必须把那流氓本人连同吉尔达一起弄到手，又不冒连累自身的风险。这方面，咱们如今自然有扬这个得力的帮手；他是个顶好的头领，只要事关他自己对亲王的那份仇恨，他完全靠得住。”
“再说，你手上还有那三四十号人——雇佣兵和地痞——都是海姆斯凯克、范杜斯他们替你招来的。”
“正是。我随手就可以拨六个人跟随扬。这就是咱们的第一步，我亲爱的贝雷斯坦，”他加重语气补了一句，“把吉尔达弄到手，把那流氓擒住。只是告诉我，如今那无赖身上带着的、一旦被查出可能连累你的，是些什么文书？”
“我不得不给本·伊萨耶写了一封信，告诉他务必先亲自确认吉尔达安然无恙，再把三千盾付给那流氓。这封信是我亲笔所写，签着我的名字。”

===Original===

Then there is a formal order to Ben Isaje to pay over the money, but that was writ in the usual way by the public scrivener and is signed with the cipher which I always use in all monetary transactions with the Jew. He keeps these formal documents in his archives and all his clients use a cipher in the same way.”
“How is that formal order worded?”
“As far as I remember it runs thus: ‘In consideration of valuable services rendered to me by the bearer of this note, I desire you to pay him the sum of 3,000 guilders out of my monies which lie with you at interest.’ The cipher signature consists of the words ‘Schwarzer Kato’ surmounted by a triangle.”
“And is that cipher known to anyone save to Ben Isaje?”
“Alas! it is known to my father. We both use it for private business transactions.”
“But to Gilda?” insisted Stoutenburg. “Would Gilda know it if she saw it?”
“She could not be certain of it﻿ ﻿… though, of course, she might guess. Schwarzer Kato is the name of a tulip raised by my father, and the triangle is a sign used sometimes by our house in business. But it would be mere conjecture on her part.”
“Then everything will still be for the best, never fear, my good Beresteyn,” exclaimed Stoutenburg, whose hard, cruel face was glowing with excitement. “Chance indeed has been on our side throughout this business. An you will trust me to finish it now; you’ll have no cause for anxiety or regrets. Come! let us find Jan at once! I have a few orders to give him, and then mean to be on my way to Ryswyk tonight.”
He rose to his feet and now the glitter in his hollow eyes appeared almost inhuman. He was a man whose whole soul fed upon hatred, upon vengeance planned and accomplished, upon desire for supreme power; and at this moment his scheme for murdering the Stadtholder was backed by one for obtaining possession of the woman he loved, and being revenged on the man who had insulted and jeered at him.
Beresteyn, always ready to accept the leadership of his friend, followed him in silence down the street. After awhile they once more came upon Jan, who apparently had never moved all this while from his post of observation.
“Well?” asked Stoutenburg in a scarce audible whisper, “has he not gone yet?”
“Not yet,” replied Jan.
Stoutenburg cast a quick, almost furtive glance in the direction of the house where he had experienced such dire humiliation a brief half hour ago.

===Chinese===

“另外还有一张给本·伊萨耶的正式付款凭单，但那同惯常做法一样，出自公共代书人的手笔，签的是我在同这犹太人的一切银钱往来中一直用的那个密押。这类正式文书他都存在自己的案卷里，他的主顾们也都是同样用密押的。”
“那张凭单上怎么写的？”
“我记得大意是这样的：‘兹因持单人于本人有重要效劳，请从存于足下处生息之本人款项内，支付该人三千盾。’密押签名是‘施瓦策·卡托’两个词，上头加一个三角记号。”
“这个密押，除了本·伊萨耶，还有旁人知道吗？”
“唉！我父亲知道。我们父子俩的私人生意往来都用它。”
“那吉尔达呢？”斯陶滕堡追问，“吉尔达见了认得出吗？”
“她断不定……当然，也可能猜到。‘施瓦策·卡托’是我父亲培育的一种郁金香的名字，那个三角记号有时是我们家做生意用的记号。但她至多只能是猜测。”
“那就万事仍会称心，别怕，我亲爱的贝雷斯坦。”斯陶滕堡喊道，他那张冷酷的脸上放着兴奋的光，“这件事自始至终，运气实在都站在我们这边。你只管放心把这残局交给我来收尾；你不会有可焦虑、可懊悔的地方。走！这就去找扬！我有几道命令要交代他，然后我今晚就动身去赖斯韦克。”
他站起身，此刻他那双深陷的眼睛里的凶光，几乎不像人所有。他这个人，整个灵魂以仇恨为食，以谋划和践行复仇为食，以至高权力之欲为食；此刻，他谋杀执政的阴谋，又添了一重旁的计划作后盾：把心爱的女人弄到手，并向那个羞辱过他、讥笑过他的人复仇。
贝雷斯坦向来甘愿听朋友领头，一言不发地跟他沿街走去。过了一会儿，他们又到了扬的跟前——看样子，这一晌他压根没挪过守望的岗位。
“怎么样？”斯陶滕堡用几乎听不见的耳语问，“他还没走？”
“还没。”扬答道。
斯陶滕堡朝那所房子飞快地、几乎是偷偷地瞥了一眼——就在半个钟头前，他在那里蒙受了那般奇耻大辱。

===Original===

A curious whistling sound escaped through his clenched teeth, a sound such as many a wild beast makes when expectant of prey. Then he drew Jan further away from the house, fearful lest his words were wafted toward it on the wind.
“Keep him in sight, Jan,” he commanded, “until he goes to the house of Mynheer Hals in the Peuselaarsteg, whither he means to go for supper. There you may safely leave him for an hour, and go directly to the house of my Lord of Heemskerk whom you know.
