Ask him for half a dozen of his foreign mercenaries; tell him they are for my immediate service. These men will then help you to keep our knave in sight. He will leave Haarlem at moonrise, and you must never lose his track for a moment. Presently he should be escorting a lady in the direction of Rotterdam. If he does this﻿—if he travel south toward that city, do not molest him, only keep him in sight, and the moment he arrives at Rotterdam come and report to me at Ryswyk. But,” he added more emphatically, “if at any time it appears to you that he is turning back with the lady toward Haarlem come upon him at once with your men and seize him together with any companions he may have with him. You understand?”
“Perfectly, my lord. While he travels southwards with the lady, we are only to keep him in sight; when he and the lady arrive at Rotterdam we must report to you at Ryswyk, but the moment he turns back toward Haarlem we are to fall on him and seize him and his companions.”
“The lady you will treat with the utmost respect,” resumed Stoutenburg with an approving nod, “the rascal and his companions you may mishandle as much as you like, without, however, doing them mortal injury. But, having taken the whole party prisoner, you will forthwith convey them to the molens at Ryswyk, where you will find me. Now is all that clear?”
“Nothing could be clearer, my lord,” repeated Jan firmly. “We follow him while he travels south, but seize him with his company and the lady if he turn back toward Haarlem. Nothing could be easier.”
“You will not let him slip through your fingers, Jan?” said Stoutenburg earnestly.
Jan laughed and shrugged his shoulders.
“You said that this work would help to forward our cause,” he said simply. “I ask no questions. I believe you and obey.”
“That’s brave! And you will take great care of the lady, when she falls into your hands?”

===Chinese===

一阵古怪的哨音从他咬紧的牙缝里挤出来——那是许多野兽候食时发出的声音。随后他把扬又引得离那所房子远些，生怕自己的话随风飘到那边去。
“盯住他，扬，”他下令道，“直到他去佩瑟拉尔小巷哈尔斯先生（Mynheer）的家——他打算去那儿吃晚饭。到了那里，你可以放心丢开他一个钟头，径直去海姆斯凯克勋爵府上，你认得他的。向他讨六名外邦雇佣兵；就说是我立时要差用的。这几个人随后帮你盯住咱们那位无赖。他将在月出时分离开哈勒姆，你务必一刻也不丢他的踪迹。不久他就该护送一位小姐往鹿特丹方向去了。他若是这么走——若是携那位小姐南下往那城去，不要惊动他，只盯住，等他一到鹿特丹，你就到赖斯韦克来报我。不过，”他的语气更加着重，“只要你发觉他带着那位小姐掉头回哈勒姆，就立刻带上你的人扑上去，把他连同随行的一干人等统统拿下。明白吗？”
“完全明白，大人。他携小姐南行时，我们只须盯住不放；他与小姐一到鹿特丹，我们就得来赖斯韦克向您禀报；而他一掉头回哈勒姆，我们就得扑上去，把他和他的同伙一并擒住。”
“那位小姐，你们要以最恭谨的礼数相待，”斯陶滕堡赞许地点点头，接着说，“那流氓和他的同伙，随你们怎么折腾，只是不许伤他们性命。把这一干人全部拿下之后，你们立刻把他们押到赖斯韦克的磨坊去，我在那儿等你们。都清楚了？”
“再清楚不过了，大人。”扬语气坚定地复述道，“他南行，我们就跟着；他若携同小姐和同伴掉头回哈勒姆，我们就擒住他们。再容易不过了。”
“你可不会让他从你指缝里溜走吧，扬？”斯陶滕堡恳切地说。
扬笑了，耸耸肩。
“您说过，这桩差事于我们的大业有助推之功，”他简单地说，“我不多问。我信您，听命行事。”
“这才像话！那位小姐落到你们手里之后，你会好好照看她吧？”

===Original===

“I understand that she is my lord’s future lady,” rejoined Jan, with the same calm simplicity which makes the perfect soldier and the perfect servant, and which promised obedience without murmur and without question.
“Yes, Jan. The lady is my future wife,” said Stoutenburg. “Treat her as such. As for the man﻿ ﻿… I want him alive﻿ ﻿… do not kill him, Jan, even if he provoke you. And he will do that by his insolence, I know.”
“My lord shall have his enemy alive,” said Jan, “a helpless prisoner﻿ ﻿… but alive.”
“Then good luck to you, Jan,” concluded Stoutenburg with a sigh of satisfaction. “I am well pleased with you. In the near future I shall be happy to remember that the high offices of State and those around my person must be filled by those who have well deserved of them.”
He put out his thin, nervy hand and Jan fell on one knee in order to kiss it with fervour and respect. The son of John of Barneveld could still count on the loyalty of a few who believed in him, and who looked on his crimes as a necessary means to a glorious end.
A few moments later Beresteyn and Stoutenburg had disappeared in the darkness of the narrow street, and Jan remained alone at his post of observation.

===Chinese===

“我明白，她是大人未来的夫人。”扬答道，仍是那副沉静质朴的口吻——正是这口吻造就完美的军人和完美的仆从，许下不辞不怨、不问缘由的服从。
“是的，扬。这位小姐是我未来的妻子，”斯陶滕堡说，“要按这个身份对待她。至于那个人……我要他活着……不许弄死他，扬，哪怕他招惹你们。我知道，他那副张狂劲儿准会招惹你们。”
“大人定能得到活着的仇敌，”扬说，“一个动弹不得的囚徒……但活着。”
“那就祝你顺利，扬。”斯陶滕堡满意地舒了口气，作结道，“我很看好你。用不了多久，我会乐意记起：国家的高位和我身边的人，都该交给配得上它们的人。”
他伸出瘦而暴着筋的手，扬单膝跪下，热忱而恭敬地吻了它。约翰·巴内费尔德的儿子，依旧能指望几个人对他的忠心——这些人信他，把他的罪行看作通往辉煌目标的必要手段。
片刻之后，贝雷斯坦和斯陶滕堡便消失在窄街的黑暗里，只剩扬独自守在他的岗位上。

## XXVI Back to Houdekerk / 二十六 回豪德凯尔克
===Original===

And now back once more in the kingdom of the night and of the frost, of the darkness and of silence, back along the ice ways on a swift and uninterrupted flight.
The moon is less kind now, fitful and coy; she will not peep out from behind the banks of clouds save at rare intervals; and the clouds are heavy; great billows, clumsy in shape as if weighted with lead; the moon plays a restless game of hide and seek amongst them for the bewilderment of the skater, to whom last night she was so kind.
They come tumbling in more and more thickly from the south﻿—those clouds﻿—driven more furiously by the gusty wind. Brother northeaster has gone to rest, it is the turn of the south wind now﻿—not the soft south wind of summer, but a turbulent and arrogant fellow who bellows as loudly as he can, and who means to have a frolic in this world of ice and snow from which his colder brethren have exiled him until now.
Straight at the head of the skater, it expended the brunt of its fury, sending his hat flying in one direction and in wanton delight leading him into a mad chase after it; then when once more he was on his way﻿—hat in hand this time﻿—it tore with impish glee at his hair, impeded his movements, blew doublet and sash awry.
What a chase! what a fight! what a run! But Dondersteen! we do defy thee, O frolicsome south wind! aye, and the darkness too! Back to Houdekerk, the first stage on the road to fortune.
It is not nearly so cold now that brother northeaster has yielded to his madcap brother from the south! gusty and rough and a hand-to-hand fight for progress all the time, with tears running down the cheeks, and breath coming in gasps from the chest! It is not so cold, and the ice is less crisp, its smooth skin is furrowed and wrinkled, soft and woolly beneath the touch of the steel blades; but the snow still lies thickly upon the low-lying ground, and holds in its luminous embrace all the reflections which the capricious moon will lend it.
For the first half hour, while the moon was still very brilliant and the night air very still, it seemed to Diogenes as if the loneliness around him was only fictitious, as if somewhere﻿—far away mayhap﻿—men moved in the same way as he did, swiftly and silently over the surface of the ice. It seemed to him in fact that he was being followed.

===Chinese===

如今又回到了黑夜与冰霜的王国，黑暗与寂静的王国，回到冰路上，一路疾驰，不停不歇。
月亮此刻却不那么殷勤了，忽隐忽现，羞羞答答；她不肯从云堆后面探头，难得露上一面；云又重：一垛一垛的大浪，形状笨拙，像坠了铅；月亮在它们中间玩着一场不安分的藏猫猫，存心叫滑冰人发懵——昨夜她对他还那样好。
那些云从南边翻滚而来，越聚越密——被一阵阵狂风赶得越来越急。东北风兄弟歇班了，如今轮到南风——不是夏天那软和的南风，而是一个暴烈骄横的家伙，扯开嗓门吼，打定主意要在这冰雪世界里撒一回野——这地方，他那些更冷的兄弟们一直放逐他到今天。
它把满腔怒火劈头砸向滑冰人，把他的帽子刮向一边，又坏心眼地引着他疯追一气；等他重新上路——这回帽子攢在手里——它又促狭地撕扯他的头发，碍他的手脚，把紧身上衣和腰带吹得歪七扭八。
好一场追！好一场斗！好一趟跑！可是雷公在上！我们就藐视你，爱撒野的南风！对，还有这黑暗，一并藐视！回豪德凯尔克去——财运大道的第一程。
东北风兄弟让位给南边那位莽撞兄弟之后，天远没有先前冷了！一路狂风大作，寸寸都是白刃战，泪水顺着两颊淌，气一口口从胸腔里抽！天不那么冷了，冰也不那么脆了，它光滑的皮肤起了沟、起了皱，钢刃过处又软又茸；可低地上积雪仍厚，仍用发亮的怀抱接住那喜怒无常的月亮肯借给它的一切光。
头半个钟头，月亮还极亮，夜气还极静，第欧根尼只觉得四下的孤寂是装出来的，仿佛在什么地方——兴许很远——有人同他一样，在冰面上又快又无声地移动。他简直觉得，自己被人跟上了。

===Original===

He tried to make sure of this, straining his ears to listen, and now and then he caught very distinctly the sound of the metallic click of several pairs of skates. His senses, trained to over-acuteness through years of hard fighting and of campaigning, could not easily be deceived; and presently there was no doubt in his mind that Nicolaes Beresteyn or the Lord of Stoutenburg had set spies upon his track.
This knowledge caused him only to set his teeth, and to strike out more vigorously and more rapidly than before; those who followed him were fairly numerous﻿—over half a dozen he reckoned﻿—the only chance of evading them was, therefore, in flight. He took to noting the rolling banks of cloud with a more satisfied eye, and when, after the first hour or so, the light of the waning moon became more dim and even at times disappeared completely, he took the first opportunity that presented itself of making a detour over a backwater of the Meer, which he knew must bewilder his pursuers.
Whether the pursuit was continued after that, he could not say. His eyes trying to pierce the gloom could tell him nothing; but there were many intricate little byways just south of the Meer over backwaters and natural canals, which he knew well, and over these he started on an eccentric and puzzling career which was bound to baffle the spies on his track.
Whenever he spoke subsequently of the many adventures which befell him during the first days of this memorable New Year, he never was very explicit on the subject of this night’s run back to Houdekerk.
As soon as he had rid himself﻿—as he thought﻿—of his pursuers, he allowed his mind to become more and more absorbed in the great problem which confronted him since he had pledged his word to Mynheer Beresteyn to bring the jongejuffrouw safely back to him.
He now moved more mechanically over the iceways, taking no account of time or space or distance, only noting with the mere eye of instinct the various landmarks which loomed up from time to time out of the fast gathering darkness.
This coming darkness he welcomed, for he knew his way well, and it would prove his staunch ally against pursuit. For the rest he was conscious neither of cold, of hunger nor of fatigue. Pleasant thoughts helped to cheer his spirits and to give strength to his limbs. His brief visit to Haarlem had indeed been fruitful of experiences.

===Chinese===

他要弄个确实，侧耳细听，果真时时清晰地听见好几双冰鞋的金属脆响。他的感官，经多年苦战和行伍磨得超平常地锐利，轻易骗不过；不多时他心里已无疑问：尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦或是斯陶滕堡大人，在他身后安了眼线。
知道了这个，他只是咬紧牙关，蹬得比先前更猛更快；跟来的人着实不少——他估摸不下六七个——甩掉他们的唯一指望，就在脚底下。他看那翻滚的云堆，眼神满意多了；头一个钟头过去，下弦月的光越来越暗，有时索性没了，他便抓佳头一个机会，绕上梅尔湖一处死水湾——他知道这一绕，必叫追兵晕头转向。
此后人家还追不追，他说不准了。他的眼睛想穿透黑暗，什么也告诉不了他；但梅尔湖正南有许多曲折的小岔道，穿死水、越天然沟渠，他都熟；他踏上这些水路，东一拐西一绕，走出一串邪门的路线，管保把身后的探子绕个晕头转向。
后来每当他讲起这个难忘的新年头几天里的种种奇遇，对这夜奔回豪德凯尔克的一程，他从来不怎么细说。
他一自认为甩脱了追兵，心思便越来越沉进那桩大难题里去——自从他向贝雷斯坦先生（Mynheer）许下诺言，要把小姐平平安安带回到他身边，这难题就横在了他面前。
此刻他在冰路上滑得近乎机械，不计时间，不计空间，不计远近，只凭本能的眼角，留意着从渐浓的黑暗里不时浮现的一座座界标。
这渐浓的黑暗他倒欢迎，因为路他熟，黑暗做对抗追兵的盟友，最是可靠。其余的，冷也好，饿也好，乏也好，他一概不觉。愉快的念头给他的精神打气，给他的腿脚添力。哈勒姆那短短一遭，实在不虚此行。

===Original===

A problem confronted him which he had made up his mind to solve during his progress across the ice in the night. How to keep his word to Nicolaes Beresteyn, and yet bring the jongejuffrouw safely back to her father.
She would not, of course, willingly follow him, and his would once again be the uncongenial task of carrying her off by force if he was to succeed in his new venture.
A fortune if he brought her back! That sounded simple enough, and the thought of it caused the philosopher’s blood to tingle with delight.
A fortune if he brought her back! It would have to be done after he had handed her over into the care of Mynheer Ben Isaje at Rotterdam. He was pledged to do that, but once this was accomplished﻿—his word to Nicolaes Beresteyn would be redeemed.
A fortune if he brought her back! And when he had brought her back she would tell of his share in her abduction, and instead of the fortune mayhap the gallows would be meted out to him.
’Twas a puzzle, a hard nut for a philosopher to crack. It would be the work of an adventurer, of a man accustomed to take every risk on the mere chance of success.
But Gilda’s image never left him for one moment while his thoughts were busy with that difficult problem. For the first time now he realized the utter pathos of her helplessness. The proud little vixen, as he had dubbed her a while ago, was after all but a poor defenceless girl tossed hither and thither just to suit the ambitions of men. Did she really love that unscrupulous and cruel Stoutenburg, he wondered. Surely she must love him, for she did not look the kind of woman who would plight her troth against her will. She loved him and would marry him, her small white hand, which had the subtle fragrance of tulips, would be placed in one which was deeply stained with blood.
Poor young vixen, with the sharp tongue that knew how to hurt and the blue eyes that could probe a wound like steel! It was strange to think that their soft glances were reserved for a man whose heart was more filled with hate for men than with love for one woman.
“If I loved you, little vixen,” he once murmured apostrophizing the elusive vision which lightened the darkness around him, “if I loved you, I would break my word to that dastard who is your brother﻿ ﻿… I would not take you to Rotterdam to further his ambition, but I would carry you off to please myself.

===Chinese===

一桩难题摆在他面前，他打定主意趁夜里的冰路把它解开：怎样既不背对尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦的诺言，又把小姐平平安安送回她父亲身边。
她当然不会乖乖跟他走；这桩新买卖要做成，那不讨好的差事——武力劫人——又得落在他头上。
把她带回去，就是一笔家财！这话听着够简单，这念头一想起，哲学家浑身的血都欢快地发麻。
把她带回去，就是一笔家财！可这得等他先把她交到鹿特丹本·伊萨耶先生手里之后才成。这事他有诺在先，可一旦办完——欠尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦的话也就还清了。
把她带回去，就是一笔家财！可等他真把她带回去，她会说出他在劫持一案里扮演的角色，到时候给他的，兴许不是家财，而是绞架。
这是一道难题，哲学家啻起来也硌牙的硬果子。这活儿，得冒险家来干，得一个惯于单凭一线成功之望就把险全押上的人来干。
可他苦思这道难题的当口，吉尔达的身影一刻也不曾离开他。此刻他头一回意识到，她那份无依无靠，是何等的可怜。他不久前还唤作“骄傲的小狐狸”的她，终究不过是个赤手空拳的可怜姑娘，被人颠来倒去，只为凑合男人们的野心。她当真爱那个不择手段、心狠手辣的斯陶滕堡吗？他想。想来必是爱的，因为她不像那种肯违背本心许身的女子。她爱他，要嫁他；她那只小小的白手，那只带着郁金香幽香的手，将要放进一只浸透鲜血的手里。
可怜的小狐狸，那条会伤人的利舌，那双能像钢针一样探进伤口的蓝眼睛！想想也怪：那一汪汪温柔的目光，竟留给了一个心里装的对世人的恨、多于对一个女人爱的男人。
“我若爱你，小狐狸，”有一刻他哺哺地呼唤着那个照亮四周黑暗、又倏忽难捉的影子，“我若爱你，我就背了对那条毒棍——你哥哥——的诺言……我不带你去鹿特丹成全他的野心，我要把你劫走，成全我自己。”

===Original===

I would take you to some distant land, mayhap to my unknown father’s home in England, where the sounds of strife and hatred amongst men would only come as a faint and intangible echo. I would take you to where roses bloom in profusion, and where in the spring the petals of apple-blossoms would cover you like a mantle of fragrant snow. There I would teach that sharp tongue of yours to murmur words of tenderness and those perfect blue eyes to close in the ecstasy of a kiss. But,” he added with his habitual lighthearted laugh, “I do not love you, little vixen, for heigh-ho! if I did ’twere hard for my peace of mind.”
---
When Diogenes neared the town of Leyden he heard its church clocks ring out the hour of three. Close by the city walls he took off his skates, preferring to walk the short league which lay between him and Houdekerk.
He was more tired than he cared to own even to himself, and the last tramp along the road was inexpressibly wearisome. But he had seen or heard nothing more of his pursuers; he was quite convinced that they had lost track of him some hours ago. The south wind blew in heavy gusts from over the marshlands far away, and the half-melted snow clung sticky and dank against the soles and heels of his boots. A smell of dampness in the air proclaimed the coming triumph of the thaw. The roads, thought Diogenes, would be heavy on the morrow, impassable mayhap to a sledge, and the jongejuffrouw would have to travel in great discomfort in a jolting vehicle.
At last in the near distance a number of tiny lights proclaimed the presence of a group of windmills. It was in one of these that Pythagoras and Socrates had been ordered to ask for shelter﻿—in the fifth one down the road, which stood somewhat isolated from the others; even now its long, weird arms showed like heavy lines of ink upon the black background of the sky.
Diogenes almost fell up against the door; he could hardly stand. But the miller was on the lookout for him, having slept only with half an eye, waiting for the stranger whose emissaries had already paid him well. He carried a lantern and a bunch of keys; his thin, sharp head was surmounted with a cotton nightcap and his feet were encased in thick woollen hose.

===Chinese===

“我要带你去一个遥远的地方，兴许去英格兰我那素未谋面的父亲的家，在那里，人间的争斗与仇恨，不过是一声微弱缥缈的回音。我要带你去玫瑰开得泼泼洒洒的地方，春天里，苹果花的瓣子会像一件芬芳的雪氅，盖你满身。在那儿，我要教你那条利舌呢哺温存话，教你那双完美的蓝眼睛，在一个吻的迷醉里合上。可是——”他照例轻快地一笑，又添上一句，“我不爱你，小狐狸，因为，嗐！我若爱你，我这颗心怕要永无宁日了。”
---
第欧根尼挨近莱顿城时，听见城里教堂的钟敲了三点。就在城墙近旁他卸下冰鞋，宁肯步行走完到豪德凯尔克的那一小段里格路。
他累得很，累得连对自己都不肯认；这最后一段跋涉，乏得没法形容。好在追兵的影子声响再没出现；他深信几个钟头前他们就摸丢了他的踪迹。南风一阵紧似一阵，从远处的沼泽地刮来；半融的雪又黏又湿，粘在靴底靴跟上。空气里一股潮味，宣告解冻的胜利即将到来。明天，第欧根尼想，路上要泥泞了，雪橇兴许都过不去，小姐只好坐着颠簸的车，一路受大罪。
终于，不远处几星小小的灯火，报出了一群风车的所在。毕达哥拉斯和苏格拉底奉命去借宿的，正是其中一座——沿大路数过去的第五座，同其余几座离得稍远；即便此刻，它那长长的、鬼影似的风翼，仍像几道浓墨的线条，涂在漆黑的天幕上。
第欧根尼几乎是跌撞到门上的；他快站不住了。磨坊主却正候着他，只合了半只眼睡，专等这位来客——来客的先遣早已把银子付足了。他提着灯笼，攢着一大串钥匙；尖瘦的头顶扣一顶布睡帽，脚上裹着厚厚的毛袜。

===Original===

It took him some time to undo the many heavy bolts which protected the molens against the unwelcome visits of night marauders, and before he pushed back the final one, he peered through a tiny judas in the door and in a querulous voice asked the belated traveller’s name.
“Never mind my name,” quoth Diogenes impatiently, “and open thy door, miller, ere I break it in. I am as tired as a nag, as thirsty as a dog and as hungry as a cat. The jongejuffrouw is I trust safe: I am her majordomo and faithful servant, so open quickly, or thy shoulder will have to smart for the delay.”
I have Diogenes’ own assurance that the miller was thereupon both obedient and prompt. He﻿—like all his compeers in the neighborhood﻿—found but scanty living in the grinding of corn for the neighbouring peasantry, there was too much competition nowadays and work had not multiplied in proportion. Optimists said that in a few years time the paralysing effects of the constant struggle against Spain would begin to wear off, that the tilling of the soil would once more become a profitable occupation and that the molens which now stood idle through many days in the year would once more become a vast storehouse of revenue for those who had continued to work them.
But in the meanwhile the millers and their families were oft on the verge of starvation, and some of them eked out a precarious livelihood by taking in wayfarers who were on their way to and from the cities and had sundry reasons﻿—into which it was best not to inquire﻿—for preferring to sleep and eat at one of these out-of-the-way places rather than in one of the city hostelries.
Diogenes had made previous acquaintance with his present landlord; he knew him to be a man of discretion and of boundless cupidity, two very useful qualities when there is a secret to be kept and plenty of money wherewith to guard it.
Therefore did Diogenes order his companions to convey the jongejuffrouw to the molens of Mynheer Patz, and there to keep guard over her until his own return.
Patz looked well after his belated guest’s material comfort. There was some bread and cheese and a large mug of ale waiting for him in the wheelhouse and a clean straw paillasse in a corner. The place smelt sweetly of freshly ground corn, of flour and of dry barley and maize, and a thin white coating of flour﻿—soft to the touch as velvet﻿—lay over everything.

===Chinese===

磨坊上着一道道粗门闩，防的是夜里不速的劫匪，他费了好些工夫才一一退开；推最后一道之前，还凑在门上小小的窥孔前，用抱怨的腔调问深夜来客的姓名。
“少问我的名字，”第欧根尼不耐烦道，“快开门，磨坊主，不然我可要撞门了。我累得像头驽马，渴得像条狗，饿得像只猫。小姐托天之福安然无恙：我是她的大管事和忠仆，快开，再磨蹭，你的肩膀就要替这耽搁吃痛了。”
磨坊主随即又听话又利落，这话我有第欧根尼本人的担保。他同左近所有同行一样，靠替四乡农户磨面糊口，日子紧巴巴：如今竞争太烈，活计却没跟着多。乐观的人说，再过几年，常年同西班牙抗衡的麻痹效应就会消退，种地会重新成为赚钱的营生，那些一年里闲着大半年的风车磨坊，也会重新变成一座大财库，报答那些一直守着它们干的人。
可眼下，磨坊主们和他们的家小常常在挨饿的边上，有些人便做起了收留行客的营生，勉强贴补——那些人来往于城市之间，各有种种缘故——缘故还是别打听的好——宁肯在这些偏僻地方吃住，也不进城里的客店。
第欧根尼同这位现下的房东早打过交道；他知道这人嘴严，又贪得无厌——要藏一桩秘密，又有的是银子来看住它，这两样品性再有用不过。
所以第欧根尼才命令伙伴们把小姐送到帕茨先生的磨坊，在那里看守着她，直到他回来。
帕茨把深夜来客的吃住照应得妥妥帖帖。轮机房里给他备着面包、干酪和一大杯麦酒，角落里有一床干净的草垫。屋里一股新磨的麦香、面粉香、干大麦和玉米香，甜丝丝的；薄薄一层面粉，摸上去绒似的软，罩在样样东西上。

===Original===

Diogenes ate and drank and asked news of the jongejuffrouw. She was well but seemed over sad, the miller explained; but his wife had prepared a comfortable bed for her in the room next to the tiny kitchen. It was quite warm there and Mevrouw Patz had spread her one pair of linen sheets over the bed. The jongejuffrouw’s serving woman was asleep on the kitchen floor; she declared herself greatly ill-used, and had gone to sleep vowing that she was so uncomfortable she would never be able to close an eye.
As for the two varlets who had accompanied the noble lady, they were stretched out on a freshly made bed of straw in the weighing-room.
Patz and his wife seemed to have felt great sympathy for the jongejuffrouw, and Diogenes had reason to congratulate himself that she was moneyless, else she would have found it easy enough to bribe the over-willing pair into helping her to regain her home.
He dreamt of her all night; her voice rang in his ear right through the soughing of the wind which beat against the ill-fitting windows of the wheelhouse. Alternately in his dream she reviled him, pleaded with him, heaped insults upon him, but he was securely bound and gagged and could not reply to her insults or repulse her pleadings. He made frantic efforts to tear the gag from his mouth, for he wished to tell her that he had not lost his heart to her and cared nothing for the misery which she felt.

===Chinese===

第欧根尼吃着喝着，问起小姐的情形。她一切都好，只是愁得慌，磨坊主说；他妻子已在小厨房隔壁的屋里给她铺了张舒服的床。那里相当暖和，帕茨夫人（Mevrouw）把她唯一的一副亚麻床单铺在了床上。小姐的贴身女仆睡在厨房地板上；她口口声声说自己受了天大的委屈，一面赌咒说她这样子难受得一辈子合不上眼，一面倒睡着了。
至于陪同贵小姐来的那两个恶仆，此刻正四仰八叉躺在过磅房里新铺的草铺上。
帕茨夫妇看去对小姐极表同情；她也身无分文，第欧根尼为此暗自庆幸——不然，收买这对巴不得效劳的夫妻帮她回家，对她实在易如反掌。
他整夜梦见她；她的声音穿过扑打轮机房歪窗的风声，一路响在他耳边。梦里她时而咒他，时而求他，时而把羞辱劈头盖脸砸给他；可他被捆得结结实实，嘴也堵着，骂不能还口，求不能推拒。他发疯般要把堵嘴的布扯下来，因为他想告诉她：他并没有把心丢在她身上，她那份苦，他半点也不放在心上。

## XXVII Thence to Rotterdam / 二十七 再往鹿特丹
===Original===

He only caught sight of the jongejuffrouw later on in the morning when she came out of the molens and stepped into the sledge which stood waiting for her at the door.
The thaw had not been sufficiently heavy, nor had it lasted a sufficient number of hours to make a deep impression on the thick covering of snow which still lay over the roads. The best and quickest mode of travelling﻿—at any rate for the next few hours﻿—would still be by sledge, the intervening half-dozen leagues that lay between Houdekerk and Rotterdam could be easily covered in the day provided an early start was made and no long halts allowed for meals.
Diogenes had made arrangements for the start to be made by seven o’clock. A dull light of pale rosy grey hung over the snow-covered landscape, and far away on the horizon line that same rose-grey light was just assuming a more brilliant hue. He sent Mevrouw Patz up to the jongejuffrouw to acquaint her with the plans for the day, and to beg her to give these her approval.
Mevrouw Patz returned with the message that the jongejuffrouw was ready to start at any hour which Mynheer would command and was otherwise prepared to obey him in all things.
So Diogenes, standing well out of sight, watched Gilda as she came out of the door of the molens and remained for one moment quite still, waiting for the sledge to draw up. She looked fragile this morning, he thought, and her face looked tiny and very pale within the soft frame of the fur hood which covered her head. For a second or two it seemed to him as if she was looking round somewhat anxiously, with a frown upon her smooth forehead﻿—puzzled and almost frightened﻿—as if she expected and at the same time feared to see someone or something.
The next second the cloud appeared to lift from her face and Diogenes even thought﻿—but in this he may have been mistaken﻿—that a sigh of relief escaped her lips.
After that she stepped into the sledge, closely followed by Maria.
Pythagoras and Socrates had been well drilled in their duties toward the jongejuffrouw and Diogenes noted with satisfaction that his brother philosophers did their best to make the lady as comfortable as possible with a pillow or two bought at Leyden the day previously and the warm rugs from Haarlem which they wrapped carefully round her feet.

===Chinese===

直到上午过半，他才见到小姐——她走出磨坊，登上门前候着的雪橇。
解冻得不够透，时辰也不够长，还动不了路面上那层厚雪的筋骨。最妥最快的走法——至少未来几个钟头——仍是雪橇：豪德凯尔克到鹿特丹之间这六里格，只要赶早动身、吃饭不多耽搁，一天走完绰绰有余。
第欧根尼安排好七点动身。一片淡淡的玫瑰灰的幽光，罩着雪封的原野；远处地平线上，那同一片玫瑰灰正透出愈来愈亮的颜色。他派帕茨夫人（Mevrouw）上楼去见小姐，禀明今日的行程，请她俯允。
帕茨夫人带回话来：但凭先生（Mynheer）吩咐几点动身，小姐几点都依得；此外万事也都备着听命于他。
于是第欧根尼躲得远远地，望着吉尔达走出磨坊的门，静立了一刻，等雪橇靠上来。今天早晨她显得单薄，他想，毛皮风帽衬着她那张脸，脸显得小小的，十分苍白。有一两秒钟，他觉得她像在不安地四下张望，光洁的额头蹙着——又疑惑又近乎惊惶——仿佛既盼着、又怕看见某个人、某样东西。
下一瞬，那片阴云仿佛从她脸上散了，第欧根尼甚至觉得——这一处他也许看岔了——她唇边逸出一声宽慰的叹息。
随后她登进雪橇，玛丽亚紧跟着上了。
对侍候小姐的差事，毕达哥拉斯和苏格拉底早被调教得烂熟；第欧根尼满意地看着两位哲学家同仁尽力让夫人坐得舒服：头天在莱顿买的几只靠枕，从哈勒姆带来的暖毯，仔仔细细裹在她脚上。

===Original===

Maria, dignified and unbending, did her best to prevent those rascals from doing their duty in this manner, but soon her own wants got the better of her pride, and shivering with cold she was glad enough to allow Pythagoras to roll a thick horse-cloth about her knees.
A few moments later a start was made to the accompaniment of lusty cheering from the miller and his wife, both of whom were pleasant﻿—even obsequious to the last.
The stolid peasant who held the reins urged his horses on to a brisk trot as soon as they had reached the flat open road. The three philosophers rode at some little distance behind the sledge, ready only to push forward if some marauder or footpad showed signs of molesting the sledge.
Diogenes caught only a few brief glimpses of the jongejuffrouw during the day; once at Zegwaard where there was a halt for dinner, then at Zevenhuisen and Hillegersberg where horses and men were ready for a rest. But she never seemed to see him, passing quickly in and out of the small huts or cottages to which Pythagoras or Socrates escorted her from a respectful distance. She never spoke to either of these worthies on those occasions, nor did she question any orders for halting or restarting.
To those who attended on her, however, at the halting places, to the cottagers or millers who brought her milk and bread to eat she was graciousness itself, and whenever it was time to go, Diogenes before leaving had invariably to listen to the loud praises of the beautiful jongejuffrouw with the sweet, sad face.
As to his own existence, she seemed hardly aware of it; at Zevenhuisen, when she went back to the sledge, Diogenes was not very far from where she passed. Moreover he was quite sure that she had seen him, for her head was turned straight in the direction where he stood, hat in hand, waiting to see her comfortably settled in the sledge, before remounting. It was in the early part of the afternoon and once more bitterly cold﻿—no doubt she felt the return of the frost, for she seemed to give a little shiver and pulled the hood more closely over her face.
The roads had been very heavy earlier in the day with their carpet of partially melted snow, but now this surface had frozen once more and the track was slippery like glass under the sledge, but terribly trying for the horses.

===Chinese===

玛丽亚端着架子，硬邦邦地，起先拼命不让那两个泼皮这般尽忠，不多时，自身的需要压倒了她的骄傲，她冷得发抖，乐得由毕达哥拉斯拿一条厚马毯裹住她的膝盖。
片刻后队伍启程，磨坊主夫妇高声欢呼相送——两位始终和气，到末了几乎谄媚。
一到平坦的大道，那个木讷的赶车农夫便策马小跑起来。三位哲学家骑马缀在雪橇后面不远，只在有什么劫道的、剪径的意图不轨时，才准备抢上前去。
一天里第欧根尼只匆匆望见小姐几眼：一回在泽赫瓦尔德停车用饭，一回在泽芬赫伊森和希勒赫斯贝赫，人马都该歇脚了。可她似乎从没看见他：她匆匆出入那些小屋小舍，毕达哥拉斯或苏格拉底恭恭敬敬隔开一段，送她进去。那种时候她从不同那两位好汉说话，行止歇走，也从无异言。
可对歇脚处照应她的人，对送牛奶送面包的村户或磨坊主，她和悦到了十分；每逢上路之前，第欧根尼总免不了要听人高声夸赞那位面容甜美而含愁的美丽小姐。
至于他自己这个人，她仿佛全然不知有其存在；在泽芬赫伊森，她走回雪橇时，第欧根尼离她经过的地方不远。而且他确信她看见了他，因为她的头正正朝向他立着的地方——他手里拿着帽子，候着她舒舒服服坐定，再上马。
那时刚过晌午，天又冷得刺骨——她无疑是觉出了回冻，只见她轻轻一颤，把风帽往脸上拉得更紧了。
清晨的路面上覆着半融的雪，泥泞难行；此刻这层表面又冻了回去，雪橇底下的道滑得像玻璃，马却苦不堪言。

===Original===

Progress was necessarily slow and wearisome both to man and beast, and the shades of evening were beginning to gather in very fast when at last the wooden spire of Rotterdam’s Groote Kerk emerged out of the frozen mist.
Diogenes﻿—as he had done before at Leyden and at Zegwaard﻿—pushed on ahead now; he wanted to reach the house of Ben Isaje in advance of the jongejuffrouw and prepare the Hebraic gentleman against her coming. The little town with its intricate network of narrow streets intersected by canals did not seem imposing to the eye. Diogenes marvelled with what thoughts the jongejuffrouw would survey it﻿—wondering no doubt if it would prove the end of her journey or merely a halt on the way to some other place more distant still from her home.
Ben Isaje appeared to be a person of some consequence in Rotterdam, for the moment he questioned a passerby as to where the Jewish Mynheer resided, there were plenty of willing tongues ready to give him information.
Having followed accurately the instructions which were given to him, Diogenes found himself presently at the top of a street which was so narrow that he reckoned if he stretched out his legs, his feet would be knocking against opposite walls. Anyhow, it looked almost impassable for a rider. He peered down it somewhat dubiously. It was very badly lighted; two feeble lamps alone glimmered at either end of it, and not a soul was in sight.
Close to where his horse was standing at the corner of that same street the word “*Tapperij*” writ in bold letters and well lit by a lamp placed conveniently above it, invited the tired wayfarer to enter. This philosopher was not the man to refuse so insinuating an invitation. He dismounted and leaving his horse in charge of an ostler, he entered the taproom of the tiny hostel and, being both tired and thirsty, he refreshed himself with a draught of good Rhyn wine.
After which he collected more information about the house of Mynheer Ben Isaje. It was situate about midway down that narrow street round the corner, and was easily distinguishable through its crooked and woebegone appearance, and the closely shuttered projecting window on the ground floor.
A very few minutes later Diogenes had identified the house from the several descriptions which had been given him.

===Chinese===

人困马乏，行程自然又慢又熬；鹿特丹大教堂的木塔尖终于从冻雾里浮现出来时，暮色已经收拢得很快了。
第欧根尼——同先前在莱顿和泽赫瓦尔德一样——这会儿拍马先行；他要赶在小姐前头到本·伊萨耶的宅子，先给这位希伯来先生通个气，教他有所准备。这座小城，窄街密如织网，沟渠纵横其间，看去并不起眼。第欧根尼揣想着小姐会用怎样的心思打量它——多半在猜：这里是旅程的尽头呢，还是不过是去往另一处、离她家更远的地方途中的一站。
本·伊萨耶在鹿特丹看去颇有几分体面：他刚向一个路人打听那位犹太先生住在哪儿，立时便有许许多多热心的舌头争相指点。
按着指点一路找去，第欧根尼很快来到一条街的街口；那街窄得他估摸着，若把两条腿一伸，两只脚就会磕到两边的墙。总之，骑马的人几乎没法通行。他半信半疑地朝里张望。街上灯昏暗极了，只有两盏微弱的灯在街两头幽幽地亮，一个人影也没有。
就在他的马立足的那条街角，“*Tapperij*（酒馆）”几个遒劲的大字，被上方一盏灯照得亮堂堂的，正招引疲倦的行客进去。我们这位哲学家可不是推却这般殷勤相邀的人。他下了马，把马托给马夫，走进这家小客店的酒室，又累又渴，灌下一大杯上好的莱茵酒，精神为之一振。
随后他又打听到本·伊萨耶先生宅子的详情。它就在拐角那条窄街的中段，一望便知：一副歪歪倒倒、愁眉苦脸的模样，底层那扇凸出的窗子百叶闭得严严实实。
没过几分钟，第欧根尼已凭着几番描述，认准了那座房子。

===Original===

Ben Isaje’s abode proved to be a tiny shop with a tall pointed gable sitting above it like a sugar-loaf hat. Its low casement window was securely barred with stout wooden shutters, held in place by thick iron bars. The upper part of the house looked to be at perpetual enmity with the lower, for it did not sit straight, or even securely above the humble ground floor below. The upper floor moreover projected a good three feet over the front door and the shop window, whilst the single gable sat askew over the lot.
From the house itself﻿—as Diogenes stood somewhat doubtfully before it﻿—there came the pungent odour of fried onions, and from the one next door an equally insistent one of damp leather. The philosopher thought that it was high time to swear, and this he did lustily, anathematizing in one comprehensive oath every dirty Hebrew and every insalubrious Dutch city that he had ever come across.
After which he examined the abode of Mynheer Ben Isaje more closely. In the pointed gable, just under the roof, a tiny window with a light behind it seemed to be blinking out of the darkness like the single eye of some inebriate loafer. Seeing that the small casement was partially open and concluding that someone at any rate must be making use of that light up there, Diogenes at last made up his mind to knock at the door; and as there was no knocker and he never carried a riding whip he gave the substantial oak panel a vigorous kick with his boot.
Whereupon the light up above immediately went out, just as if the one-eyed inebriate had dropped off to sleep.
This sudden extinguishing of the light, however, only served to prove to Diogenes that someone was up and astir inside the house, so without more ado he proceeded to pound more forcibly against the door with his foot, to shout at the top of his voice, and generally to make a rousing noise﻿—an art of which he was past master.
Soon he heard a soft grating behind the judas, and he felt﻿—more than he saw﻿—that a pair of eyes were peering at him from within.
“Open, Mynheer Ben Isaje,” he cried loudly and peremptorily, “ere I rouse this entire evil-smelling neighbourhood with my calls. Open I tell you ere I break in your door first and your nose﻿—which I suspect to be over long and over ruddy﻿—afterwards.”

===Chinese===

本·伊萨耶的居处原来是家小小铺面，头顶上压着一座高尖的人字山墙，活像一顶糖面包帽子。低矮的平窗用厚实的木百叶闩死，外面还加着粗铁条。房子的上半截同下半截像结着永世的仇：它端不正，也坐不牢，就那么歪在寒酸的底层上头。二楼还朝大门和铺窗探出足足三尺，独一面的山墙，更是整个儿坐偏了。
第欧根尼将信将疑地站在房前，房里飘出一股煎洋葱的冲味，隔壁又送来一股同样执拗的湿皮子味。哲学家觉得此时不骂更待何时，便痛痛快快骂了一场，一句总咒，把他此生遇见过的每一个肮脏的希伯来人、每一座不卫生的荷兰城，一网打尽。
骂完，他把本·伊萨耶先生的居处又细看了一遍。尖山墙的屋顶底下，有一扇小窗，窗后一点灯光，正从黑暗里眨眼，像醉汉闲汉独一只的眼。见那扇小窗半开着，料想上面至少有人在用那点光，第欧根尼终于打定主意叫门；门上没有门环，他又从不带马鞭，便抬起靴子，朝那厚实的橡木门板结结实实踹了一脚。
楼上的灯应声立灭，活像那只独眼醉汉倒头睡去了。
不过这灯说灭就灭，反倒向第欧根尼证明：屋里有人醒着、动着。于是他不再客气，用脚把门擂得更响，放开嗓门高喊，总之弄出一场惊天动地的动静——这门手艺，他是炉火纯青的大师。
很快他听见窥孔后一声轻轻的刮响，他感到——比看见还真切——有一双眼睛正从里面打量他。
“开门，本·伊萨耶先生，”他厉声断喝，“再不开，我就要叫喊得把这一带臭街烂巷全吵起来。我说，快开门，不然我先砸你的门，再砸你的鼻子——我疑心那鼻子未免太长、太红。”

===Original===

“ ’Tis too late to transact business now,” came in a feeble high-pitched voice from behind the narrow judas, “too late and too dark. The shop is closed.”
“ ’Tis not with your shop that I have to do, master,” quoth Diogenes impatiently, “but with yourself, if indeed you are Mynheer Ben Isaje, as I gravely suspect that you are.”
“What do you want with Ben Isaje?” queried the timorous voice, “he hath gone home for the night. His house is situate﻿ ﻿…”
“His house shall be *verdommt* if you parley any longer behind that grating, man; aye and this shop too, for if you do not open that door immediately I will break the windows, for my business brooks no delay, and I must needs get into this house as best I can.”
But despite his threat, no attempt was made to draw the bolts from within, whereupon Diogenes, whose stock of patience was never inexhaustible, and who moreover wished to give value to his threats, took a step backwards and then with a sudden spring threw his whole weight against the oak door; a proceeding which caused the tumbledown house to shake upon its foundations.
The next moment the timorous voice was once more raised behind the judas:
“Kindly have patience, gentle sir. I was even now about to open.”
Diogenes heard the drawing of more than one heavy bolt, then the grinding of a key in the lock; after which the door was partially opened, and a thin face with hooked nose and sunken cheeks appeared in the aperture.
To imagine that any man could hold a door against Diogenes when he desired to pass through it was to be totally unacquainted with that philosopher. He certainly would have smashed in the door of Ben Isaje’s abode with his powerful shoulders had it been kept persistently closed against him; but as it was, he only gave it a push with his knee, flinging it wide open thereby, and then stepped coolly into the narrow ill-lighted passage.
There was a blank wall each side of him, and a door lower down on the left; straight ahead a narrow ladder-like staircase was half lost in the gloom.
The anxious janitor had hastily retreated down the dark passage at sight of the towering figure which now confronted him, and in his fright he must have dropped the lantern which apparently he had been carrying. There it lay on the floor, fortunately still alight, so Diogenes picked it up and holding it high above his head he took a closer survey of the man.

===Chinese===

“这么晚了，不做生意啦。”窄窥孔后传来一个细而尖的声音，“太晚，也太黑。铺子打烊了。”
“我不是来同你的铺子打交道，老板，”第欧根尼不耐烦道，“是同你本人——你若当真是本·伊萨耶先生；我严重疑心你正是。”
“找本·伊萨耶做什么？”那怯生生的声音问，“他回家过夜去了。他的宅子坐落……”
“他的宅子？你在这栅栏后再嚼半句舌头，它就见鬼（verdommt）去，伙计；这铺子也一样。再不立时开门，我就砸窗了——我的事刻不容缓，这宅子我无论如何是要进的。”
可任他威吓，里面毫无拔闩的动静。于是第欧根尼——他的耐心存货从来有限，何况也想叫威吓兑现——退后一步，随即猛地一纵，把全身的分量撞在橡木门上；这一下，震得这座要塌的房子在地基上直晃。
下一刻，那怯生生的声音又在窥孔后响起：
“请稍安勿躁，好心的老爷。我这就开门。”
第欧根尼听见不止一道重闩被拉开，然后是钥匙在锁眼里滞涩地转动；门随后开了一道缝，缝里露出一张瘦脸：钩鼻子，凹脸颊。
以为有人能拦住门、不让第欧根尼过——这么想的人，全然不识得这位哲学家。那扇门若死死闭着，他定能用那副铁肩把本·伊萨耶的宅门撞个稀烂；眼下门既开了缝，他只用膝盖一顶，把它撞得大开，随即若无其事地跨进那条又窄又暗的过道。
他两边都是白墙，左手靠里有一扇门；正前方一架梯子似的窄楼梯，半截没在昏暗里。
那位惶惶的门房一见眼前这尊高塔般的人影，早慌忙退进黑过道深处，慌乱中想是把手里的灯笼掉了。灯笼就躺在地上，幸好还亮着；第欧根尼拾起来，高举过头顶，把这人细细打量了一番。

===Original===

“You are Ben Isaje,” he said calmly, as he held the light close to the man’s face and then let it travel over his spare and shrinking form; “your dress and nose do proclaim your race. Then pray tell me what was the use of making such a to-do, seeing that I had business with you and therefore meant to come in.﻿ ﻿… Now take this lantern and lock your front door again, after which you had best conduct me to a room where I can talk privately with you.”
No doubt there was something in the stranger’s face and attitude which reassured the Jew, for after a few more seconds of anxious hesitancy, he did take the lantern from Diogenes’ hand and then shuffled back to the street door which he once more carefully barred and bolted.
After which with the aid of one of the many large keys which hung by a steel chain in a bunch from his waist, he unlocked the door in the passage and standing a little to one side he bade his belated guest walk in.

===Chinese===

“你就是本·伊萨耶。”他平静地说，一面把灯凑近那人的脸，又让灯光扫过他瘦削瑟缩的身子；“你的衣着和鼻子，早就把你的族类公告于众了。那么请说说，又吵又闹的何苦来？须知我有事找你，横竖是要进来的。……现在，提上这盏灯，把你临街的门再锁上，然后最好领我去一间屋，我好同你密谈。”
无疑，来客的脸色和做派里有些什么，叫这犹太人放了心：又惶惶地迟疑了几秒，他终于从第欧根尼手里接过灯笼，蹭回临街的门边，又仔细上了闩、落了锁。
随后他从腰间一串钢链拴着的一大把钥匙里拣出一把，打开过道里那扇门，侧身让在一边，请这位深夜来客进去。

## XXVIII Check / 二十八 受挫
===Original===

The room into which Diogenes now stepped looked at first sight to be almost devoid of furniture: it was only when the Jew had entered and placed the lantern down upon a wooden table at one end of the room that the philosopher realized where he was.
The dark low walls showed themselves lined with solid oak chests and presses, each with massive hinges and locks, rusty and covered with dust, but firm enough to withstand for many an hour the depredations of thieves. Ben Isaje was obviously a jeweller by trade and this was the shop where he kept his precious goods: no wonder then that he looked with obvious fear on his belated visitor with the powerful shoulders and vigorous limbs, seeing that to all appearances he was at the moment alone in the house.
Like all jewellers settled in the Dutch cities at this time Ben Isaje carried on a number of other trades﻿—some of which were perhaps not altogether avowable. He acted as banker and moneylender, and general go-between in financial transactions, some of which had political aims. Discretion was of necessity his chief stock-in-trade, and his small cargo of scruples he had thrown overboard long ago.
He was as ready now to finance a conspiracy against the Stadtholder as against the Archduchess or Don John, provided he saw huge monetary profits in the deal, and received bribes with a calm conscience both from Maurice of Nassau and the Lord of Stoutenburg. But once he was liberally paid he would hold to his bond: it was only by keeping the good graces of all political parties that he remained free from molestation.
Diogenes had known exactly what to expect when Nicolaes Beresteyn gave him the letter and bond to present to Ben Isaje; he was, therefore, not surprised in the least when he saw before him the true type of financial agent whom already he had met more than once in his life before.
Ben Isaje, who was the depositary of vast sums of money placed in his house by clients of substance and of note, wore a long, greasy kaftan of black cloth, which was worn threadbare at the elbows and the knees, and the shop wherein he transacted business both for governments and private individuals which oft times involved several million guilders, had only a few very rickety chairs, one or two tables blackened with dirt and age, and a piece of tattered carpet in one corner as sole expressions of comfort.

===Chinese===

第欧根尼一脚跨进的这间屋，乍看几乎家徒四壁：直到那犹太人跟进来，把灯笼搁在屋尾一张木桌上，哲学家才明白自己到了什么地方。
幽暗的低墙边，排着一口口结实的橡木箱柜，铰链锁具都极粗重，锈着，蒙着尘，却结实得够贼人们啻上半天。本·伊萨耶显然是做珠宝生意的，这里正是他存放贵重货物的铺面：难怪他望着这位肩膀宽阔、四肢健壮的深夜来客，眼里的惧意明明白白——瞧这光景，他此刻是独自一人在家。
同当时定居荷兰各城的珠宝商一样，本·伊萨耶还兼着好几门别的生意——其中有些恐怕上不得台面。他做银行家、放债人，在银钱交易里充当总掮客，有的交易还带着政治图谋。嘴严，自然是他立命的本钱；至于良心，那一小舱存货，他早就抛下海去了。
如今，资助一场反对执政的阴谋，同资助一场反对女大公或唐·约翰的阴谋，于他一样现成——只要这买卖里看得出大笔的利钱；拿骚的莫里斯和斯陶滕堡大人送来的贿赂，他都受得心安理得。可一旦收足了钱，他就守约不渝：只有讨得各党各派的好感，他才能保得自身无扰。
当初尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦把信和契据交给他去见本·伊萨耶时，他早就料定会遇到什么；所以此刻见到眼前这位地地道道的银钱代理人——这种人他一生中打过的交道不止一回——他一点也不意外。
本·伊萨耶，这位代豪门贵客保管着大宗银钱的守财人，身上一件黑布长袍，油光光，肘弯膝盖都磨得露了线；而这间替官家私人办过不知多少桩动辄几百万盾生意的铺子，全部的体面只是几把摇摇晃晃的破椅子、一两张脏旧发黑的桌子，和角落里一块烂地毯。

===Original===

But all these facts were of course none of Diogenes’ business. At his host’s invitation he had sat down on one of the rickety chairs and then proceeded to extract some papers from out the inner lining of his doublet.
“It would save time,” he began dryly, and seeing that the man still eyed him with suspicion, “if you would cease to deny that you are Ben Isaje, jeweller of Rotterdam. I have here some papers which I must deliver into the said Ben Isaje’s own hands: they are writ by Mynheer Nicolaes Beresteyn of Haarlem and do explain the purport of my visit here.”
“From Nicolaes Beresteyn,” quoth the other with an obvious sigh of relief. “Why did you not name him before, sir? I am always at Mynheer Nicolaes Beresteyn’s commands. Indeed my name is Ben Isaje. An you have cause to doubt it, sir.﻿ ﻿…”
“Dondersteen! but I never did doubt it, man, from the moment I saw the end of your hooked nose through the aperture of your door. So no more talk now, I pray you. Time is getting on. Here is the letter which Mynheer Beresteyn bade me present to you.”
He handed over the letter to Ben Isaje which was writ in Beresteyn’s own hand and duly signed with his own name. The Jew took it from him and drawing a chair close to the light on the table he unfolded the paper and began to read.
Diogenes the while examined him attentively. He was the man who after this night would have charge of Gilda, at the bidding of her own brother; he﻿—Diogenes﻿—would after this night become a free agent, his pledge to Beresteyn would be redeemed and he would be free﻿—in an hour’s time mayhap﻿—to work for his own ends﻿—to restore the jongejuffrouw to her sorrowing father, by taking her by force from this old Jew’s keeping and returning with utmost speed and in utmost secrecy the very way he had just come. A fortune of 500,000 guilders awaited him in Haarlem, provided he could cajole or threaten Gilda in keeping his share of her original abduction a secret for all times.
How this could be done he had not yet thought on; but that it could be done he had no manner of doubt. An interview with the lady either this night or on the morrow, a promise to take her back to her father at once if she swore a solemn oath never to betray him, and he might be back in Leyden with her tomorrow eve and in possession of a fortune the following day.

===Chinese===

不过这一切，自然与第欧根尼毫不相干。主人相让，他便在一张破椅上坐下，从紧身上衣的夹层里抽出几纸文书。
“这能省些工夫，”他干巴巴地开了口，见那人仍在狐疑地打量他，“你若肯不再抵赖自己就是鹿特丹的珠宝商本·伊萨耶的话。我这里有几纸文书，必须交到这位本·伊萨耶本人手中：是哈勒姆的尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦先生所写，说明了我此来的用意。”
“尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦来的。”对方说，明显松了一口气。“那您先前怎么不提他呢，老爷？贝雷斯坦先生之命，我无有不从。我确实就是本·伊萨耶。您若信不过，老爷。……”
“雷公在上！我从你家门缝里瞧见你那钩鼻子的鼻尖起，就没疑过，伙计。好了，别再啰嗦，我求你。时候不早了。这就是贝雷斯坦先生命我呈给你的信。”
他把信递给本·伊萨耶——信是贝雷斯坦亲笔，署名画押俱全。犹太人接了，把椅子挪到桌灯光下，展开信纸读了起来。
第欧根尼一面细细端详他。就是这个人，过了今夜，就要奉她亲哥哥之命，看管吉尔达；而他——第欧根尼——过了今夜便成了自由之身，欠贝雷斯坦的诺言还清了，他就可以——兴许不出一个钟头——为自己的目标行事了——从这老犹太人的看守里把她强夺出来，沿着他方才来的那条路，飞快地、极其隐秘地把她送回她那伤心父亲身边。
五十万盾的家财在哈勒姆等他——只要他能连哄带吓，叫吉尔达把她当初被劫一案里他扮演的角色，永远烂在肚里。
怎么个做法，他还没想；能不能做成，他半点不疑。今夜或明日同小姐见上一面，许她一句“你若郑重立誓永不卖我，我立时送你回父亲身边”，那么明晚他就能同她一道回到莱顿，后天，家财到手。

===Original===

No wonder then, that with these happy thoughts whirling in his head, he could scarcely restrain his temper while Ben Isaje read the long letter through, and then reread it again a second time.
“Have you not finished, sir?” he exclaimed at last with marked impatience, “meseems the letter is explicit enough.”
“Quite explicit, sir, I thank you,” replied Ben Isaje, as he slowly folded up the letter and slipped it into the pocket of his kaftan. “I am to assure myself that the Jongejuffrouw Gilda Beresteyn, who is in your charge, is safe and well and hath no grave complaints to make against you, beyond that you did seize her by force in the streets of Haarlem. After which I am to see that she is conveyed with respect and safety to my own private house which is situate outside this city, or to any other place which I might think fitting, and there to keep her in comfort until such time as Mynheer Beresteyn desires. All that is quite clearly set forth in the letter, sir, and also that in payment for your services you are to receive the sum of 3,000 guilders which I am to give you in exchange for the formal bond which you will duly present.”
The Jew spoke very deliberately﻿—too deliberately, in fact, for Diogenes’ endurance. Now he broke in impatiently.
“Is that all that is set forth in the letter?”
The Jew smiled somewhat sardonically.
“Not quite all,” he said, “there is, of course, question in it of payment to myself.”
“And certain conditions too, I imagine, attached to such payment. I know that Mynheer Nicolaes Beresteyn is prudent beyond his years.”
“There is but one condition, sir, which enjoins me to keep a watchful eye on the jongejuffrouw once she is under my roof: to set a watch over her and her movements, and never, if possible, to let her out of my sight; he suggests that she might at any time make an attempt at escape, which he strictly commands me to frustrate, and in point of fact he desires me to look upon his sister as a prisoner of war not even to be let out on parole.”
Diogenes’ low, prolonged whistle was his only comment on what he had just heard.
“Mynheer Beresteyn also suggests to me, sir,” continued the Jew with marked affability, “the advisability of keeping a watchful eye over you until such time as the jongejuffrouw is safely housed under my roof.”

===Chinese===

难怪本·伊萨耶把长信从头读完、又从头再读第二遍时，他脑袋里转着这些美事，几乎按捺不住性子。
“您还没读完吗，先生？”他终于嚷起来，不耐烦之极，“依我看这信写得够明白了。”
“够明白，够明白，多谢老爷。”本·伊萨耶答，一面慢条斯理把信折起，塞进长袍口袋。“我要确认，您看管着的吉尔达·贝雷斯坦小姐安然无恙，除哈勒姆街头被您强行劫持一事外，对您别无重大怨言。此后我要安排她体面而安全地移往我城外的私宅，或我认为合适的任何别处，在那里好生安顿，直到贝雷斯坦先生需要之时。信里写得清清楚楚，老爷；还写明，作为您辛劳的酬报，您应得三千盾，由我亲手付与，换回您届时呈上的正式契据。”
犹太人说得极从容——对第欧根尼的耐性来说，实在从容过了头。他忍不住插了嘴。
“信里写的就是这些？”
犹太人颇有几分讥诮地笑了。
“不全。”他说，“信里自然也提到付给我的报酬。”
“想必还有几项条件，拴在那报酬上。我知道尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦先生持重得不像他这个年纪。”
“条件只有一条，老爷：小姐一旦进了我的家门，就要我盯紧她——派人监视她和她的动静，只要做得到，绝不让她离开我的视线；他提示说，她随时可能图谋逃走，严令我务必挫败；说实在的，他要我把他的妹妹当作一名战俘看管，连假释都不许。”
第欧根尼低低地、长长地吹了声口哨——这是他对刚听到的话仅有的评论。
“贝雷斯坦先生还向我建议，老爷，”犹太人接着说，语气格外和蕥，“在小姐安安稳稳住进我家之前，对您也留一分神。”

===Original===

“You will find that injunction somewhat more difficult to follow, my friend, than you imagine,” retorted Diogenes with a ringing laugh, “an you’ll take my advice you will have extra watchmen posted outside your door.”
“I have valuable things as well as monies stored in this house, sir,” rejoined the Jew simply. “I have a picked guard of ten men sleeping here every night, and two watchmen outside my door until dawn.”
Once more a long, low whistle escaped from the philosopher’s lips.
“You are careful, my friend!” he said lightly.
“One has to be careful, sir, against thieves and housebreakers.”
“And will your picked guard of ten men escort the jongejuffrouw to your private house this night?”
But the other slowly shook his head in response.
“The lady and her escort,” he said, “must, I fear me, accept the hospitality of this hovel for tonight.”
“But﻿ ﻿…”
“My wife is away, sir, visiting her father in Dordrecht. She will only be home tomorrow. In the meanwhile my house is empty, and I am spending my nights here as well as my days.”
“But﻿ ﻿…”
“It will not be a great hardship for the jongejuffrouw, sir,” broke in the Jew again, “she will be made as comfortable for the night as maybe﻿—she and her attendant too. I have a serving woman here who will see to the beds and the supper. Then tomorrow I can send a messenger to my private house to prepare my wife the moment she arrives, against the coming of the jongejuffrouw. ’Tis situate but half a league from here, and she would then be sure of a welcome equal to her worth.”
Then as Diogenes was silent﻿—since he felt perplexed and anxious at this unlooked-for turn of events and this first check to his plans﻿—Ben Isaje continued with even greater affability than heretofore:
“Indeed, sir, and is it not better for the lady’s own comfort? She will be overfatigued when she arrives, and delighted﻿—I know﻿—at finding a nice bed and supper ready for her. Is it not all for the best?” he reiterated pleasantly.
But Diogenes was not satisfied. He did not like the idea of losing sight of Gilda altogether, quite so soon.
“I do not care to leave the jongejuffrouw,” he said, “until I see her safely on her way to your house.”
“Nor need you leave her, sir. There is a small room at the back of this shop, to which you are heartily welcome for the night.

===Chinese===

“你会发现这条差使办起来比你想象的难些，我的朋友。”第欧根尼朗声大笑，回敬道，“听我一句劝，在你门外多派几个看守吧。”
“我屋里存着钱财，也存着值钱的物件，老爷。”犹太人平平地答道。“我有一队十人的亲卫，夜夜宿在这里；门外还有两个更夫，守到天亮。”
哲学家唇间又逸出一声悠长的低哨。
“你可真仔细，我的朋友！”他轻飘飘地说。
“防贼防盗，老爷，不由得不仔细。”
“那你的十人亲卫，今夜就护送小姐去你的私宅？”
对方却缓缓摇头。
“小姐和她的随行，”他说，“恐怕今夜只能将就这寒舍了。”
“可是……”
“我内人不在家，老爷，去多德雷赫特探望她父亲了。明天才回得来。眼下私宅里空着，我也就日也在这儿，夜也在这儿。”
“可是……”
“这对小姐不算什么大委屈，老爷。”犹太人又插进来，“今夜自有法子叫她安顿得舒舒服服——她那女仆也一样。我这里有个仆妇，铺床备饭都由她照应。明天一早我可以差人去私宅，我内人一到家就让她备办起来，迎候小姐。私宅离这儿不过半里格，到那时，她准能得一份配得上她身份的迎接。”
第欧根尼不作声了——这意外的变故、他计划遭到的头一记挫折，叫他心乱，叫他焦灼——本·伊萨耶见状，愈发和颜悦色地接着说：
“说真的，老爷，这不正合小姐自己的安逸吗？她到时早累乏了，一见现成的床铺和晚饭等着她——我知道——保管欢喜。这样岂不两全？”他满面和气，又追问了一遍。
可第欧根尼不满意。这么快就完全看不见吉尔达，他不喜欢。
“我不愿离开小姐，”他说，“除非亲眼看着她安安全稳上你宅子的路。”
“那您也不必离开她，老爷。铺子后头有间小屋，今夜诚心留给您歇宿。”

===Original===

It is usually occupied by some of my guard, but they can dispose themselves in other rooms in the house. They are sturdy fellows, sir, and well-armed,” continued the Jew, not without significance, “and I trust that they will not disturb you with their noise. Otherwise, sir, you are most welcome to sleep and sup under this roof.”
Diogenes murmured vague thanks. Indeed, he was not a little troubled in his mind. The plans which he had formed for the second abduction of Gilda would prove more difficult of execution than he had supposed. The Jew had more than the customary prudence of his race, and Beresteyn had made that prudence and the measures which it suggested a condition of payment.
Between the prudence of Beresteyn and that of Ben Isaje, it was difficult to see how an adventurous plan could succeed. Three philosophers against a picked guard of ten men, with two more to keep watch outside the door, did not seem a promising venture. But Diogenes would not have been the happy-go-lucky soldier of fortune that he was, had he paused for long at this juncture in order to brood over likely failure, or had he not been willing to allow Chance a goodly share in the working out of his destiny.
It certainly was useless to argue any of these matters further with Ben Isaje; fate had willed it that the philosopher should spend this night under the same roof as the jongejuffrouw with a watch of twelve picked men﻿—not counting the Jew himself﻿—set over him, and to rebel against that fate now were puerile and useless.
So he murmured more audible thanks for the proffered hospitality, and put on as good-humoured an air over the matter as he could.
From the distance now there came the sound of jingling bells and the clatter of horses’ hoofs upon the cobblestones of the streets.
“ ’Tis the jongejuffrouw,” exclaimed Diogenes, springing to his feet.
“The sledge cannot turn into this narrow way,” rejoined Ben Isaje, “will you go meet the lady, sir, at the top of the street where she must needs dismount, and escort her hither, while I go to give orders to the serving woman. Your men,” he added, as Diogenes at once rose and went to the door, “and the horses can put up at the hostelry close by where no doubt they have halted even now.”
But already Diogenes was halfway down the passage; soon he was at the front door fumbling in the dark for the heavy bolts.

===Chinese===

“平日是我几个卫兵住的，他们可以到宅里别的屋去安置。都是些结实汉子，老爷，兵器齐全。”犹太人接着说，话里有话，“谅他们吵不着您。除此之外，老爷，在这片屋檐下吃住，您再受欢迎不过。”
第欧根尼含含糊糊道了谢。说真的，他心里烦恼不小。他拟下的再度劫走吉尔达的计划，看来办起来要比他料的难。这犹太人比他族类惯常的谨慎还要多三分，而贝雷斯坦又把这份谨慎、连同谨慎生出的种种布置，定成了付款的条件。
夹在贝雷斯坦的谨慎和本·伊萨耶的谨慎中间，一个冒险的计划怎样才成得了，难说。三位哲学家对一队十人的亲卫，门外还添两个更夫——这买卖看着不甚乐观。可第欧根尼若在这关口停步长久、净琢潣着多半的失败，若不肯让“机运”也占他命运的一大份，他也就不是他这么个听天由命、随遇而安的亡命之徒了。
这些事再同本·伊萨耶理论，自然无用；天意已定：哲学家今夜要与小姐同一片屋檐，头顶上还压着十二名精选的看守——犹太人自己还不在内；此刻同这天意闹别扭，既孩子气，也无益。
于是他把道谢说得更清楚了些，又给这桩事尽量装上一副好脾气的面孔。
这时，远处传来铃铛的叮当和马蹄叩击街石的脆响。
“是小姐来了。”第欧根尼一跃而起。
“雪橇拐不进这条窄巷。”本·伊萨耶应道，“您去街口迎一迎小姐吧，老爷，她必得在那儿下橇——再护送她过来，我呢，去吩咐仆妇。您的人，”第欧根尼当即起身朝门走去时，他又添上一句，“和马匹可以安顿在近处的客店，想必这会儿已经落脚了。”
可第欧根尼早已到了过道半截；转眼便到了临街门前，摸黑去寻那几道重闩。

===Original===

Ben Isaje followed him more deliberately, lantern in hand. He unlocked the door, and the next moment Diogenes was once more out in the street, walking rapidly in the direction whence came the occasional pleasing sound of the tinkling of sleigh-bells.

===Chinese===

本·伊萨耶提着灯笼，不慌不忙跟了上来。他开了锁，下一刻，第欧根尼又站在街上，循着那断续可闻、悦耳的雪橇铃声，快步迎了上去。

## XXIX Check Again / 二十九 再挫
===Original===

Though the jongejuffrouw seemed inexpressibly tired and weak, her attitude toward Diogenes lost nothing of its cold aloofness. She was peeping out under the hood of the sledge when he approached it, and at sight of him she immediately drew in her head.
“Will you deign to descend, mejuffrouw,” he said with that slight tone of good-humoured mockery in his voice which had the power to irritate her. “Mynheer Ben Isaje, whose hospitality you will enjoy this night, lives some way up this narrow, insalubrious street, and he has bidden me to escort you to his house.”
Silently, and with a great show of passive obedience, Gilda made ready to step out of the sledge.
“Come, Maria,” she said curtly.
“The road is very slippery, mejuffrouw,” he added warningly, “will you not permit me﻿—for your own convenience’ sake﻿—to carry you as far as Ben Isaje’s door?”
“It would not be for my convenience, sir,” she retorted haughtily, “an you are so chivalrously inclined perhaps you would kindly convey my waiting woman thither in your arms.”
“At your service, mejuffrouw,” he said with imperturbable good temper.
And without more ado, despite her screams and her struggles, he seized Maria round her ample waist and round her struggling knees at the moment that she was stepping out of the sledge in the wake of her mistress.
The lamp outside the hostel at the corner illumined for a moment Gilda’s pale, wearied face, and Diogenes saw that she was trying her best to suppress an insistent outburst of laughter.
“Hey there!” he shouted, “Pythagoras, Socrates, follow the jongejuffrouw at a respectful distance and see that no harm come to her while I lead the way with this featherweight in my arms.”
Nor did he deposit Maria to the ground until he reached the door of Ben Isaje’s house; here, when the *mevrouw* began to belabour him with her tongue and with her fists, he turned appealingly to Gilda:
“Mejuffrouw,” he said merrily, “is this abuse not unmerited? I did but obey your behests and see how I must suffer for mine obedience.”
But Gilda vouchsafed him no reply, and in the darkness he could not see if her face looked angered or smiling.
Ben Isaje, hearing the noise that went on outside his house, had already hastened to open the door. He welcomed the jongejuffrouw with obsequious bows.

===Chinese===

小姐看去说不出的疲惫虚弱，可她对第欧根尼的态度，那份冷淡疏远分毫不减。他走近雪橇时，她正从风帽下朝外张望，一看见他，立刻缩回了头。
“请小姐屈尊下车吧。”他说，嗓音里带着那丝好脾气的调侃——最能惹恼她的就是这调子。“本·伊萨耶先生——今夜您将叨扰他的款待——就住在这条又窄又脏的街上头一点，他命我护送您去他府上。”
吉尔达一声不响，摆足了一副逆来顺受的架势，准备下橇。“走吧，玛丽亚。”她简短地说。
“路上滑得很，小姐。”他又提醒道，“为着您自己方便——可否容我把您抱到本·伊萨耶家门口？”
“那可不是为我的方便，先生。”她傲然回敬，“您既有这般侠骨柔肠，劳驾把我这侍女也这样抱过去吧。”
“遵命，小姐。”他说，好脾气纹丝不动。
说时迟那时快，玛丽亚正跟着女主人迈下雪橇，他也不多话，不顾她又尖叫又挣扎，一把攔住她那肥硕的腰和乱蹬的膝盖，抱了起来。
街角客店外的灯，照亮了吉尔达苍白倦怠的脸一瞬；第欧根尼看见，她正竭力压着一阵非要发作不可的笑。
“喂！”他喊道，“毕达哥拉斯，苏格拉底，隔着恭敬的距离跟着小姐，护她周全——我抱着这位轻飘飘的宝贝开道！”
直到本·伊萨耶家门口，他才把玛丽亚放下；这位夫人（mevrouw）抄起舌头和拳头一齐招呼他，他便一脸委屈地转向吉尔达：
“小姐，”他乐呵呵地说，“这顿臭骂冤不冤？我不过奉了你的吩咐，瞧瞧，服从的下场就是这样。”
可吉尔达赏他的只有一个不理不睬；黑暗里他也看不清她的脸是怒是笑。
本·伊萨耶听见门外闹哄哄，早抢着开了门，一躬到地，谄笑着把小姐迎进去。

===Original===

Behind him in the dark passage stood a lean and tousled-looking serving woman of uncertain years who was as obsequious as her master. When Gilda, confused and wearied, and mayhap not a little tired, advanced timorously into the narrow passage, the woman rushed up to her, and almost kneeling on the floor in the lowliness of her attitude, she kissed the jongejuffrouw’s hand.
Diogenes saw nothing more of Gilda and Maria after that. They vanished into the gloom up the ladder-like staircase, preceded by the tousled but amiable woman, who by her talk and clumsy attempts at service had already earned Maria’s fulsome contempt.
“You, too, must be hungry, sir,” murmured a smooth affable voice close to Diogenes’ elbow. “There is a bite and a drink ready for you; will you sup, sir, ere you go to bed?”
Before, however, following Ben Isaje into the shop Diogenes exchanged a few words with his brother philosophers, who, impassive and unquestioning, had escorted the jongejuffrouw to the door, and now stood there awaiting further orders. Diogenes suggested their getting supper and a bed in the hostelry at the top of the street in company with their driver; the horses too should all be stabled there.
“I am going to spend the night under this tumbledown roof,” he said, “but remember to sleep with one eye open and be prepared for a summons from me at any hour of the night or morning. Until that comes, however, do not leave the hostel. Care well for the horses, we may have need of them tomorrow. Good night! pleasant dreams! Do not forget that tomorrow five hundred guilders will fill each of your pockets. In the meanwhile here is the wherewithal to pay for bed and supper.”
He gave them some money and then watched the two quaint figures, the long one and the round one, until they were merged in the blackness of the narrow street. Then he went within. Ben Isaje once more closed and bolted the front door and the two men then went together into the shop.
Here an appetizing supper had been laid ready upon the table and a couple of tallow candles burned in pewter sconces.
Ben Isaje at once invited his guest to eat and drink.
“Not before we have settled our business together, master,” said the latter as he dragged a chair towards him, and sitting astride upon it, with his shapely legs thrust well out before him, he once more drew a paper from out the lining of his doublet.

===Chinese===

他身后的黑过道里，立着一个精瘦、头发蓬乱的仆妇，年纪看不出，谄媚同她主人不相上下。吉尔达迷迷瞪瞪、倦意重重——只怕还不止是倦——怯生生往窄过道里走时，那妇人扑上前来，那副卑屈样子简直要跪到地上，捧起小姐的手就吻。
打那以后，第欧根尼再没见着吉尔达和玛丽亚。她们随着那蓬头却和气的妇人，爬上梯子似的楼梯，没进昏暗里去了——那妇人一路的絮叨和笨手笨脚的殷勤，早赚足了玛丽亚的满心鄙夷。
“您怕也饿了，老爷。”第欧根尼胳膊肘边，一个圆滑和气的声音哺哺道。“一口吃的、一口喝的都给您备下了；上床前用点晚饭吗，老爷？”
不过，在跟着本·伊萨耶进铺子之前，第欧根尼先同两位哲学家同仁交代了几句——他们面无表情、不问缘由地把小姐送到门口，正立着候命。第欧根尼提议他们去街口的客店，同赶车的一起吃晚饭、睡一宿；马也一并拴在那儿。
“今夜我就睡在这片快塌的屋檐底下。”他说，“可记住，睡觉要睁一只眼，夜里也好，清早也好，我随时可能叫你们。叫你们之前，不许出客店。把马照看好，明天兴许用得着。晚安！好梦！别忘了，明天五百盾会装满你们一人一只口袋。眼下呢，这是床钱饭钱。”
他给了他们一些钱，然后望着那两副滑稽身影——一长一圆——没进窄街的黑影里，才转身进去。本·伊萨耶重新关上临街的门，上了闩，两人随后一同进了铺子。
桌上一顿香气诱人的晚饭已经摆好，白锡烛台上点着两支牛油烛。
本·伊萨耶立刻请客人用饭。
“先办完咱们的正事再吃，老板。”后者答道，一面把一把椅子拖到跟前，跨骑上去，两条漂亮的腿远远伸在前面，又从上衣夹层里抽出一纸文书。

===Original===

“You are satisfied,” he resumed after a slight pause, “that the lady whom I have had the honour of bringing into your house is indeed the Jongejuffrouw Gilda Beresteyn, sister of your client Mynheer Nicolaes Beresteyn of Haarlem?”
“I am quite satisfied on that point,” replied the Jew, whose thin, bent form under the rigid folds of the black kaftan looked curiously weird in the feeble yellow light. His face was narrow and also waxlike in hue and the flickering candlelight threw quaint, distorted shadows around his long hooked nose.
“Then,” said Diogenes blandly while he held out a folded paper to Ben Isaje, “here is the bond signed by Mynheer Beresteyn wherein he orders you to pay me the sum of 3,000 guilders in consideration of the services which I have rendered him.”
But Ben Isaje did not take the paper thus held out to him.
“It is too late,” he said quietly, “to transact business tonight.”
“Too late!” exclaimed Diogenes with a blunt oath. “What in thunder do you mean?”
“I mean, sir, that you must try and curb your natural impatience until tomorrow.”
“But I will not curb mine impatience another moment, *plepshurk*,” cried the philosopher in a rage, “I have fulfilled my share of a bargain, ’tis only a *verdommte Keerl* who would shirk paying his own share on the nail.”
“Nor would Mynheer Beresteyn desire me to shirk his responsibilities, I assure you,” rejoined the Jew suavely, “and believe me, sir, that you will not lose one grote by waiting until the morrow. Let a good supper and a comfortable bed freely offered you atone for this unimportant delay. You still hold Mynheer Beresteyn’s bond: tomorrow at the first business hour you shall be paid.”
“But why any delay at all?” thundered Diogenes, who indeed misliked this way of doing business. “Why not pay me the money now?﻿—at once, I will gladly forego the supper and sit all night upon your doorstep, but have my money in my pocket.”
“Unfortunately, sir,” said Ben Isaje with imperturbable amiability, “I am quite helpless in the matter. I am not the sole master of this business, my wife’s brother shares my profits and my obligations. Neither of us is at liberty to pay out a large sum of money, save in the presence of the other.”
“You and your partner know how to trust one another,” said Diogenes with a laugh.

===Chinese===

“你已确认，”他略停了停，接着说，“我有幸领进你家的这位小姐，确是吉尔达·贝雷斯坦小姐，你主顾、哈勒姆的尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦先生的妹妹？”
“这一点我完全确认。”犹太人答。昏黄的微光里，他黑袍僵直的褶子裹着那副瘦削伛偻的身形，透着说不出的怪。他的脸窄，颜色蜡黄，烛焰摇曳，在他那长长的钩鼻四周投下古怪扭曲的影子。
“那么，”第欧根尼和颜悦色地说，一面把一纸折好的文书递向本·伊萨耶，“这就是贝雷斯坦先生签押的契据，着他付我三千盾，作为我为他效劳的酬报。”
可本·伊萨耶没有去接那纸递过来的文书。
“太晚了。”他不动声色地说，“今晚不办公事。”
“太晚！”第欧根尼粗粗地咒了一声，叫起来。“你这话到底什么意思，活见鬼！”
“我是说，老爷，您得想法子把您天生的急性子拴到明天。”
“可我的急性子一刻也拴不住了，*蠢货（plepshurk）*。”哲学家怒喝道，“买卖里我那一份早已交割清楚，当面赖账这种事，只有*该死的货色（verdommte Keerl）*才干得出来。”
“贝雷斯坦先生也断不愿我替他赖掉责任，我向您担保。”犹太人柔声柔气地应道，“信我，老爷，等到明天，您一个格罗特也少不了。一顿好晚饭、一张舒服的床，敞开供给您，就抵了这点无关紧要的耽搁吧。契据仍在您手里：明天头一个营业时辰，钱就付您。”
“可为什么非耽搁不可？”第欧根尼雷吼起来，他实在看不惯这种做买卖的路数。“为什么不现在就付？——立刻，晚饭我可以不吃，在你门槛上坐一宿也行，只要钱进了我的口袋。”
“不巧，老爷，”本·伊萨耶带着岿然不动的和气说，“这件事我做不得主。这买卖我不是独东家，我内兄同我共利润、也共义务。两人不同在一处，谁也无权支付大笔款项。”
“你和你这位合伙的，倒真知道怎么互相信任。”第欧根尼笑道。

===Original===

The Jew made no comment on this, only shrugged his shoulders in that calm manner peculiar to his race, which suggests the Oriental resignation to compelling fate.
Diogenes﻿—inwardly fuming﻿—thought over the matter very quietly for a few moments: it was obviously as useless to argue this matter out with Ben Isaje, as it had been to combat his dictum anent the jongejuffrouw spending the night under his roof, and as usual the wholesome lesson of life which the philosopher had learnt so thoroughly during his adventurous career stood him in good stead now: the lesson was the one which taught him never to waste time, temper or words over a purposeless argument.
That one shrug of Isaje’s shoulders had told him with dumb eloquence that no amount of persuasion on his part would cause the banker to swerve from his determination. The money would be forthcoming on the morrow but not before, and there were ten picked men somewhere in the house at the present moment to prevent Diogenes from settling this matter in a primitive and efficient way by using his fists.
So in this instance too﻿—disappointed though he was﻿—he quickly regained his good humour. After all, the Jew was right: a night’s delay would not spell a loss, and was well compensated for by a good supper and cosy bed.
With his habitual lighthearted laugh and careless shrug of the shoulders, he folded the paper up again and once more slipped it carefully into the inner lining of his doublet.
“You are right, sir,” he said, “ ’twere foolish to allow choler to spoil the appetite. I am as hungry as the dog of a Spaniard. By your leave I’ll test the strength of your ale and tomorrow ere I leave your house you shall pay me over the money in the presence of your trusting brother-in-law. Until then the bond remains with me, and I hold myself responsible for the safety of the jongejuffrouw. So I pray you be not surprised if I forbid her removal from this house until I have exchanged this bond for the sum of 3,000 guilders.”
After which he drew his chair close to the table, and fell to all its good cheer with a hearty will. Ben Isaje, hospitable and affable to the last, waited on him with his own hands.

===Chinese===

犹太人不答话，只耸了耸肩，那种他的族类特有的从容耸法，透着东方人对天命的逆来顺受。
第欧根尼——心里窝着火——面上却安安静静把这事掠了片刻：同本·伊萨耶把这场官司辩出结果，显然同先前驳他“小姐必须宿在他屋檐下”的断言一样白费；而生活教给哲学家的那门有益功课——他冒险生涯里学得透熟——此刻又派上了用场：这功课教的是，绝不在无谓的争执上浪费时间、脾气和口舌。
伊萨耶那一耸肩，无声胜有声地告诉他：任凭他巧舌如簧，也休想叫这银行家偏离主意半分。钱明天会来，今天不来；而且此刻宅子不知哪个角落里正窝着十名精壮，专等第欧根尼想用拳头把这件事原始而高效地了结时好用上。
于是这一回也一样——失望归失望——他很快又找回了好兴致。毕竟犹太人说得不错：耽搁一夜不等于折财，一顿好晚饭、一张暖和床，足堪补偿。
他照例轻快地一笑，无所谓地一耸肩，把那纸文书重新折好，小心塞回上衣夹层。
“你说得对，先生。”他说，“为斗气坏了胃口，那是傻子。我饿得像条西班牙狗。叨光了，我要尝尝你家麦酒的劲道；明天离开你宅子之前，请你那位互信的内兄在场，把钱点给我。在那之前，契据在我手里，小姐的安危也归我负责。所以，在我把契据换回三千盾之前，我不许她挪出这座宅子一步——到时候请你别惊讶。”
说完，他把椅子拉近桌子，痛痛快快地投入了满桌的丰美。本·伊萨耶殷勤和气到底，亲自动手给他布菜斟酒。

## XXX A Nocturne / 三十 夜曲
===Original===

It was only natural that, though tired as he was and enjoying an unusually contented mind, Diogenes was nevertheless unable to get to sleep.
He had had a very good supper and had parted at an early hour from his host. Ben Isaje had been amiable even deferential to the last, and indeed there had been nothing in the Jew’s demeanour to arouse misgivings in the most suspicious mind.
The lean and tousled serving woman had prepared a clean and comfortable bed in the narrow alcove within the wall panelling of the small room which adjoined the shop, but though the weary philosopher wooed sleep with utmost persistence, it resolutely refused to be lured to his pillow. At first the arrival of the night watchmen had kept him awake: for they made their entrance with much jangling of swords and loud and lusty talk. There was apparently a good solid partition between his room and the shop because as soon as the watchmen were settled at their post their voices only reached Diogenes’ ear like a muffled murmur.
A door gave from this room on the passage and this he had carefully locked; but it hung loosely on its hinges and the slightest noise in the house﻿—a heavy footfall overhead or in the shop﻿—would cause it to rattle with a weird, intermittent sound which sent sleep flying baffled away.
There were thoughts too which crowded in upon him﻿—pleasant thoughts as well as others that were a trifle sad﻿—the immediate future with its promise of a possible fortune loomed brightly enough, but the means to that happy end was vaguely disturbing the lighthearted equanimity of this soldier of fortune accustomed hitherto to grip Chance by the hair whenever she rushed past him in her mad, whirling career, and without heeding those who stood in his way.
But suddenly the whole thing seemed different, and Diogenes himself could not have told you why it was so. Thoughts of the future and of the promises which it held disturbed when they should have elated him: there was a feeling in him which he could not analyse, a feeling wherein a strange, sweet compassion seemed to form the main ingredient.

===Chinese===

虽说累得很，心里又难得地熨帖，第欧根尼却怎么也睡不着，这也是自然的事。
他吃了一顿很好的晚饭，早早同主人道了安置。本·伊萨耶一直和气，甚至到了谦卑的地步；说真的，这犹太人的做派里，实在找不出什么能叫最多疑的人起疑的地方。
那精瘦蓬头的仆妇，已在毗连铺子的那间小屋的墙板凹龛里，铺了一张干净舒服的床；可疲惫的哲学家百般向睡眠求爱，睡眠却打死不肯上他的枕头。起初，更夫们的到来叫他睡不着：他们进屋时刀剑乱响，说话粗声大气。他那间屋同铺子之间显然隔着一道厚实的板壁，因为更夫们一在岗位上安顿下来，他们的声音传到第欧根尼耳中，便只剩一片闷闷的低语。
这间屋有一扇门通向过道，他仔细锁了；可门在合页上晃晃荡荡，宅子里最轻的一点响动——头顶上或铺子里一顿重脚步——都会叫它咯咯作响，那声音断断续续，透着邪性，把睡眠惊得无功而逃。
还有种种念头涌上来挤他——有愉快的，也有几分伤感的——眼面前的日子，许着一份可能的富贵，前程够亮堂；可通往那个好结局的手段，却隐隐搅扰着这位亡命之徒的轻快心境：他向来是在机运发疯打旋地冲过他身边时，一把揪住她头发的，挡道的从不放在眼里。
可突然间，整件事变了个样，为什么如此，第欧根尼自己也说不上。对未来的念头，对前途许诺的念头，本该叫他振奋，此刻却叫他不安：他心里有一种说不清的感觉，那感觉里，一种奇异的、温柔的怜惜，仿佛是主要的成分。

===Original===

The philosopher who had hitherto viewed life through the rosy glasses of unalterable good-humour, who had smiled at luck and ill-luck, laughed at misfortune and at hope, suddenly felt that there was something in life which could not be dismissed lightheartedly, something which really counted, though it was so intangible and so elusive that even now he could not give it a name.
The adventurer, who had slept soundly and dreamlessly in camp and on the field, in the streets of a sacked town or the still smouldering battlements of a fortress, could find no rest in the comfortable bed so carefully prepared for him in the house of Ben Isaje the Jew. The murmur of voices from the shop, low and monotonous, irritated his nerves, the rattling of the door upon its hinges drove him well-nigh distracted.
He heard every noise in the house as they died out one by one; the voice of the serving woman bidding the jongejuffrouw “good night,” the shuffling footsteps of the old Jew, the heavy tread of Maria overhead, and another, light and swift which﻿—strangely enough﻿—disturbed him more completely than the louder sounds had done.
At last he could stand his present state no longer, he felt an unpleasant tingling to the very tips of his fingers and the very roots of his hair; it seemed to him as if soft noiseless steps wandered aimlessly outside his door; furtive tiny animals with feet of velvet must have run down the stairs and then halted, breathless and terrified, on the other side of those rattling wooden panels.
He sat up in bed and groping for his tinder he struck a light; then he listened again. Not a sound now stirred inside the house, only the wind soughed through the loose tiles of the roof and found out the chinks and cracks of the ill-fitting window, through which it blew with a sharp, whistling sound. From the shop there came the faint murmur of some of the watchmen snoring at their post.
Beyond that, nothing. And yet Diogenes, whose keen ear was trained to catch the flutter of every twig, the movement of every beast, could have sworn that someone was awake at this moment, in this house besides himself﻿—someone who breathed and trembled on the other side of the door.
Without a moment’s hesitation he slipped on his clothes as quickly as he could, then he pulled the curtains across in front of the alcove and paused for one second longer in order to listen.

===Chinese===

这位哲学家，一向戴着永不掉色的好脾气玫瑰色眼镜看人生，对幸运与厄运一例微笑，对灾祸与希望一例大笑，此刻忽然觉得，生活里有一种东西不能一笑了之，一种真正算数的东西——尽管它那样无形无迹、难捉难拿，连此刻他也叫不出它的名字。
这位冒险家，在营中在沙场睡得香甜无梦，在被劫之城的大街上、在要塞还在冒烟的城垛上也睡得着，如今在本·伊萨耶犹太人家里这张悉心备下的舒服床上，却找不到一点安宁。铺子里那阵低而单调的人语，磨着他的神经；门在合页上的咯咯声，几乎把他逼疯。
宅子里的响动一样一样熄下去，他一样一样都听见了：仆妇向小姐道“晚安”的声音，老犹太人趿拉趿拉的脚步，头顶上玛丽亚沉沉的脚步声——还有另一种，又轻又急的，说来奇怪，却比那些更响的动静更彻底地搅乱了他。
终于他再也受不住了：指尖发麻，发根发麻，麻得难受；他恍惚觉得，门外有软软的、无声的脚步在无目的地徘徊；准是几只长着天鹅绒脚的小东西，鬼鬼祟祟溜下楼梯，又屏住呼吸、吓坏了似的，停在了那咯咯作响的木门板另一边。
他从床上坐起来，摸出火镰打了个火；然后又听。宅子里此刻一丝声息也无，只有风从屋顶松瓦间穿过的呜咽，又寻出歪窗的缝和裂，从那里尖啸着灌进来。铺子那边，隐隐传来几个更夫在岗位上打鼾的声音。
除此之外，什么也没有。可第欧根尼——他那只锐耳，受训听得见每根细枝的颤动、每只走兽的行动——完全可以起誓：此刻这宅子里，除他之外还有一个人醒着——有一个人，正在门的那一边呼吸、发抖。
他一刻也不耽搁，飞快地把衣裳套上，拉拢凹龛前的帐子，又多停了一秒，听。

===Original===

He had certainly not been mistaken. Through the stillness of the house he heard the soughing of the wind, the snoring of the watchmen, and that faint, palpitating sound outside in the passage﻿—that sound which was as the breathing of some living, frightened thing.
Then he walked as noiselessly as he could up to the door, and with a sudden simultaneous turn of key and handle he opened it suddenly.
It opened outwards, and the passage beyond was pitch dark, but there in front of him now, white as a ghost, white as the garment which she wore, white as the marble statue of the Madonna which he had seen in the cathedral at Prague, stood the jongejuffrouw.
The candle which she carried flickered in the draught, and thus flickering it lit up her large blue eyes which she kept fixed upon him with an expression half defiant yet wholly terrified.
Frankly he thought at first that this was an apparition, a vivid embodiment of the fevered fancies which had been haunting him. No wonder therefore that he made no movement toward her, or expressed the slightest astonishment at seeing her there, all alone, in the middle of the night, not five paces away from him.
Thus they stood looking at one another for some time in absolute silence; she obviously very frightened, hesitating betwixt audacity and immediate flight, and he puzzled and with a vague sense of unreality upon him, a sense as of a dream which yet had in it the pulsating vividness of life.
She was the first to break this silence which was beginning to be oppressive. Gilda Beresteyn was not a timid woman nor was hers a character which ever vacillated once her mind was made up. The step which she had taken this night﻿—daring and unconventional as it was﻿—had been well thought out: deliberately and seriously she had weighed every danger, every risk which she ran, even those which in her pure-minded innocence she was not able fully to appreciate. Now though she was scared momentarily, she had no thought of turning back.
The old stiff-necked haughtiness of her race did not desert her for a moment, even though she was obviously at a disadvantage in this instance, and had come here as a suppliant.
“I wished to speak with you, sir,” she said, and her voice had scarce a tremor in it, “my woman was too timorous to come down and summon you to my presence, as I had ordered her to do; so I was forced to come myself.”

===Chinese===

他确实没听错。宅子的寂静里，他听见风的呜咽，更夫的鼾声，还有过道里那微弱的、悸动着的声响——那声响，像一只活着的、受惊的东西的呼吸。
于是他尽量无声地走到门边，钥匙和把手同时一拧，猛地拉开门。
门朝外开，门外过道漆黑；可就在他面前，立着小姐——白得像幽灵，白得像她身上那件衣裳，白得像他在布拉格大教堂里见过的那尊大理石圣母像。
她手里的烛在穿堂风里摇曳，烛光摇曳着，照亮她那双大大的蓝眼睛；她直直望定他，眼神一半倔强，一半全是惊惶。
老实说，他起初以为这是幻象，是纠缠了他半夜的那些热切妄念活灵活现地现了形。所以难怪他没有朝她挪动一步，也没有露出半点惊讶——她就站在那里，孤身一人，半夜，离他不到五步。
两人就这样对望着，望了好一阵，绝对的沉默：她显然怕极了，在硬撑与立刻逃走之间踌躇；他又惑又恍惚，恍如梦中，可那梦里又有活生生、突突跳的真实。
先打破这份渐渐压人的沉默的是她。吉尔达·贝雷斯坦不是个胆小的女子，她的性子，一旦拿定主意，从不摇摆。今夜这一步——再大胆、再不合闺训——也是想透了的：她把每一重危险、每一分风险都掠量过，郑重其事，连那些以她的纯洁天真还掠量不透的，也掠量过了。此刻她虽一时心惊，却没有半点回头的意思。
她家世传的那副傲骨，一刻也不曾弃她而去，哪怕这一回她分明居于下风，是有求而来。
“我有话同你说，先生。”她说，嗓音几乎不带一丝颤，“我的女仆胆子小，不敢下来传你到我面前去，我吩咐过她的；只好我自己来了。”

===Original===

Though she looked very helpless, very childlike in her innocence, she had contrived to speak to him like a princess addressing a menial, holding her tiny head very high and making visible efforts to still the quivering of her lips.
There was something so quaint in this proud attitude of hers under the present circumstances, that despite its pathos Diogenes’ keen sense of humour was not proof against it, and that accustomed merry smile of his crept slowly over every line of his face.
“I am ever at your service, mejuffrouw,” he said as gravely as he could, “your majordomo, your valet﻿ ﻿… I always await your commands.”
“Then I pray you take this candle,” she said coldly, “and stand aside that I may enter. What I have to say cannot be told in this passage.”
He took the candle from her, since she held it out to him, and then stepped aside just as she had commanded, keeping the door wide open for her to pass through into the room. She was holding herself very erect, and with perfect self-possession she now selected a chair whereon to sit. She wore the same white gown which she had on when first he laid hands on her in the streets of Haarlem, and the fur cloak wherein she had wrapped herself had partially slid from her shoulders.
Having sat down, close to the table, with one white arm resting upon it, she beckoned peremptorily to him to close the door and to put the candle down; all of which he did quite mechanically, for the feeling had come back to him that the white figure before him was only a vision﻿—or mayhap a dream﻿—from which, however, he hoped not to awaken too soon.
“At your command, mejuffrouw,” was all that he said, and he remained standing quite close to the door, with half the width of the room between himself and her.
But to himself he murmured under his breath:
“St. Bavon and the Holy Virgin, do ye both stand by me now!”
“I do not know, sir,” she began after awhile, “if my coming here at this hour doth greatly surprise you, but in truth the matter which brings me is so grave that I cannot give a thought to your feelings or to mine own.”
“And mine, mejuffrouw, are of such little consequence,” he said good-humouredly seeing that she appeared to wait for a reply, “that it were a pity you should waste precious time in considering them.”
“Nor have I come to talk of feelings, sir. My purpose is of deadly earnestness.

===Chinese===

虽说她看去那样无助，那份天真里像个孩子，她竟有本事拿出公主对仆从说话的腔调：小小的头颅高高昂着，还看得出在用力压住嘴唇的颤抖。
眼下的处境，她却摆出这么一副骄傲的架势，那模样实在古怪，纵然楚楚可怜，第欧根尼那副敏锐的幽默感也招架不住，他那惯常的快活笑意，慢慢爬上了脸上每一道纹路。
“小人随时听候差遣，小姐。”他尽量板正地说，“您的大管事，您的贴身仆……但凭吩咐。”
“那就请你接过这支蜡烛。”她冷冷地说，“让开，让我进去。我要说的话，不能在这过道里讲。”
她把蜡烛递过来，他便接了，依着她的吩咐侧身让开，把门大敞着，由她进屋。她身子挺得笔直，神色从容之极，拣了一张椅子坐下。她穿的还是哈勒姆街头他头一回对她下手时那件白裙，裹身的那件皮氅，已半滑下肩头。
她在桌边坐下，一条雪白的手臂搁在桌上，颐指气使地示意他关门、放烛；他一一照办，全然是机械的，因为那感觉又回来了：眼前这白色身影不过是幻象——或者一个梦——只是这梦，他但愿不要醒得太早。
“恭候吩咐，小姐。”他只说了这一句，仍紧挨着门立着，同她之间隔着半间屋的宽。
心里却暗暗祷告：“圣巴沃，圣母马利亚，你们俩这会儿可得帮我站着！”
“我不知道，先生，”过了一会儿她开口道，“我此时此地前来，是否叫你大为惊讶；但实话说，我来为的事太重大，你的心情也好，我的心情也好，都顾不上计较了。”
“我的心情么，小姐，不值一提。”见她像是等着回话，他好脾气地说，“您若把宝贵工夫花在体察它上头，那就可惜了。”
“我也不是来谈心情的，先生。我的事，性命交关地正经。我来向你提议一桂买卖，请你应承。”

===Original===

I have come to propose a bargain for your acceptance.”
“A bargain?”
“Yes. A bargain,” she reiterated. “One I hope and think that you will find it worthwhile to accept.”
“Then may I crave the honour of hearing the nature of that bargain, mejuffrouw?” he asked pleasantly.
She did not give him an immediate reply but remained quite still and silent for a minute or even two, looking with wide-open inquiring eyes on the tall figure of the man who had﻿—to her mind﻿—done her such an infinite wrong. She noted and acknowledged quite dispassionately the air of splendour which became him so well﻿—splendour of physique, of youth and of strength, and those laughing eyes that questioned and that mocked, the lips that always smiled and the straight brow with its noble sweep which hid the true secret of his personality. And once again﻿—as on that evening at Leyden﻿—she fell almost to hating him, angered that such a man should be nothing better than a knave, a mercenary rogue paid to lend a hand in unavowable deeds.
He stood her scrutiny as best he could, answering her look of haughty condescension with one of humble deference; but the smile of gentle irony never left his lips and tempered the humility of his attitude.
“You have owned to me, sir,” resumed Gilda Beresteyn at last, “that you have been paid for the infamous work which you are doing now; for laying hands on me in the streets of Haarlem and for keeping me a prisoner at the good will of your employer. To own to such a trade, sir, is to admit oneself somewhat below the level of honest men. Is that not so?”
“Below the level of most men, mejuffrouw, I admit,” he replied imperturbably.
“Had it not been for that admission on your part, I would never have thought of coming to you with a proposal which.﻿ ﻿…”
“Which you never would have put before an honest man,” he broke in with perfect equanimity, seeing that she hesitated.
“You anticipate my thought, sir: and I am glad to find that you will make my errand even easier than I had hoped. Briefly then let me tell you﻿—as I told you at Leyden﻿—that I know who your paymaster is. A man has thought fit to perpetrate a crime against me, for a reason which no doubt he deemed expedient and which probably he has not imparted to you. Reasons and causes I imagine, sir, are no concern of yours. You take payment for your deeds and do not inquire into motives. Is that not so?”

===Chinese===

“买卖？”
“对，一桂买卖。”她重复道，“我希望、也相信，你会觉得值得应承。”
“那么可否赏个脸，容我听听这买卖的内容，小姐？”他愉快地问。
她没有立刻回答，只是静静坐着，一两分钟一言不发，睁大探究的眼睛，望着眼前这条高大身影——在她看来，这个人给她的伤害罄竹难书。她冷静地打量他，也冷静地承认：他身上那股堂堂气派，实在衬他——体魄、青春和力量的堂堂气派；那双又探问又嘲弄的笑眼，那总含着笑的唇，还有那道气度轩昂的直眉，眉底下藏着他性情的真正秘密。于是又一次——像在莱顿那个晚上——她几乎恨起他来：这样的人，竟不过是个无赖，一个拿了钱替人干龌龊事的雇佣恶棍，怎不叫人气愤。
他尽力受着她的审视，用谦卑恭敬的眼神回敬她那居高临下的傲视；可唇边那抹温和的讥诮始终不去，给他姿态里的谦卑兑了点味。
“你已经向我承认过，先生，”吉尔达·贝雷斯坦终于又开口，“你如今干的这桩无耻营生，是拿了钱的——在哈勒姆街头对我下手，凭你雇主的意旨押着我做囚徒。承认干这一行，先生，就等于承认自己比正派人的格低了几等。是不是这样？”
“比大多数人的格低，小姐，我认。”他纹丝不动地答。
“若不是有你这一认，我断不会想到来找你，提一个……”
“一个你断不会端到正派人面前的提议。”见她迟疑，他心平气和地替她接上。
“你把我的话先说了，先生：我高兴地发现，你这趟能替我省的事，比我指望的还多。那我就长话短说——同我在莱顿说的一样——我知道你的东家是谁。有个人认为对我下一桩罪是合宜的，理由想必他觉得方便，多半也没有透给你。缘由和动机，我想，先生，与你无干。你拿钱办事，不问动机。是不是这样？”

===Original===

This time Diogenes only made a slight bow in acknowledgment of her question.
