He was smiling to himself more grimly than was his wont, for he had before him the recollection of the Lord of Stoutenburg﻿—cruel, coarse, and evil, bullying and striking a woman﻿—and of Nicolaes Beresteyn﻿—callous and cynical, bartering his sister’s honour and safety to ensure his own. To the one she had plighted her troth, the other was her natural protector, dear to her through those sweet bonds of childhood which bind brother and sister in such close affection. Yet both are selfish, unscrupulous rogues, thought the philosopher, though both very dear to her, and both honest men in her sight.
“That being so, sir,” she resumed once more, “meseems that you should be equally ready to do me service and to ask me no questions, provided that I pay you well.”
“That, mejuffrouw,” he said quietly, “would depend on the nature of the service.”
“It is quite simple, sir. Let me explain. While my woman and I were having supper upstairs, the wench who served us fell to gossiping, telling us the various news of the day which have filtered through into Rotterdam. Among other less important matters, sir, she told us that the Prince of Orange had left his camp at Sprang in order to journey northwards to Amsterdam. Yesterday he and his escort of one hundred men-at-arms passed close to this city; they were making for Delft where the Prince means to spend a day or two before proceeding further on his journey. He sleeps at the Prinzenhof in Delft this night.”
“Yes, mejuffrouw?” he said, for suddenly her manner had changed; something of its coolness had gone from it, even if the pride was still there. While she spoke a warm tinge of pink flooded her cheeks; she was leaning forward, her eyes bright and glowing were fixed upon him with a look of eagerness and almost of appeal, and her lips were moist and trembling, whilst the words which she wished to speak seemed to be dying in her throat.
“What hath the progress of the Prince of Orange to do with your most humble and most obedient servant?” he asked again.
“I must speak with the Prince of Orange, sir,” she said while her voice now soft and mellow fell almost like a prayer on his ear. “I must go to him to Delft not later than tomorrow. Oh! you will not refuse me this﻿ ﻿… you cannot﻿ ﻿… I.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

这一回，第欧根尼只微微一躬，算作对她问话的应承。他笑得比往常冷了几分：他眼前浮着斯陶滕堡大人——残忍、粗鄙、邪恶，欺凌一个女人，动手打一个女人——又浮着尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦——凉血、玩世，拿妹妹的名节和安危做抵押，保他自己的。一个，她许过终身；另一个，是她天经地义的保护人，凭童年那把兄妹系得蜜甜的纽带，得她亲爱的。可两个都是自私自利、无法无天的恶棍，哲学家想——虽然两个都得她深爱，两个在她眼里都是正人君子。
“既然如此，先生，”她又接上，“那么依我看，只要我给的钱足，你也该照样乐意为为我效劳，照样不问一句。”
“那要看效劳的性质了，小姐。”他平静地说。
“简单得很，先生。听我说。我和女仆在楼上用晚饭时，侍候我们的那个丫头闲聊起来，把当日漏进鹿特丹的种种新闻讲给我们听。在那些不要紧的事里头，先生，她说奥兰治亲王已离开斯普朗的营盘，要北上去阿姆斯特丹。昨天，他带着百人卫队从本城近旁经过；他们要去代尔夫特，亲王打算在那儿盘桓一两日，再继续赶路。今夜他就宿在代尔夫特的普林岑霍夫。”
“是吗，小姐？”他说，因为她的神气忽然变了；那份冷淡去了几分，尽管骄傲还在。说话间，一片热腾腾的红晕涌上她的双颊；她身子前倾，明亮灼灼的眼睛盯住他，那眼神急切，几乎是恳求；嘴唇湿润，微微发颤，想出口的话，却仿佛正死在她的喉咙里。
“奥兰治亲王的行程，与您最卑贱最驯顺的仆人有什么相干？”他又问。
“我必须同奥兰治亲王说上话，先生。”她说，那嗓音此刻又软又润，落在他耳里，几乎像一句祷词。“我必须去代尔夫特见他，最迟明天。哦！这个你不会拒绝我的……你不能……我……”

===Original===

She had clasped her hands together, her eyes were wet with tears, and as she pleaded, she bent forward so low in her chair, that it seemed for a moment as if her knees would touch the ground. In the flickering candlelight she looked divinely pretty thus, with all the cold air of pride gone from her childlike face. A gentle draught stirred the fair curls round her head, the fur cloak had completely slipped down from her shoulders and her white dress gave her more than ever the air of that Madonna carved in marble which he had seen once in the cathedral at Prague.
The philosopher passed a decidedly shaking hand across his forehead: the room was beginning to whirl round him, the floor to give way under his feet. He fell to thinking that the mild ale offered to him by Ben Isaje had been more heady than he had thought.
“St. Bavon,” he murmured to himself, “where in Heaven’s name are ye now? I asked you to stand by me.”
It was one of those moments﻿—perfect in themselves﻿—when a man can forget everything that pertains to the outer world, when neither self-interest nor ordinary prudence will count, when he is ready to jeopardize his life, his career, his future, his very soul for the ecstasy which lies in the one heaven-born minute. Thus it was with this philosopher, this man of the moment, the adventurer, the soldier of fortune; the world which he had meant to conquer, the fortune which he had vowed to win seemed to slip absolutely away from him. This dream﻿—for it was after all only a dream, it was just too beautiful to be reality﻿—the continuance of this dream seemed to him to be all that mattered, this girl﻿—proud and pleading﻿—a Madonna, a saint, a child of innocence, was the only perfect, desirable entity in this universe.
“St. Bavon, you rogue! you are playing me false!” he murmured, as the last vestige of self-control and of prudence threatened to fall away from him.
“Madonna,” he said as with a quick movement he came forward and bent the knee before her, “I entreat you to believe that whatever lies in my power to do in your service, that will I gladly do. How can I refuse,” he added whilst that immutable smile, gentle, humorous, faintly ironical, once more lit up his face as he looked straight into hers, “how can I refuse to obey since you deign to plead to me with those lips? how can I withstand your appeal when it speaks to me through your eyes?”

===Chinese===

她双手紧紧交握，眼里噙着泪，一面恳求，一面在椅子里俯得那样低，有一瞬，膝盖像是要触到地面。摇曳的烛光里，她这副模样美得出尘：孩子气的脸上，那层冷冷的傲气全褪尽了。一阵轻风拂动她头四周浅色的鬈发，皮氅已整个滑下肩头，那身白裙，使她比先前更像他曾在布拉格大教堂见过的一尊大理石雕刻的圣母。
哲学家抬手抹过额头，那只手分明在抖：屋子开始绕着他打转，地板开始从脚下陷落。他转念想，本·伊萨耶给他上的那温和麦酒，后劲比他以为的冲。
“圣巴沃，”他哺哺自语，“你究竟在哪里，看在上天份上？我叫你帮我站着的。”
那是这样一种时刻——自身即是完满——人可以忘掉外部世界的一切，利害也好，寻常的审慎也好，统统不作数了；为了蕴藏在这天赐一分钟里的狂喜，他甘愿押上性命、前程、将来，乃至灵魂。这位哲学家，这个活在当下的人、冒险家、亡命之徒，此刻正是如此：他立志要征服的世界，他发誓要赢取的富贵，仿佛从他身边整个溜走了。这个梦——它终归只是个梦，美得不像真的——这个梦的延续，在他看来便是唯一的要紧；这个姑娘——又骄傲又恳求——一位圣母，一位圣徒，一个无辜的孩子，是这宇宙间唯一完美、唯一可欲的存在。
“圣巴沃，你这无赖！你倒出卖起我来了！”他哺喃道，最后一点自制和审慎眼看也要弃他而去。
“圣母。”他一个箭步上前，在她面前屈膝跪下，说，“我恳求你相信：凡是我力所能及、于你有用的，我都甘愿去做。我怎能拒绝，”他直直望进她的眼睛，那副恒定的微笑——温柔、谐谑、微带讥诮——又照亮了他的脸，“你既屈尊用这双唇向我恳求，我怎能拒绝服从？你的恳求既借这双眼睛向我说话，我怎能抵御？”

===Original===

“You will let me do what I ask?” she exclaimed with a little cry of joy, for his attitude was very humble and his voice yielding and kind; he was kneeling at some little distance from her, which was quite becoming in a mercenary knave.
“If it be in my power, Madonna!” he said simply.
“Then will I pay you well,” she continued eagerly. “I have thought it all out. I am rich you know, and my bond is as good as that of any man. Do you but bring me inkhorn and paper, I will give you a bond for 4,000 guilders on Mynheer Ben Isaje himself, he hath monies of mine own in trust and at interest. But if 4,000 guilders are not enough, I pray you name your price; it shall be what you ask.”
“What do you desire me to do, Madonna?”
“I desire you to escort me to Delft so that I may speak with the Prince of Orange.”
“The Prince of Orange is well guarded. No stranger is allowed to enter his presence.”
“I am not a stranger to him. My father is his friend; a word from me to him, a ring of mine sent in with a request for an audience and he will not refuse.”
“And having entered the presence of the Stadtholder, mejuffrouw, what do you propose to say to him?”
“That, sir, is naught to you,” she retorted coldly.
“I pray you forgive me,” he said, still humbly kneeling, “but you have deigned to ask my help, and I’ll not give it unless you will tell me what your purpose is.”
“You would not dare.﻿ ﻿…”
“To make conditions for my services?” he said speaking always with utmost deference, “this do I dare, mejuffrouw, and my condition is for your acceptance or refusal﻿—as you command.”
“I did not ask for your help, sir,” she said curtly. “I offered to pay you for certain services which I desire you to render me.”
Already her look of pleading had gone. She had straightened herself up, prouder and more disdainful than before. He dared to make conditions! he! the mercenary creature whom anyone could buy body and soul for money, who took payment for doing such work as would soil an honest man’s hands! It was monstrous! impossible, unthinkable. She thought that her ears had deceived her or that mayhap he had misunderstood.
In a moment at her words, at the scornful glance which accompanied them, he had risen to his feet. The subtle moment had gone by; the air was no longer oppressive, and the ground felt quite steady under him.

===Chinese===

“你肯依我所求了？”她欢喜得轻轻叫出声，因为他的姿态谦卑之极，声音又温顺又和善；他跪在离她稍远的地方，对一个拿钱卖命的无赖来说，这份礼数再得体不过。
“只要力所能及，圣母！”他简单地说。
“那我必重重谢你。”她急切地接着说。“我都盘算好了。你知道，我是有钱的，我的字据同任何男人的一样作数。你只消给我取来墨水瓶和纸，我便给你开一张四千盾的票，就开在本·伊萨耶先生名下——他手里存有我的银子，放着生息。四千盾若不够，请你开个价；你要多少，便是多少。”
“你要我做什么，圣母？”
“我要你护送我去代尔夫特，好让我同奥兰治亲王说上话。”
“奥兰治亲王防卫森严。生人不许进他的驾前。”
“我于他不是生人。我父亲是他的朋友；有我一封口信，或把我的戒指递进去求见，他不会拒绝。”
“那么见了执政之后呢，小姐，你打算对他说什么？”
“那个，先生，与你无关。”她冷冷回敬。
“请你恕罪。”他仍旧谦卑地跪着，“可你既屈尊求我相助，你若不肯告诉我你的目的，我便不给。”
“你不敢。……”
“替效劳讲条件？”他说，口气始终恭敬到了极处，“这个我敢，小姐；条件就摆在这儿，应不应允——但凭吩咐。”
“我没有求你帮忙，先生。”她简短地说。“我是出钱，买你替我办几件事。”
她脸上恳求的神色雺时没了。她重新挺直身子，比先前更傲、更鄙夷。他竟敢讲条件！他！这么个有钱就能连身带魂买走的货色，干的是正派人沾手都要脏了手的活计！荒唐！不可能，不可想象。她疑心自己耳朵听岔了，或是他没听懂。
她的话音刚落，那轻蔑的一瞥扫过来，他已站起身。那个微妙的时刻过去了；空气不再压人，脚下的大地又稳稳当当。

===Original===

Calm, smiling, good-tempered, he straightened out his massive figure as if to prepare himself for those shafts which her cruel little tongue knew so well how to deal.
And inwardly he offered up a thanksgiving to St. Bavon for this cold douche upon his flaming temper.
“I did not misunderstand you, mejuffrouw,” he said lightly, “and I am ready to do you service﻿—under a certain condition.”
She bit her lip with vexation. The miserable wretch was obviously not satisfied with the amount which she had named as payment for his services, and he played some weak part of chivalry and of honour in order to make his work appear more difficult, and to extract a more substantial reward from her. She tried to put into the glance which she now threw on him all the contempt which she felt and which truly nauseated her at this moment. Unfortunately she had need of him, she could not start for Delft alone, marauders and footpads would stop her ever reaching that city. Could she have gone alone she were not here now craving the help of a man whom she despised.
“Meseems,” she said coldly after a slight pause, “that you do wilfully misunderstand our mutual positions. I am not asking you to do anything which could offend your strangely susceptible honour, whose vagaries, I own, I am unable to follow. Will 10,000 guilders satisfy your erratic conscience? or did you receive more than that for laying hands on two helpless women and dragging one﻿—who has never done you any wrong﻿—to a depth of shame and sorrow which you cannot possibly fathom?”
“My conscience, mejuffrouw,” he replied, seemingly quite unperturbed at her contemptuous glance and insulting speech, “is, as you say, somewhat erratic. For the moment it refuses to consider the possibility of escorting you to Delft unless I know what it is that you desire to say to the Prince of Orange.”
“If it is a question of price.﻿ ﻿…”
“It is not a question of price, mejuffrouw,” he broke in firmly, “let us, an you will allow it, call it a question of mine erratic conscience.”
“I am rich, sir﻿ ﻿… my private fortune.﻿ ﻿…”
“Do not name it, mejuffrouw,” he said jovially, “the sound of it would stagger a poor man who has to scrape up a living as best he can.”
“Forty thousand guilders, sir,” she said pleading once more eagerly, “an you will take me to Delft tomorrow.”

===Chinese===

他平静地、微笑着、好脾气地舒展开魁伟的身量，仿佛在备战——她的利舌最会放的那几支毒箭。
心里却在感谢圣巴沃：这一瓢冷水，浇得好，浇灭了他烧上来的火气。
“我没有听错你的话，小姐。”他轻快地说，“我准备效劳——只在一项条件之下。”
她懊恼地咬住嘴唇。这卑贱的货色，分明是嫌她开的价不够，于是演一出骑士气、荣誉感的空戏，好把他的差事说得难上加难，从她身上再多榨几个钱。她此刻投给他的那一眼，竭力装满她心里的、此刻真叫她作呕的鄙夷。可惜她用得着他：她孤身去不成代尔夫特，半路的劫匪剪径的就会截住她，叫她永远到不了那座城。她若能独行，此刻又何必来求一个她所不齿的男人。
“依我看，”她略停了停，冷冷地说，“你是成心曲解你我的处境。我并没有要你做什么冒犯你那奇敏感的荣誉的事——它那些反复无常，我承认，我实在跟不上。一万盾，可够安抚你那善变的良心？还是说，你对两个手无寸铁的妇人下手、把其中一个——一个从不曾亏待过你的人——拖进你这辈子也无法测知的耻辱和愁苦的深渊，人家给你的，比这还多？”
“我的良心么，小姐，”他答道，对她轻蔑的眼色和侮辱的话像是浑然不觉，“正如您说，是有些善变。眼下它不肯考虑护送您去代尔夫特这回事——除非我知道，您想对奥兰治亲王说的是什么。”
“若这是价钱问题……”
“这不是价钱问题，小姐。”他断然插进，“您若容许，就称它一个名目：我那善变良心的问题。”
“我有钱，先生……我的私财……”
“千万别报数，小姐。”他乐呵呵地说，“那数目声一响，一个只好四处刮饭吃的穷人怕要站不稳。”
“四万盾，先生。”她又急切地恳求起来，“只要你明天带我去代尔夫特。”

===Original===

“Were it ten hundred thousand, mejuffrouw, I would not do it unless I knew what you wished to say to the Stadtholder.”
“Sir, can I not move you,” she implored, “this means more to me than I can hope to tell you.” Once again her pride had given way before this new and awful fear that her errand would be in vain, that she had come here as a suppliant before this rogue, that she had humbled her dignity, entreated him, almost knelt to him, and that he, for some base reason which she could not understand, meant to give himself the satisfaction of refusing the fortune which she did promise him.
“Can I not move you,” she reiterated, appealing yet more earnestly, for, womanlike, she could not forget that moment awhile ago, when he had knelt instinctively before her, when the irony had gone from his smile, and the laughter in his mocking eyes had yielded to an inward glow.
He shook his head, but remained unmoved.
“I cannot tell you, sir,” she urged plaintively, “what I would say to the Prince.”
“Is it so deadly a secret then?” he asked.
“Call it that, an you will.”
“A secret that concerns his life?”
“That I did not say.”
“No. It was a guess. A right one methinks.”
“Then if you think so, sir, why not let me go to him?”
“So that you may warn him?”
“You were merely guessing, sir.﻿ ﻿…”
“That you may tell him not to continue his journey,” he insisted, speaking less restrainedly now, as he leaned forward closer to her, her fair curls almost brushing against his cheek as they fluttered in the draught.
“I did not say so,” she murmured.
“Because there is a trap laid for him﻿ ﻿… a trap of which you know.﻿ ﻿…”
“No, no!” she cried involuntarily.
“A trap into which he may fall﻿ ﻿… unknowingly﻿ ﻿… on his way to the north.”
“You say so, sir,” she moaned, “not I.﻿ ﻿…”
“Assassins are on his track﻿ ﻿… an attempt will be made against his life﻿ ﻿… the murderers lie in wait for him﻿ ﻿… even now﻿ ﻿… and you, mejuffrouw, who know who those murderers are﻿ ﻿…”
A cry of anguish rose to her lips.
“No, no, no,” she cried, “it is false﻿ ﻿… you are only guessing﻿ ﻿… remember that I have told you nothing.”
But already the tense expression on his face had gone. He drew himself up to his full height once more and heaved a deep breath which sounded like a sigh of satisfaction.
“Yet in your candour, mejuffrouw, you have told me much,” he said quietly, “confirmed much that I only vaguely guessed.

===Chinese===

“便是一百万，小姐，我不知道你想对执政说什么，也照样不干。”
“先生，我难道说不动你吗？”她哀求道，“这件事对我的分量，我说不尽。”她的骄傲又一次败下阵来，败给一种新的、可怕的恐惧：怕她这一趟徒劳；怕她竟跑来对这个恶棍摇尾乞怜；怕她折了尊严，求了他，几乎跪了他，而他，为着某个她无法理解的卑鄙缘由，竟要享受一番回绝她亲口许下的巨款的快乐。
“我难道说不动你吗？”她又恳求了一遍，愈发恳切——女人的心性，她忘不了方才那一刻：他情不自禁跪在她面前，笑意里讥诮尽褪，嘲弄眼中的笑光，让位给一种内里的热。
他摇了摇头，不为所动。
“我不能告诉你，先生。”她哀哀地恳求，“我要对亲王说的话。”
“竟是那样要命的机密？”他问。
“你愿意，就这么叫吧。”
“一件关他性命的机密？”
“我没这么说。”
“对。是我猜的。我看猜对了。”
“既然先生这么想，为何不放我去见他？”
“好教你去向他报信？”
“你不过是瞎猜，先生。……”
“好教你去劝他中止行程。”他咬定不放，说话也放开了些，身子朝她俯得更近，穿堂风里她浅色的鬈发飘起来，几乎扫上他的脸颊。
“我没这么说。”她哺喃道。
“因为有人给他布了陷阱……一个你知情的陷阱……”
“不，不！”她不由自主地叫起来。
“一个他也许会跌进去的陷阱……不知不觉地……在北去的路上。”
“是你说的，先生。”她呜咽道，“不是我。……”
“刺客已盯上他了……有人要对他下手……凶手们正埋伏着候他……就在此刻……而你，小姐，你知道那些凶手是谁……”
一声惨叫涌到她唇边。
“不，不，不。”她喊道，“不是的……你只是瞎猜……记住，我什么也没告诉你。”
可他脸上紧张的神色已经褪去。他重新站直了全身，深深吸了一口气，听着像一声满意的叹息。
“然而小姐，你的坦白已经告诉了我许多。”他静静地说，“把我只隐隐猜到的，坐实了许多。”

===Original===

The Stadtholder’s life is in peril and you hold in your feeble little hands the threads of the conspiracy which threatens him﻿ ﻿… is that not why you are here, mejuffrouw﻿ ﻿… a prisoner, as you say, at the goodwill of my employer? I am only guessing, remember, but on your face, meseems that I can read that I do guess aright.”
“Then you will do what I ask?” she exclaimed with a happy little gasp of renewed hope.
“That, mejuffrouw, is I fear me impossible,” he said quietly.
“Impossible? But﻿—just now﻿ ﻿…”
“Just now,” he rejoined with affected carelessness, “I said, mejuffrouw, that I would on no account escort you to Delft without knowing what your purpose is with the Prince of Orange. Even now I do not know, I merely guessed.”
“But,” she entreated, “if I do own that you have guessed aright﻿—partly at any rate﻿—if I do tell you that the Stadtholder’s life might be imperilled if I did not give him a timely word of warning, if﻿ ﻿…”
“Even if you told me all that, mejuffrouw,” he broke in lightly, “if you did bring your pride down so far as to trust a miserable knave with a secret which he might sell for money on the morrow﻿—even then, I fear me, I could not do what you ask.”
“But why not?” she insisted, her voice choking in her throat in the agony of terrible doubt and fear.
“Because the man of whom you spoke just now, the man whom you love, mejuffrouw, has been more farseeing, more prudent than you or I. He hath put it out of my power to render you this service.”
“How?”
“By warning Mynheer Ben Isaje against any attempt at escape on your part, against any attempt at betrayal on mine. Mynheer Ben Isaje is prepared: he hath a guard of ten picked men on the watch, and two more men outside his door. If you tried to leave this house with me without his consent he would prevent you, and I am no match alas! for twelve men.”
“Why should he guard me so?”
“Because he will not be paid if he keep not watch over you.”
“But I’ll swear to return straightway from Delft. I’ll only speak with the Prince and return immediately.﻿ ﻿… Money! always money!” she cried with sudden vehemence, “a great man’s life, the honour of a house, the salvation of the land, are these all to be sacrificed because of the greed and cupidity of men?”
“Shall I call Mynheer Ben Isaje?” asked Diogenes placidly, “mayhap, mejuffrouw, that you could persuade him more easily than me!”

===Chinese===

“执政的性命悬于一线，而那桩威胁他的阴谋的线头，正攥在你柔弱的小手里……这不正是你来这里的缘故吗，小姐……如你所说，凭我东家的意旨做囚徒？记住，我只是在猜——可照你的脸色看，我猜得不错。”
“那么你肯依我所求了？”她轻呼一声，重新燃起的希望叫她透不过气。
“那个，小姐，我怕办不到。”他平静地说。
“办不到？可是——方才……”
“方才么，”他装出漫不经心的样子应道，“我说的是：不知道你对奥兰治亲王打什么主意，我无论如何不能护送你去代尔夫特。此刻我仍旧不知道，我只是猜。”
“可是，”她哀求道，“若我承认你猜对了——至少对了一半——若我告诉你：我若不及时向执政递一句警讯，他的性命就有危险，倘若……”
“就算你把这些全告诉我，小姐，”他轻巧地打断，“就算你把骄傲压到这地步，把一件他明天就可能换钱的机密，托付给一个下贱的无赖——就算那样，我怕也办不成你所求的。”
“可为什么不能？”她追问，喉咙里堵着声音，在那可怕的疑惧的煎灼里。
“因为方才你说的那个人，你爱的那个人，小姐，比你我都看得远、都谨慎。他已叫我无力为你效这个劳。”
“怎么无力？”
“他提醒了本·伊萨耶先生：防你逃跑，防我卖他。本·伊萨耶先生早有布置：十名精壮看守守夜，门外还添两人。没有他的首肯，你想同我离开这座宅子，他会拦下你；而我，唉！不是十二个人的对手。”
“他凭什么这样看管我？”
“因为他看不住你，就拿不到钱。”
“可我可以发誓，从代尔夫特立刻回来。我只同亲王说一句话，马上就回来。……钱！张口钱闭口钱！”她忽然激烈地喊起来，“一条伟人的性命，一户门第的名节，一国的存亡，难道都要拿去喂男人们的贪得无厌？”
“要不要我把本·伊萨耶先生请来？”第欧根尼平心静气地问，“兴许，小姐，他说服起来比我容易！”

===Original===

But at this she rose to her feet as suddenly as if she had been stung: the colour in her cheeks deepened, the tears were dry in her eyes.
“You,” she exclaimed, and there was a world of bitter contempt in the tone of her voice, “persuade you who have tricked and fooled me, even while I began to believe in you? You, who for the past half hour have tried to filch a secret from me bit by bit! with lying words you led me into telling you even more than I should! and I, poor fool I thought that I had touched your heart, or that at least there was some spark of loyalty in you which mayhap prompted you to guess that the Prince was in danger. Fool that I was! miserable, wretched fool! to think for a moment that you would lend a hand in aught that was noble and chivalrous! I would I had the power to raise the blush of shame in your cheeks, but alas! the shame is only for me, who trusting in your false promises and your lies have allowed my tongue to speak words which I would give my life now to unsay﻿—for me who thought that there was in you one feeble spark of pity or of honour. Fool! fool that I was! when I forgot for one brief moment that it was your greed and cupidity that were the props without which this whole edifice of infamy had tottered long ago; persuade you to do a selfish deed! you the abductor of women, the paid varlet and mercenary rogue who will thieve and outrage and murder for money!”
She sank back in her chair and, resting her arms upon the table, she buried her face in them, for she had given way at last to a passionate fit of weeping. The disappointment was greater than she could bear after the load of sorrow which had been laid on her these past few days.
When she heard through the chatterings of a servant that the Stadtholder was at Delft this very night, the memory of every word which she had heard in the cathedral on New Year’s Eve came back to her with renewed vividness. Delft! she remembered that name so well and Ryswyk close by, the only possible way for a northward journey! Then the molens which Stoutenburg had said were his headquarters, where he stored arms and ammunition and enough gunpowder to blow up the wooden bridge which spans the Schie and over which the Stadtholder and his bodyguard must pass.

===Chinese===

这话像针一样扎了她一下，她雺地站起来：脸上的红晕深了，眼里的泪却干了。
“你！”她喊道，声音里浸透了辛酸的轻蔑，“说服你？你骗了我、耍了我，偏偏在我刚开始信你的时候！你，这半个钟头一直在一点一点从我嘴里偷机密！拿谎话引我，说得比该说的还多！而我，可怜的傻子，还以为我触到了你的心，或者你身上至少还有一星忠义，兴许是它提点你猜到亲王有险！我多傻！下贱、可怜的傻子！竟有一瞬间以为你肯搭一把手，做一桩高贵侠义的事！但愿我有本事叫你脸上烧起羞愧的红——可惜！羞愧只归我：我信了你虚假的许诺、你的谎，由着舌头说出了如今我拿命去换也收不回的话——归我，这个以为你身上还有一星半点怜悯或荣誉的人。傻！我真傻！我竟有一小会儿忘了：正是你的贪婪，撑着这座无耻大厦，没它，早就塌了！说服你去行一件无私的壮举！你——劫掳女人的恶棍，拿钱卖命的仆厕，为了钱肯偷、肯奸、肯杀的雇佣无赖！”
她跌回椅子里，两条手臂架在桌上，把脸埋了进去——她终于绷不住，恸哭起来。连日压在她身上的悲苦之外，又添上这一场失望，她受不住了。
从仆妇的闲话里听说执政今夜就在代尔夫特，她在除夕大教堂里听见的每一个字，便重新历历在目地涌了回来。代尔夫特！这名字她记得多牢，还有近旁的赖斯韦克——北行唯一的必经之路！还有斯陶滕堡说过的那几座风车磨坊，他的大本营：里面囤着刀枪弹药，囤着足以炸塌斯希河木桥的火药——执政和他的卫队，非从那桥上过不可。

===Original===

Every word that Stoutenburg and her brother and the others had spoken that night, rang now in her ears like a knell: Delft, Ryswyk, the molens, the wooden bridge! Delft, Ryswyk, the molens, the wooden bridge! Delft.﻿ ﻿…
Delft was quite near, less than four leagues away﻿ ﻿… the Stadtholder was there now﻿ ﻿… he could be warned before it was too late﻿ ﻿… and she could warn him without compromising her brother and his friends.﻿ ﻿… Then it was that she remembered that in the room below there slept a knave who would do anything for gold.
Thus she had run down to him full of eagerness and full of hope. And now he had refused to help her, and worse still had guessed at a secret which, if he bartered or sold it, meant death to her brother and his friends.
Contempt and hate had broken down her spirit. Smothering both, she was even now ready to fall on her knees, to plead with him, to pray, to implore﻿ ﻿… if only that could have moved him﻿ ﻿… if only it meant safety for the Stadtholder, and not merely a useless loss of pride and of dignity.
Anger and misery and utter hopelessness! they were causing her tears, and she hated this man who had her in his power and mocked her in her misery: and there was the awful thought that the Stadtholder was so near﻿—less than four leagues away! Why! had she been free she could have run all the way to him﻿—that hideous crime, that appalling tragedy in which her brother would bear a hand, could be averted even now if she were free! Oh! the misery of it! the awful, wretched helplessness! in a few days﻿—hours mayhap﻿—the Stadtholder would be walking straight into the trap which his murderers had set for him﻿ ﻿… the broken bridge! the explosion! the assassin at the carriage door! She saw it all as in a vision of the future, and her brother in the midst of it all with hands deeply stained in blood.
And she could avert it all﻿—the crime, the sorrow, the awful, hideous shame if only she were free.
She looked up at last, ashamed of her tears, ashamed that a rogue should have seen how keenly she suffered.
She looked up and turned to him once more. The flickering light of the candles fell full upon his splendid figure and upon his face: it was the colour of ashes, and there was no trace of his wonted smile around his lips: the eyes too looked sunken and their light was hid beneath the drooping lids.

===Chinese===

那晚斯陶滕堡、她哥哥和其他人说的每一句话，此刻都在她耳边敲着丧钟：代尔夫特，赖斯韦克，风车磨坊，木桥！代尔夫特，赖斯韦克，风车磨坊，木桥！代尔夫特……
代尔夫特很近，不到四里格……执政此刻就在那里……还来得及报信……而且她报了信，也不必牵累她哥哥和他的朋友……于是她想起，楼下那间屋里，睡着一个为了金子什么都肯干的无赖。
她就这样满怀着热望跑下楼来找他。如今他拒绝了帮她，更糟的是，还猜着了一桩机密——那机密他若拿去交易、拿去卖，便是她哥哥和他朋友们的死路。
轻蔑和恨已经压垮了她的心气。可把这两样都掐死，她此刻甚至愿意跪下去，求他、祷告他、哀恳他……只要那能感动他……只要那意味着执政的安全，而不单单是白白折掉她的骄傲和尊严。
愤怒、悲苦、彻底的绝望！是它们逼出了她的眼泪；她恨这个攥着她命运、又在她苦难里嘲弄她的人；还有那个可怕的念头，可怕在执政近在咫尺——不到四里格！哎呀！她若自由，一路跑去都来得及——那桩骇人的罪行，那场她哥哥要插手的可怕惨剧，她若自由，此刻还来得及拦住！哦！这苦楚！这可怕、悲惨的无能为力！几天之内——兴许几个钟头——执政就会一头走进凶手们为他布好的陷阱……断桥！爆炸！车门口的刺客！她像看未来幻象一般看见了这一切，还看见她哥哥身处其间，双手浸透了血。
而这些她全能拦下——罪行、悲苦、那可怕骇人的耻辱——只要她自由。
她终于抬起头，为自己的眼泪羞愧，羞愧竟让一个无赖看见了她苦得多深。
她抬起头，再次转向他。摇曳的烛光正正落在他英挺的身量和那张脸上：脸是死灰色的，唇边寻不见惯常的笑；眼睛也陷了下去，光藏在低垂的眼睑底下。

===Original===

Her shafts which she had aimed with such deadly precision had gone home at last: in the bitterness of her heart she apparently had found words which had cut him like a lash.
Satisfied at least in this she rose to go.
“There is nothing more to say,” she said as calmly as she could, trying to still the quivering of her lips: “as you say, Mynheer Ben Isaje has carefully taken the measure of your valour and it cannot come up to a dozen picked men, even though life and honour, country and faith might demand at least an effort on their behalf. I pray you open the door. I would﻿—for mine own sake as well as your own﻿—that I had not thought of breaking in on your rest.”
Without a word he went to the door, and had his hand on the latch ready to obey her, when something in his placid attitude irritated her beyond endurance. Womanlike she was not yet satisfied: perhaps a thought of remorse at her cruelty fretted her, perhaps she pitied him in that he was so base.
Be that as it may, she spoke to him again:
“Have you nothing then to say?” she asked.
“What can I say, mejuffrouw?” he queried in reply, as the ghost of his wonted smile crept swiftly back into his pale face.
“Methought no man would care to be called a coward by a woman, and remain silent under the taunt.”
“You forget, mejuffrouw,” he retorted, “that I am so much less than a man﻿ ﻿… a menial, a rogue, a vagabond﻿—so base that not even the slightest fear of me did creep into your heart﻿ ﻿… you came to me, here, alone at dead of night with an appeal upon your lips, yet you were not afraid, then you struck me in the face like you would a dog with a whip, and you were no more afraid of me than of the dog whom you had thrashed. So base am I then that words of mine are not worthy of your ear. Whatever I said, I could not persuade you that for one man to measure his strength against twelve others were not an act of valour, but one of senseless foolishness. I might tell you that bravery lies oft in prudence but seldom in foolhardiness, but this I know you are not in a mood now to believe. I might even tell you,” he continued with a slight return to his wonted lighthearted carelessness, “I might tell you that certain acts of bravery cannot be accomplished without the intervention of protecting saints, and that I have found St.

===Chinese===

她瞄得那样准、放得那样狠的箭，终于射中了：苦毒的心竟给她挑出了字字如鞭的话。
至少这一层她满意了，便起身要走。
“没什么可说的了。”她尽量平静地说，竭力压住唇上的颤：“正如你说的，本·伊萨耶先生仔细量过你的胆量，敌不过一打精壮——哪怕性命和名节、家国和信仰都至少值得出一把力。请你开门。为了你，也为了我，我但愿不曾想到搅扰你的安眠。”
他一言不发走到门边，手已搭上门闩，正要依她——可他那份平静里有什么，刺激得她忍无可忍。女人的心性，她还不肯罢休：也许是残酷过后的悔意在啮她，也许是他这般下贱，反倒叫她可怜。
不管怎样，她又开了口：
“你就没什么要说的？”她问。
“我能说什么呢，小姐？”他应道，惯常笑容的幽魂，飞快地爬回他苍白的脸上。
“我还以为，天下没有哪个男人，肯被女人骂作懦夫还闷声受着。”
“您忘了，小姐，”他回敬道，“我远算不上一个男人……一个仆厕，一个无赖，一个游棍——下贱到您心里连怕我一丝一毫的念头都不曾有过……您来找我，到这里，深更半夜独自一人，唇上带着恳求，却不怕；随后您打我的脸，像拿鞭子抽一条狗，您不怕我，正如不怕一条挨了打的狗。我下贱到这地步，我的话自然不配入您的耳。我无论说什么，也说服不了您：一个人硬碰十二个人，不是勇，是没头脑的傻。我倒可以告诉您，勇敢常在审慎里，很少在蛮干里——可我知道，您此刻的心境不会信。我甚至可以告诉您，”他接着说，惯常那种轻快散漫微微回来了，“我可以告诉您，某些勇敢的壮举，离了护佑圣徒的搭手是办不成的，而我发现圣……

===Original===

Bavon an admirable saint to implore in such cases, but this I fear me you are not like to understand. So you see, mejuffrouw, that whatever I said I could not prove to you that I am less of a blackguard than I seem.”
“You could at least prove it to this extent,” she retorted, “by keeping silence over what you may have guessed.”
“You mean that I must not sell the secret which you so nearly betrayed﻿ ﻿… have no fear, mejuffrouw, my knowledge of it is so scanty that the Stadtholder would not give me five guilders for it.”
“Will you swear.﻿ ﻿…”
“Such a miserable cur as I am, mejuffrouw,” he said lightly, “is surely an oath-breaker as well as a liar and a thief﻿—what were the good of swearing?﻿ ﻿… But I’ll swear an you wish﻿ ﻿…” he added gaily.
“Surely you﻿ ﻿…” she began.
But with a quick gesture he interrupted her.
“Dondersteen, mejuffrouw,” he said more firmly than he had yet spoken before, “if beauty in you is tempered with pity, I entreat you to spare me now: even knaves remember become men sometimes and my patron Saint Bavon threatens to leave me in the lurch.”
He held open the door for her to pass through, and gravely held out one of the pewter candles to her. She could not help but take it, though indeed she felt that the last word between that rogue and herself had not by any means been spoken yet. But she hardly looked at him as she sailed past him out of the room, her heavy skirt trailing behind her with a soft hissing sound.
As soon as she heard the door shut to behind her, she ran up the stairs back to her own room with all speed, like a frightened hare.
Had she remained in the passage one instant longer she would have heard a sound which would have terrified her; it was the sound of a prolonged and ringing laugh which roused the echoes of this sleeping house, but which had neither mirth nor joy in its tone, and had she then peeped through a keyhole she would have seen a strange sight. A man who in the flickering candlelight looked tall and massive as a giant took up one of the wooden chairs in the room, and after holding it out at arm’s length for a few seconds, he proceeded to smash it viciously bit by bit until it lay a mass of broken debris at his feet.

===Chinese===

“……巴沃，在这类事上是个再好不过的求告对象——只是这个，我怕您未必能懂。所以您瞧，小姐，我无论说什么，也证明不了我比看上去少几分无赖。”
“至少有一层你证明得了。”她回敬道，“把你可能猜到的事，烂在肚里。”
“您是说我不得出卖那个您差一点就自己招了的机密……放心，小姐，我知道的那点皮毛，执政连五个盾也不肯出。”
“你肯起誓……”
“我这样一条下贱的癞皮狗，小姐，”他轻飘飘地说，“自然既是誓棍，又是谎精，又是贼——起誓有什么用？……不过您要起，我就起……”他乐呵呵地补了一句。
“你总不至于……”她刚开口。
可他一个疾速的手势把她截住了。
“雷公在上，小姐，”他说得比先前任何时候都硬，“您的美若掺得有一分怜惘，我求您此刻饶了我：无赖有时也会记起自己曾是人，而我的主保圣巴沃，眼看要撒手不管我了。”
他拉开门请她通过，又郑重地把一支白锡烛台递给她。她不能不接，虽然她分明觉得，这个无赖同她之间的最后一句，还远没有说。可她经过他身边飘然出屋时，几乎没看他一眼，厚重的裙裾拖在身后，发出轻柔的嘶嘶声。
一听见门在身后关拢，她便像只受惊的野兔，一口气飞跑回自己楼上房里。
她若在过道里再多留一瞬，就会听见一个准会吓坏她的声音：一阵悠长、洪亮的狂笑，震得这座睡宅四壁回响，笑声里却没有半分欢欣；她若再从钥匙孔里偷看一眼，还会看见一幅怪景：一条汉子，在摇曳烛光里高大得像巨人，抄起屋里一把木椅，直臂平举了几秒钟，随后狠狠地把它一点一点砸碎，直到它成为脚边一堆断木残片。

## XXXI The Molens / 三十一 磨坊
===Original===

Less than half a league to the southeast of Ryswyk﻿—there where the Schie makes a sharp curve up toward the north﻿—there is a solitary windmill﻿—strange in this, that it has no companions near it, but stands quite alone with its adjoining miller’s hut nestling close up against it like a tiny chick beside the mother hen, and dominates the mud flats and lean pastures which lie for many leagues around.
On this day which was the fourth of the New Year, these mud flats and the pasture land lay under a carpet of half-melted snow and ice which seemed to render the landscape more weird and desolate, and the molens itself more deserted and solitary. Yet less than half a league away the pointed gables and wooden spires of Ryswyk break the monotony of the horizon line and suggest the life and movement pertaining to a city, however small. But life and movement never seem to penetrate as far as this molens; they spread their way out toward ’S Graven Hage and the sea.
Nature herself hath decreed that the molens shall remain solitary and cut off from the busy world, for day after day and night after night throughout the year a mist rises from the mud flats around and envelops the molens as in a shroud. In winter the mist is frosty, in summer at times it is faintly tinged with gold, but it is always there and through it the rest of the living world﻿—Ryswyk and ’S Graven Hage and Delft further away only appear as visions on the other side of a veil.
Just opposite the molens, some two hundred paces away to the east, the waters of the Schie rush with unwonted swiftness round the curve; so swiftly in fact that the ice hardly ever forms a thick crust over them, and this portion of an otherwise excellent waterway is﻿—in the winter﻿—impracticable for sleighs.
Beyond this bend in the river, however, less than half a league away, there is a wooden bridge, wide and strongly built, across which it is quite easy for men and beasts to pass who have come from the south and desire to rejoin the great highway which leads from Delft to Leyden.
In the morning of that same fourth day in the New Year, two men sat together in what was once the weighing-room of the molens; their fur coats were wrapped closely round their shoulders, for a keen northwesterly wind had found its way through the chinks and cracks of tumbledown doors and ill-fitting window frames.

===Chinese===

在赖斯韦克东南不到半里格的地方——就在斯希河陡然折向北去的那个河湾上——立着一座孤零零的风车磨坊（molens）；它的奇特之处在于四邻无一同类，独自矗立，紧贴着它偎着一座磨坊主的小屋，像母鸡身边偎着的一只小鸡雏，俯瞰着四周绵延许多里格的泥滩和贫瘠牧场。
这一天是新年第四天，泥滩和牧地上覆着一层半融的雪和冰，仿佛使这景色愈发荒诞凄凉，也使这磨坊愈发显得冷落孤寂。然而不到半里格外，赖斯韦克尖尖的山墙和木造的尖顶划破了地平线的单调，透出一座城市——哪怕再小——该有的生机与动静。只是这生机与动静似乎从不肯光顾这座磨坊；它们一路铺展开去，奔向海牙和大海。
造化亲自注定这磨坊要永远孤悬于喧嚣人世之外：一年到头，日复一日，夜复一夜，雾气从四周的泥滩上升起，把磨坊裹进一袭裹尸布似的大氅。冬天的雾凝着霜，夏天的雾有时微微染上金色，可它无时不在；透过它望出去，其余那个活生生的世界——赖斯韦克、海牙，以及更远的代尔夫特（Delft）——都只像帷幕另一侧的幻影。
就在磨坊的正对面，往东约二百步开外，斯希河水绕着河湾奔流，湍急得异乎寻常；实在流得太急，冰面几乎从来结不厚，这条原本极好的水路，这一段在冬天竟连雪橇也走不得。
不过绕过这道河湾，不到半里格外有一座木桥，造得宽阔结实，从南边来的人马要重上代尔夫特通往莱顿（Leyden）的大道，打桥上过极为便当。
也就是新年第四天的早晨，两个人对坐在磨坊里一间昔日称重用的屋子里；皮袄紧裹在肩头，因为尖利的西北风正从破败门扇和歪斜窗框的缝隙里钻进来。

===Original===

Though a soft powdery veil﻿—smooth as velvet to the touch and made up of flour and fine dust﻿—lay over everything, and the dry, sweet smell of corn still hung in the close atmosphere, there was little else in this room now that suggested the peaceful use for which it had been originally intended.
The big weighing machines had been pushed into corners, and all round the sloping walls swords, cullivers and muskets were piled in orderly array, also a row of iron boxes standing a foot or so apart from one another and away from any other objects in the room.
The silence which reigned over the surrounding landscape did not find its kingdom inside this building, for a perpetual hum, a persistent buzzing noise as of bees in their hives, filtrated through the floor and the low ceiling of this room. Men moved and talked and laughed inside the molens, but the movement and the laughter were subdued as if muffled in that same mantle of mist which covered the outside world.
The two men in the weighing-room were sitting at a table on which were scattered papers, inkhorns and pens, a sword, a couple of pistols and two or three pairs of skates. One of them was leaning forward and talking eagerly:
“I think you can rest satisfied, my good Stoutenburg,” he said, “our preparations leave nothing to be desired. I have just seen Jan, and together we have despatched the man Lucas van Sparendam to Delft. He is the finest spy in the country, and can ferret out a plan or sift a rumour quicker than any man I know. He will remain at Delft and keep the Prinzenhof under observation: and will only leave the city if anything untoward should happen, and then he will come straight here and report to us. He is a splendid runner, and can easily cover the distance between Delft and this molens in an hour. That is satisfactory is it not?”
“Quite,” replied Stoutenburg curtly.
“Our arrangements here on the other hand are equally perfect,” resumed Beresteyn eagerly, “we have kept the whole thing in our own hands﻿ ﻿… Heemskerk and I will be at our posts ready to fire the gunpowder at the exact moment when the advance guard of the Prince’s escort will have gone over the bridge﻿ ﻿… you, dagger in hand, will be prepared to make a dash for the carriage itself﻿ ﻿… our men will attack the scattered and confused guard at a word from van Does.﻿ ﻿… What could be more simple, more perfect than that?

===Chinese===

屋里样样东西都蒙着一层细粉的软纱——摸上去滑如天鹅绒，是面粉和细尘织成的——闭塞的空气里还悬着谷物干燥的甜香；可除此之外，这间屋子如今已没有什么能让人想起它当初那桩安稳的营生。
大磅秤被推到了角落里，倾斜的四壁沿墙齐齐整整地码着剑、火绳枪和滑膛枪，还有一排铁箱，彼此相距一英尺上下，也不挨着屋里任何别的东西。
统领着四野的那种寂静，在这座建筑里却找不到它的王国：一种经久不息的嗡鸣，一种执拗的、像蜂箱里蜜蜂似的营营声，透过地板和这间屋子的低天花板渗上来。磨坊里有人在走动、说话、大笑，只是那动静和笑声都压得低低的，仿佛也裹上了罩住外面世界的那层雾的斗篷。
称重室里那两个人坐在一张桌前，桌上散乱摊着纸张、墨角瓶和笔，一口剑、两支手枪，还有两三双冰鞋。其中一人身子前倾，说得正劲：
“我看您可以放宽心了，好心的斯陶滕堡（Stoutenburg），”他说，“咱们的准备万无一失。我方才见过扬，我们一起派了卢卡斯·范·斯帕伦达姆那家伙去代尔夫特。他是全国首屈一指的探子，刺探一项密谋、筛拣一条风声，比我认得的任何人都快。他会守在代尔夫特，盯住普林森霍夫；除非出了岔子才离城，到那时他便径直来这儿向我们禀报。他是出色的飞毛腿，从代尔夫特到这座磨坊的路程，一个钟头轻轻松松就走完了。这总叫人满意了吧？”
“很好。”斯陶滕堡简短地答道。
“咱们这边的布置呢，同样十全十美，”贝雷斯坦（Beresteyn）热切地接着说，“整个事情都攥在咱们自己手里……海姆斯凯克和我各就各位，专候亲王卫队的前锋过桥的那一瞬，便点燃火药……您呢，短剑在手，只管朝马车扑过去……咱们的人听范多斯一声令下，就冲杀那四散慌乱的卫兵。……还有什么比这更简单、更完美的呢？

===Original===

Yourself, Heemskerk, van Does and I﻿ ﻿… all of one mind﻿ ﻿… all equally true, silent and determined.﻿ ﻿… You seem so restless and anxious.﻿ ﻿… Frankly I do not understand you.”
“It is not of our preparations or of our arrangements that I am thinking, Nicolaes,” said Stoutenburg sombrely, “these have been thought out well enough. Nothing but superhuman intervention or treachery can save the Stadtholder﻿—of that am I convinced. Neither God nor the devil care to interfere in men’s affairs﻿—we need not therefore fear superhuman intervention. But ’tis the thought of treachery that haunts me.”
“Bah!” quoth Beresteyn with a shrug of the shoulders, “you have made a nightmare of that thought. Treachery? there is no fear of treachery. Yourself, van Does, Heemskerk and I are the only ones who know anything at this moment of our plans for tomorrow. Do you suspect van Does of treachery, or Heemskerk, or me?”
“I was not thinking of Heemskerk or of van Does,” rejoined Stoutenburg, “and even our men will know nothing of the attack until the last moment. Danger, friend, doth not lie in or around the molens; it lurks at Rotterdam and hath name Gilda.”
“Gilda! What can you fear from Gilda now?”
“Everything. Have you never thought on it, friend? Jan, remember, lost track of that knave soon after he left Haarlem. At first he struck across the waterways in a southerly direction and for awhile Jan and the others were able to keep him in sight. But soon darkness settled in and along many intricate backwaters our rogue was able to give them the slip.”
“I know that,” rejoined Beresteyn somewhat impatiently. “I was here in the early morning when Jan reported to you. He also told you that he and his men pushed on as far as Leyden that night and regained the road to Rotterdam the following day. At Zegwaard and again at Zevenhuizen they ascertained that a party consisting of two women in a sledge and an escort of three cavaliers had halted for refreshments at those places and then continued their journey southwards. Since then Jan has found out definitely that Gilda and her escort arrived early last night at the house of Ben Isaje of Rotterdam, and he came straight on here to report to you. Frankly I see nothing in all this to cause you so much anxiety.”
“You think then that everything is for the best?”

===Chinese===

您自己、海姆斯凯克、范多斯，还有我……一条心……个个同样忠实、缄默、坚决。……可您看上去这么焦躁、这么忧心。……老实说，我不明白您。”
“我想的既不是咱们的准备，也不是咱们的布置，尼古拉斯（Nicolaes），”斯陶滕堡阴郁地说，“这些都盘算得够周全了。除了神力干预或者叛卖，什么也救不了执政（Stadtholder）——这一点我深信不疑。上帝也罢，魔鬼也罢，都懒得插手人间的事——所以神力干预，咱们不必怕。缠着我不放的，是叛卖这两个字。”
“呸！”贝雷斯坦耸耸肩道，“您把这点疑心养成一场噩梦了。叛卖？才不怕什么叛卖。眼下知道咱们明天计划的，只有您自己、范多斯、海姆斯凯克和我。您疑心范多斯叛卖，还是海姆斯凯克，还是我？”
“我想的既不是海姆斯凯克，也不是范多斯，”斯陶滕堡应道，“就连咱们的人，不到最后一刻也不会知道偷袭的事。危险，朋友，不在磨坊里，也不在磨坊四周；它潜伏在鹿特丹（Rotterdam），名字叫吉尔达（Gilda）。”
“吉尔达！事到如今，您还能怕吉尔达什么？”
“怕她什么都干得出来。朋友，您从没细想过吗？别忘了，那无赖一离开哈勒姆（Haarlem），扬就跟丢了他。起初他横穿水路往南去，有一阵子扬他们还盯得住他。可天很快黑透，那家伙顺着许多曲里拐弯的河汊，把他们甩了个干净。”
“这些我知道，”贝雷斯坦有些不耐烦地应道，“清早扬向您禀报的时候，我就在这儿。他还告诉了您，当夜他和手下一路追到莱顿，第二天又重新踏上通往鹿特丹的大道。在泽赫瓦德，后来又在泽芬赫伊曾，他们查明有一行人——雪橇上两位女眷，三骑护卫——曾在这两处歇脚打尖，随后继续南行。打那以后，扬确切查明，吉尔达和护卫一行昨儿一入夜就到了鹿特丹的本·伊萨耶（Ben Isaje）家，他便径直赶来向您禀报。老实说，这一切里头，我看不出哪一点值得您这样忧心。”
“那您是觉得事事都顺遂了？”

===Original===

asked Stoutenburg grimly, “you did not begin to wonder how it was that﻿—as Jan ascertained at Zegwaard and at Zevenhuizen﻿—Gilda continued her journey without any protest. According to the people whom Jan questioned she looked sad certainly, but she was always willing to restart on her way. What do you make of that, my friend?”
Once more Beresteyn shrugged his shoulders.
“Gilda is proud,” he said. “She hath resigned herself to her fate.”
Stoutenburg laughed aloud.
“How little you﻿—her own brother﻿—know her,” he retorted. “Gilda resigned? Gilda content to let events shape themselves﻿—such events as those which she heard us planning in the Groote Kerk on New Year’s Eve? Why, my friend, Gilda will never be resigned, she will never be content until she hath moved earth and heaven to save the Stadtholder from my avenging hand!”
“But what can she do now? Ben Isaje is honest in business matters. It would not pay him to play his customers false. And I have promised him two thousand guilders if he keeps her safely as a prisoner of war, not even to be let out on parole. Ben Isaje would not betray me. He is too shrewd for that.”
“That may be true of Ben Isaje himself; but what of his wife? his sons or daughters if he have any? his serving wenches, his apprentices and his men? How do you know that they are not amenable to promises of heavy bribes?”
“But even then﻿ ﻿…”
“Do you not think that at Rotterdam everyone by now knows the Prince’s movements? He passed within half a league of the town yesterday; there is not a serving wench in that city at this moment who does not know that Maurice of Nassau slept at Delft last night and will start northwards tomorrow.”
“And what of that?” queried Beresteyn, trying to keep up a semblance of that carelessness which he was far from feeling now.
“Do you believe then that Gilda will stay quietly in the house of Ben Isaje, knowing that the Prince is within four leagues of her door?﻿ ﻿… knowing that he will start northwards tomorrow﻿ ﻿… knowing that my headquarters are here﻿—close to Ryswyk﻿ ﻿… knowing in fact all that she knows?”
“I had not thought on all that,” murmured Beresteyn under his breath.
“And there is another danger too, friend, greater perhaps than any other,” continued Stoutenburg vehemently.
“Good God, Stoutenburg, what do you mean?”
“That cursed foreign adventurer﻿—”
“What about him?”

===Chinese===

斯陶滕堡冷冷地问，“您就不曾纳闷：为什么——正如扬在泽赫瓦德和泽芬赫伊曾查明的那样——吉尔达一路续行，竟没半句抗议？据扬问过的人说，她面色固然愁苦，可每到重新上路，她回回都是情愿的。朋友，这您怎么解释？”
贝雷斯坦又耸了耸肩。
“吉尔达心气高，”他说，“她认命了。”
斯陶滕堡放声大笑。
“您是她的亲哥哥，却这么不了解她，”他反驳道，“吉尔达认命？吉尔达听凭事态自行发展——听凭那样的事？除夕夜她在大教堂（Groote Kerk）里亲耳听见咱们谋划的那些事？咳，朋友，吉尔达永远不会认命，她不搬动天地、把执政从我复仇的手里救出去，就永远不会甘心！”
“可她如今还能怎么样？本·伊萨耶做生意讲信用，坑害主顾于他没有好处。我许了他两千盾（guilders），只要他把她当作战俘稳妥看管，连假释也不许。本·伊萨耶不会出卖我。他精明得很，不致如此。”
“本·伊萨耶本人或许如此；可他的老婆呢？他若儿女成群呢？他家的女仆、学徒和伙计们呢？您怎么知道重金许诺买不动他们？”
“可即便那样……”
“您不觉得，鹿特丹如今人人都在传亲王的行踪吗？昨天他从离城不到半里格的地方经过；这会儿那座城里没有一个女仆不知道：拿骚的莫里斯（Maurice of Nassau）昨夜宿在代尔夫特，明天就要动身北上。”
“那又怎样？”贝雷斯坦问，竭力维持一副满不在乎的做派——其实他心里早远不是那么回事了。
“那您信不信，吉尔达明知亲王离她家门不到四里格……明知他明天就要动身北上……明知我的大营就设在这里——紧挨着赖斯韦克……总之，明知她所知道的一切，还肯安安静静待在本·伊萨耶家里？”
“这些我倒没有细想，”贝雷斯坦压低嗓子咕哝。
“还有一重危险呢，朋友，兴许比哪一重都大，”斯陶滕堡激烈地接着说。
“天哪，斯陶滕堡，您指什么？”
“那个该死的外国冒险家——”
“他怎么啦？”

===Original===

“Have you then never thought of him as being amenable to a bribe from Gilda.”
“In Heaven’s name, man, do not think of such awful eventualities!”
“But we must think of them, my good Beresteyn. Events are shaping themselves differently to what we expected. We must make preparations for our safety accordingly, and above all realise the fact that Gilda will move heaven and earth to thwart us in our plans.”
“But she can do nothing,” persisted Beresteyn sullenly, “without betraying me. In Haarlem it was different. She might have spoken to my father of what she knew, but she would not do so to a stranger, knowing that with one word she can send me first and all of you afterwards to the scaffold.”
Stoutenburg with an exclamation of angry impatience brought his clenched fist crashing down upon the table.
“Are you a child, Beresteyn,” he cried hotly, “or are you wilfully blind to your danger and to mine? I tell you that Gilda will never allow me to kill the Prince of Orange without raising a finger to save him.”
“But what can I do?”
“Send for Gilda at once, tonight,” urged Stoutenburg, “convey her under escort hither﻿ ﻿… in all deference﻿ ﻿… in all honour﻿ ﻿… she would be here under her brother’s care.”
“A woman in this place at such a moment,” cried Beresteyn; “you are mad, Stoutenburg.”
“I shall go mad if she is not here,” rejoined the other more calmly, “the fear has entered into my soul, Nicolaes, that Gilda will yet betray us at the eleventh hour. That fear is an obsession﻿ ﻿… call it premonition if you will, but it unmans me, friend.”
Beresteyn was silent now. He drew his cloak closer round his shoulders, for suddenly he felt a chill which seemed to have crept into his bones.
“But it is unpractical, man,” he persisted with a kind of sullen despair. “Gilda and another woman here﻿ ﻿… tomorrow﻿ ﻿… when not half a league away.﻿ ﻿…”
“Justice will be meted out to a tyrant and an assassin,” broke in Stoutenburg quietly. “Gilda is not a woman as other women are, though in her soul now she may be shrinking at the thought of this summary justice, she will be strong and brave when the hour comes. In any case,” he added roughly, “we can keep her closely guarded, and in the miller’s hut, with the miller and his wife to look after her, she will be as safe and as comfortable as circumstances will allow.

===Chinese===

“您就从没想过，他也会吃吉尔达的一笔贿赂？”
“看在老天爷份上，伙计，别去想这种可怕的万一！”
“可咱们必须想，我的好贝雷斯坦。事态的走向同咱们原先料想的不一样了。咱们得为自身安全相机布置，更要紧的是认清一个事实：吉尔达会搬动天地来搅黄咱们的计划。”
“可她什么都做不成，”贝雷斯坦执拗地说，“除非出卖我。在哈勒姆就不同了。她本可以把知道的一切告诉我父亲，可她绝不会告诉外人——她明白，一句话就能先把我、再把你们所有人统统送上断头台。”
斯陶滕堡怒冲冲地一声呵斥，攥紧的拳头砰地砸在桌上。
“你是三岁孩子吗，贝雷斯坦，”他厉声嚷道，“还是存心闭眼不看你的危险、我的危险？我告诉你，吉尔达绝不会容我杀奥兰治亲王，而自己一根指头都不抬去救他。”
“可我能怎么办呢？”
“立刻派人去接吉尔达，就今晚，”斯陶滕堡力劝道，“派卫队把她护送到这儿来……执礼必恭……绝无半点委屈……她到了这儿，自有哥哥照看。”
“这种节骨眼上，让一个女人到这种地方来，”贝雷斯坦嚷道，“你疯了，斯陶滕堡。”
“她若不来这儿，我才要疯呢，”对方镇静了些，应道，“有一种恐惧已经钻进我的灵魂，尼古拉斯：吉尔达到了最后一刻，还是会出卖咱们。那恐惧成了魔障……你叫它预感也罢，反正它把我的胆气都磨没了，朋友。”
贝雷斯坦这下不作声了。他把斗篷往肩头拢得更紧些，因为他忽然觉出一股寒气，像是钻进了骨头。
“可这不切实际啊，伙计，”他带着一种阴沉沉的绝望硬撑着说，“吉尔达，还有另一个女人，到这儿来……明天……而事情就在不到半里格外。……”
“处决的是个暴君兼刺客，”斯陶滕堡平静地插进来，“吉尔达不是寻常女子，此刻她心里或许在这场雷厉风行的刑罚面前发怵，可时辰一到，她会坚强勇敢。再说了，”他粗声补了一句，“咱们可以把她严严实实地看管起来，安置在磨坊主的小屋里，有磨坊主和他的老婆照应，境况许可之内，她安全又舒坦。”

===Original===

We should have her then under our own eyes and know that she cannot betray us.”
As usual Beresteyn was already yielding to the stronger will, the more powerful personality of his friend. His association with Stoutenburg had gradually blunted his finer feelings; like a fly that is entangled in the web of a spider, he tried to fight against the network of intrigue and of cowardice which hemmed him in more and more closely with every step that he took along the path of crime. He was filled with remorse at thought of the wrong which he had done to Gilda, but he was no longer his own master. He was being carried away by the tide of intrigue and by the fear of discovery, away from his better self.
“You should have thought on all that sooner, Stoutenburg,” he said in final, feeble protest, “we need never have sent Gilda to Rotterdam in the company of a foreign adventurer of whom we knew nothing.”
“At the time it seemed simple enough,” quoth Stoutenburg impatiently, “you suggested the house of Ben Isaje the banker and it seemed an excellent plan. I did not think of distance then, and it is only since we arrived at Ryswyk that I realized how near all these places are to one another, and how easy it would be for Gilda to betray us even now.”
Beresteyn was silent after that. It was easy to see that his friend’s restless anxiety was eating into his own soul. Stoutenburg watched him with those hollow glowing eyes of his that seemed to send a magnetic current of strong willpower into the weaker vessel.
“Well! perhaps you are right,” said Beresteyn at last, “perhaps you are right. After all,” he added half to himself, “perhaps I shall feel easier in my conscience when I have Gilda near me and feel that I can at least watch over her.”
Stoutenburg, having gained his point, jumped to his feet and drew a deep breath of satisfaction.
“That’s bravely said,” he exclaimed. “Will you go yourself at once to Rotterdam? with two or three of our most trusted men you could bring Gilda here with absolute safety; you only need to make a slight detour when you near Delft so as to avoid the city. You could be here by six o’clock this evening at the latest, and Jan in the meanwhile with a contingent of our stalwarts shall try and find that abominable *plepshurk* again and bring him here too without delay.”
“No, no,” said Beresteyn quickly, “I’ll not go myself.

===Chinese===

“这样她便在咱们眼皮子底下，也就知道她决计出卖不了咱们。”
果然不出往常，贝雷斯坦又向朋友更坚强的意志、更强势的人格屈服了。同斯陶滕堡的交往，早已磨钝了他细腻的天性；他像一只缠在蛛网里的苍蝇，在罪孽的路上每走一步，便被那张阴谋与怯懦织成的罗网收得更紧一分，却还想挣扎。想起自己对吉尔达的亏负，他满心悔恨，可他已做不得自己的主了。阴谋的浊流和对败露的恐惧挟裹着他，把他从那个较好的自己身边越冲越远。
“这些你该早点想到，斯陶滕堡，”他作了最后一次有气无力的抗议，“咱们本不必让吉尔达跟着一个咱们一无所知的外国冒险家去鹿特丹。”
“当时看来的确简单，”斯陶滕堡不耐烦地说，“是你提议送去银行家本·伊萨耶家，那计划瞧着再好不过。那时我没把路程放在心上，直到到了赖斯韦克，我才明白这些地方彼此挨得有多近，也才明白吉尔达此刻想出卖咱们有多容易。”
此后贝雷斯坦沉默了。看得出，朋友那坐立不安的焦灼，正一点点啮进他自己的灵魂。斯陶滕堡用那双深陷灼亮的眼睛盯着他，那目光仿佛把一股强意志的磁流，注进这具更软弱的躯壳。
“唉！兴许你是对的，”贝雷斯坦终于开口，“兴许你是对的。说到底，”他半自语地补了一句，“吉尔达到了我身边，我觉得自己总算还能照看她一场，良心上兴许也好受些。”
目的既已达到，斯陶滕堡一跃而起，心满意足地深深吸了口气。
“这话才像句痛快话，”他叫道，“你这就亲自跑一趟鹿特丹？带上两三个咱们最信得过的人，万无一失地把吉尔达接来；只要临近代尔夫特时稍稍绕个弯，避开那座城就是。至迟今晚六点你就能回到这儿。这期间呢，让扬带一队咱们的好手，再去把那个可憎的下流坯（*plepshurk*）揪出来，也不许耽搁，一并带到这儿来。”
“不，不成，”贝雷斯坦急急道，“我不亲自去。”

===Original===

I could not bear to meet Gilda just yet. I will not have her think that I had a hand in her abduction and my presence might arouse her suspicions.”
Stoutenburg laughed unconcernedly.
“You would rather that she thought I had instigated the deed. Well!” he added with a careless shrug, “my shoulders are broad enough to bear the brunt of her wrath if she does. An you will not go yourself we will give full instructions to Jan. He shall bring Gilda and her woman hither with due respect and despatch, and lay the knave by the heels at the same time. Ten or a dozen of our men or even more can easily be spared today, there is really nothing for them to do, and they are best out of mischief by being kept busy. Now while I go to give Jan his instructions do you write a letter to Ben Isaje, telling him that it is your wish that Gilda should accompany the bearer of your sign-manual.”
“But.﻿ ﻿…”
“Tush, man!” exclaimed Stoutenburg impatiently, while a tone of contempt rang through his harsh voice, “you can so word the letter that even if it were found it need not compromise you in any way. You might just have discovered that your sister was in the hands of brigands, and be sending an escort to rescue her; Gilda will be grateful to you then and ready to believe in you. Write what you like, but for God’s sake write quickly. Every moment’s delay drives me well-nigh distraught.”
With jerky, feverish movements he pushed paper and inkhorn nearer to Beresteyn, who hesitated no longer and at once began to write. Stoutenburg went to the door and loudly called for Jan.
Ten minutes later the letter was written, folded and delivered into Jan’s keeping, who was standing at attention and recapitulating the orders which had been given him.
“I take a dozen men with me,” he said slowly, “and we follow the course of the Schie as far as Rotterdam. Fortunately it is passable practically the whole of the way.”
Stoutenburg nodded in approval.
“I present this letter to Mynheer Ben Isaje, the banker,” continued Jan, “and ask him at once to apprise the jongejuffrouw that she deign to accompany us.”
“Yes. That is right,” quoth Stoutenburg, “but remember that I want you above all things to find that foreigner again. You said that he was sleeping last night in Mynheer Ben Isaje’s house.”
“So I understood, my lord.”
“Well! you must move heaven and earth to find him, Jan.

===Chinese===

眼下我还受不住见吉尔达。我不能让她以为这场劫持有我一份，我一露面，反倒会惹她起疑。”
斯陶滕堡毫不在意地笑了。
“这么说，你宁可让她以为这桩事是我主使的。好啊！”他又无所谓地耸耸肩，“她真这么想，我这副肩膀也够宽，扛得住她那雷霆之怒。你既然不肯亲自去，咱们就给扬下详详细细的令。让他恭而有礼、速去速回，把吉尔达和她的使女一并接到这儿来，同时给那无赖戴上脚镣。今天抽十个十二个人手，甚至更多，都不费事，实在说他们也没什么正经事可干，找点事把他们拴住，倒省得惹是生非。这会儿我去给扬传令，你呢，给本·伊萨耶写封信，告诉他，你的意思是吉尔达该随你亲笔信的持信人一同前来。”
“可是……”
“啧，伙计！”斯陶滕堡不耐烦地喝道，刺耳的嗓音里透着轻蔑，“信上的话尽可以写成这样：就算信落到别人手里，也半点牵连不到你。你只说是刚发觉妹妹落在了强人手里，特派卫队前去搭救；这样吉尔达反倒感激你，乐意信你。随你怎么写，只是看在上帝份上，快点写。多耽搁一刻，我都几乎要疯了。”
他一阵急促狂乱的动作，把纸和墨角瓶推到贝雷斯坦跟前。贝雷斯坦不再迟疑，立刻提笔写了起来。斯陶滕堡走到门边，扬声唤扬。
十分钟后，信写好了，折起，交到扬的手里；扬挺身立正，正逐条复述领到的命令。
“我带十二个人，”他慢慢地说，“沿斯希河一路去鹿特丹。幸亏这条道几乎全程都走得通。”
斯陶滕堡点头称许。
“我把这封信呈给银行家本·伊萨耶先生（Mynheer Ben Isaje），”扬接着说，“并请他即刻禀知小姐（jongejuffrouw），请她屈尊随我们一行前来。”
“对，正是这样，”斯陶滕堡道，“可记住，我头一件要紧的事，是让你把那个外国人再找回来。你说过，他昨夜就宿在本·伊萨耶先生（Mynheer Ben Isaje）家里。”
“据我所知是这样，大人。”
“好！那你上天入地，也非把他找到不可，扬。”

===Original===

I want him here﻿—a prisoner﻿—remember! Do not let him slip through your fingers this time. It might mean life or death to us all. By fair means or foul you must lay him by the heels.”
“It should not be difficult, my lord,” assented Jan quietly. “I will pick my men, and I have no doubt that we shall come across the foreigner somewhere in the neighbourhood. He cannot have gone far, and even if he left the city we will easily come on his track.”
“That’s brave, Jan. Then come straight back here; two or three of your men can in the meanwhile escort the jongejuffrouw, who will travel by sledge. You must avoid Delft of course, and make a detour there.”
“I had best get horses at Rotterdam, my lord; the sledge can follow the left bank of the Schie all the way, which will be the best means of avoiding Delft.”
“And remember,” concluded Stoutenburg in his most peremptory manner, “that you must all be back here before ten o’clock tonight. The jongejuffrouw first and you with the foreigner later. It is not much more than eight o’clock now; you have the whole day before you. Let the sledge pull up outside the miller’s hut, everything will be ready there by then for the jongejuffrouw’s reception; and let your watchwords be ‘Silence, discretion, speed!’﻿—you understand?”
“I understand, my lord,” replied Jan simply as he gave a military salute, then quietly turned on his heel and went out of the room.
The two friends were once more alone, straining their ears to catch every sound which came to them now from below. Muffled and enveloped in the mist, the voice of Jan giving brief words of command could be distinctly heard, also the metallic click of skates and the tramping of heavily-booted feet upon the ground. But ten minutes later all these sounds had died away. Jan and his men had gone to fetch Gilda﻿—the poor little pawn moved hither and thither by the ruthless and ambitious hands of men.
Beresteyn had buried his head in his hands, in a sudden fit of overpowering remorse. Stoutenburg looked on him silently for awhile, his haggard face appeared drawn and sunken in the pale grey light which found its way through the tiny window up above. Passion greater than that which broke down the spirit of his friend, was tearing at his heartstrings; ambition fought with love, and remorse with determination.

===Chinese===

我要他到这儿来——作为囚犯——记住！这回不许再让他从你的指缝里溜走。这可关系到咱们所有人的生死。不择手段，你也必须把他擒来。”
“这应该不难，大人，”扬从容地应道，“我自会挑好人手。那外国人多半就在左近什么地方，咱们准能撞见。他走不远，就算出了城，咱们也轻易寻得着他的踪迹。”
“这才是好样的，扬。那么事毕径直回这儿来；这期间由两三个手下护卫小姐，她坐雪橇走。代尔夫特当然是必得避开的，到那儿绕个弯。”
“小人最好在鹿特丹备马，大人；雪橇可以沿斯希河左岸一路行来，那样避开代尔夫特最稳妥。”
“还要记住，”斯陶滕堡用最专断的口吻收束道，“今晚十点以前，你们务必全都回到这儿。小姐先到，你带着那个外国人随后。眼下不过八点来钟，你们有的是工夫。让雪橇停在磨坊主小屋外，届时屋里迎接小姐的一切都会备妥；你们的口号是：‘缄默、谨慎、迅速！’——明白吗？”
“明白，大人，”扬简单地应道，行了个军礼，随即干脆利落地转身出屋去了。
两个朋友重又独处一室，竖起耳朵捕捉此刻楼下传来的每一丝声响。隔着雾的裹挟，扬简短发令的嗓音听得真切，还有冰鞋金属的咔嗒声、沉甸甸的靴子踏地的脚步声。可十分钟后，这一切声响都消歇了。扬和他的人动身接吉尔达去了——这可怜的小卒子，正被一双双冷酷而野心勃勃的手拨来拨去。
贝雷斯坦忽然被一阵排山倒海的悔恨攫住，把头埋进了双手。斯陶滕堡默默看了他半晌；一束灰白的光从头顶的小窗透进来，照得他那张憔悴的脸愈发扭曲凹陷。一种比击垮他朋友精神的更炽烈的激情，正撕扯着他的心弦；野心与爱搏斗，悔恨与决断搏斗。

===Original===

But through it all the image of Gilda flitted before his burning eyes across this dimly-lighted room, reproachful and sweet and tantalizingly beautiful. The desire to have her near him in the greatest hour of his life on the morrow, had been the true mainspring which had prompted him to urge Beresteyn to send for her. It seemed to him that Gilda’s presence would bring him luck in his dark undertaking so heavily fraught with crime, and with a careless shrug of the shoulders he was ready to dismiss all thoughts of the wrong which he had done her, in favour of his hopes, his desire, his certainty that a glorious future in his arms would compensate her for all that he had caused her to endure.

===Chinese===

可是在这一切之中，吉尔达的身影仍在他灼热的眼前掠过，穿过这间昏暗的屋子，带着嗔怪，带着甜美，带着撩人的艳色。明天是他此生最重大的时刻，想让她守在自己身边的欲望，正是催他撺掇贝雷斯坦去接她的真正发条。在他看来，吉尔达在场，便给他这桩罪孽深重的黑暗勾当带来好运；于是他肩膀一耸，满不在乎地预备把亏负她的种种念头统统撇开，换上他的希望、他的欲望，以及他的笃信：将来投入他怀抱的一片光明前程，足以补偿他害她忍受的一切。

## XXXII A Run Through the Night / 三十二 夜驰
===Original===

That same morning of this fourth day of the New Year found Gilda Beresteyn sitting silent and thoughtful in the tiny room which had been placed at her disposal in the house of Mynheer Ben Isaje, the banker.
A few hours ago she had come back to it, running like some frightened animal who had just escaped an awful﻿—but unknown﻿—danger, and had thrown herself down on the narrow bed in the alcove in an agony of soul far more difficult to bear than any sorrow which had assailed her during the last few days.
A great, a vivid ray of hope had pierced the darkness of her misery, it had flickered low at first, then had glowed with wonderful intensity, flickered again and finally died down as hope itself fell dying once more in the arms of despair.
The disappointment which she had endured then amounted almost to physical pain; her heart ached and beat intolerably and with that disappointment was coupled a sense of hatred and of humiliation, different to any suffering she had ever had to bear before.
A man could have helped her and had refused: he could have helped her to avert a crime more hideous than any that had ever blackened the pages of this country’s history. With that one man’s help she could have stopped that crime from being committed and he had refused﻿ ﻿… nay more! he had first dragged her secret from her, word by word, luring her into thoughts of security with the hope that he dangled before her.
He knew everything now: she had practically admitted everything save the identity of those whose crime she wished to avert. But even that identity would be easy for the man to guess. Stoutenburg, of course, had paid him to lay hands on her﻿ ﻿… but her brother Nicolaes was Stoutenburg’s friend and ally, and his life and that of his friends were now in the hands of that rogue, who might betray them with the knowledge which he had filched from her.
No wonder that hour after hour she lay prostrate on the bed, while these dark thoughts hammered away in her brain. The Prince of Orange walking unknowingly straight to his death, or Nicolaes﻿—her brother﻿—and his friends betrayed to the vengeance of that Prince. Ghosts of those who had already died﻿—victims to that same merciless vengeance﻿—flitted in the darkness before her feverish fancy: John of Barneveld, the Lord of Groeneveld, the sorrowing widows and fatherless children﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

新年第四天的同一个早晨，吉尔达·贝雷斯坦（Gilda Beresteyn）坐在银行家本·伊萨耶先生（Mynheer Ben Isaje）宅子里拨给她用的那间小屋中，沉默出神。
几个钟头前，她逃回这间屋子，像一头刚躲过大祸——却不知是什么祸——的受惊野兽，一头扑倒在内室凹龛里那张窄床上，灵魂里的苦楚，比过去几天里任何一件伤她的事都更难煎熬。
一道巨大而鲜明的希望之光，曾刺穿她苦难的黑暗；起初它闪烁得微弱，随即烧得璀璨夺目，又闪了几闪，终于熄灭——希望本身也再度咽气，倒进绝望的怀抱。
她随后咽下的那场失望，几乎抵得上皮肉的疼痛；心口作痛，跳得叫人受不住；随失望一起来的，还有恨，还有羞辱——那是她从前受过的任何苦楚都比不上的另一种苦。
有一个人本来能帮她，却拒绝了。他本可以帮她拦下一桩罪行，一桩比这个国家史册上任何污点都更丑恶的罪行。只消他一个人搭手，她就能叫那桩罪行胎死腹中，可他拒绝了……不止如此！他还先把她的秘密一个字一个字地掳了去，把希望吊在她眼前，诱她生出安全的幻念。
如今他什么都知道了：除了那伙她要拦下其罪行的人是谁，她等於全招了。可就连这个，那人也轻易猜得出来。不用说，是斯陶滕堡出钱雇他对她下手的……而她哥哥尼古拉斯正是斯陶滕堡的朋友和同党，他的性命、他那些朋友的性命，如今都攢在那恶棍手里——他完全可以拿着从她这儿偷去的秘密，把他们统统卖掉。
无怪她一个钟头又一个钟头地直挺挺躺在床上，任这些阴郁的念头在脑子里一下一下捶打。奥兰治亲王浑然不觉地径直走向死亡；要么就是尼古拉斯——她的哥哥——和他那些朋友，被出卖给亲王的雷霆之怒。那些已经死了的人——同一场无情清算的牺牲品——的鬼影，在她烧热的幻念前的黑暗里飘来飘去：约翰·巴内费尔德（John of Barneveld）、格罗内费尔德老爷（Groeneveld）、悲恸的寡妇和无父的孤儿……

===Original===

and in their trail the ghost of the great Stadtholder, William the Silent murdered﻿—as his son would be﻿—at Delft, close to Ryswyk and the molens, where even now Nicolaes her brother was learning the final lesson of infamy.
When in the late morning Maria came into the room to bring her mistress some warm milk and bread, and to minister to her comforts, she found her dearly loved jongejuffrouw wide-eyed and feverish.
But not a word could she get out of Gilda while she dressed her hair, except an assurance that their troubles﻿—as far as Maria could gauge them﻿—would soon be over now, and that in twenty-four hours mayhap they would be escorted back to Haarlem.
“When, I trust, that I shall have the joy of seeing three impudent knaves swing on gibbets in the market place,” quoth Maria decisively, “and one of them﻿—the most impudent of the lot﻿—drawn and quartered, or burnt at the stake!” she added with savage insistence.
When Gilda was ready dressed, she asked for leave to speak with Mynheer Ben Isaje. The Jew, obsequious and affable, received her with utmost deference, and in a few words put the situation before her. Mevrouw Isaje, he said, was from home: he had not been apprised of the jongejuffrouw’s coming, or his wife would have been ready to receive her at his private house, which was situated but half a league out of Rotterdam. But Mevrouw Isaje would return from the visit which she had been paying to her father in the course of the afternoon, until that hour Mynheer Ben Isaje begged that the jongejuffrouw would look upon this miserable hovel as her property and would give what orders she desired for the furtherance of her comfort. In the afternoon, he concluded, an escort would once more be ready to convey the jongejuffrouw to that same private house of his, where there was a nice garden and a fine view over the Schie instead of the confined outlook on squalid houses opposite, which was quite unworthy of the jongejuffrouw’s glance.
Gilda did not attempt to stay the flow of Ben Isaje’s eloquence: she thanked him graciously for everything that he had already done for her comfort.

===Chinese===

跟在他们身后的，还有那位伟大执政（Stadtholder）的鬼魂：沉默者威廉——在代尔夫特遇刺，他儿子日后也将这样死——就在赖斯韦克和那座磨坊的近旁；就在此刻，她的哥哥尼古拉斯还在那里修习他身败名裂的最后一课。
上午将尽，玛丽亚（Maria）进屋来给小姐端温牛奶和面包，张罗着伺候她，却见她心疼的小姐（jongejuffrouw）睁着眼，烧得发热。
她替她梳头的当儿，吉尔达一句话也问不出来，只说得准一点：她们的麻烦——就玛丽亚能掂量的而言——很快就要了结，兴许不出二十四小时，就会有人护送她们回哈勒姆。
“到那时候，”玛丽亚斩钉截铁地说，“我指望能亲眼看三个胆大包天的恶棍在集市上吊上绞架，乐上一乐，”她又恶狠狠地补了一句，“其中一个——最无法无天的那个——还得挨车裂，或者上火刑柱！”
吉尔达穿戴停当，便请求同本·伊萨耶先生一见。那犹太人谦卑和气，接待她礼数周到，三言两语把眼下的情形摆给她听。他说，伊萨耶夫人（Mevrouw Isaje）不在家：小姐要来，事先没人知会他，不然他妻子早该在他私宅里候着了——那宅子离鹿特丹不过半里格。不过伊萨耶夫人下午就能从她父亲那里做客回来；在那之前，本·伊萨耶先生恳请小姐把这寒陋的小屋当自己的产业看待，要什么尽管吩咐，只求她住得妥帖。到下午，他收尾道，还会备好一队护卫，再送小姐到他那座私宅去；那里有座好园子，望得见斯希河的美景，不像这里，对面尽是逼仄肮脏的破房子，实在配不上小姐的眼睛。
本·伊萨耶口若悬河，吉尔达并不去拦，只为他待她的周到殷勤，落落大方地谢了。

===Original===

Maria﻿—more loquacious, and bubbling over with indignation﻿—asked him when this outrageous confinement of her person and that of her exalted mistress at the hands of brigands would cease, and if Mynheer Ben Isaje was aware that such confinement against the jongejuffrouw’s will would inevitably entail the punishment of hanging.
But thereupon Mynheer Ben Isaje merely rubbed his thin hands together and became as evasive first and then as mute as only those of his race can contrive to be.
Then Gilda﻿—making an effort to speak unconcernedly﻿—asked him what had become of the men who had brought her hither from Haarlem.
“They spent half the night eating and drinking at the tavern, mejuffrouw,” said the Jew blandly.
“Ah!” rejoined Gilda quietly, “methought one of them had found hospitality under your roof.”
“So he had, mejuffrouw. But this morning when I called him﻿—for I had some business to transact with him﻿—I found his room already empty. No doubt he had gone to join his companions at the tavern. But the rascal’s movements need not disturb the jongejuffrouw for one moment. After today she need never set eyes on him again.”
“Save when he is hanging on a gibbet in the Groote Markt,” broke in Maria viciously. “I for one never go to see such sights, but when that rascal hangs it shall be a holiday for me to go and get a last look at him.”
---
Later on in the day, Ben Isaje, more affable and obsequious than he had ever been, came to announce to the jongejuffrouw that her sledge was awaiting her at the top of the street.
Silently and resignedly as had been her wont these past two days Gilda Beresteyn, wrapping her cloak and hood closely round her, followed Mynheer Ben Isaje out of the house. Maria walked immediately behind her, muttering imprecations against brigands, and threatening dire punishments against every Jew.
Though it was only three o’clock in the afternoon, it was already quite dark in this narrow street, where tall gables almost touched one another at the top: only from the tiny latticed windows feeble patches of yellow light glimmered weirdly through the fog.
The sledge was waiting at the top of the street, as Mynheer Ben Isaje had said. Gilda shuddered as soon as she caught sight of it again; it represented so much that was vivid and tangible of her present anxiety and sorrow.

===Chinese===

玛丽亚话更多，一腔义愤直往外冒，问他：强人们把她本人和她高贵的小姐这样横加拘禁，几时是个头？本·伊萨耶先生可知道，这般违背小姐心意关押她们，到头来免不了是要上绞刑的？
可本·伊萨耶先生听了，只把两只瘦手搓了搓，先推三阻四，随即一声不吭——那份哑功，也只有他那一族的人才使得出来。
随后吉尔达——竭力说得若无其事——问她从哈勒姆被带来的那几个人的下落。
“他们半个晚上都在酒馆里大吃大喝，小姐（mejuffrouw），”犹太人温和地说。
“哦！”吉尔达淡淡地应道，“我还当其中一位在您府上叨光住下了。”
“确曾住过，小姐。可今早我去叫他——我有点买卖要同他办——却发现屋里已经空了。不用说是上酒馆找他那些同伴去了。不过那恶棍的行踪，小姐半刻也不必挂心。过了今天，她再也不必见他一眼。”
“除非是他在大集市（Groote Markt）吊上绞架的时候，”玛丽亚恶狠狠地插嘴，“我这个人从不看那种热闹，可那恶棍吊起来那天，对我来说就是过节，我非要去看他最后一眼不可。”
---
晚些时候，本·伊萨耶比先前任何时候都更和气、更谦卑，来禀告小姐：她的雪橇已在街口候驾。
一如这两天来的习惯，吉尔达·贝雷斯坦默默顺命，把斗篷和风帽裹紧，跟着本·伊萨耶先生出了门。玛丽亚紧随其后，嘴里咒骂着强人，扬言要叫每一个犹太人都遭大报应。
虽说才下午三点钟，这条窄街上却已相当黑了：高高的人字山墙到了顶上几乎要碰头；只有一扇扇小小的格子窗里，一片片昏黄的灯光，透过雾气幽幽地闪。
雪橇果然候在街口，正如本·伊萨耶先生所说。吉尔达一眼重新望见它，便打了个寒嚇；它身上活生生、实打实地系着她此刻的忧惧与哀伤。

===Original===

It stood upon an open marketplace, with the driver sitting up at his post and three horses harnessed thereto. The small tavern was at the corner on the left, and as Gilda walked rapidly up to the sledge, she saw two of the men who had been escorting her hitherto, the thin man with the abnormally long legs, and the fat one with the red nose and round eyes: but of the third tall, splendid figure she did not catch one glimpse.
The two men nudged one another as she passed, and whispered excitedly to one another, but she could not hear what they said, and the next moment she found herself being handed into the vehicle by Ben Isaje, who thereupon took humble leave of her.
“You are not coming with us, mynheer?” she asked in astonishment.
“Not﻿ ﻿… not just yet, mejuffrouw,” murmured the Jew somewhat incoherently, “it is too early yet in the afternoon﻿ ﻿… er﻿ ﻿… for me to﻿ ﻿… to leave my business﻿ ﻿… I have the honour to bid the jongejuffrouw ‘Godspeed.’ ”
“But,” said Gilda, who suddenly misliked Ben Isaje’s manner, yet could not have told you why, “the *mevrouw*﻿—your wife﻿—she is ready to receive me?”
“Of a truth﻿—certainly,” replied the man. Gilda would have given much to question him further. She was quite sure that there was something strange in his manner, something that she mistrusted; but just as she was about to speak again, there was a sudden command of “Forward!” the driver cracked his whip, the harness jingled, the sledge gave a big lurch forward and the next moment Gilda found herself once more being rushed at great speed through the cold night air.
She could not see much round her, for the fog out in the open seemed even more dense than it was inside the city and the darkness of the night crept swiftly through the fog. All that she knew for certain was that the city was very soon left behind, that the driver was urging his horses on to unusual speed, and that she must be travelling along a river bank, because when the harness rattled and jingled less loudly than usual, she could hear distinctly the clink of metal skates upon the ice, as wayfarers no doubt were passing to and fro.

===Chinese===

雪橇停在一处开阔的集市上，车夫端坐座上，套着三匹马。左边街角上是那家小酒馆。吉尔达快步走向雪橇时，望见一路护卫她的那几个人里的两个——瘦高个儿两条腿出奇地长的那一个，红鼻子圆眼睛的胖子：可那第三个高大堂堂的身影，她连一眼也没燋见。
她走过时，那两人彼此用胳膊肘捣了捣，兴奋地交头接耳；她们说的话一个字也听不见，转眼间，本·伊萨耶已把她搀上雪橇，随即恭恭敬敬地作别。
“您不同我们一起去吗，先生？”她愕然问。
“不……眼下还……还不成，小姐，”犹太人口齿有些零乱地喃喃道，“下午还太早……呃……叫我……叫我丢下买卖……我谨祝小姐一路平安。”
“可是，”吉尔达说——她忽然不喜欢本·伊萨耶的做派了，虽说不出为什么——“夫人（*mevrouw*）——您的太太——她预备好接我了吗？”
“千真万确——预备好了。”那人答道。吉尔达甘愿拿出许多来再多问他几句。她拿得准他的神色里有蹊跷，有一样她信不过的东西；可她正要再开口，忽听一声“出发！”，车夫长鞭一甩，挽具叮当，雪橇猛地朝前一冲，转眼间，吉尔达发觉自己又在寒冷的夜风里疾驰了。
四周她看不了多少，因为旷野的雾似乎比城里更浓，夜的黑暗顺着雾悄悄爬来。她能断定的只有：城很快甩在了身后；车夫把马催得快得反常；她走的一定是沿河的路——因为每逢挽具的叮当声稀下来，她便听得真切，冰上有金属冰鞋的咔嗒声，想来是行人在来来往往。

===Original===

Solitary as she was﻿—for Maria and her eternal grumblings were poor company﻿—she fell to thinking again over the future, as she had done not only last night but through the past few interminable days; it almost seemed as if she had never, never thought of anything else, as if those same few days stretched out far away behind her into dim and nebulous infinity.
During those days she had alternately hoped and feared and been disappointed only to hope again: but the disappointment of last night was undoubtedly the most bitter that she had yet experienced. So bitter had it been that for a time﻿—after its intense poignancy had gone﻿—her faculties and power of thinking had become numbed, and now﻿—very gradually, unknown at first even to herself, hope shook itself free from the grip of disappointment and peeped up at her out of the abyss of her despair.
Did that unscrupulous knave really have the last word in the matter? had his caprice the power to order the destiny of this land and the welfare of its faith?
Bah! the very thought was monstrous and impossible. Was the life of the Prince of Orange to be sacrificed because a rascal would not help her to give him that word of warning which might save him even now at the eleventh hour?
No! Gilda Beresteyn refused to believe that God﻿—who had helped the armies of the Netherlands throughout their struggle against the might of Spain﻿—would allow a rogue to have so much power. After all, she was not going to be shut up in prison! she was going to the house of ordinary, respectable burghers; true, they were of alien and of despised faith, but they were well-to-do, had a family, serving women and men.
Surely among these there would be one who﻿—amenable to cajoleries or to promises﻿—would prove to be the instrument sent by God to save the Stadtholder from an assassin’s dagger!
Gilda Beresteyn, wrapped in this new train of thought, lost count of time, of distance and of cold: she lived during one whole hour in the happiness of this newly-risen hope, making plans, conjecturing, rehearsing over in her mind what she would say, how she would probe the loyalty, the kindness of those who would be around her tonight.
Delft was so near!

===Chinese===

她形单影只——玛丽亚和她的没完没了的嘟囔算不得良伴——便又琢磨起前路来，同昨夜一样，同这没完没了的几天里的每一刻一样；她仿佛从来、从来没想到过别的事，仿佛还是这同样的几天，在她身后远远地铺展开去，漫进昏昧的无穷。
那些天里，她一阵希望、一阵惧怕，屡屡失望，又屡屡重新希望；可昨夜那场失望，无疑是她至今尝过的最苦的一场。苦到那阵锥心的滋味过去之后，她的心智和思索的气力竟麻木了一阵；而现在——非常缓慢地，起初连她自己也未曾察觉——希望从失望的钳制里挣脱出来，从她绝望的深渊底上，探出头来望她。
那个无法无天的恶棍，果真在这件事上说一不二？他的一时兴之所至，果真握有权力，裁定这片土地的命运、这片土地上信仰的安危？
呸！这念头本身就荒唐，就不可能。难道就因为一个恶棍不肯帮她递一句警告——一句哪怕此刻、在这最后关头还救得了他的话——奥兰治亲王就得赔上性命？
不！吉尔达·贝雷斯坦不肯信：上帝——在尼德兰诸军对抗西班牙强权的整场苦斗里一直扶助他们的上帝——会容一个泼皮掌这么大的权。说到底，她不是要去坐牢！她要去的是寻常体面的市民之家；不错，人家是异教，是被人轻贱的教门，可他们家境殷实，有儿有女，有男仆女仆。
这中间总会有一个吧——哄得动也好，许得动也好——终归会成为上帝派下的工具，把执政从刺客的匕首底下救出来！
吉尔达·贝雷斯坦沉浸在这条新的思路里，忘了时辰，忘了路程，忘了寒冷：整整一个钟头，她活在这新升起来的希望的快乐里，盘算着，揣度着，在心里一遍遍演练她该说什么，今晚在她身边的那些人，她怎样去试探他们的忠心、他们的善意。
代尔夫特这么近！

===Original===

and after all even Maria might be bribed to forget her fears and her grievances and to become that priceless instrument of salvation of which Gilda dreamed as the sledge flew swiftly along through the night.
It was Maria who roused her suddenly out of these happy fancies. Maria who said plaintively:
“Shall we never get to that *verdommte* house. The Jew said that it was only situate half a league from Rotterdam.”
“We must be close to it,” murmured Gilda.
“Close to it!” retorted Maria, “we seem to be burning the ground under the horses’ hoofs﻿—we have left Rotterdam behind us this hour past.﻿ ﻿… It is the longest half league that I have ever known.”
“Peep out under the hood, Maria. Cannot you see where we are?”
Maria peeped out as she was bid.
“I can see the lights of a city far away on our right,” she said. “From the direction in which we have been going and the ground which we have covered I should guess that city to be Delft.”
“Delft!” exclaimed Gilda, smothering a louder scream.
The driver had just pulled up his horses, allowing them to go at a walk so as to restore their wind and ease them for awhile. Gilda tried her best to peer through the darkness. All that she could see were those lights far away on the right which proclaimed the distant city.
A chill struck suddenly to her heart. Ben Isaje had lied! Why? She was not being taken to his house which was situate half a league outside Rotterdam﻿ ﻿… then whither was she being taken? What new misery, what new outrage awaited her now?
The lights of the distant city receded further and further away from her view, the driver once more put his horses at a trot, the sledge moved along more smoothly now: it seemed as if it were going over the surface of the river. Delft was being left behind, and the sledge was following the course of the Schie﻿ ﻿… on toward Ryswyk.﻿ ﻿…
The minutes sped on, another quarter of an hour, another half hour, another hour in this dread suspense. The driver was urging his horses unmercifully: he gave them but little rest. It was only when for a few brief moments he put them at walking pace, that Gilda heard﻿—all around her as it seemed﻿—that metallic click of skates which told her that the sledge was surrounded by men who were there to watch over her and see that she did not escape.

===Chinese===

再说，连玛丽亚兴许也可以买通，叫她忘了害怕、忘了委屈，做成那件无价的救世工具——雪橇在夜里飞驰，吉尔达一路梦着的正是它。
把她从这些快活的念想里猛地唤醒的，正是玛丽亚。玛丽亚哀声说：
“咱们到底还到得了那所该死的（*verdommte*）宅子不？那犹太人说，离鹿特丹不过半里格地。”
“该是快到了。”吉尔达喃喃道。
“快到了！”玛丽亚顶回来，“瞧这马蹄子底下跟踩着火似的——鹿特丹攦在身后都一个钟头了。……这半里格，是我这辈子走过的最长的一段。”
“从风帽底下瞧瞧，玛丽亚。看不见咱们到哪儿了吗？”
玛丽亚依言往外燋了燋。
“我燋见右手边老远有一座城的灯火，”她说，“照咱们走的方位、过的地界猜，那座城该是代尔夫特。”
“代尔夫特！”吉尔达失声喊，硬生生把更响的尖叫压了回去。
车夫刚把马勒住，放缓成走，让它们喘口气、松泛一阵。吉尔达竭力往黑暗里张望。她看得见的，只有右手边远处那片灯火，说明远处有座城。
一股寒气猛地击中心口。本·伊萨耶撒了谎！为什么？她要被带去的，并不是他那座坐落在鹿特丹城外半里格的宅子……那她这是被带去哪儿？还有什么新的苦楚、新的欺辱在等着她？
远城的灯火在她眼里越退越远，车夫重新策马小跑，雪橇这会儿走得平稳了：像是行在河面上。代尔夫特被抛在身后，雪橇沿着斯希河的流向……直奔赖斯韦克。……
分分秒秒飞驰而去，一刻钟，又半点钟，又一个钟头，就在这骇人的悬心里熬着。车夫毫不怜惜地催马，几乎不给它们喘息。只有他把马放缓走那么短短几瞬的工夫，吉尔达才听得见——仿佛四下里都是——冰鞋那种金属的咔嗒声；那声音告诉她：雪橇四周尽是人，是看守她、防她逃走的人。

## XXXIII The Captive Lion / 三十三 被囚的狮子
===Original===

Beresteyn was sitting at the table in the weighing-room of the molens: his elbows rested on the table, and his right hand supported his head; in the feeble light of the lantern placed quite close to him, his face looked sullen and dark, and his eyes, overshadowed by his frowning brows, were fixed with restless eagerness upon the narrow door.
Stoutenburg, with hands crossed over his chest, with head bare and collar impatiently torn away from round his neck, was pacing up and down the long, low room like a caged beast of prey.
“Enter!” he shouted impatiently in response to a loud knock on the door. Then as Jan entered, and having saluted, remained standing by the door, he paused in his feverish walk, and asked in a curiously hoarse voice, choked with anxiety:
“Is everything all right, Jan?”
“Everything, my lord.”
“The jongejuffrouw?﻿ ﻿…”
“In the hut, my lord. There is a good fire there and the woman is preparing some hot supper for the lady.”
“How does she seem?”
“She stepped very quietly out of the sledge, my lord, the moment I told her that we had arrived. She asked no questions, and walked straight into the hut. Meseemed that the jongejuffrouw knew exactly where she was.”
“The woman will look after her comforts well?”
“Oh, yes, my lord, though she is only a rough peasant, she will try and do her best, and the jongejuffrouw has her own waiting woman with her as well.”
“And the horses?”
“In the shed behind the hut.”
“Look after them well, Jan: we may want to use them again tomorrow.”
“They shall be well looked after, my lord.”
“And you have placed the sentry outside the hut?”
“Two men in the front and two in the rear, as you have commanded, my lord.”
Stoutenburg drew a deep breath of satisfaction: but anxiety seemed to have exhausted him, for now that his questions had been clearly answered, he sank into a chair.
“All well, Nicolaes,” he said more calmly as he placed a reassuring hand upon his friend’s shoulder.
But Nicolaes groaned aloud.
“Would to God,” he said, “that all were well!”
Smothering an impatient retort Stoutenburg once more turned to Jan.
“And what news of the foreigner?” he queried eagerly.
“We have got him, my lord,” replied Jan.
“By God!” exclaimed Stoutenburg, “how did you do it?”
His excitement was at fever pitch now. He was leaning forward, and his attitude was one of burning expectancy.

===Chinese===

贝雷斯坦坐在磨坊称重室的桌旁：两肘支在桌上，右手托着头；在搁得离他很近的那盏风灯的微光里，他的脸显得阴沉黝黑，紧锁的眉下，一双眼睛焦灼不安地死盯着那扇窄门。
斯陶滕堡双臂交叉抱在胸前，光着头，领子早被他不耐烦地从脖颈上扯开，正像一头关在笼里的猛兽，在这间狭长的低顶屋里来回踱步。
“进来！”门外一阵重重的叩门声，他不耐烦地喝道。扬进来行过礼，便立在门边；他也停下那焦灼的脚步，用一种焦虑得发噎的、异样嘶哑的嗓音问：
“一切都妥帖吗，扬？”
“一切妥帖，大人。”
“小姐呢？……”
“在小屋里，大人。屋里火烧得旺，那婆娘正给小姐备热晚饭呢。”
“她看着怎么样？”
“我一说到了，她就悄没声地下了雪橇，大人。她什么也没问，径直走进了小屋。依我看，小姐心里明明白白自己到了什么地方。”
“那婆娘会把她伺候舒坦吗？”
“哦，会的，大人。她虽说只是个粗手粗脚的乡下婆娘，总会尽心尽力，何况小姐身边还带着自己的贴身女仆呢。”
“马呢？”
“在小屋后头的棚子里。”
“好生照看，扬：明天说不定还要用。”
“一定照看好，大人。”
“小屋外头派了岗哨没有？”
“前头两个，后头两个，全遵您的号令，大人。”
斯陶滕堡深深吸了一口满意的气：可焦虑仿佛已把他熬干了，问的话一一条分缕析地得了答复，他便一屁股陷进椅子里。
“都好了，尼古拉斯，”他把手安放在朋友肩头宽慰着，说话也平静了些。
可尼古拉斯高声呻吟起来。
“但愿上帝，”他说，“保佑一切真都好了！”
斯陶滕堡把一句不耐烦的反问咽了回去，又转向扬。
“那外国人有什么消息？”他急切地追问。
“我们把他拿到手了，大人。”扬答道。
“上帝为证！”斯陶滕堡失声道，“你们怎么办到的？”
他的兴奋这时已到了白热。他身子前倾，一副望眼欲穿的架势。

===Original===

His hollow eyes were fixed upon Jan’s lips as if they would extract from them the glad news which they held. Whatever weakness there was in Stoutenburg’s nature, one thing in him was strong﻿—and that was hatred. He could hate with an intensity of passion worthy of a fine cause. He hated the Stadtholder first, and secondly the nameless adventurer who had humiliated him and forced him to lick the dust: wounded in his vanity and in his arrogance he was consumed with an inordinate desire for revenge. The hope that this revenge was now at last in sight﻿—that the man whom he hated so desperately was now in his power﻿—almost caused the light of mania to dance in his glowing eyes.
“How did you do it, Jan?” he reiterated hoarsely.
“It was not far from the molens,” said Jan simply, “until then he gave us the slip, though we spied him just outside Delft on our way to Rotterdam this morning. My impression is that he went back to Rotterdam then, and that he followed the jongejuffrouw’s sledge practically all the way. Close to the molens he was forced to draw a little nearer as it was getting very dark and probably he did not know his way about. I am convinced that he wished to ascertain exactly whither we were taking the jongejuffrouw. At any rate, I and some of our fellows who had lagged in the rear caught sight of him then﻿ ﻿…”
“And you seized him?” cried Stoutenburg with exultant joy.
“He was alone, my lord,” replied Jan with a placid smile, “and there were seven of us at the time. Two or three of the men, though, are even now nursing unpleasant wounds. I myself fared rather badly with a bruised head and half-broken collarbone.﻿ ﻿… The man is a demon for fighting, but there were seven of us.”
“Well done, Jan!” cried Beresteyn now, for Stoutenburg had become speechless with the delight of this glorious news; “and what did you do with the rogue?”
“We tied him securely with ropes and dragged him along with us. Oh! we made certain of him, my lord, you may be sure of that. And now I and another man have taken him down into the basement below and we have fastened him to one of the beams, where I imagine the northwest wind will soon cool his temper.”
“Aye, that it will!” quoth Stoutenburg lustily. “Take the lantern, Jan, and let us to him at once. Beresteyn, friend, will you come too? Your hand like mine must be itching to get at the villain’s face.”

===Chinese===

他那双凹陷的眼睛盯住扬的嘴唇，仿佛要从那两片唇上，把含着的好消息硬吮出来。斯陶滕堡的天性里无论藏着什么软弱，有一样东西在他身上是炽烈的——那就是恨。他恨起来那股激情的烈度，配得上一桩大义。他头一个恨执政，第二个恨那个逼他受辱、逼他舐尘土的无名冒险家：虚荣和傲慢都受了创伤，他便被一股没有节制的复仇欲煎熬着。眼看这仇终于报得着了——那个被他恨到极处的人已落进他的手心——他那双灼灼的眼睛里，几乎跳跃起一种癫狂的光。
“你们怎么办到的，扬？”他哑着嗓子又问了一遍。
“就在离磨坊不远的地方，”扬简单地说，“在那之前他一直甩得脱我们，虽说他今早在我们去鹿特丹的路上、刚出代尔夫特那会儿被我们瞅见过。依我看，他那时折回了鹿特丹，随后就差不多一路缀着小姐的雪橇。挨近磨坊时天已经黑透了，路他多半又不熟，只得逼得贴近了些。我认定他是想确切探明我们究竟把小姐带到哪里去。反正，我和几个落在后头的弟兄那时候就瞧见了他……”
“你们就把他拿了？”斯陶滕堡狂喜地喊道。
