“那样的事我们想也不想，”毕达哥拉斯一脸虔敬地咕哝。
一道道商量的眼色彼此递来递去。这些破烂上衣底下，跳着的不止一颗好心。呸！这人明天就要上绞架了，何苦不给个将死的人一点乐子？
倘若死前几个钟头看这两张吓人的稻草脸也算乐子的话。
扬不在。他陪着老爷搬那些铁箱子去了。
“五分钟，老蘑菇脸，”留下来管事的那个发了话。
旁的人全点头应承。
可对囚徒他们一点险也不冒。乐子，同朋友聊上五分钟，行！越狱，不行。于是众人围成一个宽宽的大圈，坐到听不见的地方，醒着的三十来个人肩挨着肩。圈子正中，是拴在梁上的笑面骑士——像只捆好的肉鸡，反正明天他要上绞架。
风灯就搁在他脚边，好让他最后再看朋友们一眼；几步开外，是他出鞘的剑——众人把他撂倒时，扬从他手里收走的。
那番窃窃私语他一直听着——他知道他的哲人兄弟来了。愿无赖恶棍们的上帝降福他们，报答这份忠心。
“圣巴沃啊，这会儿指点指点我，怎么使唤这两个宝贝！”
还有一桩事没办完，一句诺言许下了，就必须兑现；应许下的那笔钱财，还在等着把小姐安然带回她父亲身边的人！
“我的上帝，若不是为了这破了的肩、裂了的胯！……离明天还有好些个钟头呢。”
“老伙计！”耳边响起一声嘶哑的耳语，“你怎么落到这步田地的？”
“他们到鹿特丹把小姐接走了，”他也悄声答道。“我刚从代尔夫特回来，在那儿办了点事，正瞧见扬站在雪橇边上，小姐那时正跨进去。他带着十来个人。我觉出他们不怀好意——可我不能不跟……我得弄清他们要把她带到哪儿。……”
“见鬼（Verdommt）！”苏格拉底压着嗓子咆哮。“你怎么不带我们一起去？”
“我原想着一弄明白就回去叫你们……可天黑……又是从背后，这七个家伙扑上来……他们把冰鞋当标枪使……

===Original===

mine were still on my feet﻿ ﻿… I had only Bucephalus.﻿ ﻿… A blow from one of the heaviest blades cracked my shoulder, another caught me on the hip. There were seven of them,” he reiterated with a careless laugh, “it was only a question of time, they were bound to bring me down in the end.”
“But who has done this?” queried Pythagoras with an oath.
“A lucky rogue on whom God hath chosen to smile. But,” he added more seriously and sinking his voice to the lowest possible whisper, “never mind about the past. Let us think of the future, old compeers.”
“We are ready,” they replied simultaneously.
“A knife?” he murmured, “can you cut these confounded ropes?”
“They took everything from us,” growled Socrates, “ere they let us approach you.”
“Try with your hands to loosen the knots.”
“What ho! you brigands, what are you doing there?”
In a moment the circle around broke up. A crowd of angry faces were gathered closely round the philosophers, and more than one pair of rough hands were laid upon their shoulders.
“Play fair, you two!” cried Piet the Red, who was in command, “or we’ll tie you both to the nearest beams and await my lord’s commands.”
“Easy, easy, friend,” quoth Diogenes with a pleasant laugh, “my nose was itching and my compeer held on to my arm while he tried to reach my nose in order to scratch it.”
“Then if it itch again,” retorted the man with an equally jovial laugh, “call for my services, friend. And now, you two scarecrows! the five minutes are over. Jan will be here in a moment.”
But they formed up the circle once more, kind and compassionate. Jan was not yet here, and the rogues had had a warning: they were not like to be at their tricks again.
“Never mind about me,” whispered Diogenes hurriedly as Pythagoras and Socrates, baffled and furious, were giving forth samples of their choicest vocabularies. “You see that Chance alone can favour me an she choose, if not﻿ ﻿… ’tis no matter. What you can do for me is far more important than cheating the gallows of my carcase.”
“What is it?” they asked simply.
“The jongejuffrouw,” he said, “you know where she is?”
“In the hut﻿—close by,” replied Socrates, “we saw the sledge draw up there.﻿ ﻿…”
“But the house is well guarded,” murmured Pythagoras.
“Nor would I ask you to run your heads in the same noose wherein mine will swing tomorrow. But keep the hut well in sight.

===Chinese===

我的还绑在脚上……我手里只有布西发拉斯（Bucephalus）。……最沉的一副冰刀砍在我肩上，砍裂了；另一刀正着胯骨。他们有七个人，”他漫不经心地笑着又说了一遍，“不过是早晚的事，他们横竖要把我撂倒。”
“可这是谁干的？”毕达哥拉斯咒骂着问。
“一个走运的无赖，上帝拣中了他，朝他笑了一笑。不过，”他添了一句，口气郑重了，声音压到最低的耳语，“过去的不必管它。咱们想想将来吧，老伙计们。”
“我们候着呢，”两人齐声应道。
“有刀吗？”他喃喃道，“能割断这些该死的绳子不？”
“家伙什全叫他们收走了，”苏格拉底咆哮道，“才放我们挨近你。”
“用手解解那些结看。”
“喂嗬！你们这帮强盗，在那儿做什么？”
霎时间，四围的圈子散了。一圈怒冲冲的脸紧紧围住两位哲人，好几双粗手按上了他们的肩膀。
“规矩点，你们俩！”红发皮特喝道，他正管着事，“不然把你们俩也拴到最近的梁上，等老爷发落。”
“别急，别急，朋友，”第欧根尼笑得愉快，“我鼻子痒，我这位伙计一只手抓住我的胳膊，一只手够过来给我挠痒痒。”
“那要是再痒，”那人一样乐呵呵地回敬，“朋友，只管叫我来伺候。好了，你们俩个稻草人！五分钟到了。扬这就回来。”
可他们又把圈子围拢了，好心肠，慈悲意。扬还没回来，那两个无赖也得了警告：谅他们不敢再玩花样。
“别管我，”第欧根尼急急地耳语，这时毕达哥拉斯和苏格拉底正又急又怒，往外倒着他们压箱底的字眼。“你们瞧，全凭运气肯不肯照应我，它若肯……若不肯……也不要紧。你们能为我做的，可比从绞架上骗走我这具皮囊要紧得多。”
“什么事？”他们直截了当地问。
“小姐，”他说，“知道她在哪儿吗？”
“在小屋里——就在近旁，”苏格拉底答道，“我们眼看着雪橇停在那儿。……”
“可那屋子看守得严，”毕达哥拉斯咕哝。
“我也不至于要你们把脑袋伸进明天要吊我这颗的同一条绞索里。可你们给我盯死了那座小屋。

===Original===

At any hour﻿—any moment now there may be a call of sauve qui peut. Every man for himself and the greatest luck to the swiftest runner.”
“But why?”
“Never mind why. It is sure to happen. Any minute you may hear the cry﻿ ﻿… confusion, terror﻿ ﻿… a scramble and a rush for the open.”
“And our opportunity,” came in a hoarse whisper from Socrates. “I think that I begin to understand.”
“We lie low for the present and when sauve qui peut is called we come straight back here and free you﻿ ﻿… in the confusion they will have forgotten you.”
“If the confusion occurs in time,” quoth Diogenes with his habitual carelessness, “you may still find me here trussed like a fowl to this *verdommte* beam. But I have an idea that the Lord of Stoutenburg will presently be consumed with impatience to see me hang﻿ ﻿… he has just finished some important work by the bridge on the Schie﻿ ﻿… he won’t be able to sleep and the devil will be suggesting some mischief for his idle hands to do. There will be many hours to kill before daylight, one of them might be well employed in hanging me.”
“Then we’ll not leave you an instant,” asserted Pythagoras firmly.
“What can you do, you two old scarecrows, against the Lord of Stoutenburg who has thirty men here paid to do his bidding?”
“We are not going to lie low and play the part of cowards while you are being slaughtered.”
“You will do just what I ask, faithful old compeers,” rejoined Diogenes more earnestly than was his wont. “You will lie very low and take the greatest possible care not to run your heads into the same rope wherein mayhap mine will dangle presently. Nor will you be playing the part of cowards, for you have not yet learned the A.B.C. of that part, and you will remember that on your safety and freedom of action lies my one chance, not so much of life as of saving my last shred of honour.”
“What do you mean?”
“The jongejuffrouw﻿—” he whispered, “I swore to bring her back to her father and I must cheat a rascal of his victory. In the confusion﻿—at dawn tomorrow﻿—think above all of the jongejuffrouw.﻿ ﻿… In the confusion you can overpower the guard﻿—rush the miller’s hut where she is﻿ ﻿… carry her off﻿ ﻿… the horses are in the shed behind the hut﻿ ﻿… you may not have time to think of me.”
“But﻿ ﻿…”
“Silence﻿—they listen.﻿ ﻿…”
“One of us with the jongejuffrouw﻿—the other to help you﻿—”
“Silence﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

随时——随时可能喊一声“各自逃命”（sauve qui peut）。人各顾各，腿脚最快的运气最大。
“可为什么？”
“别问为什么。一定会来的。随时你们会听见那声喊……乱作一团，人人丧胆……你争我抢，没命地往空旷处跑。”
“那也就是我们的机会，”苏格拉底嘶哑地低语。“我想我有点开窍了。”
“眼下我们伏着不动，等‘各自逃命’一喊出来，我们径直回到这儿放你……乱起来他们早把你忘了。”
“若乱子来得及时，”第欧根尼带着他那份惯常的满不在乎说，“你们没准还能在这儿找着我，像只肉鸡似的拴在这根见鬼的（verdommte）梁上。不过我有数，斯陶滕堡老爷这会儿巴不得立时三刻看我吊起来，急得心焦……他在斯希河桥边刚忙完一件要紧的活计……他睡不着的，魔鬼自会给他那双闲着的手寻些恶事做。天亮前还剩好些钟头要打发，其中挑一个吊死我，倒也不算白费。”
“那我们一步也不离开你，”毕达哥拉斯斩钉截铁地说。
“你们这两个老稻草人，对着身边养着三十个雇钱的打手的斯陶滕堡老爷，能做什么？”
“你被人宰的时候，我们可不躲着装孬种。”
“你们就照我说的做，忠心的老伙计，”第欧根尼答道，比他素常要认真。“你们伏得低低的，千万小心，别把脑袋伸进说不定一会儿就吊上我这颗的同一条绳套。那也算不得装孬种——那出戏你们连字母都还没认全呢；你们记住：我仅剩的那一线机会——与其说是活命的机会，不如说是保全我最后一丝体面的机会——全落在你们的平安和行动自如上。”
“你什么意思？”
“小姐——”他低声道，“我发过誓要把她送回她父亲身边，我也不能让一个恶棍赢个全套。乱起来的时候——明天拂晓——头一件事想小姐。……乱起来你们能制住看守——冲进她所在的磨坊小屋……把她抢出来……马在小屋后头的棚里……你们未必顾得上我。”
“可是……”
“嘘——有人听。……”
“我们一个带小姐——另一个来帮你——”
“嘘……

===Original===

I may be a dead man by then﻿—the jongejuffrouw remember﻿—make for Ryswyk with her first of all﻿—thence straight to Haarlem﻿—to her father﻿—you can do it easily. A fortune awaits you if you bring her safely to him. Fulfil my pledge, old compeers, if I am not alive to do it myself. I don’t ask you to swear﻿—I know you’ll do it﻿—and if I must to the gallows first I’ll do so with a cry of triumph.”
“But you﻿ ﻿…”
“Silence!” he murmured again peremptorily, but more hoarsely this time for fatigue and loss of blood and tense excitement are telling upon his iron physique at last﻿—he is well-nigh spent and scarce able to speak. “Silence﻿—I can hear Jan’s footsteps. Here! quick! inside my boot﻿ ﻿… a wallet? Have you got it?” he added with a brief return to his habitual gaiety as he felt Socrates’ long fingers groping against his shins, and presently beheld his wallet in his compeer’s hand. “You will find money in there﻿—enough for the journey. Now quick into the night, you two﻿—disappear for the nonce, and anon when sauve qui peut rings in the air﻿—tonight or at dawn or whenever this may be, remember the jongejuffrouw first of all and when you are ready give the cry we all know so well﻿—the cry of the fox when it lures its prey. If I am not dangling on a gibbet by then, I shall understand. But quick now!﻿—Jan comes!﻿—Disappear I say!﻿ ﻿…”
Quietly and swiftly Socrates slipped the wallet with some of the money back into his friend’s boot, the rest he hid inside his own doublet.
Strange that between these men there was no need of oaths. Pythagoras and Socrates had said nothing: silent and furtive they disappeared into the darkness. Diogenes’ head sank down upon his breast with a last sigh of satisfaction. He knew that his compeers would do what he had asked. Jan’s footsteps rang on the hard-frozen ground﻿—silently the living circle had parted and the philosophers were swallowed up by the gloom.
Jan looks suspiciously at the groups of men who now stand desultorily around.
“Who was standing beside the prisoner just now?” he asks curtly.
“When, captain?” queries one of the men blandly.
“A moment ago. I was descending the steps. The lantern was close to the prisoner; I saw two forms﻿—that looked unfamiliar to me﻿—close to him.”
“Oh!” rejoined Piet the Red unblushingly, “it must have been my back that you saw, captain.

===Chinese===

到那时我兴许已是个死人——记住小姐——先带她奔赖斯韦克——再从那儿直取哈勒姆——去她父亲那儿——你们轻易办得到。把她平安带到，一笔财产等着你们。我若不能亲口兑现，老伙计们，替我兑现我的诺言。我不用你们起誓——我知道你们办得到——我若非先上绞架不可，我也是喊着一嗓子得胜上去。”
“可你……”
“闭嘴！”他又断然地低喝，这回嗓音更哑了——疲惫、失血和紧绷的亢奋，终于在他那铁打的身躯上显出威力——他几乎虚脱，话都快说不出了。“闭嘴——我听见扬的脚步了。来！快！我靴筒里……有只皮夹？拿到了没有？”苏格拉底细长的手指在他小腿上摸索，转眼间皮夹已握在伙计手里，他于是短暂地找回平日的快活，添了一句。“里头有钱——路上够使了。快，你们两个，钻进夜里去——这会儿先躲开；等‘各自逃命’的喊声在空中响起——今夜也好，拂晓也好，不管什么时候——头一件记着小姐；等你们得了手，就喊我们都熟的那声叫——狐狸诱猎物时的那声叫。到那时我若还没吊在绞架上，我自然明白。可现在快！——扬来了！——我叫你们躲开！……”
苏格拉底悄没声、麻利地把留了些钱的皮夹塞回朋友的靴筒，余下的钱藏进了自己怀里。
说来奇怪，这几个人之间从不需要起誓。毕达哥拉斯和苏格拉底一言未发：悄没声、鬼鬼祟祟地，他们隐进了黑暗。第欧根尼的头最后一次满足地叹着气垂到胸前。他知道他的伙计们会办到。扬的脚步敲在冻得铁硬的地面上——那活人的圈子悄无声息地分开，两位哲人被幽暗吞没了。
扬狐疑地打量着四下散立着的一堆堆人。
“方才谁站在囚徒旁边？”他简短地问。
“什么时候，头儿？”有人滑头滑脑地反问。
“刚不久。我正下台阶。风灯挨着囚徒；我看见两条人影——瞧着眼生——贴着他。”
“哦！”红发皮特脸不红心不跳地应道，“头儿，您瞧见的准是我的背影。

Willem and I were looking to see that the ropes had not given way. The prisoner is so restless.﻿ ﻿…”
Jan﻿—not altogether reassured﻿—goes up to the prisoner. He raises the lantern and has a good and comprehensive look at all the ropes. Then he examines the man’s face.
“What ho!” he cries, “a bottle of spiced wine from my wallet. The prisoner has fainted.”

威廉和我正查看绳子有没有松。这囚徒老不老实待着。……”
扬——心下终不踏实——走到囚徒跟前。他举起风灯，把所有绳子仔仔细细、上上下下看了一遍。然后端详那人的脸。
“喂嗬！”他喊道，“从我的褡裢里取一瓶香料酒来。囚徒晕过去了。”

## XXXVII Dawn / 三十七 拂晓
===Original===

What a commotion when dawn breaks at last; it comes grey, dull, leaden, scarce lighter than the night, the haze more dense, the frost more biting. But it does break at last after that interminable night of excitement and sleeplessness and preparations for the morrow.
Jan has never closed an eye, he has scarcely rested even, pacing up and down, in and out of those gargantuan beams, with the molens and its secrets towering above his head. Nor I imagine did those noble lords and mynheers up there sleep much during this night; but they were tired and lay like logs upon straw paillasses, living over again the past few hours, the carrying of heavy iron boxes one by one from the molens to the wooden bridge, the unloading there, the unpacking in the darkness, and the disposal of the death-dealing powder, black and evil smelling, which will put an end with its one mighty crash﻿—to tyranny and the Stadtholder’s life.
Tired they are but too excited to sleep: the last few hours are like a vivid dream; the preparation of the tinder, the arrangements, the position to be taken up by Beresteyn and Heemskerk, the two chosen lieutenants who will send the wooden bridge over the Schie flying in splinters into the air.
Van Does too has his work cut out. General in command of the forces﻿—foreign mercenaries and louts from the country﻿—he has Jan for able captain. The mercenaries and the louts know nothing yet of what will happen tomorrow﻿—when once the dawn has broken﻿—but they are well prepared; like beasts of the desert they can scent blood in the air; look at them polishing up their swords and cleaning their cullivers! they know that tomorrow they will fight, even though tonight they have had no orders save to see that one prisoner tied with ropes to a beam and fainting with exposure and loss of blood does not contrive to escape.
But the Lord of Stoutenburg is more wakeful than all. Like a caged beast of prey he paces up and down the low, narrow weighing-room of the molens, his hands tightly clenched behind his back, his head bare, his cloak cast aside despite the bitter coldness of the night.
Restless and like a beast of prey; his nostrils quiver with the lust of hate and revenge that seethes within his soul.

===Chinese===

天总算破晓时，好一阵骚动；那黎明来的是灰的、浊的、铅一般沉的，比夜色亮不了多少，雾更浓，霜更咬人。可经过那一夜无休无止的亢奋、无眠和为明天的张罗，它到底还是来了。
扬一眼都没合过，几乎连歇也没歇，来来回回地踱，在那些巨人般的梁木间穿进穿出，头顶上悬着磨坊和磨坊的秘密。我想，上头那些老爷先生们这一夜也没怎么睡；但他们乏了，直挺挺躺在草垫子上，像根根木头，一遍遍回味着方才那几个钟头：一只只沉重的铁箱从磨坊搬到木桥，桥头的卸载，黑暗里的开箱，还有那送命的火药如何安放——黑的，气味呛人，只消轰然一响，便了断——了断暴政，也了断执政的性命。
他们乏，却亢奋得睡不着：最后那几个钟头像一场鲜亮的梦；引火药的备制，种种布置，贝雷斯坦和海姆斯凯尔克要占的位置——这两位选定的副手，将把斯希河上的木桥炸成碎片送上天。
范·多斯也有他忙不完的差事。他统领全军——外邦的雇佣兵和乡下来的粗汉——手下有扬这员能干的队长。雇佣兵和粗汉们还不知道明天——只等天一亮——会发生什么；可他们准备停当；像荒漠里的野兽，他们闻得出空气里的血腥；瞧他们擦亮刀剑、收拾火绳枪那股劲！他们知道明天有仗打，虽说今夜接到的唯一号令，不过是看好一个囚徒——被绳子拴在梁上、因冻馁失血而昏迷的囚徒——别叫他脱逃。
可斯陶滕堡老爷比谁都清醒。他像一头笼中的猛兽，在磨坊低矮狭窄的称重室里来回踱步，双手紧紧攥在背后，光着头，大氅擞在一边，全不管夜气多么苦寒。
烦躁不安，像一头猛兽；他鼻孔翕张，满是被恨与复仇的欲焰——它们正在他灵魂里翻滚。

===Original===

Two men doth he hate with a consuming passion of hatred, the Stadtholder Prince of Orange, sovereign ruler of half the Netherlands, and a penniless adventurer whose very name is unknown.
Both these men are now in the power of the Lord of Stoutenburg. The bridge is prepared, the powder laid, tomorrow justice will be meted out to the tyrant; God alone could save him now, and God, of a surety, must be on the side of a just revenge. The other man is helpless and a prisoner; despite his swagger and his insolence, justice shall be meted out to him too; God alone could save him, and God, of a surety, could not be on the side of an impudent rogue.
These thoughts, which were as satisfying to the Lord of Stoutenburg as food placed at an unattainable distance is to a starving beast, kept him awake and pacing up and down the room after he had finished his work under the bridge.
He could not sleep for thinking of the prisoner, of the man’s insolence, of the humiliation and contempt wherewith every glance he had brought shame to his cheeks. The Lord of Stoutenburg could not sleep also for thinking of Gilda, and the tender, pitying eyes wherewith she regarded the prisoner, the gentle tone of her voice when she spoke to him, even after proof had been placed before her that the man was a forger and a thief.
The Lord of Stoutenburg could not sleep and all the demons of jealousy, of hatred and of revenge were chasing him up and down the room and whispering suggestions of mischief to be wrought, of a crime to be easily committed.
“While that man lives,” whispered the demon of hate in his ear, “thou wilt not know a moment’s rest. Tomorrow when thy hand should be steady when it wields the dagger against the Stadtholder, it will tremble and falter, for thoughts of that man will unsettle thy nerves and cause the blood to tingle in thy veins.”
“While that man lives,” whispered the demon of revenge, “thou wilt not know a moment’s rest. Thou wilt think of him and of his death, rather than of thy vengeance against the Stadtholder.”
“While that man lives,” whispered the demon of jealousy more insistently than did the other evil spirits, “Gilda will not cease to think of him, she will plead for him, she will try mayhap to save him and then﻿—”
And the Lord of Stoutenburg groaned aloud in the silence of the night, and paused in his restless walk.

===Chinese===

他恨两个人，恨得炽烈焚心：一个是执政奥兰治亲王，半壁尼德兰的君临之主；一个是身无分文的冒险家，连名字都无人知晓。
这两个人，如今都攥在斯陶滕堡老爷手心里。桥备好了，药埋下了，明天那暴君就要领受惩处；如今只有上帝救得了他，而上帝，笃定地，必站在正义复仇的一边。另一个束手就缚，已成囚徒；任他骄横，任他放肆，他也要领受惩处；只有上帝救得了他，而上帝，笃定地，绝不会站在一个无礼的无赖那一边。
这些念头之于斯陶滕堡老爷，就像搁在够不着的地方的食物之于一头饿兽——聊以慰饥，却更催饿——桥下的活计干完之后，它们让他醒着，在屋里来回踱步。
想着那囚徒，想着那人的放肆，想着他每一眼投来的轻蔑和羞辱——烧得他双颊发烫——他睡不着。想着吉尔达，想着她望那囚徒时眼神里的温柔怜惜，想着她同他说话时的轻声细语——哪怕把那人伪造文书、偷窃为盗的凭证摆到她眼前之后，依然如此——斯陶滕堡老爷也睡不着。
斯陶滕堡老爷睡不着，嫉妒、仇恨与复仇的群魔追得他在屋里团团转，附耳低语，撺掇他去干一桩祸事，一桩唾手可得的罪恶。
“那人一日不死，”恨魔在他耳边低语，“你就一日不得安生。明天，你的手本该稳稳地把匕首递向执政，它却会发抖、会迟疑，因为对那人的念头会搅乱你的心神，叫你血管里的血乱窜。”
“那人一日不死，”复仇魔低语，“你就一日不得安生。你满脑子会是他，是他的死，而不是你对执政的复仇。”
“那人一日不死，”嫉妒魔比别的邪灵更固执地低语，“吉尔达就断不了对他的念想，她会为他求情，她会想方设法救他，到那时——”
于是斯陶滕堡老爷在夜的沉寂里高声呻吟起来，烦躁的脚步也停住了。

===Original===

He drew a chair close to the table, and sat down; then resting his elbows upon the table, he buried his head in his hands, and remained thus motionless but breathing heavily like one whose soul is fighting a losing battle.
The minutes sped on. He had no means of gauging the time. It was just night, black impenetrable night. From down below came the murmur of all the bustle that was going on, the clang of arms, the measured footsteps which told of other alert human creatures who were waiting in excitement and tense expectancy for that dawn which still was far distant.
The minutes sped on, on the leaden feet of time. How long the Lord of Stoutenburg had sat thus, silent and absorbed, he could not afterwards have said. Perhaps after all he had fallen asleep, overcome with fatigue and with the constant sleeplessness of the past few days. But anon he was wide awake, slightly shivering with the cold. The tallow candle was spluttering, almost dying out. With a steady hand the Lord of Stoutenburg snuffed the smouldering wick, the candle flickered up again. Then he rose and quietly walked across the room. He pulled open the door and loudly called for Jan.
A few minutes later Jan was at the door, silent, sullen, obedient as usual.
“My lord called?” he asked.
“Yes,” replied Stoutenburg, “what hour is it?”
“Somewhere near six I should say, my lord. I heard the tower-clock at Ryswyk strike five some time ago.”
“How long is it before the dawn?”
“Two hours, my lord.”
“Time to put up a gibbet, Jan? and to hang a man?”
“Plenty of time for that, my lord,” replied Jan quietly.
“Then see to it, Jan, as speedily as you can. I feel that that man down below is our evil genius. While he lives Chance will be against us, of that I am as convinced as I am of the justice of our cause. If that man lives, Jan, the Stadtholder will escape us; I feel it in my bones: something must have told me this in the night﻿—it is a premonition that comes from above.”
“Then the man must not live, my lord,” said Jan coldly.
“You recognize that too, Jan, do you not?” rejoined Stoutenburg eagerly. “I am compelled in this﻿—I won’t say against my will, but compelled by a higher, a supernatural power. You, too, believe in the supernatural, do you not, my faithful Jan?”
“I believe, my lord, first and foremost in the justice of our cause. I hate the Stadtholder and would see him dead.

===Chinese===

他把一把椅子拖到桌边坐下；两肘支在桌上，头埋进双手里，就这样一动不动，只是呼吸沉重，像一个灵魂正在打一场败仗的人。
时间一分分飞逝。他没法计量时刻。只是夜，黑得透不进一丝光的夜。楼下传来忙乱的营营声，兵器的锿锵，还有规律的脚步声——那声音告诉他，还有别的不曾合眼的人，也正怀着兴奋和紧绷的期待，等候那还很远的黎明。
时间迈着铅一样的步子，一分分擞过去。斯陶滕堡老爷这样坐了多久，沉默，出神，事后他自己也说不上来。也许他终究是睡着了，被疲惫和连日的不眠压倒了。可不一会儿他又完全醒转，微微冷得发抖。脂油蜡烛噴咐作响，眼看要灭。斯陶滕堡老爷用沉稳的手剪了剪将熄的灯芯，烛火又窜了起来。随后他起身，静静穿过房间，拉开房门，高声唤扬。
几分钟后，扬到了门口，同往常一样，无言、阴沉、恭顺。
“大人叫我？”他问。
“是，”斯陶滕堡答道，“什么时辰了？”
“我想六点前后了，大人。赖斯韦克的钟楼敲五点，已有些时候了。”
“离天亮还有多久？”
“两个钟头，大人。”
“搭一座绞架，扬，来得及吗？再吊死一个人？”
“那是绰绰有余的，大人。”扬平静地回答。
“那这事就交给你，扬，越快越好。我觉出来了，底下那个人就是我们的凶神。他一日不死，运气就一日与我们作对；这一点我信得同信我们大业的正义一样死。那人若活着，扬，执政就会从我们手心溜走；我骨头缝里都觉得出来：夜里定有什么告诉了我——这是上头降下来的预感。”
“那这人就不能活，大人。”扬冷冷地说。
“你也看出来了，扬，是不是？”斯陶滕堡急切地应道。“这件事上我是受了逼迫——我不说违背我的意愿，是受了一种更高的、超自然的力量逼迫。你也信超自然，是不是，我忠心的扬？”
“我信的，大人，头一件、最要紧的一件，是我们大业的正义。我恨执政，巴不得他死。

===Original===

Nothing in the world must place that great aim of ours in jeopardy.”
Stoutenburg drew a deep breath of satisfaction.
“Then see to the gibbet, my good Jan,” he said in a firm almost lusty voice, “have it erected on the further side of the molens so that the jongejuffrouw’s eyes are not scandalized by the sight. When everything is ready come and let me know, and guard him well until then, Jan, guard him with your very life; I want to see him hang, remember that! Come and tell me when the gallows are ready and I’ll go to see him hang﻿ ﻿… I want to see him hang.﻿ ﻿…”
And Jan without another word salutes the Lord of Stoutenburg and then goes out.
And thus it is that a quarter of an hour later the silence of the night is broken by loud and vigorous hammering. Jan sees to it all and a gibbet is not difficult to erect.
Then men grumble of course; they are soldiers and not executioners, and their hearts for the most have gone out to that merry compeer﻿—the Laughing Cavalier﻿—with his quaint jokes and his cheerful laugh. He has been sleeping soundly too for several hours, but now he is awake. Jan has told him that his last hour has come: time to put up a gibbet with a few stiff planks taken from the storeroom of the molens and a length of rope.
He looks round him quite carelessly. Bah! death has no terrors for such a splendid soldier as he is. How many times hath he faced death ere this?﻿—why he was at Prague and at Madgeburg where few escaped with their lives. He bears many a fine scar on that broad chest of his and none upon his back. A splendid fighter, if ever there was one!
But hanging? Bah!
The men murmur audibly as plank upon plank is nailed. Jan directs operations whilst Piet the Red keeps guard over the prisoner. Two or three of the country louts know something of carpentering. They do the work under Jan’s watchful eye. They grumble but they work, for no one has been paid yet, and if you rebel you are like to be shot, and in any case you lose your pay.
And Diogenes leaning up against the beam watches with lazy quaintly smiling eyes the preparations that are going on not a hundred paces away from him. After a while the darkness all around is beginning to yield to the slow insistence of dawn. It rises slowly behind the veils of mist which still envelop the distant East.

===Chinese===

世间万事，都不能叫我等这桩大业陷于险地。”
斯陶滕堡深深吸进一口满足的气。
“那就去办绞架吧，我的好扬，”他说，嗓音坚定，几乎称得上雄赳赳，“把它立在磨坊那一头，免得那景象污了小姐的眼睛。一切齐备就来禀我；在那之前给我看好他，扬，拿你的性命看好他；我要亲眼看他吊起来，记住！绞架一妥当就来告诉我，我要去看他吊……我要亲眼看他吊。……”
扬再无一言，向斯陶滕堡老爷行了个礼，出去了。
于是一刻钟后，夜的寂静被响亮起劲的锤声敲破了。扬一手督办，搭一座绞架并非难事。
弟兄们自然要啼咕；他们是兵，不是刽子手，况且他们的心多半早已向着那位快活的伙计——笑面骑士——向着他的新鲜玩笑和他的痛快笑声。他酣睡了几个钟头，这会儿醒了。扬告诉他，他末一个钟头到了：是时候用几块从磨坊库房里拆来的硬木板和一段绳子，搭一座绞架了。
他环顾四近，满不在乎。呸！死吓不住他这样一条好汉。他从前多少回直面过死亡？——布拉格他去过，马格德堡他也去过，那两处活着出来的没有几个。他宽阔的胸前带着好些体面的伤疤，背上却一条也没有。天底下若有真好汉，他就是一条！
可绞刑？呸！
一块板子钉上又一块板子，弟兄们的怨声听得见地响着。扬督着工，红发皮特看守着囚徒。粗汉里有两三个懂点木匠活。他们在扬的眼皮底下干活。嘟囔归嘟囔，活照干——工钱还没有一个人拿到手，你若抗命，多半吃枪子，横竖工钱是别想领了。
第欧根尼斜倚着梁木，用一双慵懒、含着古怪笑意的眼睛，望着离他不足百步的这场张罗。过了一阵，四围的黑暗开始向黎明缓缓的坚持让步。黎明在仍裹着远方天际的雾幔后面，慢慢升起来。

===Original===

Gradually an impalpable greyness creeps around the molens, objects begin to detach themselves one by one out of the gloom, the moving figures of the mercenaries, the piles of arms heaped up here and there out of the damp, the massive beams slimy and green which support the molens, and a little further on the tall erection with a projecting arm round which great activity reigns.
Diogenes watches it all with those same lazy eyes, and that same good-humoured smile lingering round his lips. That tall erection over there which still looks ghostlike through the mist is for him. The game of life is done and he has lost. Death is there at the end of the projecting arm on which even now Jan is fixing a rope.
“Death in itself matters but little,” mused the philosopher with his gently ironical smile. “I would have chosen another mode than hanging﻿ ﻿… but after all ’tis swift and sure; and of course now she will never know.”
Know what, O philosopher? What is it that she﻿—Gilda﻿—with the fair curls and the blue eyes, the proud firm mouth and round chin﻿—what is it that she will never know?
She will never know that a nameless, penniless soldier of fortune has loved her with every beat of his heart, every thought of his brain, with every sinew and every aspiration. She will never know that just in order to remain near her, when she was dragged away out of Rotterdam he affronted deliberately the trap into which he fell. She will never know that for her dear sake, he has borne humiliation against which every nerve of his splendid nature did inwardly rebel, owning to guilt and shame lest her blue eyes shed tears for a brother’s sin. She will never know that the warning to the Stadtholder came from him, and that he was neither a forger nor a thief, only just a soldier of fortune with a contempt for death, and an unspoken adoration for the one woman who seemed to him as distant from him as the stars.
But there were no vain regrets in him now; no regret of life, for this he always held in his own hand ready to toss it away for a fancy or an ideal﻿—no regret of the might-have-been because he was a philosopher, and the very moment that love for the unattainable was born in his heart he had already realized that love to him could only mean a memory.

===Chinese===

一片难以捉摸的灰色渐渐爬满磨坊四围，物件开始从幽暗里一件件显出形来：走动着的雇佣兵的身影，这里一堆那里一堆、为避潮气架起的兵器，支撑磨坊的滑腻发绿的巨大梁木，还有稍远处那座带一条横臂的高架子——架子周围正忙得热火朝天。
第欧根尼用那双依旧慵懒的眼睛望着这一切，唇边依旧挂着那抹好脾气的笑。那边那座高架子——隔着雾还像鬼影似的——是为他搭的。人生的牌局打完了，他输了。死神就蹲在那条横臂的头上，这会儿扬正在横臂上系绳子。
“死本身算不得什么，”哲人带着他淡淡讥诮的笑想道，“换我自己挑，不会挑绞刑……不过它到底干脆、利落；当然啦，如今她永远不会知道了。”
不知道什么，哲人啊？她——吉尔达——那个金色鬈发、蓝眼睛、高贵坚定的嘴和圆圆的下巴的她——永远不会知道的是什么？
她永远不会知道，一个无名无姓、身无分文的雇佣冒险家，爱她用的是心口的每一次跳动、脑中的每一个念头、每一根筋络、每一分热望。她永远不会知道，只为了留在她近旁，当她被人从鹿特丹劫走时，他是自己迎着那个陷阱走过去、甘心落进去的。她永远不会知道，单单为了她，他忍下了那种屈辱——他天性里每一根神经都在内里呐喊反抗的屈辱——认下了罪名与羞耻，只为不让她的蓝眼睛为一个哥哥的罪流淌眼泪。她永远不会知道，给执政的警报出自他手；他既非伪造文书的骗子，也非偷儿，只是一个把死亡看得轻如鸿毛的冒险家，只揣着一句没说出口的倾慕，献给那个在他眼里远得像星辰的女人。
可他此刻没有无谓的追悔；不惋惜性命，性命从来就攥在他自己手里，为一时兴起、为一个理想，随时掷得出去；也不惋惜那些“倘若当初”，因为他是哲人——那份对不可得之物的爱才在他心里落地的那一刻，他早已明白，爱于他，注定只能是一段回忆。

===Original===

Therefore when he watched the preparations out there in the mist, and heard the heavy blows upon the wooden planks and the murmurs of his sympathizers at their work, he only smiled gently, self-deprecatingly, but always good-humouredly.
If the Lord of Stoutenburg only knew how little he really cared.

===Chinese===

所以，望着雾里那边的张罗，听着木板上一记记重锤、听着同情他的人们干活的咕哝，他只是浅浅地笑，自嘲地笑，却始终好脾气地笑着。
斯陶滕堡老爷若是知道，他真正把这一切看得多么轻，那才好呢。

## XXXVIII The Hour / 三十八 时辰
===Original===

A curiously timid voice roused the philosopher from his dreams.
“Is there aught I can do for you, sir? Alas! my friend the Lord Stoutenburg is deeply angered against you. I could do nothing with him on your behalf.”
Diogenes turned his head in the direction whence had come the voice. He saw Nicolaes Beresteyn standing there in the cold grey mist, with his fur cloak wrapped closely up to his chin, and his face showing above the cloak, white and drawn.
The situation was not likely to escape Diogenes’ irrepressible sense of humour.
“Mynheer Beresteyn,” he exclaimed; “Dondersteen! what brings your Mightiness here at this hour? A man on the point of death, sir, has no call for so pitiable a sight as is your face just now.”
“I heard from my Lord Stoutenburg what happened in the hut last night,” said Beresteyn in a faltering voice, and determined not to heed the other’s bantering tone. “You exonerated me before my sister﻿ ﻿… sir, this was a noble act﻿ ﻿… I would wish to thank you﻿ ﻿…”
“And do so with quaking voice and shaking knees,” quoth Diogenes with unalterable good-humour, through which there pierced however an obvious undercurrent of contempt. “Ye gods!” he added with a quaint sigh, “these men have not even the courage of their infamy!”
The words, the tone, the shrug of the shoulders which accompanied these, stung Nicolaes Beresteyn’s dormant dignity to the quick.
“I do not wonder,” he said more firmly, “that you feel bitter contempt for me now. Your generosity for which I did not crave hath placed me momentarily at a disadvantage before you. Yet believe me I would not be outdone by you in generosity; were it not for my allegiance to the Lord Stoutenburg I would go straight to my sister now and confess my guilt to her﻿ ﻿… You believe me I trust,” he added, seeing that Diogenes’ merry eyes were fixed mockingly upon him, “did fate allow it I would gladly change places with you even now.”
“I am about to hang, sir,” quoth Diogenes lightly.
“Alas!”
“And you are forced, you say, to play a craven’s part; believe me, sir, I would not change places with you for a kingdom.”
“I do believe you, sir,” rejoined Beresteyn earnestly, “yet I would have you think of me as something less of a coward than I seem. Were I to make full confession to my sister now, I should break her heart﻿—but it would not save your neck from the gallows.”

===Chinese===

一声出奇怯懦的嗓音，把哲学家从梦里唤醒。
“有什么我能为您效劳的吗，先生？唉！我的朋友斯陶滕堡老爷对您动了大怒。我替您说不动他。”
第欧根尼（Diogenes）朝声音来处转过头去。他看见尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦（Nicolaes Beresteyn）站在冷灰的雾里，皮氅一直裹到下巴，氅上的那张脸，白而扭曲。
这样的场面，第欧根尼那压不住的谐谑感，是断不会放过的。
“贝雷斯坦先生（Mynheer Beresteyn），”他扬声道，“该死的雷公！什么风把您这尊贵人吹到这儿来了？一个将要死的人，先生，实在用不着看您眼下这张可怜的脸。”
“我听斯陶滕堡老爷说了昨夜小屋里的事，”贝雷斯坦结结巴巴地说，打定主意不去理会对方话里的调侃，“您在我妹妹面前开脱了我的罪……先生，这是高尚的举动……我想谢您……”
“于是嗓音发着抖、膝盖打着颤来谢，”第欧根尼说，那副好脾气纹丝不动，底下却分明渗着一股轻蔑的暗流。“诸神啊！”他又古怪地叹了一声，“这帮人，连干坏事的胆量都没有！”
这些话，这个腔调，还有随之而来的那一下耸肩，把尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦沉睡的自尊扎了个透。
“您如今对我怀着刻骨的轻蔑，我不奇怪，”他说得坚定了些，“您那份我并没乞求的慷慨，一时间叫我在您面前矮了半截。可请相信我，论慷慨我不会输给您；要不是我忠于斯陶滕堡老爷，我这会儿就径直去见我妹妹，向她和盘招认我的罪……您信我吧，我想您是信的，”他见第欧根尼那双快活的眼睛正嘲弄地盯住他，又补了一句，“倘若命运许可，就是此刻，我也甘愿同您换一换位置。”
“我就要上绞架了，先生。”第欧根尼轻飘飘地说。
“唉！”
“而您呢，照您说，是被迫扮一个懦夫的角色；请相信我，先生，拿一个王国来换，我也不愿同您换位置。”
“这个我信，先生，”贝雷斯坦恳切地应道，“可我盼您把我看得比表面上的我少几分懦气。我此刻若向妹妹彻底招认，会伤了她的心——却救不下您脖子上这架绞索。”

===Original===

“And a rogue’s neck, sir, is of such infinitely less value than a good woman’s heart. So I pray you say no more about it. Death and I are old acquaintances, oft hath he nodded to me en passant, we are about to become closer friends, that is all.”
“Some day my sister shall know, sir, all that you have done for her and for me.”
The ghost of a shadow passed over the Laughing Cavalier’s face.
“That, sir, I think had best remain ’twixt you and me for all times. But this I would have you know, that when I accepted the ignoble bargain which you proposed to me in my friend Hals’ studio, I did so because I thought that the jongejuffrouw would be safer in my charge then than in yours!”
Beresteyn was about to retort more hotly when Jan, closely followed by half a dozen men, came with swift, firm footsteps up to the prisoner. He saluted Beresteyn deferentially as was his wont.
“Your pardon, mynheer,” he said, “my lord hath ordered that the prisoner be forthwith led to execution.”
Nicolaes’ pale face became the colour of lead.
“One moment, Jan,” he said, “one moment. I must speak with my lord﻿ ﻿… I﻿ ﻿…”
“My lord is with the jongejuffrouw,” said Jan curtly, “shall I send to tell him that you desire to speak with him?”
“No﻿—no﻿—that is I﻿ ﻿… I﻿ ﻿…” stammered Nicolaes who, indeed, was fighting a cruel battle with his own weakness, his own cowardice now. It was that weakness which had brought him to the abject pass in which he now stood, face to face with the man he had affected to despise, and who was about to die, laden with the crimes which he Nicolaes had been the first to commit.
Stoutenburg’s influence over him had been paramount, through it he had lost all sense of justice, of honour and of loyalty; banded with murderers he had ceased to recognize the very existence of honesty, and now he was in such a plight morally, that though he knew himself to be playing an ignoble role, he did not see the way to throw up the part and to take up that of an honest man. One word from him to Gilda, his frank confession of his own guilt, and she would so know how to plead for the condemned man that Stoutenburg would not dare to proceed with this monstrous act.
But that word he had not the courage to speak.

===Chinese===

“何况一个恶棍的脖子，先生，同一个好女子的心相比，价钱本就贱得没法比。所以这事请您不必再提。死神和我是老相识了，他常在路过时朝我点点头；我们不过就要处得更近乎些，仅此而已。”
“终有一天，我妹妹会知道，先生，您为她、为我做下的一切。”
一抹淡淡的阴影掠过笑面骑士（the Laughing Cavalier）的脸。
“那件事，先生，我想最好永远只留在你我二人之间。不过有一点我要让您知道：当初在朋友哈尔斯的画室里，我接受您提的那桩不上台面的交易，是因为我认定，小姐交在我手里，总比交在您手里稳当！”
贝雷斯坦正要更火爆地回嘴，扬带着身后紧随的六条汉子，迈着又快又稳的脚步走到囚徒跟前。他照着素来的规矩，恭恭敬敬向贝雷斯坦行了个礼。
“恕我打搅，先生，”他说，“老爷有令，囚徒即刻押去处刑。”
尼古拉斯苍白的脸变成了铅色。
“等一等，扬，”他说，“等一等。我必须同老爷说话……我……”
“老爷正陪着小姐，”扬简短地说，“要不要我派人禀告，说您想同他说话？”
“不——不——就是说，我……我……”尼古拉斯结结巴巴。此刻他确实正同自己的软弱、自己的怯懦打着一场残酷的仗。正是那份软弱，把他带到眼下这卑贱的境地，同这个他一向装作鄙视的人面对面站着——而这个人就要死了，身上驮着的，正是他尼古拉斯最先犯下的那些罪。
斯陶滕堡对他的影响向来是压倒一切的；借着这影响，他失去了对公道、名誉和忠义的一切知觉；同杀人凶手们结成一伙之后，他连诚实这东西的存在都不认得了。如今他在德行上落到这般田地：明知自己扮的是个下作角色，却找不到脱下这身戏服、改扮一个正人的路。只消他对吉尔达说一句话，坦白招认自己的罪，她自然会懂得怎样替这判了死的人求情，求到斯陶滕堡不敢把这件伤天害理的事做下去。
可那句话，他没有勇气说出口。

===Original===

With dull eyes and in sullen silence he watched Piet the Red untying under Jan’s orders the ropes which held the prisoner to the beam, and then securing others to keep his arms pinioned behind his back. The mist now was of a faint silvery grey, and the objects around had that mysterious hushed air which the dawn alone can lend. The men, attracted by the sight of a fellow creature in his last living moments, had gathered together in close knots of threes and fours. They stood by, glowering and sombre, and had not Jan turned a wilfully deaf ear to their murmurings he would have heard many an ugly word spoken under their breath.
These were of course troublous and fighting times, when every man’s hand was against some other, when every able-bodied man was firstly a soldier and then only a peaceable citizen. Nor was the present situation an uncommon one: the men could not know what the prisoner had done to deserve this summary punishment. He might have been a spy﻿—an informer﻿—or merely a prisoner of war. It was no soldier’s place to interfere, only to obey orders and to ask no questions.
But they gave to the splendid personality of the condemned man the tribute of respectful silence. Whilst Jan secured the slender white hands of the prisoner, and generally made those awful preparations which even so simple a death as hanging doth demand, jests and oaths were stilled one by one among these rough fighting men, not one head but was uncovered, not a back that was not straightened, not an attitude that was not one of deference and attention. Instinct﻿—that unerring instinct of the soldier﻿—had told them that here was no scamp getting his just reward, but a brave man going with a careless smile to his death.
“Has mynheer finished with the prisoner,” asked Jan when he saw that Piet had finished his task and that the prisoner was ready to be led away. “Is there aught your greatness would still desire to say to him?”
“Only this,” said Beresteyn firmly, “that were his hands free I would ask leave to grasp them.”
A look of kindly amusement fell from the prisoner’s eyes upon the pale face of the young man.
“I have never known you, sir, save by a quaint nickname,” continued Beresteyn earnestly, “but surely you have kith and kin somewhere. Have you no father or mother living whom you will leave to mourn?”

===Chinese===

他目光滞涩，阴沉地一言不发，看着红发皮特（Piet the Red）奉扬的命令解开把囚徒绑在梁上的绳索，又另换几道，把他的双臂反缚在背后。这会儿雾已是淡淡银灰色，四周的物事都罩着一种只有黎明才给得出的、神秘而屏息的神气。那些人被一个同类临终的景象引了来，三个一簇、四个一伙地聚拢。他们站在一旁，虎着脸，阴沉沉的；若不是扬存心装聋，一定会听见他们压着嗓子说的许多难听话。
这自然是乱世，是动刀兵的年月：人人伸手掐着别人的脖子，凡是拿得动刀枪的汉子，先是兵，其次才轮得到做安分的百姓。眼前的场面也不算稀罕：这伙人无从知道囚徒干了什么，该领这份就地正法。他也许是奸细——告密的——也许不过是个战俘。当兵的不该多管，只该听令，不该多问。
可对这位待死之人堂堂的气度，他们献上了敬肃的沉默做贡礼。扬捆缚囚徒那双细白的手，做着绞刑这样简单的死法也少不了的那几样可怕的预备时，这些粗豪的武人中间，笑话和咒骂一声接一声地歇了；没有一颗头不光着，没有一条背不挺直，没有一副姿态不是恭立注目的。本能——士兵那从不失手的本能——告诉他们：眼前不是恶棍领他应得的报，而是一条好汉，带着满不在乎的笑，走向他的死。
“先生同囚徒的话，说完了吗？”扬见皮特完事了、囚徒已可押走，便问道。“您这位贵人，还有什么话要对他说的吗？”
“只有一句，”贝雷斯坦坚定地说，“倘若他的手是自由的，我想求个准，同他握一握。”
囚徒的眼里落下一抹温厚的笑意，罩在年轻人苍白的脸上。
“我从不认得您，先生，只认得一个古怪的诨名，”贝雷斯坦恳切地接着说，“可您总不会没有亲故吧。您没有健在的父亲母亲，留他们为您哀悼吗？”

===Original===

The prisoner made no immediate reply, the smile of kindly amusement still lingered round his lips, but presently with an instinctive gesture of pride, he threw back his head and looked around him, as one who has nothing to fear and but little to regret. He met the sympathetic glance cast on him by the man who had done him﻿—was still doing him﻿—an infinite wrong, and all round those of his mute and humble friends who seemed to be listening eagerly now for the answer which he would give to Mynheer. Then with a quick sweep his eyes suddenly rested on the wooden erection beyond the molens that loomed out so tragically through the mist, pointing with its one weird arm to some infinite distance far away.
Something in the gentle pathos of this humble deference that encompassed him, something mayhap in the solemnity of that ghostly arm suddenly seemed to melt the thin crust of his habitual flippancy. He looked back on Beresteyn and said softly:
“I have a friend, Frans Hals﻿—the painter of pictures﻿—tell him when next you see him that I am glad his portrait of me is finished, and that I asked God to bless him for all his goodness has meant to me in the past.”
“But your father, sir,” urged Beresteyn, “your kindred.﻿ ﻿…”
“My father, sir,” replied Diogenes curtly, “would not care to hear that his son had died upon the gallows.”
Beresteyn would have spoken again but Jan interposes once more, humbly but firmly.
“My lord’s orders,” he now says briefly, “and time presses, mynheer.”
Beresteyn stands back, smothering a sigh. Jan on ahead, then Piet the Red and the six soldiers with the prisoner between them. A few steps only divide them from the gruesome erection that looms more solidly now out of the mist. Beresteyn, wrapping his head up in his cloak to shut out sound and sight, walks rapidly away in the opposite direction.

===Chinese===

囚徒没有立刻回答，那抹温厚的笑还挂在唇边；不多时，他带着一个出于天性的骄傲姿势，仰起头环顾四周，像一个无所畏惧、也无可追悔的人。他接住那个人投来的怜惜的一瞥——那人亏负过他、且此刻仍在亏负他，亏负到无穷；他又接住四下里那些沉默而谦卑的朋友们的一圈目光——他们此刻仿佛都急切候着他会给先生的回答。随后他的目光倏地一扫，落在了磨坊那边那座木架子上：它从雾里凄凄惶惶地显出来，用它那条怪异的手臂，直指着无穷远处。
这份环绕着他的谦卑里温柔的哀婉，也许还有那条鬼影般的手臂的庄严肃穆，有一样东西，忽然间仿佛把他素常轻佻的薄壳融化了。他回望贝雷斯坦，轻声说：
“我有一个朋友，弗兰斯·哈尔斯（Frans Hals）——画画的画家——下次见着他，请告诉他：我很高兴，他替我画的那幅像画完了；还告诉他，我求了上帝赐福给他，谢他往日待我的一切好处。”
“可您的父亲呢，先生，”贝雷斯坦追问，“您的亲眷。……”
“我的父亲，先生，”第欧根尼简短地答，“不会乐意听说他儿子死在绞架上。”
贝雷斯坦还想再说什么，扬却又一次插进来，谦卑而坚决。
“老爷的命令，”他这会儿说得干脆，“时候紧了，先生。”
贝雷斯坦退到一旁，咽下一声叹息。扬在前头，随后是红发皮特和六名士兵，囚徒夹在他们中间。再有几步，他们就到那座狰狞的木架跟前了——它此刻从雾里显出更结实的轮廓。贝雷斯坦把头裹进氅里，隔断声与光，快步朝相反的方向走了。

## XXXIX “Sauve Qui Peut” / 三十九 “各自逃命”
===Original===

Then it is that, out of the thickness of the fog a figure suddenly emerges running and panting: a man has fallen up against the group of soldiers who have just halted beside the gibbet.
“It is Lucas of Sparendam come back from Delft,” they cry as soon as they recognize the stained face, wet with the frost and the mist.
Already Jan﻿—who with Piet’s help was busy with the rope﻿—has heard the name. His wan, thin face has become the colour of ashes.
“Lucas of Sparendam back from Delft,” he murmurs, “the Lord save us all!”
Lucas of Sparendam was sent yesterday to Delft by the Lord of Stoutenburg to spy and to find out all that was going on inside the Prinzenhof where slept the Stadtholder and his bodyguard of one hundred men-at-arms: and now he has come back running and panting: his clothes torn, his face haggard and spent. He has run all the way from Delft﻿—a matter of a league and a half! Why should a man half kill himself by endeavouring to cover a league and a half in one hour?
“A drop of hot wine for Lucas,” cries one of the soldiers. “He is faint.”
The other men﻿—there are close on forty all told﻿—crowd round the gibbet now, those in charge of the prisoner have much ado to keep the space clear. They don’t say anything just yet, but there is a strange, restless look in their eyes and their lips tremble with all the unspoken questions. Only two men remain calm and silent, Jan has never ceased in his task of adjusting the ropes, and the prisoner stands quite still, bound with cords, and neither looking on Lucas nor yet on the gibbet above him. His eyes are half closed and there is a strained look on his merry face as if he were trying to listen to something that was too far off to hear.
But one man in the meanwhile is ready with the bottle of spiced wine, the best cordial there is for a fainting man. The others make way for him so that he can minister to Lucas. And Lucas drinks the wine eagerly, then he opens his eyes.
“We are betrayed,” he murmurs.
“Great God!” exclaims Jan dully.
“Betrayed!”
“What does it mean?”
No one heeds the prisoner now. They all crowd around Lucas. Jan calls out his orders in vain: Piet the Red alone listens to what he says, the others all want to know what Lucas means. They had been in the thick of a plot of course, they all knew that: a guet-apens had been prepared by the Lord of Stoutenburg for the Stadtholder whom he hates.

===Chinese===

就在这时，浓雾深处猛地冲出一条奔跑喘息的人影：一个人一头撞在刚停在绞架旁的那队士兵身上。
“是卢卡斯·范·斯帕伦丹，从代尔夫特回来了！”他们一认出那张叫霜和雾浸湿的、满是污渍的脸，便嚷起来。
扬——他正和皮特一起摆弄绳子——已经听见了这个名字。他那张清瘦的灰白脸，顿时成了死灰的颜色。
“卢卡斯·范·斯帕伦丹从代尔夫特回来了，”他喃喃道，“主啊，救救我们大家！”
卢卡斯·范·斯帕伦丹昨天奉斯陶滕堡老爷之命去了代尔夫特，去探听普林森霍夫里的一切动静——执政带着他一百名卫士就宿在那里面：此刻他跑着喘着回来了：衣裳撕烂了，脸憔悴不堪、精疲力竭。他是从代尔夫特一路跑来的——足足一里格半！一个人何苦拼命，硬要在一个钟头里赶完一里格半？
“给卢卡斯弄口热酒，”一名士兵喊道，“他要晕了。”
其余的人——总共将近四十个——此刻全围拢到绞架跟前，看守囚徒的那几个费了好大的劲才把地方腾出来。他们一时还都不作声，可眼睛里有一种古怪的不安，嘴唇被一肚子没出口的问题抖索着。只有两个人沉静无声：扬从没停下手里调理绳子的活计；囚徒一动不动站着，绳索缚身，既不朝卢卡斯看，也不朝头顶的绞架看。他双眼半阖，那张快活的脸上有一种绷紧的神情，仿佛在侧耳听一样太远、听不真切的声音。
好在有一个人已备好了那瓶香料酒——晕倒的人最好的提神物。众人给他让开路，好让他照应卢卡斯。卢卡斯急急灌下酒，睁开了眼睛。
“我们暴露了。”他喃喃道。
“老天爷！”扬木然失声。
“暴露了！”
“这是什么话？”
这会儿谁也顾不上囚徒了。众人全围住了卢卡斯。扬白费力气地喊着号令：只有红发皮特一人还听他的话，旁人都只想弄明白卢卡斯是什么意思。他们全都卷在一桩阴谋的正中心，这个他们心里都有数：斯陶滕堡老爷给他痛恨的执政设下了一个埋伏（guet-apens）。

===Original===

The heavy boxes of course﻿—gunpowder﻿ ﻿… to blow up the wooden bridge when the Stadtholder and his escort are halfway across!
Of course they had all guessed it, thought on it all through the night while they polished the arms﻿—the swords and the pistols and the cullivers﻿—which had been served out to them. They had guessed of course﻿—the foreign mercenaries who were always in the thick of every conspiracy and well paid for being so﻿—they had been the first to guess and they had told the country louts who only grinned enjoying the prospect of the fun.
But now they were betrayed. Lucas of Sparendam had come back with the news, and even Jan stopped in his hideous task in order to listen to what he had to say.
“It all happened yesterday,” quoth Lucas as soon as he had recovered his breath, “the rumour began in the lower quarters of the town. Nobody knows who began it. Some say that a foreigner came into the city in the early morning and sat down at one of the taverns to eat and drink with the Prince’s soldiers.”
“A foreigner?”
Jan turns to look on the prisoner and encounters his mocking glance. Smothering a curse he resumes his task of adjusting the rope upon the gibbet, but his fingers are unsteady and his work doth not progress.
“Yes, a foreigner,” continued Lucas volubly, “though it all has remained very mysterious. The Prince’s soldiers spoke of it amongst themselves﻿ ﻿… the foreigner had said something about a guet-apens, a plot against the Stadtholder’s life on his way to the North﻿ ﻿… then one of the officers heard the rumour and carried it to one of his superiors﻿ ﻿… By the evening it had reached the Stadtholder’s ears.”
“Then what happened?” they all asked eagerly.
“Nothing for some hours,” replied Lucas, “but I know that spies were sent round in every direction, and that by midnight there was general talk in the city that the Stadtholder would not continue his journey to the North. When the captain of the guard came to him for orders the Prince said curtly: ‘We do not start tomorrow!’ As soon as I heard of this I made preparations. It was then an hour after midnight. I was still alert and listening: all around me﻿—as I made ready to leave the city﻿—I heard rumours among the soldiers and spies of the Stadtholder, of their knowledge of a lonely spot﻿—a deserted molens﻿—near Ryswyk where they declared many men did lately congregate.

===Chinese===

那些沉重的铁箱，自然是——火药……等执政和他的卫队走到桥心，就把那座木桥炸上天！
他们自然全都猜着了，擦着发下来的兵器——刀剑、手枪、火绳枪——的那一整夜，他们把这事想了又想。他们自然猜着了——那些外邦雇佣兵，哪桩阴谋里都少不了他们的份，也回回拿足酬劳——他们头一个猜破，又说给了乡下来的粗汉们听，粗汉们只咧着嘴笑，巴不得这场热闹。
可如今他们暴露了。卢卡斯·范·斯帕伦丹带着消息回来了，连扬都停下了他那份狰狞的活计，好听他说。
“都是昨天的事，”卢卡斯一缓过气就说，“谣言是从城下区起的头。没人知道是谁先传的。有人说，一早有个外国人进城，在一家酒馆坐下，同亲王的士兵们一起吃喝。”
“外国人？”
扬回过头去看那囚徒，正撞上他讥诮的目光。他把一句咒骂咽回去，接着干绞架上理绳子的活，可手指发颤，活计全无进展。
“是的，一个外国人，”卢卡斯滔滔不绝地接着说，“不过这事从头到尾透着蹊跷。亲王的士兵们私下传说……那外国人说了些什么埋伏啦、暗算啦，说执政北上途中有人要害他的性命……后来一名军官听见了风声，稟到了他的上官那里……到傍晚，就传到执政耳朵里了。”
“后来怎样？”众人急问。
“有几个钟头没动静，”卢卡斯答道，“可我知道探子已撒向四面八方；到半夜，满城都在传，说执政不继续北上了。卫队长来请令时，亲王只干脆地说了一句：‘我们明天不动身！’我一听说这个，立刻收拾。那时刚过后半夜一点。我仍旧竖着耳朵四下听：在我打点出城的当口，四围全是执政的兵丁和探子的传言，说他们已经知道一个僻静地方——一座废弃的磨坊——就在赖斯韦克附近，他们断言近来常有大批人聚在那里。

===Original===

I heard too that soon after dawn the Prince’s guard would make straight for the molens, so I put on my snow shoes and started to run, despite the darkness and the fog, for we are all betrayed and the Stadtholder’s soldiers will be on us in a trice.”
Hardly are the words out of Lucas Sparendam’s mouth than the commotion begins, the disbanding; there is a roar and a bustle and a buzz: metal clashing, men rushing, cries of “we are betrayed! sauve qui peut!”
At first there is a general stampede for the places where the arms are kept﻿—the muskets, the swords and cullivers﻿—but these are thrown down almost as soon as they are picked up. They are no use now and worse than useless in a flight. But pistols are useful, in case of pursuit. “Quick, turn, fire!﻿ ﻿… so where are the pistols?﻿ ﻿… Jan, where are those pistols?”
There are not enough to go round: about a dozen were served out last night, and there are forty pairs of hands determined to possess one at least. So they begin to fight for them, tearing one another to pieces, shouting execrations, beating round with bare fists, since the other arms have already been laid down.
Now the confusion becomes worse than any that might reign among a herd of animals who are ready to rend one another: they tear the clothes off one another’s back, the skin off one another’s face: fear﻿—hideous, overwhelming, abject fear, has made wild beasts of these men. The mist envelops them, it is barely light in this basement beneath the molens: lanterns have long ago been kicked into extinction. The hot breath of forty panting throats mingles with the mist, and the heat of human bodies fever-heated with passion, fights against the strength of the frost. The frozen ground yields under the feet, clots of mud are thrown up by the stampede, from the beams up aloft the heavy icicles melt and drip monotonously, incessantly down upon those faces, red and perspiring in an agony of demented fear.
Jan and Piet the Red stand alone beside the prisoner: a sense of duty, of decency hath kept their blood cool. Until they are relieved from their post of guarding this man by orders from their lord, they will not move. Let the others rage and scream and tumble over one another, there must be at least a few soldiers among this rabble.
And the prisoner looks on all this confusion with eyes that dance and sparkle with the excitement of what is yet to come.

===Chinese===

我还听说天一亮，亲王的卫队就直扑这座磨坊，于是我套上雪鞋就跑，天黑雾大也顾不得——我们全暴露了，执政的兵转眼就到。”
卢卡斯·范·斯帕伦丹的话音未落，骚动就开始了，队伍散了架；一片吼声、一阵忙乱、一团嗡嗡：铁器相撞，人影奔突，喊成一片：“暴露了！各自逃命（sauve qui peut）！”
起初众人一窝蜂抢向存兵器的地方——火枪、刀剑、火绳枪——可这些东西到手几乎立刻又扔下了。这时候它们没用，逃起命来更是累赘。手枪却有用，万一有人追。“快，转身，开火！……手枪呢？……扬，手枪在哪儿？”
僧多粥少：昨夜统共发下十来支，却有四十双手，个个铁了心至少要摸到一支。于是他们抢起来了，你撕我打，咒骂连天，赤手空拳乱挥——别的兵器早撂下了。
这会儿的乱劲，比一群要互相撕咬的牲口还要不堪：他们扯下彼此背上的衣裳，抓破彼此脸上的皮肉：恐惧——骇人的、压倒一切的、丧尽体面的恐惧——把这些人都变成了野兽。雾裹着他们，磨坊底下这地窖里天光稀薄：风灯早叫人踢灭了。四十条喘息的喉咙里喷出的热气混进雾里，一具具烧得滚烫的身子，同严寒较着劲。冻地在脚下踩得发软，奔逃的人潮翻起一块块冻泥；头顶的梁上，沉重的冰凌融化着，单调地、不住地滴落在那些脸上——一张张在癫狂的恐惧里熬煎得通红冒汗的脸。
扬和红发皮特独自守在囚徒身旁：责任感和体面感让他们的血还是凉的。不等老爷下令换下他们看守此人的岗，他们就一步不动。让旁人去咆哮、去嚎叫、去滚作一团吧，这帮乌合之众里，总得有几个像样的兵。
而那囚徒望着这一片混乱，眼睛里闪动着光，为一个就要到来的什么，兴奋得发亮。

===Original===

Torn rags and broken accoutrements soon lie in a litter in the mud, trampled in by forty pairs of feet. There is not one face now that is not streaked with blood, not one throat that is not hoarse with terror﻿—the terror of the unknown.
In vain Jan from his post beside the prisoner shouts, harangues, appeals, threatens! A fight? yes! defeat? why not? but betrayal!﻿ ﻿… no, no, let’s away. The Stadtholder is fiercer than any Inquisitor of Spain﻿ ﻿… his cruelty last February almost turned the nation against him. But now﻿—this second conspiracy﻿—Stoutenburg again! what hope for his followers?
The horrors of last February perpetrated in the Gevangen Poort of ’S Graven Hage still cause many a rough cheek to blanch at their recollection. Men had gone mad who had heard the cries which pierced those stone walls then. One executioner had thrown down his bloody tools and fled from the place like one possessed! Van Dyk and Korenwinder, Slatius and the rest had been in hell ere a merciful death at last released them from the barbaric cruelty of the Prince of Orange.
“No, no! such a fate cannot be risked. We are betrayed! let us fly!”
Suddenly one man starts to run.
“I am for the coast!” he shouts, and incontinently takes to his heels.
“Sauve qui peut!”
Like irresponsible creatures they throw down the very weapons for which they have been fighting. The one man has given the signal for the run. Everything now is thrown aside, there is no thought save for flight.
A splashing of the mud, a general shout, a scramble, a clatter﻿—they run﻿—they run﻿—crying to those who are behind to follow and run too.
In five minutes the dark basement is clear of noise﻿—a litter of broken arms lies in one heap close by, others are scattered all over the ground in the mud, together with torn clothing, rags of leather and of cloth and great red pools that mingle with the melted ice.
The mist surrounds it all, this abandoned battle field wherein fear was the victor over man. The swiftly flying figures are soon swallowed up by the grey wall which lies dense and heavy over the lowland around; for a time they appear like ghosts with blurred outlines of torn doublets and scraps of felt hats placed awry; then the outline gets more dim as they run, and the kindly mist hides them from view.
Under the molens all is silent now. Jan and Piet the Red guard the prisoner alone.

===Chinese===

撕烂的破布、断裂的行头，很快东一片西一片陷在烂泥里，叫四十双脚踩进泥中去。这会儿没有一张脸不带血道子，没有一副嗓子不叫恐惧——对未知的恐惧——喊哑。
扬在囚徒旁边他的岗位上喊着、训着、劝着、吓着，全是白费！打一仗？行！吃了败仗？又有何妨！可暴露了！……不，不，快走。执政比西班牙的哪个宗教法官都狠……他去年二月的酷烈，几乎叫全国倒戈。而如今——这第二桩阴谋——又是斯陶滕堡！他的党羽还有什么指望？
去年二月海牙狱门监狱（Gevangen Poort）里造下的那些恐怖，如今想起来，仍叫多少张粗豪的脸刷地变白。当年穿透那些石墙的惨叫，听过的人都疯了！有一个刽子手擞下血淋淋的家什，像中了邪似的从那地方逃了！范·戴克和科伦温德，斯拉蒂乌斯（Slatius）和其余诸人，早在慈悲的死神最后把他们从奥兰治亲王的野蛮酷刑里解脱出来之前，就已经下过地狱了。
“不，不！这样的下场赌不得。我们暴露了！快飞跑！”
猛地有一个人撒腿跑了。
“我奔海边去！”他喊着，说跑就跑。
“各自逃命！”
像一群没了魂的畜生，他们把方才还在抢的兵器也扔了。那一个人打了逃跑的信号。这会儿什么都擞下了，脑子里只剩一个念头：逃。
烂泥四溅，喊声大作，跌跌撞撞，一片杂沓——他们跑——他们跑——还朝落在后头的喊，叫他们跟着一起跑。
五分钟后，黑地窖里没了声响——一堆断枪残械就近堆作一处，其余的散落满地，陷在烂泥里，同撕烂的衣裳、皮带布条的碎片混在一起，还有一摊摊化开的冰水里掺着的大片血红。
雾把这一切都围拢了，这座被丢弃的战场——在这里，恐惧战胜了人。疾奔的身影很快被那堵又浓又沉、压在四野低地上的灰墙吞没；有一阵，他们还像些鬼影，看得清破上衣的模糊轮廓和歪戴的毡帽碎片；再跑下去轮廓就愈发淡了，好心的雾把他们遮得没了踪影。
磨坊底下，这会儿一片寂静。扬和红发皮特独自看守着囚徒。

===Original===

The gallows are ready or nearly so, but there is no one to send to the Lord of Stoutenburg to tell him this﻿—as he hath commanded﻿—so that he may see this man hang whom he hates. And it would not be safe to leave the prisoner unguarded. Only from time to time Jan looks to see that the ropes still hold fast, but for the most part his eyes are fixed upon the mist on his left, for that way lies Delft, and from thence will loom out by and by the avenging hordes sent by the Prince of Orange.
Now that all those panting, perspiring human creatures have gone, the frost is more bitter, more biting than before; but neither Piet nor Jan seem to heed it, though their flesh is blue with the cold. Overhead there is a tramp of feet; the noble mynheers must have heard the confusion, they must have seen the flight; they are even now preparing to do in a slightly more dignified way what the foreign mercenaries and the louts from the country have done so incontinently.
The prisoner, hearing this tramp of feet over his head, looks more alertly around him. He sees that Jan and Piet have remained on guard even whilst the others have fled. He also sees the pile of heaped-up arms, the broken metal, the rags and the mud, and through the interstices of the wooden steps the booted feet of the mynheers running helter-skelter down; and a mad, merry laugh﻿—that holds a world of joy in its rippling tones﻿—breaks from his lips.
The next moment from far away comes a weird cry through the mist. A fox on the alert tries to lure his prey with that quaint cry of his, which appeals to the young birds and encourages them to come. What should a fox be doing on these ice-covered tracks? he must have strayed from very far, from over the moor mayhap beyond Gonda; hunger no doubt hath made a wanderer of him, an exile from his home.
Jan listens﻿—greatly astonished﻿—what should a fox be doing here? Piet is impassive, he knows nothing of the habits of foxes; sea-wolves are more familiar to him. With his eyes Jan instinctively questions the prisoner:
“What should a fox be doing here on these icebound flats?” he mutely asks.
But the prisoner apparently cares nothing about the marvels of nature, cares nothing about exiled foxes. His head is erect, his eyes dance with glee, a happy smile lights up his entire face.
Jan remembered that the others last night had called the wounded man the Laughing Cavalier.

===Chinese===

绞架齐备了，或者说差不多了，可是没人能派去稟报斯陶滕堡老爷——照他的命令，好让他来看他恨的这个人吊死。而囚徒无人看守也不稳妥。扬只是不时查看绳子是否仍捆得牢，其余时候，他的眼睛都钉在左边的雾上：那边是代尔夫特的方向，奥兰治亲王派来的复仇大军，转眼就要从那边雾里压出来了。
那些喘着、淌着汗的活人一走，霜寒比先前更苦、更咬人了；可皮特和扬谁也不大理会，虽然冻得皮肉发青。头顶上响起一片脚步杂沓；那几位高贵的先生们想必听见了乱子，也看见了溃逃；这会儿他们正准备着，以稍微体面些的做派，去干外邦雇佣兵和乡间粗汉方才干得那样慌不择路的事。
囚徒听见头顶的脚步声，警醒地四下张望。他看见扬和皮特在旁人逃散之后仍守着他。他也看见那堆摆起的兵器、断折的铁器、破布和烂泥，还透过木阶的缝隙，看见先生们穿靴子的脚乱哄哄地奔下台阶；一阵狂放的、快活的笑——那笑声的每一折都盛满欢喜——从他唇间迸了出来。
下一刻，远处穿过雾传来一声怪叫。一只警觉的狐狸正用它那古怪的叫声诱猎物——那叫声引得雏鸟心痒，壮着胆子凑近。一只狐狸跑到这些冰封的道上做什么？它必定迷了很远的路，兴许从贡达（Gonda）那边的荒原外头游荡过来；不用说，饥饿叫它成了浪游者，离乡的客。
扬侧耳听着——好生诧异——狐狸跑到这儿来做什么？皮特无动于衷，狐狸的习性他一概不知；海狼他倒熟些。扬本能地用眼睛去问那囚徒：
“狐狸跑到这片冰滩上来做什么？”他无声地问。
可那囚徒显然全不在意自然界的奇观，也不在流亡的狐狸。他昂着头，眼里闪着欢欣，一脸喜气的笑照亮了整张脸。
扬记起来，昨夜大伙儿管这伤号叫笑面骑士。

===Original===

A Cavalier he looked, every inch of him; the ropes mattered nothing, nor the torn clothing; proud, triumphant, happy, he was laughing with all the lighthearted gaiety which pertains to youth.
The Laughing Cavalier forsooth. Lucky devil! if he can laugh! Jan sighed and marvelled when the Lord of Stoutenburg would relieve him from his post.

===Chinese===

他看上去确确实实是一位骑士，通身上下每一寸都是；绳子算不得什么，撕烂的衣裳也算不得什么；骄傲，得胜，快活，他笑着，带着青春独有的那副没心没肺的欢畅。
名副其实的笑面骑士。走运的家伙！他还笑得出来！扬叹了口气，暗自纳闷，斯陶滕堡老爷到底几时才来换他的岗。

## XL The Loser Pays / 四十 败者付账
===Original===

Nicolaes Beresteyn had not gone far when Lucas of Sparendam came running with the news. He heard it all, he saw the confusion, the first signs of sauve qui peut.
At first he was like one paralyzed with horror and with fear; he could not move, his limbs refused him service. Then he thought of his friends﻿—some up in the molens, others at various posts on the road and by the bridge﻿—they might not hear the confusion and the tumult, they might not see the coming sauve qui peut; they might not hear that the Stadtholder’s spies are on the alert, and that his bodyguard might be here at any time.
Just then the disbanding began. Nicolaes Beresteyn pushed his way through the fighting, quarrelling crowd to where Lucas of Sparendam, still exhausted and weak, was leaning up against a beam.
“Their lordships up in the molens,” he said in a voice still choked with fear, “and the Lord of Stoutenburg in the hut with the jongejuffrouw﻿ ﻿… Come and tell them at once all that you know.”
And he dragged Lucas of Sparendam in his wake.
The Lord of Stoutenburg was at Gilda’s feet when Beresteyn ran in with Lucas to tell him the news.
After he had given Jan the orders to prepare the gallows for the summary execution of the prisoner he had resumed his wild, restless pacing up and down the room. There was no remorse in him for his inhuman and cowardly act, but his nerves were all on the jar, and that perpetual hammering which went on in the distance drove him to frantic exasperation.
A picture of the happenings in the basement down below would obtrude itself upon his mental vision; he saw the prisoner﻿—careless, contemptuous, ready for death; Jan sullen but obedient; the men murmuring and disaffected. He felt as if the hammering was now directed against his own head, he could have screamed aloud with the agony of this weary, expectant hour.
Then he thought of Gilda. Slowly the dawn was breaking, the hammering had ceased momentarily; silence reigned in the basement after the turbulence of the past hour. The Lord of Stoutenburg did not dare conjecture what this silence meant.
The thought of Gilda became more insistent. He snatched up a cloak and wrapping it closely round him, he ran out into the mist. Quickly descending the steps, he at once turned his back on the basement where the last act of the supreme tragedy would be enacted presently.

===Chinese===

尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦（Nicolaes Beresteyn）没走出多远，斯帕伦达姆的卢卡斯（Lucas of Sparendam）便飞跑着带来消息。他听全了，也看见了那阵慌乱——各自逃命（sauve qui peut）的头一批征兆。
起先他像被惊骇和恐惧钉住了，动弹不得，四肢不听使唤。随后他想起他的朋友们——有的在磨坊上头，有的在大路边、桥旁各守岗位——他们兴许听不见这场慌乱和喧腾，看不见就要到来的各自逃命；兴许听不到执政的探子已然警觉、他的卫队随时会到。
就在这时，队伍开始散了。尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦从推揉争吵的人群里挤过去，挤到斯帕伦达姆的卢卡斯跟前——他还虚脱着，有气无力，正倚在一根梁上。
“各位老爷在磨坊上头，”他说，嗓音还叫恐惧噵着，“斯陶滕堡老爷在小屋里陪着小姐……来，把你所知道的一五一十立刻寪给他们。”
他拽着斯帕伦达姆的卢卡斯，拖他在自己身后走。
贝雷斯坦拉着卢卡斯跑进来报信时，斯陶滕堡老爷正跪坐在吉尔达脚边。
给扬下完备绞架、就地正法囚徒的命令之后，他又恢复了那种狂乱不安的来回踱步。对这桩不人道又怯懦的行径，他没有半分悔恨，可他的神经根根都在震颤，远处那没完没了的锤击声，把他逼得几乎发狂。
楼下地窖里那一幕，总不由分说闯进他心里的眼：囚徒——满不在乎，一脸轻蔑，从容待死；扬沉着脸却唯命是从；手下们嗡嗡抱怨，离心离德。他觉得那锤击这会儿敲的全是他自己的脑袋，这煎熬悬心的一个钟头，痛得他真想放声尖叫。
随后他想起了吉尔达。天色慢慢破晓，锤击声暂时停了；经过方才一个钟头的骚乱，地窖里静了下来。这静意味着什么，斯陶滕堡老爷不敢猜。
吉尔达的念头越催越紧。他抓起一件斗篷紧紧裹上，跑进雾里。他快步下了台阶，一转身，把地窖抛在身后——这出大悲剧的最后一幕，转眼就要在那里上演。

===Original===

He felt like a man pursued, with the angel of Nemesis close to his heels, hourglass in hand to mark the hour of retribution.
He hoped to find rest and peace beside Gilda; he would not tell her that he had condemned the man to death. Let her forget him peaceably and naturally; the events of today would surely obliterate other matters from her mind. What was the life of a foreign vagabond beside the destinies of Holland which an avenging God would help to settle today?
The Lord of Stoutenburg had walked rapidly to the hut where he hoped to find Gilda ready to receive him. He knocked at the door and Maria opened it to him. To his infinite relief she told him that the jongejuffrouw had broken her fast and would gladly speak with him.
Gilda, he thought, looked very pale and fragile in the dim light of two or three tallow candles placed in sconces about the room. There were dark circles round her eyes, and a pathetic trembling of her lips proclaimed the near presence of tears.
But there was an atmosphere of peace in the tiny room, with its humble little bits of furniture and the huge earthenware stove from which the pleasing glow of a wood fire emanated and shed a cheerful radiance around.
The Lord of Stoutenburg felt that here in Gilda’s presence he could forget his ambitions and his crimes, the man whom he was so foully putting to death, his jealousies and even his revenge.
He drew a low chair close to her and half-sitting, half-kneeling, began speaking to her as gently, as simply as his harsh voice and impatient temperament would allow. He spoke mostly about the future, only touching very casually on the pain which she had caused him by her unjust suspicions of him.
Gilda listened to him in silence for awhile. She was collecting all her willpower, all her strength of purpose for the task which lay before her﻿—the task of softening a hardened and treacherous heart, of rousing in it a spark of chivalry and of honour so that it showed mercy there where it now threatened injustice, cruelty and almost inhuman cowardice.
A brave man’s life was in the hands of this man, who professed love for her; and though Gilda rejected that love with contempt, she meant, womanlike, to use that love as a mainspring for the softened mood which she wished to call forth.

===Chinese===

他觉得自己像个被人追索的人，复仇天使紧贴脚跟，手里擑着沙漏，掐算报应的时辰。
他指望在吉尔达身边觅得安宁；他不会告诉她，他已判了那人死刑。且让她平平静静、自然而然地把他忘掉；今日这些大事，定能把别的事情从她心里挤出去。一个外国的流浪汉的命，比起今日就要由复仇的上帝亲手裁断的荷兰的国运来，算得了什么？
斯陶滕堡老爷快步走到小屋，指望吉尔达已预备好见他。他叩门，玛丽亚（Maria）来开了。她告诉他，小姐用过早饭了，乐意见他——这叫他大大松了一口气。
在屋里壁灯上两三支牛油烛的昏光里，他觉得吉尔达显得非常苍白纤弱。她眼圈发黑，嘴唇可怜地微微颤着，说明眼泪就在近处。
可这间小屋自有一种安宁的气氛：几件寒素的家具，一座大陶炉，柴火宜人的红光从炉里漫出来，把四周照得暖融融的。
斯陶滕堡老爷觉得，在这里，在吉尔达面前，他可以忘掉他的野心和罪孽，忘掉他正卑鄙地处死的那个人，忘掉他的妒火，甚至他的复仇。
他把一张矮椅拉近她身边，半坐半跪着，用他那副粗嗓门和急躁性子容许的最大限度的温柔和质朴，同她说起话来。他谈的多半是将来，只随口带了一带她那些无端的疑心加给他的苦痛。
吉尔达默默地听了一阵。她正攢起全部意志、全部定力，应付摆在面前的这桩差事——软化一颗铁硬奸诈的心，在里头拨起一星侠气和荣誉的火苗，叫它在如今威胁着不义、残忍和近乎非人怯懦的地方，生出怜悯来。
一条勇士的命，就攢在这个口口声声爱她的男人手里；吉尔达虽鄙弃这份爱，却像女人惯常的那样，打算拿这份爱做发条，拨出她要唤起的那副软化心肠。

===Original===

The first thought that had broken in upon her after a brief and troubled sleep was that a brave young life would be sacrificed today to gratify the petty spite of a fiend. She had been persuaded yesterday that the man who﻿—though helpless and pinioned﻿—stood before her in all the splendour of manhood and of a magnificent personality was nothing but a common criminal﻿—a liar, a forger and a thief.
Though this thought should have made her contented, since by bringing guilt home to a man who was nothing to her, it exonerated her brother whom she loved, she had felt all night, right through the disturbing dreams which had floated through her consciousness, a leaden weight sitting upon her heart, like the sense of the committal of some great and irreparable wrong. Indeed, she felt that if here in this very place which he had filled last night with his exuberant vitality, she had to think of him as silent and cold for all eternity, such a thought would drive her mad.
The Lord of Stoutenburg’s honeyed words fell unheeded on her ear; his presence near her filled her with horror; she only kept up a semblance of interest in him, because he held the fate of another man in the hollow of his hand.
She was preparing in her mind what she was going to say to him, she rehearsed the words which were most likely to appeal to his callous nature. Already she was nerving herself for the supreme effort of pleading for a brave man’s life when suddenly the tramping of heavy feet outside the hut, confused shouts and clang of arms, caused Stoutenburg to jump to his feet.
The door was torn open, and Nicolaes Beresteyn stood for a moment on the threshold, pale, speechless, with body trembling and moisture thick upon his brow. Lucas of Sparendam was close behind him equally pale and still.
At first sight of her brother Gilda had uttered a little cry of joy; but that cry soon died upon her lips. Beresteyn had scarcely looked on her, his glance at once had found that of Stoutenburg, and the two men seemed to understand one another.
“We are betrayed?” cried Stoutenburg hoarsely.
Beresteyn nodded in reply.
“How?”
Lucas of Sparendam in short jerky sentences retold once more the tale of all that had happened at Delft: the Prince of Orange warned, the spies which he had sent broadcast, the bodyguard which even now was on its way.

===Chinese===

那一夜浅浅的、纷乱的睡眠之后，头一个闯进她心里的念头便是：今天，一条勇敢年轻的生命，就要为满足一个魔头的区区怨毒而牺牲了。昨天，她被说服了：那个男人——虽被缚着、无能为力，却以十足的男人气概和堂堂人格立在她面前——不过是个寻常罪犯，一个撒谎者、伪造者和贼。
这念头本该叫她心安才是：把罪坐实在一个与她无关的人身上，便洗清了她深爱的哥哥。可一整夜，穿过那些飘过她意识的乱梦，她始终觉得心头压着一块铅，像犯下一桩重大而无可挽回的错事的那种感觉。
确实，昨夜是他那蓬勃的生气充满了这间屋子；如今若要她在这里想他永远沉默、永远冰冷——这样一个念头，会把她逼疯。
斯陶滕堡老爷甜蜜蜜的话，落在她耳里一个字也没进心；他挨近她，只叫她满心憎怖；她对他维持着一份关注的假象，只因另一个人的命数，正扣在他的掌心里。
她在心里备着要对他说的话，一遍遍默排那些最可能打动他那副冷硬心肠的词句。她正给自己鼓劲，要为一条勇士的命做最后一搏，小屋外忽然响起沉重的脚步、纷乱的喊叫和兵器的锿锵，斯陶滕堡一跃而起。
门被猛地推开，尼古拉斯·贝雷斯坦在门槛上站了一瞬，面无人色，说不出话，浑身发抖，额头上凝着一层浓汗。斯帕伦达姆的卢卡斯紧跟在他身后，一样苍白，一样僵着。
一眼看见哥哥，吉尔达轻轻欢喜地叫了一声；可那声欢呼随即死在唇边。贝雷斯坦几乎没看她，他的目光立刻找上斯陶滕堡的目光，两个男人似乎彼此都明白了。
“咱们被出卖了？”斯陶滕堡哑声喊。
贝雷斯坦点头作答。
“怎么泄的？”
斯帕伦达姆的卢卡斯用一句句急促的短句，又把代尔夫特发生的一切讲了一遍：奥兰治亲王得了警讯，派出四下的探子，还有卫队——此刻已在路上。

===Original===

“They know of this place,” murmured Beresteyn between quivering lips, “they might be here at any moment.”
Through the open door there came the noise of the men fighting, the cries of rage and of fear, the clatter of metal and the tramping of many feet.
“They are scared and half mad,” said Lucas of Sparendam, “in five minutes the sauve qui peut will commence.”
“We are quite near the coast,” said Stoutenburg with outward calm, though his voice was choked and his tongue clove to the roof of his mouth, “go you and tell the others, Beresteyn,” he added, turning to his friend, “then collect all our papers that are in the molens. Thank God there are only a few that might compromise us at all. Heemskerk and van Does will help you, they are not like to be seized with panic. We can then make quietly for Scheveningen, where the boats are ready. There is a sledge here and a pair of horses which I shall need; but it is less than a league to Scheveningen, and you can all walk it easily. Tell the others not to lose time and I will follow with the sledge as soon as may be. There is no cause for a panic and we can all save ourselves.”
Beresteyn made ready to go. He took less pains than Stoutenburg to conceal his terror and his knees frankly shook under him. At the door he paused. He had suddenly remembered Gilda.
She had risen from her chair and stood now like a statue carved in stone, white to the lips, wide-eyed, her whole expression one of infinite horror.
It had all been lies then, all that Stoutenburg had told her yesterday! He had concealed the monstrous truth, lying to her with every word he uttered. Now he stood there pale and trembling, the traitor who in his turn has been betrayed. Fear and blind rage were fighting their last deathly battle in his soul. The edifice of his treachery was crumbling around him; God’s hand﻿—through an unknown channel﻿—had set the limit to his crimes. Twice a traitor, he had twice failed. Already he could see the disbanding of his mercenary troops, the beginning of that mad, wild flight to the coast, and down the steps of the molens his friends too were running helter-skelter, without thought of anything save of their own safety.
It would be so immeasurably horrible to fall into the Stadtholder’s hands.
And Gilda, pale and silent, stood between the two men who had lied to her, outraged her to the end.

===Chinese===

“他们知道这地方了，”贝雷斯坦嘴唇哆嗦着喃喃道，“随时可能到这里。”
敞开的门外传来手下人斗殴的声响、狂怒与惊惶的喊叫、金属的碰撞和许多双脚的踏动。
“他们吓破了胆，半疯了，”斯帕伦达姆的卢卡斯说，“不出五分钟，就要各自逃命了。”
“我们离海岸很近，”斯陶滕堡说，外表镇定，嗓音却发噵，舌头直贴上膛，“你去传给大家，贝雷斯坦，”他转向朋友，又添一句，“再把磨坊里咱们的文书统统收拢。谢天谢地，能牵连咱们的总共没几份。海姆斯凯克和范多斯会帮你，他们不至于慌了神。然后咱们悄悄往斯海弗宁恩（Scheveningen）去，船都备好了。这里有一辆雪橇和一对马，我用得着；好在离斯海弗宁恩不到一里格，你们走着去毫不费事。叫大家别耽搁，我尽快驾雪橇跟上。没什么可慌的，咱们人人都保得住性命。”
贝雷斯坦动身要走。他掩藏惊惶的工夫不及斯陶滕堡，两条腿明明白白地打着颤。到了门口他顿住，忽然想起了吉尔达。
她已从椅子上立起，此刻像一尊石雕，白到唇边，睁大着眼，整副神情是无边的惊骇。
原来全是谎话，斯陶滕堡昨天对她说的全是谎话！他把骇人听闻的真相藏了，吐出的每一个字都是谎。此刻他站在那里，苍白发抖——这叛贼轮到自己被人出卖了。恐惧和盲目的狂怒，正在他灵魂里打最后一场致命的仗。他叛逆的大厦正朝四面塌下来；上帝的手——借着一条不为人知的渠道——给他的罪划了界。两番为叛，两番落空。他已看得见他那雇佣兵马的溃散，看得见那场疯野的奔海大逃亡的开头；磨坊的石阶上，他的朋友们也正抱头鼠窜，心里除了自身性命再无他想。
落进执政的手里，那可就惨得没有底了。
而吉尔达，苍白无言，站在那两个对她撒谎、把她欺辱到底的男人中间。

===Original===

Nicolaes was a traitor after all; he had cast the eternal shroud of shame over the honour and peace of his house. An God did not help him now, his death would complete that shame.
She tried to hold his glance, but he would not look at her; she felt that his wrath of her almost bordered on hatred because he believed that she had betrayed them all. His eyes were fixed upon his leader and friend, and all the anxiety which he felt was for that one man.
“You must not delay, Nicolaes,” said Stoutenburg curtly, “go, warn the others and tell them to make for Scheveningen. But do you wait for me﻿—we’ll follow anon in the sledge and, of course, Gilda comes with us.”
And Beresteyn said firmly:
“Of course, Gilda comes with us.”
She was not afraid, even when he said this, even when his fierce glance rested upon her, and she was too proud to make an appeal to him. It was her turn now to avert her glance from him; to the bottom of her soul she loathed his cowardice, and the contempt with which she regarded him now was almost cruel in its intensity.
He went out of the room followed by Lucas of Sparendam, and now she was once more alone with the Lord of Stoutenburg.
“Gilda,” he cried with a fierce oath, “when did you do this?”
“It was not I, my lord,” she replied calmly, “you and Nicolaes did all that lay in your power to render me helpless in this. God knows I would not have betrayed you﻿ ﻿… it is His hand that hath pointed the way to one who was more brave than I.”
“ ’Tis false,” he exclaimed violently, “no one knew of our plans save those who now must flee because like us they have been betrayed. No sane man would wilfully put his head in the halter, and there are no informers amongst us.”
“You need not believe me, my lord,” she rejoined coldly, “an you do not wish. But remember that I have never learnt the art of lying, nor could I be the Judas to betray my own brother. Therefore do I pledge you my word that I had no share in this decree of God.”
“If not yourself,” he retorted, “you spoke of it to someone﻿ ﻿… who went to the Stadtholder﻿ ﻿… and warned him! to someone﻿ ﻿… someone who﻿ ﻿… Ah!” he cried suddenly with a loud and ghoulish scream wherein rage, horror and fear and a kind of savage triumph too rang out, “I see that I have guessed aright. You did speak of what you knew﻿ ﻿… to the miserable knave whom Nicolaes paid to outrage you﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

尼古拉斯到底是个叛贼；他把那袭永世的耻辱之衣，罩在了自家门楣的清誉与安宁上。上帝若此刻不救他，他的死会把这份耻辱补全。
她想接住他的目光，可他不看她；她觉出他对她的怒已近乎恨，因为他认定是她把大家全卖了。他两眼钉着他的首领兼朋友，他全部的忧急，都为着那一个人。
“你别耽搁，尼古拉斯，”斯陶滕堡简短地说，“去，警告大家，叫他们往斯海弗宁恩去。你在那儿等我——我们随后就坐雪橇来，自然，吉尔达同我们一起走。”
贝雷斯坦坚定地说：
“自然，吉尔达同我们一起走。”
她不怕，连他说这话时也不怕，连他那凶悍的目光落在她身上时也不怕；她也太高傲，不肯向他求告。这回轮到她把目光从他身上挪开了；她从心底憎恶他的怯懦，此刻她看他的那份轻蔑，烈得近乎残忍。
他出屋去了，斯帕伦达姆的卢卡斯跟在后面；她于是又同斯陶滕堡老爷单独相对了。
“吉尔达！”他狠狠咒骂着喊道，“这是什么时候干下的？”
“不是我，大人，”她平静地答，“是您和尼古拉斯，千方百计叫我在这件事里无能为力。上帝知道，我本不会出卖你们……是他的手，把路指给了一个比我更勇敢的人。”
“胡说，”他狂暴地喊，“知道咱们计划的，只有那些如今同咱们一样被出卖、只得逃命的人。没有哪个神志清醒的人肯自己把脑袋往绞索里套，咱们中间没有告密的。”
“您不必信我，大人，”她冷冷地应道，“若您不愿信的话。可请您记着，我从不曾学过说谎的艺，也做不成出卖亲哥哥的犹大。所以我向您担保：上帝的这一道旨意里，没有我的份。”
“若不是您亲手，”他反唇相讥，“您也把它说给了什么人……说给了一个去找执政的人……报了信！说给了一个人……一个……啊！”他猛地放出一声鬼哭似的尖喊，愤怒、惊骇、恐惧，还有几分疯狂的得意，一齐从里头迸出来，“看来我猜对了。您果然把知道的事说了……说给了尼古拉斯雇来凌辱您的那个可怜恶棍……

===Original===

and you offered him money to betray your own brother.”
“It is false!”
“It is true﻿—I can read it in your face. That man went to Delft yesterday﻿—he was captured by Jan on his way back to Rotterdam. He had fulfilled your errand and warned the Prince of Orange and delivered me and all my friends into hands that never have known mercy.”
He was blind with passion now and looked on her with bloodshot eyes that threatened to kill. But Gilda was not cast in the same mould as was this traitor.
Baffled in his crime, fear had completely unmanned him, but with every cry of rage uttered by Stoutenburg she became more calm and less afraid.
“Once more, my lord,” she said quietly in the brief interval of Stoutenburg’s ravings and while he was forced to draw breath, “do I pledge my word to you that I had no hand in saving the Stadtholder’s life. That God chose for this another instrument than I, I do thank Him on my knees.”
While she spoke Stoutenburg had made a quick effort to regain some semblance of composure, and now he contrived to say quite calmly and with an evil sneer upon his face:
“That instrument of God is an I mistake not tied to a post with ropes like an ox ready for the butcher’s hand. Though I have but sorry chances of escape myself and every minute hath become precious, I can at least spend five in making sure that his fate at any rate be sorrier than mine.”
Her face became if possible even paler than before.
“What do you mean to do?” she murmured.
“The man who has betrayed me to the Prince of Orange is the same man who laid hands upon you in Haarlem﻿—is that not so?”
“I cannot say,” she said firmly.
“The same man who was here in this room yesterday, bound and pinioned before you?” he insisted.
“I do not know.”
“Will you swear then that you never spoke to him of the Prince of Orange, and of our plans?”
“Not of your plans﻿ ﻿…” she protested calmly.
“You see that you cannot deny it, Gilda,” he continued with that same unnatural calm which seemed to her far more horrible than his rage had been before. “Willingly or unwittingly you let that man know what you overheard in the Groote Kerk on New Year’s Eve. Then you bribed him into warning the Prince of Orange, since you could not do it yourself.”
“It is false,” she reiterated wildly.
Once more that evil sneer distorted his pale face.
“Well!” he said, “whether you bribed him or not matters to me but little.

===Chinese===

“还许他钱，让他出卖您的亲哥哥。”
“这是谎话！”
“是真事——您脸上写得明明白白。那人昨天去了代尔夫特——在回鹿特丹的半路上被扬拿了。他办完了您的差使，报了奥兰治亲王的信，把我、把我所有的朋友，交到了一双手里——那双手从不知怜悯为何物。”
此刻他被欲火烧瞎了眼，拿一双血红的眼睛瞪她，瞪得像要杀人。可吉尔达同这叛贼，本不是一副模子里浇出来的。
他行凶受挫，恐惧把他整个人垮掉了；可斯陶滕堡每发一声怒吼，她反倒更镇定、更不怕了。
“再说一遍，大人，”趁斯陶滕堡咆哮的间隙、趁他不得不换气的当口，她静静地说，“我向您担保：救执政性命这件事里，没有我的手。上帝为此另选了一件工具，不是我——我为这一点，跪谢上帝。”
她说话的当口，斯陶滕堡已抢着定了一定神；这会儿他居然说得相当平静，脸上挂着恶毒的冷笑：
“上帝那件工具，若我没认错，正拿绳子拴在柱子上，像头待宰的牛。虽说我自己脱身的机会也少得可怜，每一分钟都成了金子，可我至少舍得花上五分钟，保他的下场无论如何比我更惨。”
她的脸，可能的话，比先前更白了。
“您想干什么？”她喃喃道。
“向奥兰治亲王出卖我的人，就是在哈勒姆对您下手的同一个人——是不是？”
“我说不上。”她断然道。
“就是昨天在这间屋里，被绑着缚着立在您面前的那个？”他追着问。
“我不知道。”
“那么您肯起誓，说您从不曾同他讲过奥兰治亲王，讲过咱们的计划？”
“没讲过你们的计划……”她平静地分辩。
“您燋，您否认不了，吉尔达，”他接着说，还是那副反常的镇定——在她看来，比先前的狂怒可怕得多。“有意也罢，无意也罢，您把除夕夜在大教堂（Groote Kerk）里偷听到的话，泄露给了那个人。随后您买通他去给奥兰治亲王报信——既然您自己去不成。”
“是谎话！”她激烈地一口咬定。
那副恶毒的冷笑又一次扭歪了他苍白的脸。
“好啊！”他说，“您买没买通他，于我关系不大。

===Original===

I do believe that willingly you would not have betrayed Nicolaes or me or any of our friends to the Stadtholder, knowing what he is. But you wanted to cross our plans, you wanted to warn the Stadtholder of his danger, and you﻿—not God﻿—chose that man for your instrument.”
“It is not true﻿—I deny it,” she repeated fearlessly.
“You may deny it with words, Gilda, but your whole attitude proclaims the truth. Thank God!” he cried with a note of savage triumph in his voice, “that man is still a helpless prisoner in my hands.”
“What do you mean?” she murmured.
“I mean that it is good to hold the life of one’s deadliest enemy in the hollow of one’s hand.”
“But you would not slay a defenceless prisoner,” she cried.
He laughed, a bitter, harsh, unnatural laugh.
“Slay him,” he cried, “aye that I will, if it is not already done. Did you hear the hammering and the knocking awhile ago? It was Jan making ready the gibbet. And now﻿—though the men have run away like so many *verdommde* cowards, I know that Jan at any rate has remained faithful to his post. The gibbet is still there, and Jan and I and Nicolaes, we have three pairs of hands between us, strong enough to make an enemy swing twixt earth and heaven, and three pairs of eyes wherewith to see an informer perish upon the gallows.”
But already she had interrupted him with a loud cry of overwhelming horror.
“Are you a fiend to think of such a thing?”
“No,” he replied, “only a man who has a wrong to avenge.”
“The wrong was in your treachery,” she retorted, even while indignation nearly choked the words in her throat, “no honest man could refuse to warn another that a murderous trap had been laid for him.”
“Possibly. But through that warning given by a man whom I hate, my life is practically at an end.”
“Life can only be ended by death,” she pleaded, “and yours is in no danger yet. In a couple of hours as you say you will have reached the coast. No doubt you have taken full measures for your safety. The Stadtholder is sick. He hath scarce a few months to live; when he dies everything will be forgotten, you can return and begin your life anew. Oh! you will thank God then on your knees, that this last hideous crime doth not weigh upon your soul.”
“A wrong unavenged would weigh my soul down with bitterness,” he said sombrely. “My life is done, Gilda. Ambition, hope, success, everything that I care for has gone from me.

===Chinese===

“我倒真信，明知执政是什么人，您断不肯有意把尼古拉斯、把我、把咱们哪个朋友卖给他。可您存心搅咱们的计划，存心给执政报他身临的危险，而挑那个人做您工具的，是您——不是上帝。”
“这不是真的——我不认。”她毫无惧色地重复。
“您嘴上尽可以不认，吉尔达，可您整个人的姿态都在宣吐实情。谢天谢地！”他喊道，嗓音里带着一股凶蛮的得意，“那个人还是我手里的囚徒，跑不掉。”
“您什么意思？”她喃喃道。
“我是说，把死敌的性命扣在自己掌心里，滋味很好。”
“可您总不至于杀一个毫无抵抗的囚徒。”她喊道。
他笑了，一阵苦的、刺耳的、不自然的笑。
“杀了他？”他喊道，“对，我正要杀——若不是已经杀完了。方才那阵锤子敲、凿子响，您听见没有？是扬在搭绞架。这会儿——虽说那些家伙像一群该死的（*verdommde*）懦夫跑了个精光，我知道扬至少还忠心守着岗位。绞架还立在那儿，扬、我、尼古拉斯，咱们有足足三双手，捆得住一个仇敌，吊他个不上天不下地；还有三双眼，瞧得起一个告密货死在绞架上。”
可她早已用一声压倒一切惊骇的大叫打断了他。
“你是恶魔吗，竟想这种事？”
“不是，”他答，“只是一个有仇要报的人。”
“那仇，就仇在你的叛逆上，”她回敬，义愤几乎把话头噵在喉咙里，“但凡正派的人，得知有人给同伴设了杀局，没有一个会坐视不报。”
“也许。可就凭我恨的那个人的这一句报信，我的性命实际上已经完了。”
“性命只有死才能完结，”她央告道，“而您的命眼下并无危险。照您自己说的，不出两个钟头您就到海边了。您的退路，想必早已安排得周全。执政病着，活不了几个月了；他一死，一切都会被人忘掉，您可以回来，重起炉灶另开张。哦！到那天，您会跪谢上帝：这最后一桩狰狞的罪孽，没有压在您的灵魂上。”
“血仇不报，我的灵魂只会被苦毒压沉，”他阴郁地说，“我这一生完了，吉尔达。野心、指望、功业——我在乎的一切，都已离我而去。”

===Original===

Nicolaes may begin his life anew; he is young and his soul is not like mine consumed with ambition and with hatred. But for that one man, I were today Stadtholder of half our provinces and sole ruler of our United Netherlands, instead of which from this hour forth I shall be a fugitive, a pariah, an exile. All this do I owe to one man,” he added fiercely, “and I take my revenge, that is all.”
He made a feint as if ready to go. But Gilda with a moan of anguish had already held him back. Despite the loathing which the slightest contact with such a fiend caused her, she clung with both her hands to his arm.
“My lord!” she entreated, “in the name of your dear mother, in the name of all that is yet good and pure and noble in you, do not allow this monstrous crime to add to the heavy load of sin which rests upon your soul. God is just,” she added earnestly, “God will punish us all if such an infamy is done now at this supreme hour when our destinies are being weighed in the balance.”
But he looked down on her suddenly, with an evil leer which sent a chill right through her to her heart.
“Are you pleading for a man who mayhap hath sent your brother to the scaffold?” he asked.
His glance now was so dark and so cruel, the suspicion which lurked in it was so clear, that for the moment Gilda was overawed by this passion of hate and jealousy which she was unable to fathom. The quick hot blood of indignation rushed to her pale cheeks.
“It was of Nicolaes that I was thinking,” she said proudly, “if that man dies now, I feel that such a dastardly crime would remain a lasting stain upon the honour of our house.”
“The crime is on you, Gilda,” he retorted, “in that you did betray us all. Willingly or unwittingly, you did deliver me into the hands of my most bitter enemy. But I pray you, plead no more for a knave whom you surely must hate even more bitterly than I do hate him. The time goes by, and every wasted minute becomes dangerous now. I pray you make yourself ready to depart.”
She had not given up all thoughts of pleading yet; though she knew that for the moment she had failed, there floated vaguely at the back of her mind a dim hope that God would not abandon her in this her bitterest need.

===Chinese===

“尼古拉斯还可以重活一场；他年轻，灵魂也不像我，没叫野心和仇恨烧空。可若不是那一个人，我今天已是半数行省的执政、尼德兰联省独一无二的君侯；如今倒好，从这一刻起，我成了逃犯、弃民、流亡客。这一切都拜一个人所赐，”他狠狠地补了一句，“我这就报仇，仅此而已。”
他虚晃了一下，像要走。可吉尔达一声哀叫，早已拉住了他。尽管同这样一个魔头哪怕沾一沾指头都叫她憎恶，她还是双手死死抓住他的手臂。
“大人！”她哀求道，“凭您亲爱的母亲，凭您心里尚存的一切善良、纯洁和高贵，别让这桩骇人的罪，再压上您灵魂里那一副深重的罪担。上帝是公义的，”她又恳切地说，“值此咱们的命运正在天平上称量的关头，若再行这等丑事，上帝会降罚咱们全体。”
可他突然俯视她，那眼光邪佞，一股寒气直透进她的心。
“您求情的这个人，”他问，“兴许正是把您哥哥送上断头台的那一个，您还替他求？”
他此刻的目光那样阴、那样狠，里面潜藏的猜疑那样分明，一时间吉尔达竟被这股她参不透的恨与妒的激情镇住了。义愤的热血，倏地涌上她苍白的脸颊。
“我想的是尼古拉斯，”她傲然道，“那个人此刻若死了，我觉得这桩卑怯的罪行，会在咱们家门誉上留一块洗不掉的污渍。”
“罪在你身上，吉尔达，”他反诘，“因为你把咱们全卖了。有意也罢，无意也罢，你亲手把我交进了我死敌的手心。可我求你了，别再替一个恶棍求情，你恨他必定恨得比我还狠。光阴在走，如今糟蹋的每一分钟都是危险。请你收拾收拾，准备动身。”
她还没有断绝求情的念头；虽然她知道此刻自己是败了，可她心底仍浮着一丝模糊的希望：在这最苦的难关里，上帝不会撇下她。

===Original===

He had helped her in her direst trouble; He had averted the hideous treachery which threatened to stain her father’s honoured name and her own with a hideous mark of shame; surely He would not allow this last most terrible crime to be committed.
No doubt that vague frame of mind, born of intense bodily and mental fatigue, betrayed itself in the absent expression in her eyes, for Stoutenburg reiterated impatiently:
“I can give you a quarter of an hour wherein to make ready.”
“A quarter of an hour,” she murmured vaguely, “to make ready?﻿ ﻿… for what?”
“For immediate departure with me and your brother for Belgium.”
Still she did not understand. A deep frown of puzzlement appeared between her brows.
“Departure?﻿—with you?﻿—what do you mean, my lord?” she asked.
